--- Page 10 ---
<meta>This page contains an entry from the Suda, a massive 10th-century Byzantine Greek encyclopedia. It provides a biographical sketch of Apollinarius of Laodicea, a prolific 4th-century Christian scholar and theologian, comparing him to the Great Cappadocian Fathers.</meta>

<page-type>text</page-type>
<sig>a 2</sig>

# FROM THE WORKS OF SUIDAS <note>original: "EK TON TOU SOUIDA"; refers to the Suda, a massive 10th-century Byzantine encyclopedia</note>

Apollinarius of Laodicea in Syria lived during the days of the Emperors Constantine and Julian the Apostate, and continued until the beginning of the reign of Theodosius the Great. He was a contemporary of the admired Basil and Gregory of Cappadocia <note>referring to the influential Church Fathers Basil the Great and Gregory of Nazianzus</note>. Indeed, he was an associate of both of them, as well as of Libanius the Sophist <note>a famous pagan teacher of rhetoric</note> and several others. This man was not only a grammarian and skilled in the arts of poetry, but he had trained much more extensively in philosophy; he was also a versatile orator <term>rhetor: a professional public speaker and teacher of persuasion</term>. He wrote thirty volumes in prose against the impious Porphyry <note>a Neoplatonist philosopher who wrote a famous critique of Christianity</note>, and he also translated the entire Hebrew Scripture into heroic verse <note>he rewritten the Bible in the style of Homeric epic poetry</note>. He also wrote letters and many other commentaries on the Scriptures.

Philostorgius <note>an Arian church historian whose work survives mostly in fragments</note> also makes mention of Apollinarius in his own history, saying: "For Apollinarius flourished during those times in Laodicea of Syria, as did Basil in Caesarea of Cappadocia, and Gregory in Nazianzus. (This latter place is a station <note>a military or postal relay station</note> within that same Cappadocia.) At that time, these three men were the champions of the 'same substance' <term>homoousios: the orthodox doctrine that Jesus is of the same essence as God the Father</term> against those of the 'different substance' <term>heteroousios: the Arian view that the Son’s essence is different from the Father’s</term>. They far surpassed all the previous and subsequent leaders of that sect up to my own time, so much so that even Athanasius <note>the famous defender of Orthodoxy from Alexandria</note> was judged as a child compared to them. For they had progressed to the furthest extent in what is called 'secular education,' and they possessed great experience in the Holy Scriptures—both in reading them and in ready memorization. This was especially true of Apollinarius, for he was even able to understand the Hebrew language. Furthermore, each was highly capable of writing in his own particular style. For Apollinarius, the style of commentary-writing was by far his best, whereas Basil [excelled] in the panegyric style..." <note>The text ends mid-sentence, describing the different literary strengths of these Church Fathers.</note>

<summary>This page describes the life and intellectual achievements of Apollinarius of Laodicea, highlighting his expertise in poetry, philosophy, and Hebrew, and comparing his rhetorical skill to Basil the Great and Gregory of Nazianzus.</summary>
<keywords>Apollinarius of Laodicea, Suda, Philostorgius, Basil the Great, Gregory of Nazianzus, Athanasius, Homoousios, Church Fathers, Byzantine Encyclopedia, biblical commentary</keywords>

--- Page 11 ---
<meta>This page continues the Suda's entry on the 4th-century scholar Apollinarius of Laodicea, specifically finishing a quoted passage from the Arian historian Philostorgius. Philostorgius is comparing the rhetorical and literary styles of Apollinarius, Basil the Great, and Gregory of Nazianzus.</meta>

...being most brilliant. As for Gregory, when compared with both of them, his style of speaking provided a broader foundation for his writing; one might say his style was richer than that of Apollinarius, but clearer than that of Basil.

While they possessed such great power in speaking and writing, these men also presented a character that was no less attractive to the public eye. Thus, through their appearance, their spoken words, and the many writings they distributed, they drew into their own circle <note>original: "koinonian," meaning fellowship or communion</note> all those who were susceptible to being won over by them in any way.

Philostorgius the Arian <term>Arianism: a 4th-century theological movement that held Jesus was created by God the Father and was not co-eternal with Him; Philostorgius was a historian who followed this view</term> wrote these things about them in passing.

<summary>This page concludes a comparison between the church fathers Apollinarius, Basil, and Gregory, praising their combined rhetorical skill and personal charisma. It notes that even the historian Philostorgius, despite his opposing theological views, recognized their immense influence.</summary>
<keywords>Gregory of Nazianzus, Basil of Caesarea, Philostorgius, rhetoric, Arianism, ecclesiastical history, character, literary style</keywords>

--- Page 12 ---
<meta>This page continues from the previous discussion of Apollinarius of Laodicea, Basil of Caesarea, and Gregory of Nazianzus. While the previous page focused on their rhetorical styles as noted by the historian Philostorgius, this page transitions to a passage from the historian Sozomen, detailing the political and educational challenges these men faced under the Emperor Julian.</meta>

### FROM THE WORKS OF SOZOMEN.

->a 2<-

Julian the Apostate <note>original: "parabates," a title meaning "the transgressor," commonly used by Christian writers to refer to Emperor Julian (r. 361–363 AD), who abandoned Christianity to restore Roman paganism</note> held this same opinion and took it as an opportunity against all Christians. Even when he had no formal charges to bring against them, he begrudged those who refused to offer pagan sacrifices their rights as equal citizens; he barred them from participating in public assemblies and the marketplace, and did not allow them to serve as judges, hold public office, or share in any civic honors. Furthermore, he did not permit their children to be educated in the works of the Greek poets and writers, nor to attend the schools of those who taught them. 

For he was deeply annoyed by Apollinarius the Syrian—who was highly trained in every branch of knowledge and literary style—as well as by Basil and Gregory the Cappadocians, who were outshining the famous orators of that day. Some of these men followed the doctrines established at the Council of Nicaea <note>The council in 325 AD that defined the orthodox relationship between God the Father and Jesus</note>, while others were from the Arian sect <term>Arianism: a belief that Jesus was a created being, subordinate to God the Father</term>. Believing that the power of persuasion was developed solely through such studies, Julian forbade Christians from training in the classical Greek disciplines.

It was then that this same Apollinarius, making timely use of his vast learning and natural talent, composed a history of the ancient Hebrews in heroic verse <note>dactylic hexameter, the meter of Homer's Iliad</note> to replace the poetry of Homer. He covered the history of the Jews from the beginning down to the reign of Saul, and divided the entire work into twenty-four parts. He gave each volume a title named after the letters of the Greek alphabet, matching them in both number and order <note>just as the Iliad and Odyssey are each divided into 24 books corresponding to the alphabet</note>. He also authored comedies modeled on the plays of Menander, and imitated the tragedies of Euripides and the lyric poetry of Pindar. To put it simply, he took the subject matter of the Holy Scriptures and used it to recreate the entire "circle of learning" <term>liberal arts: the standard Greco-Roman curriculum, including grammar, rhetoric, and philosophy</term>. In a short time, he produced an equal number of works...

<summary>This page describes Emperor Julian’s decree banning Christians from teaching or studying classical Greek literature and the ingenious response of Apollinarius the Syrian, who rewrote the Bible in the style of classical poets like Homer and Euripides.</summary>
<keywords>Julian the Apostate, Apollinarius of Laodicea, Basil of Caesarea, Gregory of Nazianzus, education ban, Greek literature, Homeric verse, Menander, Pindar, liberal arts</keywords>

--- Page 13 ---
<meta>This page continues the account of Apollinarius of Laodicea's literary response to the Emperor Julian’s decree. The previous page ended mid-sentence, describing how Apollinarius used his vast learning and noble nature to defend the Christian faith through literature.</meta>

-ature, and produced works that were equal in weight and power, matching the themes, phrasing, style, and structure of those Greeks who had been most esteemed in these fields. Consequently, if people did not honor antiquity so much, and did not consider what is familiar to be so dear, they would, I believe, value the diligent work of Apollinarius equally with the ancient authors and would teach it. They would admire his natural genius even more, seeing as each of the ancients applied themselves to only one discipline, whereas he practiced all of them and, in a time of urgent need, reproduced the excellence of each.

<margin>*</margin> Nor is his treatise addressed to the Emperor himself—or to the Greek philosophers—unknown; he titled it *On the Truth*. In this work, without relying on the testimony of the Holy Scriptures, he demonstrated through reason alone that they had wandered from the proper way of thinking about God. For the Emperor, mocking these efforts, wrote to the most prominent bishops of that time: "I have read, I have understood, I have condemned." <note>original: "anegnon, egnon, kategnon." A famous laconic wordplay by Julian intended to dismiss Christian intellectualism with three rhyming Greek words.</note>

But they wrote back to him in response: "You have read, but you did not understand; for if you had understood, you would not have condemned." <note>This witty retort mirrors Julian's own style, suggesting that the Emperor’s rejection of the faith was a failure of his own intellect.</note> There are some who attribute this letter to Basil, the leader <term>leader: here, "prostates," referring to his role as Bishop</term> of the churches in Cappadocia, and this is not unlikely. But whether it was his or not, it is right to admire the courage and education of whoever wrote it.

<summary>This page describes how Apollinarius of Laodicea created a Christian curriculum of equal literary merit to the Greek classics to bypass Emperor Julian’s ban, and recounts a famous, sharp exchange of letters between the Emperor and the Christian bishops.</summary>
<keywords>Apollinarius of Laodicea, Emperor Julian, Basil of Caesarea, Cappadocia, Christian education, classical literature, On the Truth, Julian's wordplay, Greek philosophy</keywords>

--- Page 14 ---
<meta>This page continues the account of the Apollinarii, father and son, and their literary response to the Emperor Julian’s decree. The previous page discussed their defense of the faith against the Emperor’s mockery and established their reputation for genius in multiple disciplines.</meta>

# FROM THE WRITINGS OF SOCRATES SCHOLASTICUS

<margin>
Socrates in the Tripartite History, Book 3, Chapter 16.
See also Nicephorus, Book 11, Chapter 12.
</margin>

The law of the Emperor <note>Emperor Julian the Apostate, who reigned 361–363 AD.</note>, which barred Christians from participating in Greek education <note>original: "hellenikes paideias," referring to the classical curriculum of literature, philosophy, and rhetoric.</note>, made the Apollinarii—whom we mentioned earlier—more famous. Since both were experts in literature—the father in grammar and the son in rhetoric <term>rhetoric: the original term "sophistikon" refers to the professional teaching of public speaking and persuasive argument</term>—they proved themselves useful to Christians during this present crisis. The father, a grammarian, immediately reorganized the study of grammar into a Christian framework. He translated the books of Moses into what is called heroic meter <note>original: "heroïkou metrou," the dactylic hexameter used by Homer for the Iliad and Odyssey.</note>, along with all the historical books of the Old Testament. Some he composed in dactylic meter, while others he crafted dramatically in the form of tragedy; indeed, he employed every kind of rhythmic meter so that no style of the Greek language would be unfamiliar to Christians. Meanwhile, the younger Apollinarius, who was highly trained in public speaking, set the Gospels and the teachings of the Apostles into the form of dialogues, just as Plato had done among the Greeks.

<summary>This page describes how Apollinarius the Elder and his son bypassed Emperor Julian's ban on Christian education by rewriting the Bible in classical Greek literary forms, including epic poetry, tragedy, and Platonic dialogues.</summary>
<keywords>Socrates Scholasticus, Apollinarius of Laodicea, Julian the Apostate, education decree, Christian literature, dactylic hexameter, Platonic dialogues, Old Testament tragedy</keywords>

APOLLINARIUS I

# APOLLINARIUS’S TRANS-
# LATION OF THE PSALTER.

### Psalm 1.
->A song of David, prophet and king.<-

<margin>I. Blessed <note>original Latin: "Beatus", the opening word of the Psalm in the Vulgate</note></margin>
<image-desc>An ornate initial woodcut 'O' featuring a central flower/rosette pattern within a circular border, surrounded by decorative leafy scrollwork. The initial spans approximately six lines of text.</image-desc>
**O**h, blessed is the man who has not walked
in the counsel of the ungodly,
nor set his step in the paths
of the wrathful,
nor sat in the destructive seats
of the spoilers;
but has a heart in accord
with the divine law,
singing the heavenly law both by night and by day.
He shall be like a sapling by watery paths,
bringing forth its honey-sweet fruit in due season,
blooming unceasingly with leaves that never fall;
may all things he accomplishes be directed according to his need.
Not such are the reckless, never such;
but like dust, which the wind has scattered from the earth.
Therefore the sinners shall never stand up in judgment,
nor the sin-minded in the counsels of the wise and righteous;
because the King <note>Referring to God</note> knows the path of the holy righteous;
therefore the generation of the wretched shall perish root and branch.

<sig>A</sig>

<summary>This page contains the opening of a 4th-century Greek poetic paraphrase of the Book of Psalms, attributed to Apollinarius, which renders the biblical text into classical Homeric hexameters. This specific section translates Psalm 1, contrasting the stability of those who follow God's law with the fleeting nature of the wicked.</summary>

<keywords>Apollinarius of Laodicea, Psalter, Psalm 1, hexameter, Davidic poetry, Late Antiquity, Greek literature</keywords>

<meta>This page continues the poetic paraphrase of the Psalter by Apollinaris of Laodicea, moving from the first Psalm to the second. The translation maintains the elevated, epic tone of the Greek hexameters.</meta>

<header>A P O L L I N A R I U S.</header>

->Psalm 2.<-

> This psalm was found without a title,
> though joined to the verses among the Hebrews;
> but even so, I sing of you, David, in the second place with sounding rhythms,
> since among them it is sent forth without such a heading.

<margin>II. Why <note>original Latin: "Quare", the opening word of the Psalm in the Vulgate</note></margin>
Why have the tribes combined with empty-minded, turbulent swelling,
and the kings of the earth stood gathered and ready?
And their rulers assembled together, coming as one,
against the All-King and against the Christ of God.
"Let us be troubled by their bonds no longer; let us cast away their yokes."
But the Lord who rules on high laughed at them from heaven,
and He held those senseless ones in derision.
Then He shall speak to them all, having become relentlessly angry,
and breathing out the fury of His heart, He shall terrify them all.
"I Myself was established by Him as leader with great power,
upon the holy mountain of Zion, magnifying the spring of youth,
proclaiming the decree of the heavenly King."
"You are my Son," the Father God spoke to me,
"on this very day I have been pleased to beget You.
Ask of me, so that You may receive the nations as Your inheritance from my hand,
and I shall place all the ends of the earth under Your possession.
You shall shepherd these with a rod of iron,
and shatter them like the vessels of a potter."
Now therefore, O kings of mortal men, understand;
be instructed, you who rule the heights under heaven.
Fear Him, and mix your joys of service with trembling;
and exult in Him, taking your delight with a fearful heart.
Lay hold of wisdom <note>The Greek text uses "sophia" (wisdom), whereas the standard biblical text usually says "instruction" or "discipline."</note>, lest the Good One be angry,
and you all perish together from the path of the godly.

<summary>This page presents Psalm 2 rendered into Homeric-style Greek hexameters. It depicts the futile rebellion of earthly rulers against God and His Anointed King, concluding with a call for kings to serve the Lord with fear.</summary>

<keywords>Apollinaris of Laodicea, Psalm 2, hexameter, Christology, Zion, Messianic prophecy, Davidic kingship</keywords>

# PSALM 2
A 2

...whenever He rouses His flaming spirit in heavy wrath, 
blessed are all those together who trust always in Him.

---

### Psalm 3

->The mournful third song of David, when fleeing<-
->his son Absalom, the king left his throne.<-
<note>The text uses a Homeric-style title to set the scene of David’s flight during his son’s rebellion (2 Samuel 15).</note>

<margin>3. O Lord</margin>
<note>original: "Dni." (abbreviation for Latin Domine)</note>
Why do my enemies so abound here around me? 
Many are rising up and opposing me as foes, 
saying to my soul that there is no help for me from God. 
But You, O King, <note>The poet uses the term "King" to refer to God.</note> fight for me continually, 
being my glory, lifting my head high above my neck. 
I call out crying, and He heard from His holy mountain. 
I myself laid down to sleep, and sweet sleep held me; 
I rose up when the King supported my strength. 
I will never shrink back in fear of tens of thousands of people, 
though they encircle me and press upon me all at once. 
Arise, and save me, O most high Savior of mine, 
for You have struck all my empty-boasting enemies. 
Moreover, You have shattered the terrible teeth of sinners. 
From You comes light, O King; grant Your blessing to Your people.

---

### Psalm 4

->The fourth psalm among David’s songs was composed.<-

<margin>4. When I called</margin>
<note>original: "Inuocantē" (abbreviation for Latin Inuocantem)</note>
The God of my righteousness heard me as I prayed; 
He heard me in my distress, and quickly warded off my pain. 
Be gracious, and O King, hear my prayers. 
O men, how long do you nourish a heart heavy with grief? 
Why do you set your heart on vanity and all lies? 
Know how the King showed His holy one to be wonderful; 
the heavenly King Himself shall hear me as I cry out.

# APOLLINARIS

Let a way far better than folly be established for you; 
ponder in your beds the cares of your own heart. 
Let hope and the sacrifice of righteousness honor the King. 
Many say, "Who will offer us life-giving prosperity?" 
But for us, the token is the golden light of Your countenance; 
You have granted all joy to my heart. 
From sweet grain, and from care-forgetting wine, <note>The Greek word *lathikēdēs* is a classic Homeric epithet meaning "sorrow-banishing" or "care-forgetting," here applied to the wine mentioned in the Psalm.</note> 
and from the life-bearing strength of oil, He has filled them. 
Together in peace I shall lie down to sleep, 
because in hope, O Blessed One, You alone sustain me.

->Psalm 5.<-
->The fifth song for her who receives the inheritance,<-
->David shouted with a joyful cry.<-
<note>The reference to "her who receives the inheritance" is a Greek poetic interpretation of the Hebrew musical term "Nehiloth" found in the title of Psalm 5, often interpreted by early commentators as referring to the Church.</note>

<margin>his words</margin>
<note>original: "s. verba", likely an abbreviation for the Latin "sua verba" (his words) or "sancta verba" (holy words).</note>
O highest Shepherd, hearken to our words; 
quickly understand my voice, and look upon my prayer from afar. 
You Yourself are Lord and God; I myself entreat You always. 
In the morning, the Lord shall hear our voice; 
from the dawn I shall stand openly in Your presence. 
<margin>c c</margin>
Because You are not a God who desires sin; 
nor shall any worker of evil dwell beside You, 
nor shall the lawless man share a hearth with Your sight. 
You have hated all workers of reckless folly alike; 
You have become the destroyer of the lying tongue among all. 
May God detest the deceitful and murderous man. 
But I shall enter into Your house, into Your home; 
in awe of You, I shall embrace Your pure temple. 
May Your righteousness lead me; for the sake of my enemies, 
make straight the path before Your eyes. 
For they never carry truth upon their lips;

<meta>This page continues the Greek hexameter paraphrase of the Psalms. It concludes the final verses of Psalm 5 and introduces Psalm 6. The translator continues to use Homeric-style language to match the Greek "epic" vocabulary used by the author to rewrite the biblical text.</meta>

# PSALM 5

The heavy-envying hearts of these men have become vain;
their throat is like an open grave,
and they have always been occupied with deceitful words on their tongue.
Judge them, O King; let them fall utterly from their own evil schemes,
and according to the multitude of their sins
let them be driven out from their possessions, for they have provoked Your power.
But let all those who pray to You, O King, be glad;
let them rejoice, and may You dwell among them.
Those who love Your name shall boast,
because You honor the man who is the witness to Your righteousness.
You have crowned us as with a glorious shield of good will. <note>The Greek "shield of good will" (eudokies hoplon) follows the Septuagint imagery, where God’s favor protects the believer like a massive shield.</note>

---

### Psalm 6.

->The King sang the sixth with the dear songs of David,<-
->a gentle, clear melody concerning the eighth.<- 
<note>The "eighth" refers to the musical term *Sheminith* found in the Hebrew title of Psalm 6, often interpreted in early Christian thought as a reference to the Day of Judgment or the Resurrection.</note>

<margin>
6
Lord, do not in Your anger
</margin>
<note>original: "Domine ne in furore," the Latin incipit for Psalm 6.</note>
O Pure One, let not Your heavy-wrathful spirit rebuke me,
nor let Your much-aching anger discipline me;
but have mercy on my powerlessness, O King.
Be my healer, and bring rest to my bones from their turmoil,
and to my soul, which is greatly bewildered and shaken.
And You, O Blessed One, how long will You increase my distress in Your anger?
Release my heart from necessity, setting aside Your former wrath;
deliver me, All-King, for the sake of Your mercy.
For who, being shackled by death, would remember You?
And who in the house of the dead <note>original: "aidao" (Hades). The poet uses the classical Greek underworld term to represent the biblical Sheol.</note> would sing a song of praise to You?
Indeed, I have labored in my groanings;
always throughout the night I shall wash my bed,
and with my tears I soak my sorrowful couch.
Distress, weighing upon me, has troubled my sight;
I have grown old among all my enemies.

A 3

# APOLLINARIS

Let a wrath greater than your previous recklessness be born in you; 
ponder the cares of your own dear heart while upon your beds. 
May hope and the sacrifice of righteousness together honor the King. 
Many say: “Who will show us life-giving prosperity?” 
But for us, the golden light of Your countenance is a sign; 
You have bestowed all gladness upon my heart. 
From sweet grain and from care-forgetting wine <note>original: "λαθικηδέος οἴνου" (lathikēdeos oinou). This is a classic Homeric epithet for wine, meaning "banishing sorrow."</note>, 
and from the strength of life-sustaining oil, He has filled us. 
As one, I will lie down in peace, milking the harvest of sleep <note>original: "ὕπνον ἀμέλγων" (hypnon amelgōn). A poetic, pastoral metaphor for drawing out or "milking" the benefits of rest.</note>, 
because, O Blessed One, You alone sustain me in hope.

## Psalm 5.

->For the fifth melody of her who receives the inheritance at the end,<-
->David shouted with a joyous cry.<-
<note>The "inheritance" refers to the Septuagint title of this Psalm, which early Christian commentators often interpreted as the Church inheriting the promises of God.</note>

<margin>5. words</margin> <note>original: "verba," likely a reference to the Latin incipit of Psalm 5, "Verba mea auribus" (Give ear to my words).</note>
Hearken to our words, O highest Shepherd; 
understand my voice quickly, and look upon my cry. 
You are the King and God; I myself entreat You always. 
O King, You shall hear our voice at the dawn; 
<margin>cc</margin>
from the dawn I shall stand before You openly, waiting; 
for You are not a God who ever desired wickedness. 
No evil-plotting dweller shall remain beside You, 
nor shall the lawless man be a guest within Your sight. 
You have hated all those who work at sin together; 
You have become the destroyer of the lying tongue among all men. 
God would abhor the deceitful and bloodthirsty man. 
But as for me, I will enter into Your house, 
and in fear I will embrace Your pure sanctuary. 
May Your righteousness lead me; for the sake of my enemies 
make straight the path before Your eyes. 
For they never carry the truth upon their lips;

# PSALM 5.

But for them, their envious hearts have become vain;
their throat is like an open grave <note>original: "λαυκανίη" (laukaniē). A poetic term for the throat or gullet, here mirroring the imagery of Psalm 5:9.</note>,
and they have always concerned themselves with deceitful words upon their tongues.
Judge them, O Lord; let them fall utterly from their evil counsels,
and according to the multitude of their own sins,
let them be driven out from their possessions, because they have provoked Your might.
Truly, let all who pray to You, O King, be glad;
let those men not rejoice [without You], for You dwell among them.
Those who love Your name shall boast,
because You honor the man who is a minister of righteousness.
You have crowned us as if with the glorious shield of Your favor.

### Psalm 6.

-> The King sang the sixth melody through the dear limbs of David, <-
-> a gentle, clear-toned song concerning the eighth. <-
<note>The reference to the "eighth" (Greek: *ogdoatē*) follows the Septuagint title of Psalm 6 (*hyper tēs ogdoēs*). In early Christian tradition, the "eighth day" symbolized the Resurrection or the world to come.</note>

<margin>6. O Lord, not in fury</margin> <note>original: "Ϛ Dne ne in furore," the Latin incipit for Psalm 6, *Domine, ne in furore tuo arguas me*.</note>
Let not Your pure spirit, heavy with wrath, rebuke me,
nor let Your very painful anger discipline me;
but have mercy on me, O King, for I am weak;
be my healer, and cause the shaking of my bones to cease,
and also my soul, which is tossed about, greatly distressed.
And You, O Blessed One, being angry, how long will You increase Your wrath?
Loosen my heart from necessity, putting aside Your former resentment,
save me, O All-King, for Your own sake.
For who, being bound by death, would remember You?
And who, in the pit of Hades, would sing a song of praise to You?
Indeed, I have grown weary with my groanings;
every night I shall wash my bed <note>original: "φαόφρονα λέκτρα" (phaophrona lektra). Literally "bright-minded beds," a sophisticated poetic way to describe the place of one's thoughts at night.</note>,
and with my tears I shall soak my soft couch.
Wrath, weighing heavy, has troubled my sight;
I have grown old among all my enemies.

### APOLLINARIS.

Depart suddenly from your painful wickedness!
For the Lord <note>original: "ἑστίη" (hestiē). Literally "hearth," used here as a poetic metaphor for God as the center and protector of the home/soul.</note> has heard the sound of my weeping.
God has heard the voice of our prayers;
the most high God has received our petition.
Let shame and turmoil befall my enemies;
let them be very quickly put to shame as they turn back.

Once Cush insulted David, but David, having been reproached,
entrusted these words to his friends for the famous shepherd; <note>The "famous shepherd" refers to David himself in his youth, or perhaps to the Chief Musician/God; Apollinaris often uses Homeric epithets for biblical figures.</note>

7
O Lord my God <note>original: "Dñi d. g", an abbreviation for the Latin "Domine Deus meus."</note>
</margin>
O King, I have always placed my trust in Your kindness;
release me from all who pursue me, and save me.
Lest a man snatch away my heart like a lion,
while I am entirely without strength and no protector is near.
But if I have done that which rouses Your wrath,
if I carry my hands in hateful and lawless deeds,
if I myself have evilly struck a doer of evil [who was at peace with me];
then let me fall helpless and suffering at the hands of my enemies.
Let a hostile man take my life while I still live,
and let him trample my life into the earth with his feet,
and defile my much-celebrated glory in the dust.
But arise in Your anger from Your mighty thrones,
and lift Yourself up against the ends <note>original: "πείρασιν" (peirasin). This refers to the "boundaries" or "utmost rage" of the enemies.</note> of my destructive foes.
Awake at Your commands, granting strength,
and let the whole assembly of the peoples encircle You;
over them may You, Blessed One, lead, being raised high on high.
The King Himself shall always be the dispenser of justice to the peoples.
Judge me, O King, according to the law of my righteousness;

# P S A L M 7

and may You grant things that accord with our righteous spirit.
Let there be an end to wickedness, but guide the righteous man.
For You alone examine the hidden depths of the heart and the reins <note>original: "νεφροὺς" (nephrous), literally kidneys; in ancient thought, these were considered the seat of the most secret emotions and conscience.</note>.
As a holy King, keep a helping hand over me,
always rescuing the upright tribes of mortal men.
The King is forever holy, a wise judge and lawgiver,
mighty in strength, yet possessing a spirit that bears no malice,
not fearing to rouse a swift wrath when necessary.
Cease, before He whets His long, gleaming sword,
and bends His bow, for it is already prepared.
He has fashioned weapons of destructive death against the wicked;
for those who burn with rage, He has prepared bitter arrows.
Behold now the lawless work of his [the enemy's] labor.
He conceived misery, and brought forth painful sin.
He dug a great chasm, hollowing it out with evil intent;
let him fall into the very pit of mischief he fashioned.
Let the pain return swiftly upon his own head,
and let his own violent pride descend upon his crown.
I will sing a worthy song of praise to the King of justice,
and I will proclaim the name of the Most High with a clear-sounding lyre <note>original: "φόρμιγγι" (phormingi), a classical stringed instrument used by Homeric bards.</note>.

<margin>9 I will give thanks <note>original: "Confitebor," the Latin opening of Psalm 9.</note></margin>
## Psalm 9
### An immortal hymn of David for the hidden secrets, for the sake of the morning light.

With my whole heart, O King, I will sing Your praise;
I will declare Your works clearly to all mortals.
In You I will find lovely gladness and fulfillment in joy.
I will celebrate Your most high name upon the lyre <note>original: "κιθάρῃ" (kitharē). The kithara was a professional version of the lyre, used in formal performances.</note>,
when the enemy is turned back by his own malice.
May they be destroyed, perishing before Your eyes.
For You understood my abusers according to the law,
and in the rule of justice, You sat upon Your mighty throne.
You threatened the nations, and the sinner perished instantly;
You blotted out their name forever among all people.
The weapons of the enemy have failed, and You overthrew their cities.
Their memory has perished utterly with a great noise;
But the heavenly King remains forever.
Justice has established His most high seat.

<meta>This page continues Apollinaris of Laodicea’s poetic paraphrase of the Psalms. Following the conclusion of Psalm 8, the text transitions into a lengthy hexameter rendition of Psalm 9. In the Septuagint and Vulgate traditions (which this manuscript follows), Psalms 9 and 10 are joined as a single poem, whereas modern Protestant and Hebrew bibles separate them. The translator uses "King" and "Immortal One" to render the divine names in an epic, Homeric style.</meta>

### PSALM 9

...so that He might judge the glorious ends of the earth with justice,
and extend straight judgment to His own peoples.
The immortal God is a resting-place from misery for the poor,
and He stands as a sufficient helper <note>original: "ἐπιτάρροθος" (epitarrothos). A Homeric term for a divine helper or ally in battle.</note> in times of heavy-heartedness.
You have become the hope of all who have learned Your name;
for You, O Shepherd, have not forsaken those who long for You.
Always play the lyre for the King dwelling in Zion;
announce the deeds of the Immortal One to the crowded nations;
for He remembered the fallen while seeking their blood,
nor did He at all forget the cry of the suffering poor.
O Savior from enemy men, have mercy on me,
You who lift me up even from the gates of death,
so that I may sing all Your praise at the gates of Zion.
I will rejoice greatly in Your help that wards off evil <note>original: "ἀλεξικάκοισιν ἀρωγαῖς" (alexikakoisin arōgais). "Alexikakos" is a traditional epithet of deities like Apollo or Heracles, meaning "averting evil."</note>;
the nations have fallen into their own miseries;
in the snare they used to hide, their own foot was caught.
The King bringing straight justice has become clearly manifest;
the sinner is shackled by the works of his own hands.
Let the sinners turn back again into Hades,
even all the nations that have forgotten the God who exists forever.
For He will not always and continuously forget the beggar,
nor does the steadfast hope of the weak ever perish.
Arise, let not man prevail by force;
let the tribes be judged before Your eyes.
Establish, O King, a law-giving hymn <note>original: "θεσμογράφον ὕμνον" (thesmographon hymnon). A creative rendering of the Hebrew word for "fear" or "law-giver," framing God's authority as a poetic decree.</note> over them;
let the nations know they are but of mortal race.

<margin>10 Why...? <note>original: "ut quid." The Latin opening of Psalm 10, which begins here in the Hebrew numbering.</note></margin>

¶ Why, O Most High, do You stand far off from those who look for You?
Why have You turned away Your watchful eye from those who are hard-pressed?
The poor man is consumed by the arrogance of the wicked man;
the sinful men perish by their own counsels.

->### APOLLINARIS<-

...the hope for which all the wanderers cried out with good omens,
and they speak of the lawless man with words that lack weapons <note>The poet suggests that the righteous or the poor are "unarmed," defending themselves only with words against the "lawless" (wicked) man.</note>.
The reckless man has stirred the spirit of the ever-living King;
raging immensely, he does not seek God, nor does he shrink from evil,
nor does he look upon God with his arrogant eyes.
Instead, he always journeys upon faithless and idle paths.
With his very eyes, he has denied Your laws.
He will rule over all his enemies, having become a worker of evil.
For he intended in his mind to remain firm, trusting in his own wickedness.
His whole mouth is stuffed with the ruin of most shivering deceit;
he always carries a deceitful tongue, a heavy burden of pain.
He sits plotting an ambush in secret places <note>original: "θωώκοις" (thōokois). A Homeric term for a seat or assembly place.</note>,
intending to slay the innocent with hidden counsels;
his eyes are always watching the poor man.
As a deadly lion in the thickets plots evil deeds,
so that he might swiftly seize the piteous beggar,
and fulfill the greedy plans of his hunt,
to trap the wretched and the proud in his deceitful net.
But he shall fall, stooped low, before the King of the poor is called upon.
For he claimed that the counsel of the ancient King is unmindful,
and that His all-seeing gaze has turned away entirely.
<margin>»</margin> Arise for me, O King, and lift Your hand on high;
<margin>»</margin> do not at all forget the cry of the suffering poor.

You see that You increase both labor and anger together,
desiring to seize the unwilling by force with Your hands.

or were You not previously the helper <term>epitarrothos</term> <note>A Homeric epithet for a divine ally who stands beside a warrior in battle.</note> of the orphans?

### PSALM 10

...nor would one find an end to your searching out of his wickedness <note>The poet suggests that God’s scrutiny of evil is so thorough that it exhausts the very existence of that evil.</note>.
But may God reign over all things continually;
may the tribes of evil-mindedness perish from their own land.
God himself has fulfilled the desire of the steadfast;
his ear has recognized the ready longing of their heart;
so that you might judge for the orphans and those without fame,
so that no man’s arrogance might possess old age <note>Original: μεγαλαυχία (megalauchia), meaning "great boasting" or "arrogance." The poet prays that the wicked do not live to a boastful old age at the expense of the weak.</note>.

### Psalm 11

->The tenth much-prayed-of praise of David.<-

<margin>In the Lord</margin>
How will anyone say to my soul, which is obedient to God,
to leap like a bird into the mountains to find pasture?
The wicked stretch their bows, and they have placed arrows in the quiver,
eager to shoot from the darkness at those of straight minds.
In their folly, they have destroyed what you, O Shepherd, have built.
But what has the man who is cared for by God done in such a plight?
The King of all, ruling in his most glorious temple,
having obtained the starry heaven as his high-necked throne <note>A vivid Homeric image of the heavens as a majestic seat.</note>,
<margin>2</margin>
stretches his all-seeing eye upon the poor man;
and with his eyelids he searches the deeds of the mortal soul.
<margin>3</margin>
The Lord himself judges both the good and the destructive man.
He who loves injustice is proven an enemy of his own soul.
From heaven he sends down mortal-consuming <note>original: "φθισίμβροτα" (phthisimbrota). An epic epithet meaning "death-bringing" or "man-destroying."</note> snares upon the harsh;
fire and sulfur together, and the breath of a joyless storm—
this shall be the portion of their cup of many sorrows.
<margin>»</margin>
Because the Lord is righteous, and he loves justice greatly.
And his gaze shall ever behold the divine path.

-> APOLLINARIUS <-

Each spoke to the other with fault-finding words;
they uttered with their lips while planning deceits in their hearts <note>The Greek *μητιόωντες* (metiontes) is a Homeric term for "devising" or "plotting," used here to describe the internal malice of the wicked.</note>.
May God swiftly destroy all those deceit-minded lips,
and the tongue of great boasting, so arrogant and grievous.
All those who have been lifted up by their own tongues,
and who thought to bring their works upon their lips, saying:
"Which mortal is fashioned as an unblemished king over you?"
<margin>”</margin> Because of the groaning of the fameless, the wretched, and the poor,
the mighty King himself intended to arise,
to place light openly and to act with brilliance.
The divine words—the words have become unyielding,
equal in value to silver tamed in a flaming furnace,
purified sevenfold <note>Original: *μοίραις πλειοτέραις* (moirais pleioterais), literally "by many portions," though here it reflects the biblical "seven times purified."</note> according to destiny.
But may you appear as our guardian and sentinel in all things,
from now, O King, and even until the end of life.
The wanderers walk around in circles with their deceits,
even as you, being good, increase the generations of mortals in their seasons.

## Psalm 12. ##
### The twelfth hymn of mighty David. ###

<margin>How long</margin> <note>Original Latin: *Usque quo*</note>
Why have you become so greatly forgotten of me?
How long will you turn away your kingly face?
How long shall I suffer baneful griefs in my heart,
pained in my soul <note>Original: *πραπίδεσσιν* (prapidessin), an epic term for the midriff or the seat of the mind/spirit.</note> both night and day?
How long shall my enemy lift himself high above my head?
But look upon me quickly, and hear my voice.
<margin>”</margin> Give light, so that I may escape the sleep of death,
lest my enemy shout aloud that he has conquered me—
<meta>catchword: (will conquer</meta>
if I were conquered, the hostile men would rejoice.
<margin>—</margin> But I, trusting in your mercy, rejoice beyond hope.

# PSALM 13 #

may I always rejoice in my heart with your help.
To the well-fortified King I shall sing a much-entreated hymn,
and the name of the Most High I shout in clear melodies.

->Psalm 14.<-
->The thirteenth hymn of mighty David.<- <note>Original: *τρισκαιδέκατος* (triskaidekatos). Note that the hymn count—13th—differs from the Psalm number—14—provided in the rubric.</note>

<margin>14 The fool said</margin> <note>Original Latin: *Dixit insipiens*. This is Psalm 14 in the Hebrew/King James tradition, but Psalm 13 in the Septuagint/Vulgate.</note>
The fool no longer said "God" within his breast.
Devising shameful things, the hateful man is revealed by his deeds.
There is no worker <note>Original: *ἐργαπίνης* (ergapines), a rare poetic term for a laborer or worker.</note> of good, not even so much as one.
The Shepherd looked down from heaven upon the mortal race,
to see if there were any who seek the Ruler, God.
All have turned aside; they have been overcome by a common ruin.
There is no worker of good, not even so much as one.
Those ignorant of good things are always counselors of destruction,
devouring my people as if they were easily-consumed bread.
In their foolishness, they forgot to leave a place for God the King.
They were greatly terrified where there was no cause for fear,
for God is in the midst of the most righteous generations.
Someone mocked the cares of the inglorious <note>The "inglorious" or "fameless" refers to the humble or poor.</note>,
because the immortal God was made their hope.
Who would grant from Zion a helper for Israel?
When God turns back the day of slavery for his people,
then let the far-seen Israel rejoice.

->By graceful David, inscribed upon a precious pillar,<-

<margin>16 Preserve me</margin> <note>Original Latin: *Conserva*. This is Psalm 16 in the King James tradition, but Psalm 15 in the Septuagint/Vulgate numbering used here.</note>

the swift diseases of impious men have advanced.

He is before my right hand, so that He might support me.

They have shut themselves up in their own unyielding fatness <note>Original: *ἀλοιφῷ* (fat or ointment). This is a poetic rendering of the Hebrew idiom meaning they are wealthy and have become insensitive or "thick-hearted."</note>;

<meta>This page continues the Greek hexameter paraphrase of Psalm 17 (Psalm 18 in the KJV) by Apollinaris of Laodicea. -> ### PSALM 17 <-
<note>In the Septuagint numbering, this is Psalm 17. In the Protestant/King James tradition, it is Psalm 18.</note>

The twisted cords of long-mourned fate sent me forth.
But I called upon God as a helper in my distress,
shouting for the helping hand of the King from heaven.
As I called, he heard my voice from his holy temple,
<margin>folio 46</margin>
and my cry reached the faithful ears of God.
The mighty earth was shaken, and a trembling seized it all;
the foundations of the high mountains were disturbed from their place,
trembling at the divine anger and the wrath of the Lord.
As the Immortal was enraged, boundless smoke arose,
and the strength of the Blessed One's face rose up, glowing with fire;
flaming coals were kindled from him.
He descended from heaven, bowing the flaming pole of the sky;
a gloom was carried under the feet of the immortal God.
Mounted upon his chariots, he flew upon the <term>Cherubim</term> <note>Original: *χερουβίμ*. In biblical tradition, these are high-ranking angelic beings often depicted as the bearers of God’s throne or chariot.</note>;
he flew, lifted upon the wings of the howling winds,
hidden in darkness; for he wished to take the darkness as his covering.
Around him he placed a glorious veil to encircle him,
the water hidden within the moist clouds of the air.
The clouds rushed forward beneath his brightness;
coals of rising fire were mixed with hail.
Then God himself thundered from heaven;
the Most High uttered his immortal voice from the air.
He scattered them with his divine, swift-running arrows;
they all were thrown into confusion by the thick flashes of lightning.
Then the pure, water-bearing springs appeared,
and the rich foundations of the whole earth were revealed.
O Immortal One, for all things trembled at your rebuke;
at the blast of the breath of your nostrils and your wrath.
He sent forth from heaven, and quickly rescued me from necessity.
He quickly drew me out from the many-roaring waters.

<summary>This page describes a dramatic divine intervention where God descends from heaven amidst a storm of fire, smoke, and lightning to rescue David. It uses high epic Greek poetry to transform the biblical imagery of a storm-god into a Homeric-style scene.</summary>

<keywords>Theophany, Cherubim, divine wrath, David, poetic paraphrase, storm imagery, deliverance</keywords>

--- Page 37 ---
<meta>This page continues the Greek hexameter paraphrase of Psalm 17 (Psalm 18 in the KJV) by Apollinaris of Laodicea. Having described the dramatic storm and theophany of God, the poet now focuses on David’s personal deliverance and the moral reasons why God intervened. The text is written in an epic, Homeric style of Greek, typical of the 4th-century scholar Apollinaris, who sought to rewrite the Bible in classical poetic forms.</meta>

# APOLLINARIUS

He will rescue me from the enemy, in whom there is excessive strength,
and from the men who hate me, because they are stronger than I.
They struck me in my day of weakness, being girded for battle,
but my Lord fought as a champion before me, and he sustained my life.
He set me down courageously upon the wide-stretching paths.
He will rescue me from the enemy, and he will rebuke those who hate me.
May he grant a reward for my justice and a worthy glory,
this gain from the purity of my hands.
For I have guarded the beloved paths of the Immortal One;
nor did I conceive of another god in my mind—for indeed, there was none.
For I see his laws always in my living mind,
nor did I abandon the guarding of his undefiled names.
And I shall always follow in his blameless footsteps;
I will keep my mind for the King, far apart from sin <note>The word *ἀμπλακίη* (amplakie) is a poetic term for error or transgression, common in classical tragedy.</note>.
May he grant a reward for my justice and a worthy glory;
may he look upon the purity of my hands.

---

With a holy man, you would be a companion; you would become holy;
and with an understanding mind, you might learn innocence from the innocent.
Mingling in all things with a good man, you would hear good things;
nor, on the other hand, would you be twisted when present with a crooked mortal.
You yourself will always rescue the humble-minded people from ruin <note>The Greek *ἄτης* (ates) refers to a state of blind folly or moral ruin sent by the gods in classical literature.</note>;
but you shall blind the insatiable eye of the arrogant.
You yourself would make my lamp shine with great light at all times.

---

For us, the dark gloom would be flashed through by your radiance;
at your nod, I might escape the much-sorrowing trial of evils;
I might leap over a tower <note>This refers to the "scaling of a wall" mentioned in the original Psalm, here rendered as *πύργον* (pyrgon), a tower or fortification.</note>, trusting in the God who exists forever.
The paths of the immortal King are pure,
and the words of the King are as fire for the fearless.
He shields all those who place their hope in him.
For who is the blessed Immortal, except the immortal King?

It ends with a transition into praising God as the sole source of strength and protection.</summary>

# PSALM 17
->B 2<-

Or what god exists who was created apart from our God?
God has wrapped and girded me with surpassing strength;
He has made our path pure and blameless,
making my feet like the swift-footed deer <note>The Greek *κεμάδεσσιν* (kemadessin) refers to young deer or fawns, a term frequently found in Homer’s *Iliad*.</note>,
and he firmly fixed my beloved footsteps upon the highest places.
He teaches my hands to be strong and skilled in the many ways of war,
and he made my arms like a bow of solid bronze.
And you gave me a light for mortal men and a deliverer from distress,
and your right hand was always a champion for me.
By your wisdom, O Blessed One, my heart is guided always;
always your glorious wisdom teaches me.

---

I hold a firm footstep, for you have widened my paths;
no one among my enemies shall surpass me in the race by stumbling.
Nor would I cease from my force until they cease, being subdued;
nor shall a steady step come to them when they are hard-pressed,
but let them fall, tamed beneath our feet.

---

You armed me with life-sustaining strength <note>original: "ζωορέῃ" (zoorei), likely a poetic variation for strength that preserves life.</note> and noble toil in the heat of battle,
you bound the adversaries beneath my sandal.
The hostile ones were terrified in flight, turning their backs,
and those who hated me you slew with bitter destruction.
They themselves cried out, but there was no defender for them;
they called upon the Immortal One, but he did not listen to their prayers.
I would winnow them away as if they were the dust of a storm,
I would trample them like mud on the well-trodden paths.

<margin>17. 44</margin>
You rescued me from the strife of the common people, having saved me,
and you established me, O King, as the leader of the nations.
A people whom I did not know placed their necks as slaves under my yoke;
with ears quick to obey, they received my voice.
The children born of foreign lands yielded to me with lies <note>This refers to the "feigned obedience" of conquered nations mentioned in the traditional Psalm.</note>,
they withered away and came out trembling from their hidden strongholds.

# APOLLINARIS

Cease limping far away from the paths.
My King lives, and he was ever created with much praise;
may the most high God be the lord of our life.
Ever being an avenger, may he shatter the enemies together,
having subdued the people by our god-pleasing strength.
May he guard me from the malice of angry opponents;
indeed, O King, may you be far superior to those who rise up;
you shall save me from the evil-plotting of the destructive man.
Therefore, I will openly receive your song among the nations,
and I will hymn your most high name with the lyre;
for the unspeakable light of the King wards off evil.
You have fashioned mercy for the immortal anointed one <note>original: "χριστῷ" (christō), meaning "anointed one," referring here to King David but often interpreted by early Christians as a prophecy of Jesus.</note>, 
for David, and for his perpetual offspring.

<margin>19 The Heavens <note>original Latin: "Caeli," referring to the Latin title of this Psalm, *Caeli enarrant*.</note></margin>
The heavens declare the undying glory of God,

nor is there anyone who can escape his flaming ray when it appears.

# PSALM 18 <note>Psalm 19 in Hebrew/Protestant numbering.</note>

His faithful testimony plants wisdom in infants.
The judgments of God bring joy to hearts.
His shining command increases the light in one's eyes.
The fear of the Lord <note>original: "δεῖμα" (deima) – literally 'dread' or 'fear,' used here to describe the pious awe of God.</note> is always pure and firm;
the works of the King's <note>original: "ἄνακτος" (anaktos) – a Homeric term for a king or lord, here referring to God.</note> justice are always all-sufficient,

for in keeping them there is an abundant reward.

having washed myself of evil and painful recklessness.

and the meditation of my heart be before your eyes;

# Psalm 19 <note>Psalm 20 in Hebrew/Protestant numbering.</note>

->The nineteenth Psalm of David was produced.<-

<margin>20 May he hear <note>original Latin: "Exaudiat," the opening word of this Psalm in the Latin Vulgate: "Exaudiat te Dominus in die tribulationis" (May the Lord hear you in the day of trouble).</note></margin>
May God ward off the day of distress for the one who prays,
and may the immortal God of Jacob be your champion.
May he grant dear help from his holy dwelling,
and may the averter of evil come near from lovely Zion.
May he remember all your offerings,
and may he fill your burnt sacrifice with richness <note>original: "ἀλοιφῆς" (aloiphēs) – literally 'fat' or 'ointment,' referring to the richness of a burnt offering.</note>.
May the immortal one perform all that is in your heart,
and may he fulfill all your God-pleasing counsel.
We shall rejoice in the sound of your life-giving heart;
trusting in the immortal one, we shall always triumph.

# APOLLINARIS <note>Apollinaris of Laodicea, the attributed author of this poetic version of the Psalms.</note>

All the suitable gifts of your prayers have been fulfilled;
now I have learned that God saved his Christ; <note>original: "χριστὸν" (christon) – meaning "Anointed One." In the 4th century, this refers both to the Davidic king and to Jesus as the Messiah.</note>
and from heaven, with great eagerness, He hears him calling,
a glorious light from His own right hand with power.
Some, indeed, rely on horses and chariots,
but we, trusting in the Immortal One, are always greatly exalted;
their feet were bound, and they fell unawares with a great crash,
but for us who have risen, everything was made straight according to what is right. <note>original: "κατ’ αἶσαν" (kat’ aisan) – a Homeric expression meaning "according to fate" or "as is fitting."</note>
O Imperishable One, may You always save the strong-hearted King,
and may You listen whenever we call upon You in prayer.

## Psalm 20 <note>Psalm 21 in Hebrew/Protestant numbering.</note>
### This is the twentieth famous hymn of David.

<margin>21</margin>
<margin>Lord, in [your] strength <note>original Latin: "Dñe [Domine] in virtute," the opening of this Psalm in the Latin Vulgate.</note></margin>
O eternal King, spotless one, may he <note>Referring to the King/David.</note> rejoice in Your strength,
and may his soul always exult in Your help.
Because You gave him the pleasing delight of his soul,
and You fulfilled his prayer, and his lips received fruit enough;
going before him with gentle blessings, You make him glad;
You placed a crown of precious stones upon his head.
You granted a life of ease to him when he sought it,
to draw out his life continuously through long days.
He has famous glory by Your strength which wards off evil;
and You would grant him heavenly honor and even greater boast.
You would bestow all kinds of unending and ageless wealth.
May the blessed one be cheered by the appearance of Your face;
the venerable man placed his good hope in God,
and through the mercy of the Most High, he reached the borders of great courage.
May You arm Your hand against all adversaries,
may Your right hand find all those who hate You.
May they be burned up before Your eyes like a furnace;

In Your fiery wrath, You shall burn them all,
and the unwearied flame shall consume them as they perish.

and their wretched offspring from among the race of mortal men;
because, though they feared You, they plotted foolish sufferings against You,
and they devised an unstable plan within their hearts.
You have turned the backs of those who opposed You, <note>Literally "turning the back/shoulder of hearing," a poetic way to describe the enemies fleeing or being ignored by God.</note>
just as You intended when You completed Your vision.
But take courage, Shepherd, and be magnified in Your strength;
and I, for my part, shall celebrate Your celebrated power in song.

### Psalm 22 <note>Numbered as Psalm 21 in the Greek Septuagint and Latin Vulgate traditions. This is the famous "My God, my God, why have you forsaken me?" psalm.</note>

->The twenty-first song of David,<-
->sung regarding the help of the morning.<- <note>original Greek: "ἑῴας ἀμπλήψιος" (heōas amplēpsios). This translates the cryptic Hebrew title *Ayeleth Hashahar*, often rendered as "The Hind of the Dawn."</note>

<margin>22 My God <note>original Latin: "Deus meus," the start of the Psalm in the Vulgate.</note></margin>
Look upon me, O Blessed One, O Blessed One, why have You forsaken me entirely?
The words of my life are far from my failures, without cooling relief. <note>A poetic rendering of "the words of my groaning."</note>
I kept crying out to You, Blessed One, but there is no answer for me;
nor did I fail to know Your mind in my heart during the night.
But You dwell in the holy place, the unspeakable glory of Israel.
You were the manifest hope of our fathers,
and You rescued them from grievous toils when they called upon You.
They cried out to You, Blessed One, and they were saved from their distress,
nor did they find shame in their hope in Your immortality.
But I have become a worm and not a man,
a mockery among men, and a worthless insult to the people.
All who saw me before with their eyes mocked me;
they spoke with their lips, and they shook their heads in contempt:
"He placed his hope in God; let Him rescue him from ruin;
let Him snatch him from evil, if he is truly pleasing to Him."
For You, O King, released me from the secret chamber of the womb;

<meta>This page continues the poetic paraphrase of Psalm 22 (Psalm 21 in the Septuagint numbering). The previous page ended with the speaker acknowledging God's holiness and presence even in the midst of abandonment. Here, the poet continues the lament, transitioning from the speaker’s infancy to the physical and spiritual agony of the present moment, using vivid Homeric imagery to describe the enemies as wild beasts.</meta>

### APOLLINARIS

I call You my hope even when I was at my mother’s breasts;
I was cast upon Your trust while still dwelling in the secrets of the womb.
You Yourself are my God from the belly of my dear mother.
Do not stand far from me, for the day of necessity <note>The "day of necessity" refers to a time of unavoidable distress or fate.</note> is near,
and for me in my need, no other helper has been found.
Many horned calves have surrounded me,
and well-fed bulls hovered around me besides them.
They rushed at me, opening their mouths wide and terrible,
like a destructive lion roaring with a ravenous heart.
Like poured-out water, all my bones are scattered;
my heart has melted like wax in the middle of my belly.
My strength is dried up, just like a brittle potsherd; <note>A potsherd is a broken piece of ceramic pottery, used here to illustrate extreme dryness and fragility.</note>
the speech of my throat has now been parched and stuck within,
and they have dragged me down and placed me into the dust of death.
An infinite swarm of dogs surrounded me with their eyes,
and the assembly of evildoers enclosed me round about.
They pierced my hands and my feet together;
they counted all my bones with their eyes,
and after staring at me, they left me thus uncared for.
Raging boundlessly, they divided my tunics, <note>original: "χιτῶνας" (chitōnas). The poet uses the classical Greek word for a tunic or undergarment.</note>
and they cast lots, so that someone might carry off my robes. <note>original: "πέπλα" (pepla). A Homeric term for a fine robe or garment.</note>
But do not drive Your help far away from me.
Snatch my heart from the long-edged sword;
rescue my solitary life <note>original: "μουνογόναν" (mounogonan). In the Septuagint, this term (literally "only-begotten") refers to the soul as one's "only" precious life.</note> from the power of the insolent dog.
Save me, O save me King, from the grim mouth of the lion;
rescue me from the destructive horns of the unicorns. <note>original: "μουνοκερήων" (mounokerēōn). In the ancient world, "unicorns" (often referring to wild oxen or rhinos) were symbols of untamable strength and ferocity.</note>
I will declare Your name to my brothers,
and I will sing hymns to You in the midst of the God-pleasing assembly.
Let all who fear the Lord sing praise;
and let the seed of Jacob honor the King who is God.

# PSALM 21

And let the whole seed of Israel stand in awe of Him. 
For He did not despise the prayer of the poor man in former times, nor did He loathe it; 
neither did He turn away His gracious face from me; 
but He heard my pleas when I called upon Him. 
From You comes my praise in the great assembly of men; 
before the pious I would pay my dear offerings. 
The needy shall feast and satisfy their souls; 
god-fearing men shall praise the Shepherd; 
their hearts shall live for length of life <note>original: "βιότοιο" (biotoio). This implies a life that continues on, often interpreted as eternal life.</note>. 
Let the distant ends of the earth remember the Immortal One, 
and all the nations shall worship Him face to face. 
For sovereignty and kingship over the peoples belong to God. 
Those who crawl upon the earth <note>A poetic way of describing mortal human beings bound to the soil.</note> shall feast and pray; 
and all those who are shut beneath the earth shall worship Him. 
But my soul lives for Him, and I have told my own race of Him. 
Let these tribes tell the King-God of the generations to come; 
the heavens shall declare the good righteousness of God 
to a people yet being born, whom the Ageless One created in His goodness.

He settled me in a green and shady place;

# APOLLINARIS

I call You my hope even from the breasts of her who bore me.
I was entrusted to You while still dwelling in the depths of the womb;
You Yourself are my God from my dear mother’s belly.

Many horned calves have surrounded me,
and well-fed bulls have crowded all around me;
they have rushed at me, gaping with their hostile mouths,

my heart has melted like wax in the middle of my belly.
My strength has dried up just like a weak potsherd;
my tongue is stuck fast to my parched throat,
and they have dragged me down and set me in the dust of death.
A boundless swarm of dogs has encircled me from nearby,
and an evil-doing assembly of crowds has enclosed me.
They have pierced both my hands and my feet together;
they have numbered all my bones with their eyes.

and they cast lots to see who would carry off my robes.
But do not let my help wander far from me;
snatch my soul from the long-edged sword.

And save me, O King, from the mouth of the grim lion;
and deliver me from the horns of the destructive unicorns <note>original: "μουνοκερήων" (*monokerōn*). Literally "one-horned." In the ancient world, this referred to a powerful, untamable beast like the wild ox or aurochs, though famously translated as "unicorn" in older English Bibles.</note>.
I will declare Your name to my brothers,
and in the midst of the God-pleasing assembly I will sing hymns to You.

and let the seed of Jacob honor the King-God,

# APOLLINARIS

Your scepter and your rod have provided consolation to my heart;

overflowing with abundance in the face of my shivering <note>original: "ῥιγεδανῶν" (*rhigedanōn*). This is a Homeric epithet meaning "chilling" or "horrible," used here to describe the dread of the psalmist's enemies.</note> enemies.

### Psalm 23.

->Bringing forth a melody toward the glorious day of the Sabbath,<-
->David shouted aloud this twenty-third song.<-

<margin>24 Theta is the letter <note>original Latin/Greek: "24 Θη̅ est littera." This is a manuscript note likely referring to the numbering or a specific liturgical division.</note></margin>
The earth belongs to the King, and the world is the threshold of the earth,
the cities, and all those who dwell within them.
He himself established it upon the unharvested <note>original: "ἀτρυγέτοιο" (*atrygetoio*). A classic Homeric epithet for the sea, literally meaning "unvintaged" or "barren."</note> sea,
and by His own wise counsel, He crafted it above the rivers.
Who of the mortal race shall ascend the divine mountain?
Or who shall plant a beloved foot upon the holy ground?
He who possesses clean hands and an undefiled heart;
who has never surrendered his soul to vain things,
nor sworn a deceitful oath to his neighbor against his lot.
This man shall receive praise from the immortal King,
and would find the divine mercy of the Savior at once.
This is the generation of mortals desiring God the King,
longing to perceive the very God of Jacob.
Lift up your gates at once, O princes,

so that the King of Glory, having stepped forth, may pass through.
Who is this undefiled King of Glory?
The Immortal One, strong and irresistible in His rush;
the Immortal One who always delights in battle <note>original: "μνηχαρμὸς" (*mnēcharmos*). A rare poetic compound meaning "mindful of battle-joy." This reflects the "Lord mighty in battle" from the Hebrew text.</note> upon the very strong earth.

# APOLLINARIS

### PSALM 24

Lift up your gates quickly, O rulers,
and let the unbreakable bolts of the gates be spread wide;
so that the King, eager for glory, may pass within.
Who then is this King of glory, fashioned pure?
The Lord of powers alone, he is the imperishable King of glory.

### Psalm 25.

->David sang this twenty-fourth melody.<- <note>The Greek text here labels this as the "twenty-fourth" (*tetraton eikoston*) melody, though the header correctly identifies it as the twenty-fifth psalm. Such discrepancies are common in manuscript traditions where the numbering of the Septuagint and the Hebrew text differ or are miscounted by a scribe.</note>

<margin>25 at h 29 <note>original: "25 ad h κ θm̅". This is likely a liturgical or cross-referencing note in a mix of Latin and Greek abbreviations.</note></margin>
**I** exalt my heart to you, O King, in bold confidence;
may I, having placed my trust in you, never become a shameful reproach,
nor let me become a laughingstock among my destructive enemies.
For all those for whom God has become their hope are free from shame;
let those who deal treacherously in vanities be put to shame by their own evils.
May I know your paths openly, O Blessed One.
And teaching me in good and God-built <note>original: "θεοδμήτοις" (*theodmētois*). A grand epic term meaning "built by the gods," here repurposed to describe the divine origin of God's laws.</note> tracks,
may you lead me, your much-supplicating servant, according to your truth.
Since you, as a good protector, teach me all things that are fitting;
entreating you unceasingly, I run my course the whole day through.
But be mindful of your mercy.
From of old, according to all laws, you have held a compassionate heart.
Do not remember the recklessness of my stumbling youth;
but as you are God, perform deeds worthy of your mercy,
for the sake of the honored King.
God was ever gentle-minded and straight-judging;
therefore he showed the way of the holy to those of sinful mind.
He will lead the gentle spirit of men in justice,
and reveal his own paths to the mild-minded;
all the paths of the Immortal are true,
for those who seek after the testimony and the covenant <note>original: "συν ημοσύνην" (*sunēmosunēn*). An epic word for an agreement or treaty, used here to translate the biblical concept of a "covenant."</note> of God;
for the sake of the immortal, mighty King,

--- Page 51 ---
<meta>This page continues the Greek hexameter paraphrase of the Psalms attributed to Apollinaris of Laodicea. # APOLLINARIS

May you be merciful toward my many sins.
Who is the man fashioned to tremble so before the Immortal One?
Hastening toward virtue, he fulfills the law pleasing to God,
always feeding a joyful heart on the most blessed things,
and his children shall inherit the honored land.
To those who tremble before the Immortal One, there is extraordinary strength;
the name of the Immortal One brings help to those who tremble,
so that he may reveal his clear covenant to them.
I have fixed my eye unceasingly upon God,
because he has pulled my feet out of the deceitful snare.
Extend your all-seeing gaze to me from heaven, O King,
because I have become solitary and poor.
The many sorrows of my soul are weighted with grief,
but he himself will release me from cold necessity <note>original: "ῥιγεδανῆς ἀνάγκης" (rhigedanēs anankēs). A classicizing expression where "necessity" or "distress" is described with a Homeric epithet for something "shiver-inducing" or "chilling."</note>.
Be merciful, seeing my weakness and my toil.
Behold the multitude of my ruthless-hearted enemies,
who, hating me, have established a lawless enmity.
Care for my soul and guard me;
let no shame take hold of me from any side, since you have become my hope.
Because of you, I have found companions who are straight-minded and untroubled;
you have driven away every distress from Israel.

### Psalm 25.
## This is David’s twenty-fifth hymn.

<margin>26 Judge <note>original: "Judica," the Latin title for this Psalm.</note></margin> Judge me, O King, with our gentle-minded spirit;
do not subdue me, who trusts in a king of little strength <note>original: "ὀλιγηπελίῃ" (oligēpeliē). A Homeric term for weakness or powerlessness, here contrasting human frailty with divine power.</note>.
Come now, test me, and watch my counsel;
you would judge my kidneys <note>The "kidneys" or "reins" were considered the seat of deep emotion and hidden intent in ancient thought.</note> and heart with an all-warming fire.
The mercy of my eyes is caused by you,
and I have always been pleasing to your joyful will.

<meta>This page continues the Greek hexameter paraphrase of the Psalms attributed to Apollinaris of Laodicea. # PSALM 26

I have never gone to sit in seats of vanity,
nor have I traveled as a companion to deceitful wanderers.
I have loathed to be among the builders of destructive error;
I have avoided the evil and suspicious seat of the ungodly.
I will wash my hands in pure waters,
and I will circle your most pure altar with songs, O King,
until I sing a glorious praise with an auspicious sound;
and I will declare your marvelous works with careful words.
I have longed for the ever-visible seat of the divine halls,
and the blessed resting place of your glorious strength.
Do not destroy my soul along with the ungodly, O King,
nor ever grant my portion of life with murderers—
those whose hands are filled with reckless deeds,
and whose right hands are weighed down by troublesome bribes.
But I, hastening, have always led a gentle life.
Rescue me and have mercy, since I have obtained a steady footstep;
and I would sing of you, Blessed One, crying out with invocations.

---

### Psalm 27 <note>The heading follows the Septuagint numbering (26), but the text and margin align with the modern Psalm 27.</note>

## Before the majestic kingship of blameless David,
## he fashioned the twenty-sixth song for the assembly.

<margin>27 The Lord is [my] light <note>original: "27 Dns illuminat," an abbreviation for the Latin "Dominus illuminatio mea."</note></margin>
The King is my protection and my light; whom should I fear?
He himself is the champion of my life; who could terrify me?
When flesh-eaters <note>original: "σαρκοφάγοι" (sarkophagoi). A vivid Homeric term used here to describe the "wicked" who "eat up my flesh" in the traditional Psalm text.</note> were eager to approach my limbs,
my enemies and ruthless men fell down in weakness.
I do not care, not even if an entire army should come against me;
God is my hope; what fear of battle is there for me?
I requested one thing from God; this became deeply desired by me:
to dwell continually in the halls of the King,
to always watch the divine delight with my eyes,

<summary>This page concludes a poetic paraphrase of Psalm 26 and begins Psalm 27, using Homeric vocabulary to frame David’s trust in God as a heroic defense against "flesh-eating" enemies. The text emphasizes the speaker's ritual purity and his desire to dwell forever in God's "divine halls."</summary>

# APOLLINARIS

...always to attend the pure temple of the King.
The necessary time hides me in his dwellings,
covering me within the innermost recesses of the immortal hut <note>original: "καλύβης" (kalybes). A Homeric term for a hut or cabin, here used poetically for the Tabernacle.</note>.
He lifted me high above a sun-drenched, famous rock.
And now he has raised my head above my adversaries.
I have offered a sacrifice, marveling at the victim in the dwellings.
I shall sing to the Immortal One with psalms and beautiful words.
Hearken to my voice, O Blessed One, as I cry out to you;
hearken, and have mercy on me. My mind said to you:
"My heart longs to perceive your eye." <note>The "eye" of God refers to His presence or countenance.</note>
Seeking your easy-to-meet light, I ever turn myself.
Do not turn your graceful face away from me,
nor turn aside in anger from your servant.
May you yourself be a King who helps, and do not abandon me,
nor leave me, since you are my God and eternal defender.
For even if my father and lady mother left me,
the Immortal One received me and saved me by his guarding.
Order me, O Blessed One, to walk ever in your laws;
guide me in a good path because of my enemies.
Do not give me over to be a prey <note>original: "ἑλώριον" (elorion). In Homer's Iliad, this refers to bodies left as "carrion" for dogs and birds.</note> for the souls of my enemies;
for unjust witnesses stood up against me,
and lawlessness itself lied with unjust outrages.
I shall see the divine happiness with my eyes in the land of the blessed.
Place all your hope in the King and God;
take courage, and let your spirit ever be most strong;
place all your hope in the King and God.

# PSALM 27

...lest I become exactly like those descending to the earthly depths <note>original: "πυθμένα... χθόνιον" (pythmena chthonion). This refers to the pit or the underworld, known as Sheol in the Hebrew tradition.</note>.
Hearken to my prayer, O Blessed One, as I pray;
when I lift my hands toward your undefiled temple.
Do not let my heart join together with those of sinful mind,
nor suddenly subdue me along with those of wicked device <note>original: "κακορραφίης" (kakorrhaphiês). A Homeric term literally meaning "stitching together evil," often used of schemes or plots.</note>,
who speak peace to their companions,
but hide evils within their own breasts.
May they receive destructive ruin worthy of their toils;
Give to them, O King, what is worthy of their own deeds,
and let them receive gifts just as they have worked with their hands;
let them take a reward that mimics the justice of what they gave to their enemies.
Because they were altogether unmindful of the works of the Immortal One;
and they forgot the works of the Great God’s hands;
may you ever cast them down, and never raise them up at all.
The Immortal and much-praised God heard my voice,
the King himself has become my helper and my champion.
I know in my mind how much gain hope has found in his aid;
and my body has flourished like high-foliaged leaves <note>This is a vivid Homeric metaphor for vitality and renewal, comparing human health to the regrowth of a tree's canopy.</note>.
And to Him I will willingly sing a good and immense praise.
Ever was the King the force and strength of his people,
continually fighting in the forefront for his early anointed one <note>original: "χριστῷ" (christô). In this context, it refers to David as the "Anointed King," though Christian readers of the time would also see a reference to Jesus Christ.</note>.
May you save your people and your glorious inheritance,
and quickly raise them up, shepherding them with good things.

bring to the all-kingly God tender-skinned lambs;

# 32 APOLLINARIS

Bring honorable glory, worthy of His name, to the King;
pray to the Shepherd of the world in His undefiled court.
The indivisible voice of the Immortal boomed over the waters,
and the conspicuous Leader of glory thundered;
the King Himself stands over the many waters.
The voice of the All-King exists in its own strength,
the voice of the All-King is seen in distinguished works.
The divine voice shattered the very tall cedars;
may the Immortal split the strong cedars of Lebanon,
making them small as if they were a calf of Lebanon <note>original: "μόσχον" (moschon). The poet uses the Homeric style to describe the biblical image of mountains skipping like young animals under the power of God’s voice.</note>;
and the Only-Begotten <note>original: "μουνογενὴς" (mounogenēs). While this is the standard Christian term for "Only-Begotten," in this poetic context it refers to the wild ox or unicorn mentioned in the Psalm text.</note> is like a horned mountain creature.
The divine voice of the Shepherd cuts through the flame of fire,
the voice of God shaking through the desert where wild beasts are bred;
may God shake the total wilderness of Kadesh of the fields.
The sound equal to the King starts the deer on their run <note>original: "ἐλάφοισι" (elaphoisi). There is an ancient ambiguity in the Hebrew text between "making the deer give birth" and "making the oaks strip bare"; the poet here follows the "deer" interpretation.</note>,
He who boomed exceedingly, stripping bare the long thickets;
and let everyone in the temple of the blessed God sing His glory.
The King dwells there, His virtues flowing like a fountain.
May the Immortal and unceasing One sit always upon His thrones;
may He grant an unbreakable life to His own people;
may He increase the people in life-sustaining peace.

---

### Psalm 29

->The twenty-ninth holy song sounded,<-
->David having built a new house for the wise.<-

<margin>30 I will exalt <note>original: "Exaltabo" (Latin). This is the opening word of the Psalm in the Latin Vulgate, used here as a reference marker.</note></margin>
I would exalt Your power, because You received me, O Shepherd,
nor did You grant me as a joy to my exulting enemies.
Daily I cried out, and You healed my sickness;
You brought back my soul from the house of Hades <note>original: "Ἀΐδαο" (Aidao). A classical Greek name for the underworld, used here to translate the Hebrew "Sheol."</note>,
and You rescued me from those descending to the bottom of the earth.
