# 33
### PSALM 29 <note>Psalm 30 in modern Bibles. In the Septuagint and the Greek tradition followed here, the numbering is one digit behind.</note>
C

Sing to God with holy melodies on the lyre,
and let there be a praiseworthy, pure memorial for Him;
for while God is angry, his wrath is heavy-hearted,
but when He ceases His anger, the light of life is in His wisdom.
Joy shall replace the weeping of the evening by the dawn.
In my confidence I said, "Let there be no shaking of my future";
through your beauty, let there be undying strength from you for us.
But when you turned your face away, a trembling seized my limbs.
To you, O Blessed One, I cry out, and I pray to the ever-existent God.
What profit is there if I shed blood, and descend into Hades?
Shall the dust not defile you? <note>original: "μή σε κόνις μολύψειεν" (mē se konis molypseien). The poet follows the Psalmist's rhetorical question: can the dead in the dust continue to praise God's faithfulness?</note> And will it speak of your gladness?
Hearing me, the King took pity on me and stood as my helper;
He transformed my mourning, and exchanged my grief for a dance of joy;
He girded me with gladness, and warded off the day of sorrow.
My prosperity is to play the lyre for you, and the song possesses my whole being.
O Undying King of All, I will sing your praises continually.

## Psalm 30 <note>Psalm 31 in modern Bibles.</note>

->Carried along on a sacred path, this thirtieth song<-
->cries out through the divine spirit of David.<-

<margin>In you, O Lord, have I hoped. 31</margin>
Do not let me be put to shame, since you have become my hope;
in your blessed justice, O Blessed One, take care of me and guard me.
Incline your ever-hearing ear and save me more swiftly;
be our shield-bearing God as is required,
entirely like a house of help, so that you might save me;
for you yourself are my strong <note>original: "πολύχαλκον" (polykhalkon), literally "rich in bronze." A Homeric epithet used here to describe a firm or fortified dwelling place.</note> seat and my protective defense;
having become the guide of our life, may you lead us wisely.
Easily, O Undefiled One, may you save me from the hidden snare,
for you alone are the leader of our life.
Into your life-bearing hands I would place my dear spirit.

->Into your hands, O Lord, I commend my spirit.<-

# 34
### APOLLINARIS

<margin>
You have redeemed me, O Lord, God of truth. <note>original: "ἐδέμισά με dñε deus veritatis." This is the Latin phrase "Redemisti me Domine Deus veritatis" written using Greek characters and common Latin abbreviations (dñε for Domine).</note>
</margin>

As the ruler of truth, you have released me from my labors.
You have always loathed the guardian of unbreakable vanity. <note>original: "ματίης" (matiēs). This refers to empty or idols/vanities. The poet describes the "guardian" of such things as being opposed by God.</note>
But I, trusting in mercy, rejoice beyond expectation;
may I be glad, delighting my heart in your mercy.
Because you looked upon my weakness with gentle eyes,
you saved my soul from the grip of deadly distress;
nor did you trap me in the harsh hand of the enemy;
you established my footsteps on a wide and open path.

---

Have mercy on me in my weariness, for I am terribly distressed;
a heavy anger has clouded my vision,
and my belly, and my much-suffering heart.
I have come to the end of life, wasting away in constant pains,
and my years of life are fading away in groans.
My strength has failed through the wretched toil of poverty,
and a terrifying trembling has seized all my bones.
I have become an exceeding shame among all my enemies.
An object of blame to my acquaintances, and a terror to my friends outside;
those who saw me before fled far away from me.
I have been forgotten from the heart like a corpse that brings no shiver of memory.
I appeared exactly like a perished vessel, <note>A "perished vessel" or "broken jar" is a common biblical metaphor for something useless, discarded, and forgotten.</note>
having heard the proven slander of those living around me,
who gathered together against me,
plotting in their hearts to take away my life.

---

But I call upon you, God; for you are my hope,
O Blessed One, my life’s destiny <term>destiny: "κλῆροι" (klēroi), literally "lots" or "inheritances," referring to the appointed portions of a person's life</term> is in your hands;
rescue me from the hands of the hateful and the adversary.
Reveal your immortal face to your servant.
Save me for the sake of your good mercy,

---

lest shame seize me; for I called you as my helper.
Let the lawless be put to shame and brought down to the underworld. <note>original: "ἀΐδην" (aidēn). The poet uses the classical Greek term Hades to represent the biblical Sheol, the place of the dead.</note>

<summary>This page contains a poetic rendition of Psalm 31, where the speaker oscillates between gratitude for past deliverance and a harrowing description of present social isolation and physical decay.</summary>

<header>PSALM 31</header>

May the deceitful lips of men become voiceless,
all those who spoke wickedness against the holy man,
belittling him with much arrogance.
The unstinting abundance of your kindness was prepared,
which you hid and kept safely for those who live in fear of you;
you performed all kinds of help for those who sought it,
in the sight of the much-suffering human race.
May you cover them from the turmoil of mortals;
may you shelter them in your tents, far away from strife;
praise to Him who placed his marvelous mercy in a city. <note>original: "ἄστεϊ" (astei). The poet refers to the "fortified city" of the biblical text, a metaphor for God's protection.</note>
In my alarm I said, "I am cast out from your sight."
Yet from there, you immediately heard the prayers of my crying.
Let all the holy ones love the King, <note>In this poetic paraphrase, God is often addressed or referred to as "the King" (Anax or Basileus).</note>
because the King always cares for truth,
granting a worthy reward to the arrogant.
Be courageous, and let our heart be very firm,
as many of you as hope for the help of the heavenly God.

# Psalm 32

### This is the thirty-first song
### of wise David, beautifully sung. <note>The heading identifies this as the 31st song, though it is numbered 32 in the Greek Septuagint. This numbering often varies between ancient traditions.</note>

<margin>Blessed 32 <note>original: "Beati 32." This is the Latin incipit for Psalm 32, "Beati quorum remissae sunt iniquitates."</note></margin>
Blessed are those who were shown to be free from sins,
and those whose transgressions are no longer counted.
Blessed is he whose sins God has passed over,
who has a tongue that knows nothing of deceitful malice.
My aging bones rotted in hard-to-heal silence,
as I cried out unceasingly all day long.
For always, O King, your hand was heavy upon me;
I became wretched, because a thorn was fixed in my flesh. <note>The poet uses the image of a "thorn" (akantha) to describe the physical manifestation of guilt or divine discipline.</note>
I revealed my sin, and did not hide the ruin in my mind.

<meta>This page continues the poetic Greek paraphrase of Psalm 31 (Psalm 32 in modern Bibles) by Apollinaris of Laodicea. The previous page ended with the speaker beginning their confession. <page-num>36</page-num>
<header>APOLLINARIS</header>

I said, "I will confess my reckless deeds";
and you yourself have forgiven the guilt of my heart.
For this reason, the holy shall pray at every season,
even when the rush of flooding waters draws near.
You have become my tower against the distress that pours out;
make me a dear joy, setting me free from those who surround me. <note>The "surrounding" refers to enemies or troubles closing in on the psalmist.</note>
May you teach me from yourself which path I must travel;
upon you, Blessed One, I have fixed my eyes forever,
and I will rest the gaze of my eyes beneath your care.
Cease becoming just like mules and horses,
for they lack the glory of understanding.
Bind their jaws with bits of mountain-bronze, <note>original: "ὀρειχάλκοισι" (oreichalkoisi). This refers to orichalcum, a golden-colored alloy highly prized in antiquity.</note>
for they have no desire to come closer to you.
All kinds of scourges are always for the sinful,
but mercy shall surround the one who cares for God.
Let joy and gladness hold the men who please God,
and let a resounding glory be the concern of the upright in heart.

### Psalm 32 <note>This is Psalm 33 in modern English Bibles. The Septuagint, used by Apollinaris, numbers it 32.</note>

->This thirty-second hymn sounded forth,<-
->David rejoicing in the sweet music of the lyre.<-

---

<margin>Rejoice 33 <note>original: "Exultate 33." This is the Latin opening for the psalm: "Exultate, iusti, in Domino."</note></margin> Rejoice for me in the King, O men who serve justice;
immense praise is fitting for the upright.
Sing to me of the living God with a clear-toned lyre;
sing to him, letting a song loose on ten strings.
From your hearts sing a new hymn to the King of all;
let the supreme praise of justice be sung well;
because the word of the immortal God was made straight.
All the royal works of the King are very faithful;
He loves righteousness that cares for the people, and noble law.

# PSALM 32

The whole fruitful earth is filled to the brim with the kindness of God.

---

The heavens were made firm by the command of the King,
and all their host by the breath of his immortal mouth.
He gathers the roaring flow of the sea as if in a wineskin,
placing it by his skill in the hidden storehouses of the deep.
Let the glorious ends of the earth stand in awe of the Kingly God;

---

and let all who dwell upon the soil be moved by him.
For he himself spoke, and all things were brought to completion;
the Lord himself commanded, and they sprang forth all at once.
The Immortal One scattered the boundless plans of the nations,
and he brings to nothing the many-branching anxieties of the peoples;
he sets aside the decrees of the song-singing rulers. <note>original: "ὑμνοπόων" (hymnopoon). This refers to rulers who are celebrated in song or perhaps those who compose their own laws and decrees.</note>
But the counsel of the Immortal remains forever,
and the wonders of his heart from generation to birth-giving generation.
Always blessed is the race that trusts in its own King—
the people whom he chose from all others to be his own inheritance.
From heaven, the King stretched out his all-seeing gaze;
he saw the human race from his own royal chamber.
With his eyes, he watched the inhabitants of the whole earth—
he who alone fashioned the hearts of mortals with his hand,
who looks upon their deeds through all time in their innermost thoughts.
No great-famed king is saved by his own power;
nor would a giant escape through his terrifying strength.
A horse is a useless thing in wars for saving a man;
even with its fierce strength, it could not flee.
The eyes of the King are fixed upon those who revere him;
the eyes of the great God see those who tremble—
---
those who have placed their care in his divine mercy.
He rescues their souls from death that brings long sorrow,
and nourishes their spirits when they are pressed by the groans of famine.

<summary>This page contains a poetic Greek paraphrase of Psalm 33, celebrating God as the Creator whose word shaped the heavens and whose gaze monitors all human activity. It emphasizes the futility of military might and human strength compared to the protective mercy of God.</summary>

-> APOLLINARIS <-

Our heart hangs entirely upon the King,
because he has become our champion and our helper.
Let the mind rejoice in him, its heart warmed with gladness,
and in his glorious name our hope is founded.
May you yourself, O King, grant us your boundless mercy,
even as we, hoping in you, always await your aid.

### Psalm 33 <note>LXX numbering. This is Psalm 34 in modern Bibles.</note>

When he outwitted the hostile Abimelech <note>original: "Ἀβιμέλεχον." In the biblical story (1 Samuel 21), David feigns madness before Achish, the king of Gath, though the Psalm title refers to him as Abimelech. Apollinaris uses "outwitted" (literally "fooled with folly") to describe David's clever deception.</note>
and David departed, having changed his appearance;
he sang this song of victory and joy together.

<margin>I will bless (Psalm) 34 <note>original: "Benedicam 34." A Latin liturgical note likely added by a later scholar to identify the psalm by its Latin opening and Western numbering.</note></margin>

I will always call upon the King with auspicious words,
unceasingly my tongue shall bring forth his praise.
Let my heart be glorified in the immortal King;
let the gentle hear it and be well-gladdened in spirit.
Magnify with me the immortal Shepherd of the world,
and let us all, gathering together, exalt his name.
He lent a ready ear to my good cry,
and swiftly snatched me from every destructive distress.
Draw near to him and receive light in your minds,
so that you may always shine with blameless faces.
Thus the poor man cried out, and God heard his voice;
he guarded him from every shame and ruinous bane.
A ministering angel takes his station here and there,
encamping around the godly to save them.
Taste and see that the King is gentle. <note>original: "μείλιχος" (meilichos). This is a classic Homeric epithet for a kind ruler, here applied to God.</note>
Blessed forever is the man who trusts in his own King.
All you holy ones, always fear the King.
For among the godly, no one is left wanting for what they desire.
Hunger once overcame those rich in possessions when they were in want,
but the children of the godly are weighed down with great good things.

<page-num>39</page-num>

<header>PSALM 33 <note>LXX numbering. This is Psalm 34 in modern Bibles.</note></header>

Come here to me, dear children, and listen to my words,
so that I may teach you the fear of the holy <note>original: "ἀγῆος" (ageos). An epic term often used for gods or kings.</note> King.
Who is the man who desires to see a life of calm and plenty,
longing to behold many-blessed days forever?
Restrain your tongue from most shameful wickedness,
and keep your lips far from wretched deceptions.
Flee from the crafting of evil, and appear as a builder of good things,
always treading upon the lovely footsteps of peace.
---
<margin>Upon</margin> The eyes of the King look upon the righteous,
and he provides a most ready ear to their prayers.
But the Lord looks with his eyes upon those who craft sorrows,
so that he might destroy their memory from the earth.
---
<margin>With</margin> The godly cried out, and they found God as a helper;
they were guarded far from every kind of distress.
For those whose heart is shattered, God always knows how to help;
and in his spirit, he will save the man of humble mind.
---
<margin>The</margin> Many and frequent are the sorrows of the holy,
but the immortal Good One will release them from all of them.
The King himself cares for them and guards all their bones;
not one of them shall be shattered or perish.
---
The fate of sinners is made destructive and dishonorable;
and those who are reckless will always be hated.
The Lord will release the hearts of his servants from ruin;
they shall not go astray, as they have need of his help.
---
## Psalm 34 <note>LXX numbering. This is Psalm 35 in modern Bibles.</note>

->This thirty-fourth hymn was sounded,<-
->sung by David on his clear-toned lyre.<-

<margin>35 Judge [me] <note>original: "3 5 Judica." A Latin liturgical reference to the first word of the Psalm in the Vulgate (Judica, Domine).</note></margin>
O pure King, judge those who seek our destruction;
fight with great power against our enemies.
Taking up a shield and a sharp sword, be a helper.

<page-num>40</page-num>

# APOLLINARIS

Draw out the double-edged sword against the foes;
speak to my soul: "Your secure wall has been fashioned." <note>This concludes the opening plea of the Psalm, asking God to reassure the speaker's soul.</note>

---

Let reproach expose those who grieve my spirit,
turning back those who weave sorrows for me;
let them be like dust driven by dark winds;
and let the sharp messenger <note>original: "διάκτορος" (diaktoros). In Homer, this is a title for Hermes; here, Apollinaris uses it to refer to an Angel of the Lord.</note> of the Immortal one keep them away;
let their path be slippery and dark at the same time,
and let the messenger of the Immortal one quickly pursue them.
Because they secretly and vainly set a net for me;
vainly they insulted my heart with reproaches.
May a snare they do not expect seize them;
let the catch of the hunt they hid become their own prize,
perishing in the deceitful net which they themselves wove.

---

But my soul shall rejoice in the Lord,
and it shall exult in his light that wards off evil <note>original: "ἀλεξικάκῳ" (alexikakō). A classical epithet for a protective deity, here applied to the light/help of God.</note>.
All my bones shall say: "Who else, O Blessed One, is like you?"—
rescuing the poor from strong spoilers,
the poor and needy from greedy destroyers.
Witnesses rising up questioned me about things I never knew.
They fashioned a wicked reward of injustice,
seeking to leave my soul desolate <note>The Greek "ἄτερμον" suggests a boundless or endless state of distress.</note>.
But as for me, when they pressed me, I put on mournful clothing;
I subdued my heart, heavy with pain, with the toils of fasting;
and may my prayer return, turning back into my own bosom.
As for a friend or a brother, so I always grieved;
as one mourning, I was subdued in my mind, nursing my grief.
But they gathered together rejoicing against me;
scourges <note>original: "μάστιγες" (mastiges). Referring to the "smiters" or "attacks" mentioned in the Psalm.</note> were gathered together, though I did not know it at all;
their hearts were violently torn, yet the crowd did not understand.
And they mocked me excessively, and they tested me with insults.

-># PSALM 34<-

...biting their lips in silence. <note>The Greek text implies enemies whispering or suppressing a sneer.</note>
Speak, Blessed One, when will you look upon my inescapable distress?

---

Rescue my soul from that brood of evildoers;
save my <term>only-begotten</term> <note>original: "μουνογένειαν" (mounogeneian). In the context of the Psalms, this refers to one’s "dear life" or "unique soul," the only one a person has.</note> from the lions.
I shall celebrate the King in the holy assembly;
I shall sing your hymn among the mighty people.

---

Do not let those who are lawlessly hostile rejoice over me,
those who hate me for no reason, winking their eyes in malice.
For they spoke to me as if they desired peace,
but they nurse bitter thoughts, boiling with deceit.
Opening their mouths wide against me in heart-biting rage,
they said they were glad to be spectators of my ruin.
If you have seen this, Blessed One, do not pass me by;
do not distance yourself, O all-seeing giver of good gifts,
nor let my cause go unnoticed by your eyes.
Judge me, O King, according to your righteousness, O Undefiled One;
judge me by your mercy, all-seeing Shepherd.

---

Let not those hostile men gloat over us;
let their laughter never double the pain in my heart,
nor let them swallow me up in their dark throats.
Let shame seize all who delight in my downfall.
Let those who harbor arrogant thoughts be clothed in reproach.
But let joy and gladness surround the holy people;
let those who long for the peace of His servant
forever sing of the magnificent gifts of God.
Let my tongue constantly proclaim your righteousness,
singing your famous praise all the day long.

The blameless David, servant of the Immortal,
sang this thirty-fifth song to the Imperishable Ruler.

Brandish a double-edged sword against my enemies;
say to my soul: "I have become your secure shield."
---
Let disgrace rebuke those who torment my spirit;
turn back those who stitch together troubles for me.
Let them be like dust <gloss>the</gloss> driven by terrifying gusts of wind;
and let the sharp messenger of the Immortal One <note>original: "ἀθανάτοιο διάκτορος" (athanatoio diaktoros). Apollinaris uses the Homeric title for Hermes to describe the "Angel of the Lord."</note> drive them away.
Let their path be slippery and deep in darkness,
and let the messenger of the Immortal One pursue them swiftly.
For they secretly and in vain set a net for me;
vainly they shook my heart with their reproaches.
Oh, let a snare catch them when they do not expect it;
let the trap they hid become their own prize of the hunt,
perishing in the deceitful net that they themselves wove.
But my soul shall rejoice in the King,
and exult in the light of the One who wards off evil.
All my bones shall say: "Who else, Blessed One, is like You?"
Rescuing the poor man from strong oppressors,
the poor and the needy from greedy destroyers.
False witnesses rising up questioned me about things I never knew;
they repaid my kindness with a harmful wage,
leaving my soul in a state of endless bereavement.
But when I was pressed, I put on mournful clothing;
I humbled my heavy-pained heart with the labors of fasting;
and may my prayer return quickly to my own bosom. <note>This implies a prayer whispered or kept close to the heart, or one that benefits the speaker even if the subject remains unchanged.</note>
I grieved for them as for a friend or a brother;
as one dejected, I was overcome, nursing grief in my heart.
But they gathered together, rejoicing against me;
scourges <note>original: "μάστιγες" (mastiges). Referring here to people who act as "whips" or afflictions against the speaker.</note> gathered, and I did not even know it.
My inner parts were torn by force, and they felt no remorse.
They mocked me excessively, trying me with insults.

<header>P S A L M  34 <note>This is Psalm 35 in Hebrew/Protestant numbering, but Psalm 34 in the Septuagint (Greek) tradition used by the author.</note></header>

Men with insatiable secrets, gnashing their teeth upon their lips.
Say, O Blessed One, when might You look upon my inescapable distress?
Rescue my soul from this evil-flowing race.
---
But deliver my <term>only-born life</term> <note>original: "μουνογένειαν" (monogeneian). In this context, it refers to the soul as the most precious, singular thing one possesses.</note> from the lions.
I will celebrate the King beside the holy dwelling;
beside the mighty people, I will sing Your hymn.
Let those who are lawlessly hostile not rejoice over me,
those who hate me without cause, yet wink with their eyes.
For they spoke words to me that seemed like peace,
but they plot bitter things against me, raging with deceit.
Stretching their mouths wide against me in heart-biting wrath,
they spoke, rejoicing to be spectators of my misfortunes.
If You have seen this, Blessed One, do not pass me by;
do not stand far from me, All-seeing Giver of good things,
nor let my cause pass by Your eyes unacknowledged.
By Your righteous judgments, O pure King, judge me;
by Your mercy, All-seeing Shepherd, judge me,
---
lest these hostile men gloat over us;
lest their laughter ever double the anguish of my heart,
nor let them devour me with their throats.

Let joy and gladness attend the holy race
of those who always sing of the God's magnificent gifts,
desiring to see the peace of His servant.
May my tongue frequently ring out Your righteousness,
and sing Your many-famed praise all day long.

<page-num>42</page-num>
<header>A P O L L I N A R I S <note>This refers to Apollinaris of Laodicea, the 4th-century bishop to whom this Greek hexameter paraphrase of the Psalms is traditionally attributed.</note></header>

<margin>Psalm 36: "He said..." <note>original: "3 6 Dixit". The Latin "Dixit" refers to the opening of the Psalm in the Vulgate: "Dixit iniustus..." (The unjust man said...).</note></margin>
The lawless man spoke within his heart of his reckless deeds,
not carrying the fear of God the King before his eyes.
Nurturing a destructive tongue for deceitful pains,
he is always seeking to find hateful malice.
His treacherous words are concerned with error,
never loving the many-sided path of the holy.
But even upon his bed the wanderer plots reckless deeds;
he does not plan to do good, nor even think of it,
nor does he abhor the pain of many-troubled wickedness.
<margin>—</margin>O Immortal One, Your mercy reaches even to the sky;
and Your truth <term>dances</term> <note>original: "χορεύει" (choreuei). While the biblical source says God's truth "reaches" the clouds, the poet uses a word meaning to dance or move in a choir, giving the heavens a sense of joyful motion.</note> among the clouds.
Your righteousness is like the divine mountains;
You carry equal justice to the bottomless abysses;
You shall save the herds of four-footed beasts and the tribes of men together.
How You increase Your lovely goodness to its full measure!
<margin>—</margin>Under the shelter of Your wings, there shall always be hope for <term>mortal men</term> <note>original: "μερόπεσσιν" (meropessin). A classic Homeric epithet for humans, literally "those who divide the voice" or "articulate-speaking."</note>;
they flourish in the goods of Your well-trodden halls;
and may a gentle torrent of joy water them,
for the fountain of living life is with You, O Blessed One.
In Your God-pleasing light, let us see the light.
<margin>—</margin>O King, extend Your mercy to those who long for it;
and may You reveal Your righteousness to the upright in heart.
Let not the arrogant foot come upon me and shake me;
nor let the hand of the sinner, O Blessed One, disturb me.
There all the servants of recklessness have fallen;
they shall not rise again, but lie there in neglect where they fell.

---

<page-num>43</page-num>
<header>PSALM 36 <note>original: "ΨΑΛΜ. λϛ". In the Greek Septuagint numbering used here, this is Psalm 36; in most English Bibles, it is Psalm 37.</note></header>

<margin>Psalm 37. <note>original: "Psal: 37." This marginal note uses the Hebrew/Latin numbering to assist the reader.</note></margin>
Do not ever envy, I pray, when you see an <term>evil-doing man</term> <note>original: "κακοεργέα φῶτα" (kakoergea phōta). The poet uses the Homeric word "phōs" for man, giving the biblical text an epic, heroic quality.</note>,
nor be amazed at the sudden outbursts of wickedness.
For these, just like grass, are withered away in an instant,
falling swiftly like green herbs.
Lift up your hope to God and do gentle deeds;
and you shall dwell in the land, and <term>boundless wealth</term> <note>original: "πλοῦτος ἀπείρων" (ploutos apeirōn). While the Bible speaks of "faithfulness" or "security," the poet emphasizes the abundance of God's provision.</note> shall pasture you.
Rejoicing in the Immortal One, may you receive all your heart’s desires.
Reveal your ways openly to the King;
<image-desc>A small green ink decorative flourish in the right margin.</image-desc>
Hope and do not cease; He Himself shall bring all things to fulfillment.
And He will bring forth your righteousness like a bright light,
and your justice like the midday.
Entreat the mighty King, I pray, seeking His favor.
Never let a grudge against life's lot distress you,
nor let your mind be always troubled by the lawless man's folly.
Flee from terrible wrath, and leave behind your grim-faced anger.
Do not ever envy, I pray, painful sinfulness;
For those who sin shall perish in their evils,
but those who wait upon God shall possess the whole earth.
Yet a little while, and the hope of the sinner shall vanish away;
and when you search for his place, it will be found <term>lightless</term> <note>original: "ἄφωτον" (aphōton). The poet adds a visual element, suggesting the sinner's home becomes dark or non-existent.</note>.
But gentle-hearted men shall possess the whole earth,
exulting in the many blessings of peace.
The sinner will always watch for the servant of justice,
and he would gnash his raging teeth against him.
But the Most High, looking upon him, laughs sweetly,
knowing that his day of destruction is ready for him.
The evil-doers have drawn the two-edged sword,
they have stretched the string upon the invincible bow,
striving to seize the poor and to destroy the needy,
eager to slaughter with straight-aiming swords.

<header>APOLLINARIS <note>original: "ΑΠΟΛΙΝΑΡ." (APOLINAR.). This refers to Apollinaris of Laodicea, a 4th-century bishop who paraphrased the entire Psalter into classical Greek hexameter, the meter of Homer's epics.</note></header>

For whom your goodness comes, you increase abundance.

and in your light, which delights God, we shall see light.

Let not a foot of pride come upon me in my misery,

There all the servants of recklessness have fallen;
they have been driven out from there and shall not rise, left without care.

<meta>This page continues the poetic paraphrase of the Psalms by Apollinaris of Laodicea. We are now at the beginning of Psalm 36 (Hebrew Psalm 37), which traditionally offers wisdom on the contrast between the fate of the wicked and the righteous.</meta>

<page-num>43</page-num>
<header>PSALM 36 <note>Psalm 36 in the Greek Septuagint corresponds to Psalm 37 in most modern English Bibles.</note></header>

<margin>
Do not [be envious] 37. <note>original: "Noli 37." This is the Latin opening of the Psalm (*Noli aemulari*) followed by the Hebrew/Latin psalm number.</note>
</margin>

Never be envious, I say, at the sight of an **evil-doing man**,
nor be struck with wonder at the sudden successes of wickedness.
For these men, just like the grass, are quickly withered away,
falling swiftly just like the green herbs of the field.
Let your **hope** rise toward God and perform gentle deeds;
dwell within the land, and may its boundless wealth nourish you.
Rejoicing in the Immortal One, may you receive all your heart’s desires.
Openly reveal your paths to the King;
hope in Him and do not cease, for He Himself shall fulfill all things,
<image-desc>A small sketch in green ink, possibly representing a scorpion or a stylized plant/insect, is drawn in the right margin.</image-desc>
and He will bring forth your **righteousness** like the dawn light,
and your justice just like the sun at midday.
Yield to the mighty King, seeking His favor.
Never shudder <note>original: "ῥιήσης" (riēsēs). A Homeric verb meaning to shiver with fear or cold, here used for the trembling of anger.</note> with rage, lest envy for this life distress you,
while you are constantly vexed by a man’s lawless malice.
Flee from terrible wrath, and leave behind your joyless anger.
Never be envious of painful sinfulness;
for those who do wrong shall perish in their own evils,
but those who wait for God shall possess the whole earth.
For yet a little while, and the hope of the sinner will vanish away;
and though you search for his place, it will be found only in darkness.
But men of **gentle spirit** shall possess the whole earth,
exulting in the many blessings of peace.
The sinner will always keep a watch for the servant of justice,
and he will gnash his destructive teeth against him.
But the Most High, looking upon him, will laugh with delight,
knowing that his day of destruction stands ready.
The evildoers have drawn their **double-edged swords**,
they have stretched the string upon the irresistible bow,
striving to seize the beggar and destroy the poor man,
eager to slaughter those of upright heart with their straight-striking blades.

<note>The vocabulary below highlights key terms used in the translation above, emphasizing the contrast between the 'poor' (ptōchos) and 'just' (eudikiē) against the 'evil-doers' (kakoergees).</note>
**Psalm, evil-doers, righteousness, beggar, poor man, Psalm 36**

--- Page 71 ---
<meta>This page continues the poetic paraphrase of Psalm 36 (Hebrew Psalm 37). The translator, likely Apollinaris of Laodicea, continues to use Homeric Greek vocabulary and hexameter verse to render the biblical text, contrasting the ultimate downfall of the wicked with the stability of the righteous.</meta>

<page-num>44</page-num>
<header>APOLLINARIS <note>original: "Α Π Ο Λ Ι Ν Α Γ." – an abbreviation for Apollinaris of Laodicea, the purported 4th-century author of this poetic version of the Psalms.</note></header>

But their sharp sword shall pass through their own hearts,
and the Blessed One <note>original: "μάκαρ" (makar) – a term often used for the gods in Greek epic, here referring to God.</note> shall shatter their groaning bows into splinters.
A very little is better for the **righteous man** of high renown,
than if he were to gather the boundless wealth of sin.
The slender arms of the foolish shall be crushed;
but the Immortal One supports the glorious step of the righteous.
The pure King Himself knows the ways of the blameless;
the lot and inheritance of the good shall be forever more stable.
They shall not be put to shame in the harsh season of necessity;
even in chilling famine they shall be weighed down with the fullness of all things.
But the servants of sin shall utterly perish like a breath.
When the glory of the King’s enemies rises up,
they become exactly like smoke that fades away into nothing.
The **evil-doing man** borrows and does not know how to repay,
but the righteous man always shows mercy and bestows gifts on all.
Those who bless Him <note>The Greek "ἐπαφήσοντες" suggests those who reach out or touch with a blessing.</note> shall possess the prosperous earth,
but those who strike it with a curse shall utterly perish.
<margin><image-desc>A small hand-drawn green flower-like symbol or asterisk.</image-desc></margin> By divine decrees the steps of a man <note>original: "φωτός" (phōtos) – an epic word for 'man'.</note> are guided,
and God shall always delight in his pathways.
He shall not be shattered by a fall into the deadly rifts of the earth,
because the King supports his hand with His own helping palm.
Once I was young, and now old age has come upon me,
yet never have I seen the man who pleases God <note>original: "θεοτερπέα" (theoterpea) – one in whom God delights.</note> entirely in want,
nor his offspring searching for the bread that sustains life.
He has learned to show mercy and to lend all day long,
and his seed shall be like a **beautifully-leaved tree** for a blessing.
Flee from the weaving of evil and take heed of good works,
and you shall dwell in the land; for the King dearly loves what is right.
He protects those who strive for Him <note>original: "ἀθλητῆρας" (athlētēras) – literally 'athletes' or 'champions', used here for the faithful.</note> as His own.
They shall be guarded by the continuous help of the Immortal One.

<meta>This page continues the poetic paraphrase of Psalm 36 (Psalm 37 in modern Bibles), picking up from verse 24. The text remains in the Greek hexameter style of Apollinaris of Laodicea, who uses the language of Homer to express biblical truths.</meta>

<page-num>45</page-num>
<header>P S A L M  36 <note>original: "Ψ Α Λ Μ. λϛ" – In the Greek Septuagint, this is Psalm 36; in most English Bibles, it is Psalm 37.</note></header>

But those hated for their lawlessness shall be driven out,
and the tribes of the impious shall perish, pitiless of soul.
The servants of justice shall possess the whole earth,
and they shall inhabit it for endless times.
Truly, the mouth of the pious shall always sing;
with his tongue he shall proclaim royal justice.
He carries a heart teeming <note>original: "ἐγκύμονα" (enkymona) – literally "pregnant" or "swelling with," a vivid epic metaphor for a heart full of God’s law.</note> with the divine law.
Nor shall his footsteps be entirely hindered even if he should fall.
The sinner watches the servant of justice very closely,
and he seeks secretly to put him to death.
But God will not long leave him as a prize for the sinner's hands,
nor will He abandon him when he is judged, as one lacking victory.
Endure the All-King, and guard His paths,
and He shall bring you in later time to the inheritance of the land;
you shall behold the sinful tribes of those who perish.
I saw the evildoer exalted on high,
rising far above the cedars of fragrant Lebanon,
the ill-fated one! But as I passed by, he was no more;
nor did his very place appear to me as I searched for it.
Guard gentleness, that you may perceive all things clearly;
for good remnants remain for the messenger of peace.
But let the lawless race perish in utter ruin;
let the remnants of the impious waste away in bitter destruction.
The light of the King was made to be set upon the holy ones,
and for them in their trials, He sits as a swift shield-bearer <term>Shield-bearer: a protector who stands by one's side in battle</term>,
always stretching out His immortal helping hand to them.
He shall deliver those in need from the unjust violence of sinners;
they shall be saved from evils, for they have cherished a divine hope.

<page-num>46</page-num>
<header>APOLLINARIS</header>

### Psalm 37 <note>original: "Ψαλμὸς λζ" – In the Greek Septuagint, this is Psalm 37; in most English Bibles, it is Psalm 38.</note>

<margin>For the Lord's remembrance <note>original: "id dñi m" – likely an abbreviation for the Latin "in diem memoriae" or "in commemorationem," referring to the psalm's liturgical use as a memorial offering.</note></margin>
->Blameless David, recalling the mortal sufferings<-
->which he endured—and though suffering, he was superior to those pains—<-
->cried out this thirty-seventh song.<-

DO NOT let your pure spirit rebuke me in heavy wrath, 
nor let your painful anger discipline me;
for your arrows have been fixed deep within my limbs,
and you have pressed your hand hard upon me.
Who could heal my body when you are angry?
Or from what source could rest appear in my bones,
when I am driven on all sides by my gathering sins?
Griefs have risen high above my head;
they are like a heavy burden pressing upon my neck.
The wounds of my foolishness have rotted and grown foul around me.
I have become wretched, and look! my neck is bent down everywhere;
I walk the path all day long, grieving in misery.
For endless mockeries <note>original: "παίγνια" (paignia) – while often meaning 'toys' or 'playthings,' in this poetic context it refers to the delusions or the 'burning' mockers of the soul.</note> have consumed my soul;
my body knows no rest from pain.
I am cast very low, struck by the pangs of sickness,
howling with the groans of my inner heart.
O Immortal One, look upon my desire yourself;
my groaning did not escape you, who know all things.
My heart was troubled, and the vigor of my limbs departed;
the lovely light of my eyes has failed me.
Suddenly my friends were afraid, and my companions stood back.
Those who were coming near me turned away and kept their distance.
Ah, they sought my dear life with such intensity!
Ah, how they spoke vainly while hunting for evils,
sharpening their tongues all day long with deceitful insults.

<header>PSALM 37-38 <note>Greek: Ψ Α Λ Μ. λζ'</note> 47</header>

But I became like one who does not hear; 
unacquainted with all manner of words, 
like a man who understands nothing of what is heard, 
who does not know how to bring forth reproaches with his tongue. 
You are my hope; I pray to you, and may you hear me as I call. 
For I said, let me not become a source of joy to my enemies; 
when my foot slipped, the arrogant rejoiced over me. 
They hide my body, which is ready for the whip; 
and the sorrow of my eyes shall never leave me. 
I will not forget to speak of my lawless transgressions. 
My enemies flourish and are stronger than I; 
those who hate me unjustly have multiplied, 
who gave me an evil wage in exchange for good things, 
and they struck me with insults despite my righteousness. 
Do not stand far from me, All-seeing One, nor abandon me; 
send me immortal help, send your helping gaze.

### Psalm 38 <note>In the Septuagint numbering used here, this is Psalm 38; in most English Bibles, it is Psalm 39.</note>

->The glorious hymn of David, when Jeduthun <note>Jeduthun was one of the three chief musicians appointed by David for the Temple service.</note> sang<-
->the sweet melody of the thirty-eighth song.<-

<margin>Day 39 <note>original Latin: "Diei 39" – likely a liturgical marker for daily reading.</note></margin>
I said, I will guard my way far from our evils, 
whenever my many-worded tongue rouses my recklessness. 
I placed a guard like a shield upon my lips, 
when the reckless man, armed, contends against me; 
like a deaf man, and humble, I kept silence even regarding good things; 
and my pain, though laid to rest, was roused a second time. 
Within me, my spirit was warmed by a flaming torch, 
and in my meditation, an unwearied fire shall burn. 
I said, O Blessed One, tell me the end of my dear life. 
Tell me the number of years you have established for my life,

<header>APOLLINAR. <note>The abbreviated name of the author, Apollinaris of Laodicea.</note></header>

...so that I might know what else is left for me.
Indeed, O Shepherd, you have made all my days most fleeting,
and you knew the very root of my life to be of no account.
Vainly do the generations of all articulate-speaking men <note>Greek: *meropōn*. A classic Homeric epithet for humans, referring to their ability to use divided or articulate speech.</note> live;
yet truly man walks like a phantom, <note>Greek: *daidalmati*. Literally a "cunning work" or "ornate device," used here to suggest a shadowy appearance or a mere image rather than substance.</note>
tossed about in vain, his spirit stirred within him.
For some unknown person, he suffers to gather much wealth.
Was not the King made my hope and my tower?
From you yourself I received the life-giving support of my existence.
O King, you shall release me from all my transgressions;
he <note>The enemy or perhaps the personification of sin.</note> gave me as a reproachful blame to foolish men.
I stood mute and speechless, since you made me so.
O Blessed One, drive your scourges entirely away from me,
for I grieve in my spirit under your strong hand.
You have disciplined a man by rebuking him for his destructive sin,
and you have melted his helpless spirit like a spider’s web. <note>Where many English Bibles translated from the Hebrew mention a moth consuming a garment, the Greek tradition here compares human frailty to the fragility of a spider's web.</note>
Vain is the life of the articulate-speaking race.
Listen to my prayer, and give ear to my petition;
do not, O Blessed One, pass me by in silence as I shed tears.
For I am a guest on the earth and a wanderer,
just as all my fathers were likewise among the people.
Give me rest from my toils, that I may gather my dear heart,
before I go to the place from whence one never returns.

<header>PSALM 39 <note>In the Septuagint numbering; Psalm 40 in the Hebrew/KJV tradition.</note></header>

But He set my feet upon a firm rock,
guiding my step in His own paths.
Taking a melody, He placed it upon our lips,
a glorious hymn to our most-mighty King. <note>Greek: *eristhenetē basilēi*. A grand Homeric title emphasizing divine power.</note>
Many should see and be afraid, and hope in the Shepherd.
Blessed is the man for whom the glorious name of God was made gentle,
far from lying madness and vain counsel.
Many wondrous works <note>Greek: *peristambea erga*. An archaic-sounding term for marvelous or astonishing deeds.</note> have been accomplished by you, O King;
nor does any other exist who is like you in your counsels.
I have announced and recounted them, though they were beyond numbering.
Gifts for sin and sacrifice you did refuse;
but for me, you fashioned the flesh of a mortal race. <note>The Greek text here reflects the Septuagint version ("a body you prepared for me") rather than the Hebrew version ("ears you have dug/opened"). This phrasing was historically significant to early Christian writers as a prophecy of the Incarnation.</note>
You did not at all accept a burnt offering consumed by fire;
therefore I have come here, Father, to fulfill what you command.
It is written concerning me in the heading of the book, <note>Referring to the "scroll of the book."</note>
I have longed in my heart, O Blessed One, to fulfill your desire;
and I have placed your law within my innermost breast.
I told of righteousness in the loud-sounding assembly; <note>Greek: *klisiē*. Literally a tent or hut, used here in the epic sense for a gathering or camp of people.</note>
I will never entirely restrain my lips from the message;
you yourself have known my righteousness in your heart.
I have not hidden your truth, covering it in my watchful mind;
but I spoke openly of your evil-averting light,
and your dear mercy and true counsel to the assemblies.
Therefore, do not hold back your helping hand from me,
cherish me always with mercy and true counsel;
for immense pain holds me on every side;
my sins have overtaken me, and hidden the light of my eyes.
They have become more numerous than the hairs of my head,
and from weakness my spirit has flown out of my limbs.
But look upon me and come willingly as my helper,

So that I might know what else remains for me.
Indeed, you have made all my days very old, O Shepherd; <note>Greek: *poimēn*. A common Homeric epithet for a leader, here applied to God.</note>
and you have known the root of my life to be as nothing.

he is tossed about, troubled in his spirit in vain.

you have made me a reproach to foolish men.
I endured in silence, speechless, for you made me so.
Drive your scourges entirely away from me, O Blessed One;
for I pine away under the power of your thriving hand.
You have disciplined a man by rebukes for his deadly sin,
and you have made his soul waste away, helpless like a spider’s web.
The life of the mortal race is surely vain.
Hear my prayer, listen to my cry,
do not pass by my tears in silence, O Blessed One;
for I am a stranger on the earth and a sojourner,
just like my fathers were.
Give me rest from my labors, that I may recover my heart
before I go to the place from which there is no returning.

But he firmly set my feet upon a steady rock,
guiding my step along his own paths.
Taking hold of my lips, he placed a song within them,
a glorious hymn for our most-mighty King.
Many might look with fear, and place their hope in the Shepherd. <note>Greek: *poimeni*. A Homeric title for a leader or king, here used for God.</note>
Blessed is he for whom the glorious name of God has become his hope,
apart from lying madness and vain counsel.
Many wondrous deeds, O King, have you accomplished;
nor is there any other like you in your counsels.

Sacrifices for sin—you refused every offering; <note>Greek: *thusia*. This begins a famous passage cited in the New Testament (Hebrews 10) regarding the insufficiency of animal sacrifice.</note>

This was traditionally interpreted as a prophecy of the Incarnation of Christ.</note>
You did not accept at all a burnt offering dried by fire;
therefore I have come here, O Father, to fulfill what you command.
It is written concerning me in the heading of the book, <note>Greek: *bibloio karēnou*. Literally "head of the book," a translation of the Greek *kephalis*, referring to the scroll's knob or the beginning of the text.</note>
that I have desired in my heart to fulfill your wish, O Blessed One;
and I have placed your law within my innermost breast.
I declared your righteousness to men in the great assembly;
never at all shall I restrain my lips from the message;
you yourself have known the righteousness within my heart.
I did not hide your truth, covering it in my watchful mind;
but I openly spoke of your light that wards off evil,
I sent forth your light and your true counsel to the assembly.
Therefore, do not keep your helping hand far away,
warm me always with your mercy and your enduring counsel; <note>Greek: *dēitēi*. A difficult Homeric term usually meaning "hostile" or "of battle," but here likely used to mean "piercing" or "steadfast."</note>
because boundless pain holds me on every side;
my sins have overtaken me, and have hidden the light from my eyes.

and from weakness, my spirit has flown out of my limbs.

May shame seize all those who desire to destroy my heart;

## Psalm 40 <note>This is Psalm 41 in the Hebrew/Protestant tradition.</note>
### King David sang the fortieth song of praise.

<margin>Blessed is he 41 <note>original: "Beatus 41". A reference to the Latin Vulgate opening, *Beatus qui intelligit super egenum et pauperem*.</note></margin>
Happy is he who cares for the poor and the needy;
the Shepherd <note>Greek: *poimēn*. A common Homeric title for a king, used here for God as the protector of his people.</note> shall not abandon him in the day of necessity.
May he have the King as a guardian and a protector of his life,
and let him be called blessed by God throughout the whole earth.
Neither would He deliver him as a prey into the hands of his enemies,
but the Lord shall help him even upon the bed of affliction;
He has changed his bedding and loosened the sorrows of sickness.
I myself, O King, cried out for your help;
so that you might heal my sinful soul from evils.
Evil and hostile men spoke of sorrows for me,
praying, "When shall the memory of his life perish?"
One came to see me in vain, speaking uselessly;
he gathered wickedness against my grieving heart,
and going outside, he spoke, gathering everyone against me.
All the hateful men whispered against me;
they considered a heavy burden of evils against me,
devising a plot thick with lawlessness: <note>Greek: *dysnomiē*. A state of lawlessness or bad government, used here to describe the conspiracy of the wicked.</note>
"May he who has laid down to sleep never wake again!" <note>Greek: *nēgreton*. An "unwaking" sleep, a poetic way to describe death.</note>

## Psalm 42 <note>This is Psalm 42 in the Septuagint/Greek tradition, but Psalm 42 in the Hebrew/Protestant tradition as well. Note that the Greek numbering usually lags by one, but here the poet identifies it as the "forty-first" song of his collection.</note>

<margin>42 As the deer <note>original: "42 Quemadmodu[m]". The opening of the Psalm in the Latin Vulgate: *Quemadmodum desiderat cervus ad fontes aquarum* ("As the deer longs for the springs of water").</note></margin>

> Just as a young deer longs for the springs of water,
> so my soul longs for you, O King most high.
> My heart has thirsted to consume the living God; <note>The poet uses the strong verb "katabroxai" (to swallow or devour), emphasizing an intense, visceral hunger for the divine.</note>
> when shall the time come that I, a mortal, shall see God face to face?
> By day and by night alike I had tears for my bread,
> while they always said to me, "Where is your God?"

> for I shall pass through the threshold of the mortal dwelling,
> even unto the halls of the heavenly King,
> with a song of joy and the glorious sound of a festival.

> Let your hope be in God; I will sing a song of praise to Him.
> He himself is the divine healer who watches over my sight;
> my soul was shaken within my breast.
> Therefore, O King, I will call upon you from the land of Jordan,
> and from Hermon, though that mountain be but small.

With this I shall delight all those who hope in your help.
May shame seize all those who strive to destroy my heart;
let the one who plots evils against me in the depths be turned back in disgrace.
Let shame immediately seize those who exult in my ruin.

of those who always speak of the boast-worthy gifts of God—
as many as long for your evil-averting <note>The Greek word "alexikakon" is a traditional epithet for deities like Apollo or Zeus, meaning "one who wards off evil."</note> light.
But look upon me, O Blessed One, though I am poor and needy.
May you never delay to be present when I call.

## Psalm 40 <note>In modern English Bibles, this is Psalm 41.</note>
### King David sang the fortieth song of praise.

<margin>Blessed 41 <note>original: "Beatus 41". This refers to the Latin Vulgate opening of Psalm 41: "Beatus qui intelligit super egenum" (Blessed is he who considers the needy).</note></margin>Blessed is he who shall spare the poor and the needy;
the Shepherd <note>The poet uses the word "poimen" (shepherd), a title common in both the Bible and Homeric epic to describe a leader or protector.</note> will rescue him in the day of necessity.
May he have the King as a guardian and protector of his life,
and may he be heard of as blessed of God throughout the whole earth.
Nor would He deliver him as a prey <note>The word "helōr" (prey/spoil) is a quintessential Homeric term, often used for bodies left on the battlefield.</note> into the hands of his enemies,
but the King would help him even upon a bed of suffering;
He changed his bedding and loosed the sorrows of his sickness.
I myself, O King, cried out for your shattering help, <note>Apollinaris uses "rhēktēr," a martial term for one who "breaks through" enemy lines.</note>
so that you might heal my soul, which has been sinful amidst evils.
Evil and hostile men plotted sorrows for me,
praying: "When shall the memory of his life perish?"
One came in vain to see me, speaking empty words;
he gathered offense against my envious soul,
and going outside, he spoke, gathering everyone against me.
All my hateful enemies whispered together against me,
and they planned a heavy burden of evils against me,
fashioning a speech thick with lawlessness:
"Let him who has fallen asleep stay asleep forever, never to wake." <note>This reflects the Homeric "unwakeable sleep" as a metaphor for death, here used by the enemies hoping the Psalmist never recovers from his illness.</note>

<summary>This page concludes the Greek hexameter paraphrase of Psalm 40 and begins Psalm 41, emphasizing God's protection of the poor and the treachery of enemies during the speaker's illness.</summary>

<meta>This page continues the conclusion of Psalm 40 (modern Psalm 41) and begins Psalm 41 (modern Psalm 42). Apollinaris continues to use dactylic hexameter—the meter of Homer's *Iliad*—to recast the biblical text. The previous page ended with the Lord protecting the righteous man from his enemies; here, the tone shifts to the betrayal by a trusted friend, a passage traditionally interpreted by Christians as a prophecy of Judas's betrayal of Jesus.</meta>

<header>PSALM 41 <note>In modern Bibles, this is the end of Psalm 41.</note></header>

This man was a mortal of peace; for he became my hope;
though I fed him, I found him an enemy, and he placed his heel against me. <note>The Greek "meiona thēken" (made me less/lower) combined with "pternēs" (heel) is a poetic way of describing someone "lifting their heel" against another in betrayal.</note>
As I have asked, have mercy on me, that I may give them what they deserve.
From your kindness, I was then made a blessed witness.
I perceived that my enemy shall by no means boast over me in vain;
because for the sake of my innocence, O Lord, you arose as my helper,
and you rooted me continually as an attendant in your sight.
Blessed was the undying God of Israel forever;
from ages to ages, may He always be the highest.

### Psalm 42 <note>In modern Bibles, this is Psalm 42.</note>

For understanding, once given to the sons of high-spirited Korah;
the forty-first song of the chant.

<margin>42 As the deer <note>original: "42 Quemadmodu." This refers to the Latin Vulgate opening of Psalm 42: "Quemadmodum desiderat cervus" (As the deer pants).</note></margin>
Just as a young deer longs for the spring waters,
so my soul longs for you, O highest Lord;
my living heart has thirsted to consume God;
at what time shall I, a mortal, see God face to face?
Day and night alike I had tears for food like bread,
while they were always saying, "Where then is your God?"
I remembered these things, and I poured out my soul within me,
because I shall pass through the threshold of the mortal tent,
from there even unto the halls <note>The word "melathrōn" refers to the roof-beams or the inner hall of a palace, a classic Homeric term for a royal dwelling.</note> of the heavenly King,
amidst the sound of praise and the glorious noise of the festival of delight.
My soul, why do you delay? Why do you stir my mind within me?
Let God be your hope; I shall sing a song of praise to him.
He himself is the all-seeing protection of my countenance;
my soul was shaken of its own accord within my thoughts.
Therefore, O Lord, I shall call upon you from the land of Jordan,
and from the heights of Hermon, though that mountain was but small. <note>This refers to Mount Mizar, often translated as "the little hill."</note>
One dark abyss called out to another abyss,

### Psalm 43 <note>In modern Bibles, this is Psalm 43. In the Septuagint and Vulgate, it is Psalm 42.</note>

-> This is a gentle labor <note>The "labor" or "sweat" refers to the work of the poet in translating the psalm.</note> concerning the fortieths; <-
-> the prudent David sang this second song. <-

<margin>43 Judge <note>original: "43 Judica." This is the Latin name for the psalm, "Judica me, Deus."</note></margin>
**J**udge me, O King, as an unjust tribe has brought me harm;
save me from the deceitful and unrighteous man.
You are my strength; why have you sent me away to suffer so much?
Why must I wander here and there in mourning,
while the sorrow of a hostile man crushes me down?
Send forth your light and your joyful brilliance,
by whose light I shall reach your holy mountain,
and I shall share a hearth within your famous halls.
I will go now to the glorious altar of God,
to the one who gladdens me with the grace of new-blooming youth.
I will sing your praise, O Blessed One, on the sweet-sounding lyre. <note>original: "φόρμιγγι" (phorminx), the ancient Greek stringed instrument associated with Homeric bards.</note>
My soul, why do you delay? Why do you stir up my mind within?
Let God be your hope, and I shall sing a song of praise to him;
he himself is the all-seeing light of my countenance.

### Psalm 43 <note>In modern Bibles, this is Psalm 44. The numbering here follows the Septuagint and Vulgate traditions.</note>

-> For the prudent children of Korah <note>original: "Κοραίου" (Koraiou). The Korahites were a family of Levites who served as temple singers.</note> in their hearts, <-
-> this forty-third song was composed. <-

<margin>43 God, with [our ears] <note>original: "43 Deus aur" from the Latin "Deus, auribus nostris," the opening of this psalm in the Vulgate.</note></margin>
> We have heard with our own ears, O immortal Shepherd,
> what our beloved fathers told us long ago:
> the work you performed in ancient generations.
> Your hand destroyed the tribes, and you rooted our fathers in their place.
> You shattered the nations, and drove them out of their own land.
> For it was not by their own sword that they inherited the land of old,
> nor did the war-toiling arm save them from their enemies,
> but it was your hand, and the great light of your countenance,
> because in your heart you were pleased to hold them dear.
> You yourself are my King, and you have always been my God,
> the one who commanded the light of life for Jacob.
> Trusting in you, we shall strike down our enemies with our horns <note>A traditional biblical metaphor for strength and victory.</note>,
> and calling upon your name, we shall laugh at those who rise against us.
> For I do not boast in the hope of my own bow,
> nor shall a sharp-edged sword save me from evils.
> You yourself, O King and Shepherd, released us from wretchedness;
> and you have put to shame those men who hated us.
> Daily our glory is with the immortal King,
> and we shall unceasingly sing of your divine name.
> But now you have poured out shame, pushing us so far away,
> nor will you go out as a helper with our armed men as you did before.
> Turning back, we have become hidden and lesser than our enemies,
> and we are a ready spoil at the feet of those who hate us.
> You have given us up like sheep for a feast,
> and you have scattered us again, far-dispersed among the nations.
> Without a price or payment, O King, you have given away your people.

<page-num>54</page-num>
<header>APOLLINARIS <note>original: "ΑΠΟΛΙΝΑΡ." (APOLINAR.). The author is traditionally identified as Apollinaris of Laodicea, a 4th-century bishop who rewrote much of the Bible in classical Greek poetic forms.</note></header>

Nor was there any great price paid for a reciprocal exchange of sale. <note>This refers to God "selling" His people to their enemies for a pittance, as described in Psalm 44:12.</note>
You have made us a reproach to our neighbors, a laughingstock to everyone around us;
You have revealed us as a byword of many voices among the nations,
and a ridiculous shaking of the head among the mocking peoples.
All day long, a blush of shame remains before my eyes,
and the disgrace of a joyless face has covered me,
at the voice of the man who insults and reproaches me,
and before the eyes of the hostile one and the driver of my flight.
But we have not forgotten these things because of you, O Shepherd,
nor have we bypassed the patterns of your harmonies; <note>original: "ἁρμονιάων" (harmoniaon). Here used as a poetic substitute for the "covenant" or "laws" of God.</note>
nor have we turned our hearts away from you at all.
Yet you have inclined your paths far away from us.
You have fashioned us to be powerless upon floors of evil diseases,
and we have been hidden in the misty shadow of death.
If ever we have entirely forgotten our own God,
or if we have spread out our hands to another god—
will you not yourself, the Immortal One, examine all these things?
For you yourself have read the hidden recesses of the mortal heart.
For your sake, O Shepherd, we are overcome by death at all times;
we are likened to sheep whose life is destined for slaughter.
Awake, All-King! Why do you sleep so soundly now for me?
Awake, and do not drive us far away from your sight! <note>original: "βλεφάρων ἀπὸ νόσφιν" (blepharon apo nosphin), literally "away from your eyelids."</note>
Why have you turned away your glorious face?
You have utterly forgotten our labors and our poverty.
Our soul has perished, brought down even as the dust;
and our belly is pressed to the earth in misery.
Awake, All-King, stretching out a helping hand;
and deliver us from this unbroken ruin for the sake of your grace.

## Psalm 44.

->The wondrous birth of the beloved God in songs<-
->Exalt again, sons of Korah, in alternating verses.<-

<margin>45 My heart has uttered <note>original: "eructavit". This is the first word of Psalm 45 in the Latin Vulgate, used here as a reference to help the reader locate the text in Latin prayer books.</note></margin>
My heart has brought forth a goodly word;
I shall declare my works wisely to the King.
I have a tongue like the sharp-writing pen of a skilled man. <note>original: "ἀνδρὸς ὁμοίου" (andros homoiou). Literally "of a similar man," here used poetically to match the biblical "ready writer."</note>
In beauty you flash more brilliantly than the mortal race;
grace is poured out upon your wise lips.
Therefore God himself has bestowed ageless praise upon you.
O mighty one, gird your long-edged sword upon your thigh,
trusting in your beauty and in the glorious hour of your courage;
draw your bow, delay not, and ruling as a king, reign,
for the sake of gentle holiness and the work of truth.
Your own right hand shall lead you in wonders.
Your arrows are sharp and always whetted.
<image-desc>Circular library stamp in green ink on the right margin.</image-desc>
Many peoples shall fall cowering beneath you,
aimed at the hearts of the King's enemies.
Unceasingly, O Immortal One, your throne is invincible,
a straight-moving scepter, the scepter of kingly destiny.
You love righteousness, and hate lawless deeds;
Therefore God himself has poured his own ointment around you,
anointing you with the oil of gladness above all your companions,
with myrrh and oil of myrrh <note>original: "στακτή" (staktē). An expensive, high-quality myrrh oil.</note> and cassia from your robes,
out of ivory palaces, <note>original: "ἐκ νηῶν ἐλέφαντος" (ek nēōn elephantos). While 'nēōn' usually means ships, in this epic context and following the Hebrew 'hekal', it refers to temple-palaces.</note> where you may delight your soul;
your glory is the daughters of mighty kings;
at your side stood the queen in gold-shining robes,
well-clothed in form with intricate embroideries.
Hear, O daughter, and look, and incline your dear ear to me—

Forget your own people and your father's house;
because the King has longed for your venerable beauty.
For he himself was Lord over kings even before you;
let the daughters of Tyre entreat him with soothing gifts.
The wealthy of the land shall seek your favor with prayers.
All the glory of the royal daughter is within;
she is adorned, clothed in golden tassels.

you shall appoint them as rulers over all the earth.
I shall surely remember you throughout continuous generations;
from generation to generation I shall sing of your beautiful name.
Therefore the most high and blessed peoples shall hymn you,
now and thereafter and through unending offspring,
now and thereafter and into the ages to come.

->Psalm 45<-

A song concerning hidden things, which the divine lyre has brought into the light; this is the forty-fifth hymn. <note>The Greek "Κῶμος ὑπὸ κρυφίων" (Komos hypo kryphion) refers to the "secrets" or "hidden things" mentioned in the Septuagint title of this Psalm (*hyper ton kryphion*).</note>

### PSALM 45

God would never fail us even in the midst of the seas;
from the dawn he holds his all-seeing eye as our helper.
The powerful kingdoms bowed, and the tribes of men were shaken.
He cried out with a piercing voice, and the earth trembled.
The leader of heavenly powers stands as our helper;
the God of Jacob himself has become our champion.
Come, friends of the King, let us look upon his wondrous works,
how many signs he has performed upon the hard-to-bear earth.
He puts an end to wars beneath the whole arc of the sky.
He would shatter the bow, and break the impetuous shield,
and he shall burn up the bucklers in the rush of consuming fire.
Understand <note>The Greek text uses "φράζεσθε" (phrazesthe), which mirrors the famous biblical command "Be still and know."</note> <unclear>that it is necessary</unclear> because he was born the immortal God,
the highest among the nations and the master over all the earth.
The leader of heavenly powers stands as our helper;
the God of Jacob himself has become our champion.

### Psalm 46

This gentle forty-sixth song was fashioned for the mighty sons of Korah. <note>The "sons of Korah" were a family of Levites who served as temple singers and musicians in ancient Israel; many Psalms are attributed to them.</note>

<margin>All nations 47 <note>original: "Omnes gentes 47." This refers to the opening of this Psalm in the Latin Vulgate bible.</note></margin>
Clap your hands together for me, all you nations,
and sing out with a delightful voice to God the King;
because the Most High is fearful, and at the same time he was made for all,
reigning over the ends of the earth of every kind.
He subdued the peoples for us, and placed the tribes beneath our feet.
Having chosen for himself, he gave to us a dear and painless inheritance,
the portion of Jacob, which is always well-pleasing to God.
God has gone up while people shout a hymn of victory,
and the sound of the fair-sounding trumpet has rushed upward to the Lord.
Play the lyre for our God with songs upon the lyre,
sing out to our King with your own melodies.

### Psalm 47

<note>This is Psalm 48 in modern English Bibles. The numbering difference is due to the Greek Septuagint and Latin Vulgate traditions, which combine or divide certain Psalms differently than the Hebrew Masoretic text.</note>

->A delightful song was written for the sons of Korah;<-
->and they sang it for the second day of the week;<-
->I chant this together as the forty-seventh.<-

<margin>48th Great <note>original: "48^o Magnus." This refers to the Latin opening of the Psalm, "Magnus Dominus" (Great is the Lord).</note></margin>
Praiseworthy is the Immortal One, and indeed he alone is great
throughout his own city and his ever-undefiled mountain.
A joy shining wide from every side upon the much-nourishing earth:
truly the mountains of Zion on the northern side;
the city of much fame belonging to the king of great strength,
the Immortal One was made most manifest within his own towers.
And he sits as a helper for the city when she is in need.
All the kings of the earth came, gathered together,
and looking with their eyes, they were amazed in their hearts by her.
Rising up, they were thrown into turmoil, and trembling came upon them all;
frequent pangs seized them, like a woman in labor;
or when a gale drives the ships of Tarshish <note>original: Θαρσηΐδος (Tharsēidos). Tarshish was a distant port, likely in Spain, famous in the Bible for its massive, sea-going merchant ships.</note> with violence.
Just as the report was, so was the sight to the eyes,
concerning the glorious city of God, of the God of the kings of strength.
The Shepherd himself has fixed her foundation immovable.
In the midst of your people, O King, may you grant mercy.
And just as your name, O King, remains celebrated far and wide;
so also does your praise reach even to the ends of all the earth.
Your right hand is weighed down with your justice, O Shepherd.

# P S A L M 47

Let the famous mountain of Zion be the subject of our songs of joy.
Let the much-rejoicing children of lovely Judea be glad,
O undefiled one, for the sake of your holy and righteous laws.
Hasten to surround Zion on every side,
and tell of her towers clearly in your accounts.
Set her within your hearts by her own strength;
distribute her fortifications through your own power,
so that one might speak of it to the generations to come:
because he himself is always King and God alike;
ruling not only over the endless generations of old,
he shall shepherd us tirelessly, ageless in all things.

### Psalm 48

<note>This is Psalm 49 in modern English Bibles. It is a "wisdom psalm" reflecting on the vanity of riches and the certainty of death.</note>

->A sweet song followed for the strong sons of Korah,<-
<note>The "sons of Korah" were a family of Levites who served as temple singers and gatekeepers.</note>
->it carries the number forty-eight as its total.<-

<margin>Hear this. 49. <note>original: "Audin." likely a shortened form of "Audite," the Latin opening of this Psalm: "Audite haec, omnes gentes."</note></margin>
Hearken, myriad tribes, to what my mind hides within.
Let the inhabitants of the earth attend to my songs,
earth-born men, and all the tribes of the mortal race,
the poor mixed together with those who care for boundless wealth;
my sacred mouth shall speak a story to them.
The meditation of my heart shall speak wisdom.
I will incline my ear to a varied parable <note>original: αἶνον (ainon) – often a riddle or a dark saying in this context.</note>;
I will unfold a profound path from clear-sounding songs.
Why should I tremble at the chilling and helpless day?
The crookedness of my heel shall surround me.
<note>This refers to the "iniquity of my heels," a Hebrew idiom for being tripped up by enemies or one's own past sins.</note>
Rich men, confident in their strong wealth,
whose pride was excessive over their possessions.
A brother of mortal men does not appear as a ransom.
He shall not appease God with a sacrifice for the sake of the world,
having paid the price of the atonement of the immortal soul.
<note>The poet emphasizes that no amount of material wealth can pay for the "ransom" of a human soul or prevent death.</note>

<summary>This page concludes a praise of Zion’s eternal strength under God's shepherding and begins a meditation on the limits of human wealth, noting that no one can buy their way out of death.</summary>
<keywords>Zion, shepherding, sons of Korah, wealth, ransom, soul, immortality, Judea</keywords>

--- Page 91 ---
<meta>This page continues the poetic paraphrase of Psalm 48 (Psalm 49 in modern Bibles) by Apollinaris of Laodicea. The text uses Epic Greek vocabulary, drawing heavily on Homeric style to discuss the vanity of wealth and the inevitability of death.</meta>

<page-num>60</page-num>

<header>A P O L L I N A R I S</header>

He labored unceasingly, yet his life shall never end. <note>The poet refers to the futile hope of the wealthy to live forever by their own means.</note>
He might well see the wise dying, even as he tramples upon wickedness;
the fool and the witless perish together as one;
they shall leave the wealth of their halls to others, those without inheritance,
so that they shall inhabit their tombs as halls forever;
they shall remain in their dwellings throughout their generations;
they took their names from the earth, from which they came when they were overcome <note>original: δαμέντες (damentes) – a common Homeric term for falling in battle or being conquered by death.</note>.
A foolish man, though a mortal favored with honors, did not understand;
he was like the four-footed beasts, and embraced the nature of wild animals.
And may their destructive path be a snare for them;
and then indeed they shall offer praise with their own tongues.
Gathered like sheep, they took Hades as their counselor; <note>In the biblical source, they are "appointed for Sheol like sheep; death shall be their shepherd."</note>
therefore bitter <note>original: πευκεδανὸς (peukedanos) – a Homeric epithet for something sharp or piercing, often applied to grief or death.</note> death shears them with strength;
the upright shall rule them in the morning—
Hades shall be no help to them in their uselessness.
But God shall release my soul from the necessity of death;
Do not fear in your soul the wealth of mortal men,
nor when the house of a man is weighed down with goods.
For when he dies, he shall not carry all things with him to Hades,
nor shall his immense wealth go down to the grave with him.
Only while he lives shall his own soul be praised,
and men shall praise you whenever you do well for yourself.
He shall go just as surely to the ancient generation of his fathers;
sinking from the light of life and dwelling in the darkness.
A foolish man, though a mortal favored with honors, did not understand;
he was like the four-footed beasts, and embraced the nature of wild animals.

---

### Psalm 48 <note>This heading in the original Greek numbering refers to the psalm just completed; however, the subtitle below introduces the next psalm, which is Psalm 49 in the Septuagint and Psalm 50 in modern Bibles.</note>

### The forty-ninth song of David.

<summary>This page concludes the poetic meditation on Psalm 49, emphasizing that wealth cannot save one from death and that humans without understanding are no different from beasts. It ends by introducing the next psalm in the sequence.</summary>

<meta>This page transitions from the end of Psalm 48 to the beginning of Psalm 49 (numbered as Psalm 50 in modern Bibles). The previous page concluded the reflection on the vanity of wealth and the inevitability of death. This new psalm shifts to a divine courtroom setting where God summons the heavens and earth to witness His judgment of Israel, emphasizing spiritual devotion over animal sacrifice.</meta>

<page-num>61</page-num>

<header>PSALM 49</header>

<margin>The God of gods</margin>
The God of gods called the whole earth, gathering it into one,
from the rising of the shining sun unto its setting;
from Zion, the beauty of His form shines round about; <note>Zion refers to the hill in Jerusalem, often personified as the source of God's radiance.</note>
the immortal God will appear openly to articulate-speaking men, <note>original: μερόπεσσι (meropessi) — a Homeric epithet for humans, referring to their ability to speak with a divided or articulate voice.</note>
nor will anyone be ignorant of God; for He will not permit it.
Before His feet a burning fire devours;
and around Him roars a great, rushing whirlwind.
He will call the starry heaven near with His words,
and the much-nourishing earth, so that He may judge the people.
Gather all His servants to the Immortal One,
all who have pledged His covenant through sacrifice;
so that they may proclaim His lovely righteousness;
because the honored King Himself judges among all.
Listen, my tribe, and I will speak to you as is right. <note>original: ᾗ θέμις ἐστί (hē themis esti) — a classic Greek phrase meaning "as is established by law or custom."</note>
Listen to me, O Israel; for I will surely teach you;
I am God, your very God has been established for you.
I will not rebuke you with complaints about your ritual sacrifices;
for I see your burnt offerings always before me.
I care not for calves which you rear in your halls;
nor will I accept strong sheep from your flocks;
for I myself made all things—those which the forest feeds on the mountains,
and as many as are bellowed at by the mountain-dwelling herdsmen;
nor do the abundant winged birds through the air escape my notice;
rather, the self-bearing wealth of the field surrounds me.
If I were hungry, I would never need help from you;
for my own divine hand fashioned all things.
I do not accept bulls, nor will I drink the blood of goats;
offer to God a sweet sacrifice; for praise was made a sacrifice for Him,
and pay your vows of thanksgiving to the Highest of gods.
And if, being in need, you call upon me as a helper in your necessity,

<summary>In this poetic paraphrase of Psalm 49 (50), God summons the entire creation to witness a judgment where He declares that He desires heartfelt praise and the fulfillment of vows rather than the mere blood of animal sacrifices.</summary>
<keywords>Psalm 49, Psalm 50, Apollinarius of Laodicea, divine judgment, Zion, sacrifice vs praise, Epic Greek poetry, Homeric epithets</keywords>

<meta>This page continues the speech of God from the end of Psalm 49 (numbered as Psalm 50 in modern Bibles), where the Creator addresses the hypocrite who speaks of the law while acting wickedly. It then transitions into the introduction of Psalm 50 (Psalm 51 in modern Bibles), the famous prayer of repentance by King David after his sin with Bathsheba.</meta>
