<header>APOLLINARIS</header>

I am indeed your good helper, but you shall sing a song of glory to me.
Then God spoke these things to the one who sins:
"Why do you proclaim my glorious, righteous laws,
and carry my covenants upon those lips of yours?
You always hate to contemplate good thoughts,
and you have always rejected the enmity of our words. <note>The Greek implies the sinner treats God's words as if they were an enemy to be cast aside.</note>
You ran alongside the evil-minded thief,
and you appeared as a partner in the portion of lewd adulterers.
Your greedy mouth is like a pit of wickedness,
and your tongue is entwined with deceitful thoughts.
Sitting down, you spoke evil against your brother,
and you wove a helpless ruin for your own mother's son.
These things you did, and I kept silent.
You thoughtlessly imagined that I would be like you in mind;
I will set your sins before you, always rebuking you.
Understand these things, you who have forgotten the Immortal One,
lest he snatch you away when no rescuer is present.
A hymn of the heart is a blessed thing pleasing to me,
and the way of the Immortal One appears as life-sustaining for one's life."

### Psalm 51

David once yielded to unseemly lust, <note>original: Ἔρωτι (Erōti) — referring here to physical passion or desire for Bathsheba.</note>
and was caught in the theft of another's bed;
then he killed the husband of the woman.
But Nathan, appearing obedient to God,
pierced him with the arrows of his words,
and cleansed him with the medicines of rebuke;
and he, losing his unworthy state of mind,
quickly perceiving his ignoble ruin,
sought to appease the merciful Master. <note>This introductory poem by Apollinaris summarizes the biblical narrative of 2 Samuel 11-12, where the prophet Nathan confronts King David for his adultery and murder.</note>

<summary>God concludes His judgment of the hypocrite by demanding sincere devotion rather than lip service, followed by an introductory poem describing King David's repentance after his sin with Bathsheba.</summary>

and he was moved with pity toward the Great One, <note>Referring to God.</note>

---

By Nathan revealing the reckless deeds <note>original: ἀτασθαλίω (atasthaliō) — a Homeric term often used for the "reckless folly" of men that leads to their own destruction.</note> of lust,
he quenched the joy of the god-like king with groans and tears;
and the widely-famed king, calling upon God’s gentle and merciful spirit, 
set forth paths for mortals, 
so that he might offer wise counsel to those born later. 
To him, this fiftieth hymn is a protector against evil.

<margin>51 Have mercy</margin> <note>original: Miserere — the first word of the psalm in Latin, used here as a standard title for this prayer of repentance.</note>
<image-desc>A small filled triangle pointing to the right marks the beginning of the psalm text.</image-desc>
O Lord, be exceedingly merciful to me as I pray;
and destroy my lawlessness with your compassionate counsel.
Wash away my error, washing it yet more with water;
may you cleanse me far away from our reckless sin.
For I have recognized my lawlessness in my heart,
and my reckless sin is always before my eyes.
I have sinned against you, Blessed One, and worked pain near you.
So that you might appear holy in all your words,
in judgment you alone shall be superior to all others.
For indeed, my father sowed me in lawlessness,
and in reckless sins my lady mother conceived me.
And you taught me the paths of your hidden wisdom.
Sprinkling me, may you wash me clean with hyssop, <note>A plant used in ancient ritual purification ceremonies.</note>
and having bathed me, may you make me much whiter than snow.
May you appear to us as a messenger of gladness and joy;
the bones of those who are worn down shall all rejoice in you.
Turn your gaze away from my error once more,
and may you wipe out my many kinds of foolishness.
O Lord, create an undefiled heart within my breast,
and direct a steadfast spirit within our inner parts.

Do not cast me away from your sight,
nor ever withhold your holy spirit from me.
Grant me the returned garment of the light’s delight; <note>original: τερπωλὴ φάεος παλινάγρετον ἐσθήν — a metaphorical way of describing the "joy of salvation" as a radiant robe that had been lost and is now reclaimed.</note>
and with a ruling spirit, strengthen my inner might.
May I clearly signal your paths to the lawless.
And from every side, let the wanderers turn back toward you.
O light of my life, save me from the guilt of bloodshed; <note>original: ἀνδροκτασίης (androktasies) — an Epic Greek term for manslaughter, specifically referencing David’s role in the death of Uriah the Hittite.</note>
my tongue shall always lead the way in praising your justice.
O Blessed One, open my lips, and I shall cry out your righteous judgment.
If you had commanded a sacrifice, I would have sent it readily;
but you will not be gladdened by parched offerings. <note>original: καρφομένοισιν... θυέεσιν — literally "dried-up" or "withered" sacrifices, referring to burnt offerings.</note>
To the Immortal, a humble spirit is a cherished sacrifice;
a humble and mild heart you would never despise.
O Blessed One, grant happy favors to Zion according to your heart;
and let the famous walls of Jerusalem be well-built.
Then surely you would receive a sacrifice of justice in your mind;
both the parched offering and the breath rising upward.
Then indeed they shall offer up calves upon your altar.

->Psalm 51<- <note>Numbered as Psalm 52 in modern English Bibles. The Greek Septuagint numbering is followed here.</note>

this is the fifty-first song.

Unlawful insolence gushed from around your tongue;

Loving heavy wickedness instead of kindness,
speaking all manner of lawless things instead of righteousness.

You cherish every word of a deceitful tongue.

in his heart he trusted in his own possessions,
always equipped with the futile deeds of this life."
But I flourish like an olive tree near the seat of God,
welcoming divine mercy with my eyes,
both now and hereafter for all generations.

always watching for your sweet name among your holy ones.

### Psalm 52.
By the wise David, concerning the sorrowful Maeleth; <note>original: Μαελήτου (Maelētou). This refers to the Hebrew musical term "Mahalath," which some ancient translators interpreted as meaning "sorrowful" or "concerning sickness."</note>
this is the fifty-second hymn.

<margin>
*The fool has said* 53. <note>original: "Dixit insipiens 53." The Latin title for Psalm 53 in the Vulgate, which is Psalm 52 in the Greek Septuagint numbering used here.</note>
</margin>
The fool has said in his breast: "There is no God."
Devising shames, they are consumed by their own works;
suddenly, there is not even one among them who does not act lawlessly.
The Immortal One looked down from heaven upon the mortal race,
to see if there were any seeker who was searching for the Lord God.
All have turned aside; together they were shaken by evils;
suddenly, there is not even one among them who does not act lawlessly.
They are ignorant of good things, always counselors of ruin,
devouring my people like life-sustaining bread;
in their folly, they failed to pray to God the King.
They trembled without fear, when there was no need for terror;
for you have scattered all the bones of those who seek to please men with their schemes.

### Psalm 53

King David sang this song to God, when the wicked tribe of the Ziphites led Saul against what is right with a secret message; they did not spare the suppliant, but it appeared to them that David was hidden <term>among</term> <note>original: μετὰ (meta). This refers to David's concealment among the people of Ziph before they betrayed him to King Saul.</note> them. This was made the fifty-fourth <term>and</term> <note>original: καὶ (kai)</note> fourth song. <note>The poet is using the Greek Septuagint numbering; this is Psalm 54 in modern translations.</note>

<margin>
54 Save me, O God, by [your name] <note>original Latin: "54 Deus in nom[ine tuo]". The Latin title for Psalm 54 in the Vulgate.</note>
</margin>

O King, save me from evils by your name,
and having measured me against your strength, may you judge me thus.
Hear my prayer, O most sovereign shepherd;
incline your ever-listening ear to my words.
For a strange tribe of enemies has armed itself against me,
and warriors sought to take my steadfast heart,
nor did they keep the Divine King in their minds.
Truly God shall be a helper to me when I call,
and the Lord shall be the constant defender of my life.
May he turn back the sorrows upon my enemies;
in your truth, O King, may you destroy them.
But I will bring a sacrifice to you with a longing mind;
I will sing of your name, O King, for it is good.
For you have rescued my soul from every kind of distress;
and my eyes have looked down upon the destruction of my enemies.

### Psalm 54

A worthy hymn of wise David, pleasing to God,
being the fifty-fourth following four good blessings. <note>In the Greek numbering, this is Psalm 54; in modern Bibles, it is Psalm 55.</note>

-> # PSALM 54 <-

<sig>E 2</sig>

<margin>
Hear, O God <note>original Latin: "Exaudi deus." This is the opening of Psalm 55 in the Vulgate.</note>
</margin>

Hear my prayer, O most supreme shepherd, <note>Apollinaris uses the Homeric epithet "panupertate poimen" (most supreme shepherd) to address God, blending the biblical "Good Shepherd" with the epic "Shepherd of the People."</note>
nor should you entirely overlook my gentle-minded supplication;
look upon me; receive my voice within your ears.
Angered by frequent outcries, I stir my soul,
hearing the voice of the enemy and the trouble caused by the sinner.
Because they cast painful transgressions upon me,
and raging bitterly, they set a grudge within their minds.
My heart within me is struck with confusion;
the chill of death fell upon my passing life.
Fear and trembling came over me, and a poured-out darkness covered me.
And I said, "Who might grant me wings like a dove's,
so that I might fly and rest my body from its toil?"
I wish to escape far away, and I would make my lodging in the desert,
waiting for the one who saves me from <term>faintness</term> <note>original: ἀδρανίης (adranies). Referring to the exhaustion of the soul.</note> and the vast storm.
Divide their tongues and cast them into the sea;
for I have seen transgression and insolent speech throughout the city.
By day and night they circle it upon its towers;
in its midst it carries lawlessness and the trouble of sorrow.
Nor may deceitful labor leave their paths.
If an enemy had insulted me with reproaches, I would have endured it,
and I would have hidden from the speech of the one who hated me;
but you, a leader and an acquaintance, always of one mind with me;
we both tasted sweet abundance beside you,
did we not walk into the house of God with friendly thoughts?
May death come upon them, worthy of their destructive deeds,
and let them go down alive into the <term>pit of Hades</term>; <note>original: ἀΐδαο βέρεθρον (aidao berethron). A classical Greek way to describe the biblical Sheol or the grave.</note>
for they carry wickedness in the midst of their dwellings.
But I cried out, and God heard my voice.
From morning and evening and at mid-day;
He shall hear my voice as I make my plea.

Peace is restored to the heart for those who draw near, 
because they, trusting in their many numbers, set themselves against me.
May the Father of the world, hearing them, bring confusion upon them. <note>original: "γενετὴρ κόσμοιο" (genetēr kosmoio). A classical-sounding epithet for God as Creator.</note>
For they have no ransom, and they disregard God.
He stretched out his palm in retribution against them; <note>Refers to God’s judgment against those who broke their covenant.</note>
for they profaned his <term>inviolate covenant</term> <note>original: "νημετέα βέβηλον" (nēmetea bebēlon). Apollinaris uses "nēmetea" (unerring/true) to describe the sacred bond that the enemies have treated as common or profane.</note> with a strangling grip.
And they measured out harsh measures in their sights.
But from nearby, the counsel of the immortal King kept watch.
Their words flowed smoother than oil,
yet they were like deadly arrows among themselves.
Cast your cares upon the immortal King,
and may he always care for you and nourish you continually.
Never may he allow the righteous to be shaken by surging waves.
But you, O King, shall lead them down to the pit of destruction; <note>original: "βόθρον ὀλέ[θρου]" (bothron olethrou). The "pit of destruction" corresponds to the biblical concept of Sheol or the grave.</note>
murderous and deceitful men shall perish in the middle of their lives.
But I shall live on the hopes of the King.

Be merciful to me, for man tramples me underfoot;

<meta>This page continues the poetic paraphrase of the Psalms by Apollinaris of Laodicea. It contains the conclusion of Psalm 55 (Septuagint numbering, corresponding to Psalm 56 in modern Bibles) and the introductory epigram and opening lines of Psalm 56 (Septuagint numbering, corresponding to Psalm 57). The Greek text continues to use Homeric hexameters, blending biblical theology with the language of Greek epic poetry.</meta>

<page-num>69</page-num>

<header>PSALM 55</header>

I will sing hymns to the King all day long.
I do not tremble before mortal man, for God has become my hope.
They always hated me in their secret thoughts with words;
they always devised evil plans against me.
Dwelling nearby, they will hide their intentions,
always keeping watch over my heel. <note>original: "πτέρνης ἡμετέρης φυλακὴν" (pternēs hēmeterēs phylakēn). This follows the Septuagint literalism for "watching my steps" or "lying in wait for my heel."</note>
Just as they themselves lie in wait, desiring to destroy my heart;
thus in your wrath may you bring down the secret, evil-doing tribes.
Being angry in your heart, may you lead down the <term>proud-necked people</term>. <note>original: "ὑψαύχενα δῆμον" (hypsauchena dēmon). A Homeric epithet literally meaning "high-necked," used here to describe the arrogant.</note>
I have announced my pleasant life to you, O King,
and you have placed my tears before your eyes;
may my enemies be turned back by your commands.
Nor will I call upon any helping hand other than your Kingly one.
I knew that you, God, have been my <term>all-seeing overseer</term>. <note>original: "πανεπίσκοπος" (panepiskopos). A common title in early Christian Greek poetry for God as the one who watches over everything.</note>
I will boldly sing a god-pleasing song to the King,
I will sing a heartfelt word to the All-King.
I do not tremble before mortal man, for God has become my hope.
There are vows within me, for which I will sing praise to you, O Blessed One;
because you released my heart from the heavy pain of death,
saving my eyes from tears, and you snatched my feet from slipping.
May I be well-pleasing in the great light before the King God of the living.

->### PSALM 56 ###<-
<note>This is Psalm 57 in most modern English Bibles. The numbering difference stems from the Greek Septuagint tradition.</note>

This little song is also David’s, when he narrowly escaped
Saul’s unbridled madness,
he cried out this fifty-sixth song,
sitting in a cave, he who was formerly <term>hard-working in war</term>. <note>original: "ἀρεισπόνος" (areisponos). A compound word combining Ares (the god of war) and labor, signifying a veteran or warrior.</note>

**B**e merciful to me, O all-seeing King, and have pity on me;
because I always keep my dear heart emboldened in you;
trusting in the shelter under your shadowy wings,

...until the <term>spotless wickedness</term> <note>original: "ἀχράαντος ἀτασθαλίη" (achraantos atasthaliē). A striking Homeric-style oxymoron; while "atasthaliē" usually means reckless sin or wickedness, "achraantos" means pure or undefiled, suggesting here a trial that has not yet been "stained" by the speaker's own failure or perhaps the "complete" nature of the malice.</note> passes me by.

The Undying One, from whom I receive all life-giving gifts.
Sending help from heaven, he saved me from evil distress,
and he delivered to reproach those <term>wanderers</term> <note>original: "ἀλείταις" (aleitais), a Homeric term for vagabonds or sinners.</note> who trample me.

I myself lay down to sleep, troubled in secret by ruin;
men of the upper air whose teeth are arrows and shafts,
and whose sharp tongue is just like a sword.

and your unspeakable glory reaches over all the earth.
They wove inescapable nets for my feet,
and they bowed down my soul as it labored under evils;
they fell into the pit which they prepared to cause my ruin.

**My heart is ready, O All-Seeing Watcher, my heart is ready,**

swiftly arising at dawn, I will celebrate.

and I will sing a lyre-striking praise among many nations.
For your mercy is high above the gates of heaven,
and your truth flies even to the clouds.
Arise above the gates of heaven, O highest Shepherd,
and your unspeakable glory reaches over all the earth.

### Psalm 57

The inscription <note>original: "Στήλης" (Stēlēs). This refers to the "Miktam" title in the Hebrew Psalms, which the Greek Septuagint translated as "stelographia," meaning a poem fit for an inscribed pillar or monument.</note> which was composed by the care of David,
singing beautifully at the end, "Do not destroy."
This is the fifty-seventh hymn.

<header>P S A L M 57</header>

<margin>Is it truly 58?</margin>

Do you indeed speak truly with holy words?
If you judge with justice, O sons of men,
shaping error in your hearts upon the earth,
weaving lawless things with hands that do evil.
Even before birth, the <term>wanderers</term> <note>original: "ἀλεῖται" (aleitai), a Homeric term for vagabonds, here used for sinners.</note> are estranged from God;
from the womb they go astray, always lurking with lies.
They waste away just like venom-spewing serpents,
like a deaf asp that has blocked its ears in its wrath,
which does not hear the sounding voice of the enchanter,
nor do the skillful spells of the wise man soothe its heart.
God has shattered the teeth within their bitter mouths;
the King has broken the jawbones of the destructive lions.
May they come to nothing, like water that flows away in vain;
let the Lord stretch his bow until they are overcome by sickness.
Like melting wax, let them melt away in their evils;
they have forgotten the sun as its fire came upon them.
Before your thorns grew into a thicket <note>This verse is notoriously difficult in the original Hebrew; Apollinaris interprets it as God's swift judgment before the "thorns" of the wicked can fully mature.</note>,
may he consume them in blood, as if devouring them alive in his <term>curse</term> <note>original: "ἀραιῇ" (araiē), referring to a prayer for destruction or a divine curse.</note>.
The holy man shall rejoice when he sees the day of vengeance;
he shall wash his hands in the blood of the sinner.
And he shall say: "Truly, there is a fruit of great glory for the righteous man,
since God is the judge of mortal deeds upon the earth."

### Psalm 58.

Writing for the inscription of a monument for the blameless David,
he began to prophesy a faultless conclusion to his hymns;
at the time when Saul—bearing a heart unmindful of past kindness,
and having forgotten their <term>former harmonies</term> <note>A poetic reference to David playing the harp to soothe Saul's troubled spirit in 1 Samuel 16.</note>—
sent men to guard the house of his son-in-law to watch him,
so that he might grant him death as a gift and prize;
this is the fifty-eighth hymn.

<margin>Note 59</margin>
**Immortal One**, snatch me away from evil enemies,
and grant me a **day of freedom** from those who arm themselves against me.
From the beasts and murderous, destructive men of heavy sorrow
who were tracking me before; O King, swiftly save me from them.
Men who live by the law of force weigh down upon my life,
though not because of any lawlessness or sins of mine.
Far from lawlessness, I ran and found a ready path.
Awaken, O King, stand by my side and behold the deeds!
And You, Lord of powers and **Shepherd of Israel**,
stretch out Your **all-seeing eye** <note>original: "πανεπίσκοπον ὄμμα" (panepiskopon omma), a common poetic epithet for a deity who sees everything.</note> upon all the various nations.
Have no mercy on the servants of **recklessness** <note>original: "ἀτασθαλίης" (atashaliēs), a Homeric term often used for the foolish pride or "blind folly" of the suitors in the Odyssey.</note>.
At the evening hour they shall all return,
and like hungry dogs they shall haunt the city.
Their voice is unheard, yet around their lips are swords,
as if thinking, "Who can hear us?" But You shall laugh at them;
You shall hold the tribes of evil men in contempt for their sins.
I will guard my strength for the **King of great glory**,
because God always fights for me with exceeding might.
May the mercy of the King come upon me and go before me;
may You subdue the **sinners** <term>vagabonds</term> <note>original: "ἀλείτας" (aleitas), a term for those who wander or sin against divine law.</note> before our very eyes.
Do not kill them, lest they altogether forget Your law,
but scatter them easily with great force and power,
and bring their pride down to the ground, O Defender.
For the sin of their mouth and the word of their lips,
let them be caught in their own great transgressions.
Let their end be announced from a curse and a lie;
let the limit and end of life come upon those who are angry.
Let them know that God rules over Jacob,
holding the ends of all the various lands in His sovereignty.
At the evening hour they shall all return...

->**Psalm 59**<-
<note>This corresponds to Psalm 60 in the English/Hebrew numbering.</note>

This is a record of events written out of joy,
when the rest of the people had returned once more,
concerning the deeds thought to have been achieved by the King.
For a certain man, passing through the borders of Palestine
and the other Syrian regions in order,
set **Mesopotamia** ablaze with all his might.
But **Joab** himself, with God's help,
struck **Edom** in the Valley of Salt,
slaying twelve thousand men,
and holding **Sobal the Syrian** <note>referring to Zobah from the Second Book of Samuel</note> in contempt.
When David saw this likeness of good fortune,
he was filled with the **Divine Spirit**;
he commanded the general to destroy the enemy quickly,
and he sings this fifty-ninth song.

You shook the earth, stirring it up in its recklessness.
Heal its broken parts, which you have caused to tremble and spin.
O **Blessed One**, you have given your people hard things to witness;

<margin>Inscription 59</margin>
**Immortal One**, snatch me away from the evil ruin of my enemies; grant me the day of freedom <note>original: "ἐλεύθερον ἦμαρ" (eleutheron ēmar). This is a famous Homeric formula for the day a person escapes death or slavery.</note> from those who arm themselves against me.
But also rescue my heavy-pained heart from the deadly man-slayers <note>original: "ἀνδροφόνων" (androphonōn). In the Iliad, this is the standard epithet for Hector.</note> who were tracking me before; snatch me away quickly.
Strong men, who are a law unto themselves, have set upon my life, not because of any lawlessness or sins of my own.
Though I ran without lawlessness, I found a path prepared for me.
Awake, O **King**, stand beside me and see the work!
And you, O **Lord** of powers and **Shepherd of Israel**, stretching out your all-seeing eye over all kinds of nations, do not show pity to those who serve reckless deeds.
In the evening hours they shall all return, and like hungry dogs they shall haunt the city.
Their voice is unheard, yet there is a sword about their lips; let someone hear!
But you, then, shall laugh sweetly at them; you shall despise the tribes of men for the sake of their evil sins.
I will keep my strength for the far-famed **King**, because God always fights for me with exceeding power.
Let the mercy of the **King** go before me and surround me.
Having subdued my enemies before my eyes, do not kill them, lest they should completely forget your law;
but by your great force and strength, scatter them easily, O my defender, bringing their manly pride <note>original: "ἰνορέην" (inoreēn). An epic term for physical strength or virility.</note> to the ground.
For the sin of their mouth and the speech of their lips together, let them be caught in their great transgressions.

Let them know that God rules over **Jacob**, holding the tracks of the kingly earth in every way.
In the evening hours they shall all return...

## Psalm 59 <note>In most English Bibles, this is Psalm 60. The Greek numbering is one digit behind the Hebrew/English numbering for much of the Psalter.</note>

This is an inscription <note>original: "ἐπίγραμμα" (epigramma). While this usually means a short poem, here it refers to the "title" or "superscription" of the Psalm found in the Bible.</note> concerning the pouring out of deeds,
again considering the rest of the nations,

and he shall trample down the terrible arrogance of our enemies.

### Psalm 62 <note>This is Psalm 62 in the Septuagint numbering, which corresponds to Psalm 62 in some traditions and Psalm 61 in others; however, the content matches the Hebrew Psalm 62, a song of trust in God alone.</note>

-> He wrote the song of Jeduthun in the voice of David, <-
-> singing the sixty-second psalm with six thoughts. <- <note>original: "μηδέων ἓξ" (medeon hex). This is a poetic, somewhat cryptic way for the author to indicate the number 60 + 2 for the psalm title.</note>

<margin>62 for Jeduthun</margin>
Shall not our heart be subject to the Lord?
For my light comes from the help of the King.
Because he is God himself to me, and he stands over my strength.
He is my defense, and I shall never be cast out as a wanderer.
How long will you fight against a man, and all of you together slay him,
like a leaning wall and a falling fence?
They ran in thirst, and plotted to make me inglorious;
blessing with their tongues, they were nourishing curses in their hearts.
O my soul, submit to the King, be subject to God, having trusted him;
because for my hope, a life-giving song has blossomed. <note>original: "φυσίζοον ᾆσμα" (physizoon aisma). "Physizoon" is a Homeric epithet meaning "life-giving," usually applied to the earth; here it describes the psalmist's song or hope.</note>
For he is my God, and he has become my savior.
He is my defense; who could drive me from my dear nurse? <note>original: "τιθήνης" (tithenes). A poetic metaphor for protection or a place of nurturing safety.</note>
My glory and light are under the commands of the Lord;
my hope awaits the King, even the strength of his help.
Every dwelling of the people, have courage in the Lord;
pour out your hearts openly before God.
For truly God has always been a helper to us.

<meta>This page continues Apollinaris of Laodicea’s poetic reimagining of the Psalms. We are concluding the final verses of Psalm 61 (Hebrew 62) and beginning Psalm 62 (Hebrew 63). Apollinaris uses the language of Homeric epic—using words like "meropes" for mortals and "anax" for God—to make the Hebrew scriptures feel like classical Greek literature.</meta>

<page-num>76</page-num>
<header>APOLLINARIS <note>original: Α Π Ο Λ Ι Ν Α Ρ. Referring to Apollinaris of Laodicea, the author of this poetic paraphrase.</note></header>

Yet truly, the whole race of mortal men is but vanity. <note>original: "μερόπων" (meropon). A Homeric term for humans, literally meaning "those with divided voice."</note>
The lineage of mortals is deceitful to those who are wretchedly unjust.
They rushed forth from their minds, full of empty boasts, coming together as one;
never keep your mind occupied with the thoughts of the lawless,
nor set a grasping heart upon the goods of others,
nor desire the flowing stream of much-possessing wealth.
The King spoke once, and I heard these two things with my ears:
that the power belongs to God, and behold, so does the mercy of the Lord. <note>original: "ἄνακτος" (anaktos). In Greek epic, this usually refers to a high king like Agamemnon; here it refers to God.</note>
You, O Lord, will grant a reward to all according to the worth of their deeds.

# Psalm 62 <note>This follows the Septuagint numbering. In modern Bibles, this is typically Psalm 63.</note>

-> The renowned hymn of David in the desert of Judah, <-
-> this was fashioned as the sixty-second psalm. <-

<margin>Psalm 63 <note>The marginal note uses the Hebrew/Masoretic numbering, which is common in later scholarly annotations.</note></margin>
After the morning twilight, O Blessed One, Blessed One, I will dance in praise of you. <note>original: "ἀμφιχορεύσω" (amphichoreuso). Apollinaris uses a word suggesting choral dance to translate "I will seek you early."</note>
And my soul has longed for you, and my flesh even more so,
in a parched and waterless land, a great wilderness.
For you, O Blessed One, I have appeared thus in the sanctuary, full of courage,
longing to behold your power and your glory.
Because your mercy, O King, is better than the paths of life,
with my lips I will sing the praises of your fame.
Thus would I glorify you as long as I crawl through this fleeting life,
and, taking heart in you, I lift my hands to the heights.
As if with richness, my heart shall be filled with fatness; <note>original: "ἀλοιφῆς" (aloiphes). Literally "ointment" or "fat," representing the spiritual abundance of God's presence.</note>
and my mouth shall always sing with lips of gladness.
If I have been mindful of you upon my bed,
I nourished my soul with those meditations from the dawn,
because you, O Lord, appeared as a helper to us,
and I shall rejoice in the shelter of your wings.
My dear heart has leaned upon your footsteps as your companion; <note>original: "ξυνοπηδὸν" (xunopedon). A Homeric word for a faithful follower or attendant.</note>

<summary>This page concludes Psalm 61, warning against the vanity of wealth and trusting in men, and begins Psalm 62, a beautiful expression of longing for God's presence in a spiritual "wilderness."</summary>
<keywords>Apollinaris of Laodicea, Psalm 62, Psalm 63, Homeric Greek, desert of Judah, divine mercy, vanity of wealth, early morning prayer</keywords>

<page-num>77</page-num>
<header>PSALM 63 <note>original: Ψ Α Λ Μ. ξγ'. This header indicates the section starting mid-page; the first few lines are the conclusion of the previous psalm.</note></header>

And your right hand fought before me with great strength.
But they sought in vain to destroy my soul.
They shall go quickly into the lower depths of the earth.
Being delivered to the cutting power of the ravenous sword's hands;
they shall become a portion and a prey for foxes in the field.
But the king shall rejoice in his dear heart because of God.
And everyone who swears by him shall be praised with undying glory.
For every insolent mouth of the slanderers has been shut.

## Psalm 63 <note>This follows the Septuagint numbering. In modern English Bibles, this is Psalm 64.</note>

-> The clear-voiced song of David, the mighty king, <-
-> was fashioned as the sixty-third hymn. <-

<margin>Hear, O God 64 <note>original Latin: "Exaudi". This refers to the start of the Psalm in the Latin Vulgate Bible, which begins "Exaudi, Deus, orationem meam" (Hear, O God, my prayer).</note></margin>
Hear my prayer, O King, whenever I might seek you;
release my soul from distressing anxiety.
You have hidden me away from the wicked who go about in throngs,
from the multitude whose minds are set on lawless deeds.
They have sharpened their tongues like swords.
They have sent forth chilling deeds just like an arrow from a bow.
So that they might secretly shoot at the innocent man;
and suddenly, they do not fear in their hearts as they strike him.
They have firmly established a shameless word in their devices,
they planned to hide a snare with their cunning deceits.
They said, "Who could see their watchful work with their eyes?"
In their spirit, they tracked out painful wickedness;
but they became feeble while seeking out infinite things.
A man shall approach, having wise counsel from deep within,
who might raise up the life of the King who thunders on high. <note>original: "ὑψιβρεμέταο" (hypsibremetao). This is a classical Homeric epithet for Zeus, meaning "high-thundering." The author uses it here to describe the power of the Judeo-Christian God.</note>
Thus they sent forth arrows only as a little child would. <note>The poet suggests that the attacks of the wicked are as ineffective as a child's toy arrows when faced with God.</note>
Speaking overbearing things with weak tongues.
All who looked upon them were troubled at once.

<summary>This page concludes a prayer for divine protection and begins a new psalm (64 in modern Bibles) where the speaker asks for safety from secret plots and the "sharpened tongues" of the wicked. The poet Apollinaris uses classical Greek epic language to describe God's power and the eventual failure of the enemies' schemes.</summary>
<keywords>Psalm 63, Psalm 64, David, protection, wicked, arrows, high-thundering, Septuagint, epic poetry</keywords>

Yet truly, the whole race of articulate men <term>articulate men</term><note>original: μερόπων. A common Homeric epithet for humans, referring to their ability to speak with a "divided voice."</note> is vain,
a deceitful brood of mortals with their unjust scales;
they were driven from their senses, joining together in empty boasting.
Never keep a mind softened toward unrighteousness,
nor seize the plunder of others' goods,
nor long for the flow of much-possessing wealth.
The King <note>Referring to God.</note> spoke once, and twice I heard these things with my ears:
that power belongs to God, and that mercy belongs to the Sovereign.
You, O King, will grant a reward to all, worthy of their deeds.

### Psalm 62 <note>Septuagint numbering. In modern English Bibles, this is Psalm 63.</note>

-> The famous hymn of David in the desert of Judea; <-
-> this was fashioned as the sixty-second psalm. <-

<margin>Psalm 63 <note>The marginal note uses the Hebrew/Vulgate numbering.</note></margin>
After the twilight of night, O Blessed, Blessed One, I will always sing to you.
And my soul has longed for you, and my flesh even more,
in a parched and waterless land, a vast desert.
Thus I appeared to you in the sanctuary, very courageous,
desiring to see your power and your glory.
For you have a mercy, O King, better than the paths of life;
with our lips, I will celebrate your fame in song.
Thus I would honor you as long as I walk through life;
trusting in you, I lift my palms toward the heights.
My heart shall be filled as if with rich fatness <note>original: πιερίης... ἀλοιφῆς. A poetic way of describing the "marrow and fatness" of the biblical text, using the Homeric word for "rich" or "fertile."</note>;
and the lips of my mouth shall always sing with gladness.
If I was mindful of you upon my bed,
I fed my soul with meditations from the early dawn,
because you, O King, appeared to us as a helper,
and I would rejoice in the shelter of your wings.
My dear heart has leaned upon your footsteps as a companion;

### Psalm 64 <note>Septuagint numbering. In modern English Bibles, this is Psalm 64.</note>

-> The clear-toned song of David, the mighty king; <-
-> this sixty-third hymn was fashioned at the same time. <-

<margin>Hear <note>original: *Exaudi*. This is the first word of the Psalm in the Latin Vulgate (Exaudi, Deus, orationem meam), commonly used as a title.</note> 64</margin>
Hear my prayer, O King, when I entreat you;
delivering my soul from the breath of painful trouble.
You have hidden me far from the wicked who go in herds,
from the multitude whose minds care for lawless deeds.
They sharpened their tongues like swords,
and let fly their deeds like a chilling bow <note>A poetic metaphor for the flight of a "bitter arrow."</note>,
so that they might secretly shoot arrows at the innocent man;
they will not suddenly fear in their hearts when they strike him.
They have firmly established a shameless word in their devices,
and considered how to hide a snare <term>snare</term><note>original: δίκτυον. Literally a hunting or fishing net.</note> with cunning thoughts.
They said, "Who would see their watchful deed with their eyes?"
In their hearts they tracked out haughty sin.
But they were weak, searching for infinite things.
A man shall approach with wise counsel from deep within,
who might be exalted by the life of the high-thundering King <note>original: ὑψιβρεμέταο. A classic Homeric epithet for Zeus ("high-thundering"), here applied to the God of Israel.</note>.
Thus they sent forth arrows as if from a simple child,
speaking outrageous things with weak tongues.
All were troubled together, as many as looked upon them.

This allowed Christian scholars to compete with classical pagan literature on equal footing.</meta>

Nevertheless, the whole race of articulate-speaking <note>original: μερόπων. A standard Homeric epithet for humans, referring to their ability to speak with a divided or articulate voice.</note> men is in vain.
The lineage of articulate-speaking men is false with unjust miseries.
Empty-boasting men rushed from their minds, coming together as one:
Never keep a mind full of care for the unjust,
nor with a greedy heart long for the plunder of others' goods,
nor desire a stream of riches with many cattle <note>original: πολυκτήνοιο. A Homeric term meaning "rich in flocks," here used to translate the concept of worldly wealth.</note>.
Once the King <note>Referring to God.</note> spoke, and twice I heard these things with my ears:
that the strength belongs to God, and that the mercy of the Lord exists.
You, O King, shall give to all a reward worthy of their deeds.

### Psalm 63 <note>Septuagint numbering. In modern English Bibles, this is Psalm 64.</note>

-> The clear-voiced song of David, the mighty king <-
-> this sixty-third hymn was fashioned at the same time. <-

<margin>Hear, O God 64 <note>original: Exaudi. The Latin title of the Psalm.</note></margin>
Hear my prayer, O King, when I entreat you;
Free my soul from the grip of painful distress.
You have hidden me away from the wicked coming in throngs,
From the multitude of those whose minds care for lawless deeds.
They sharpened their tongues like swords,
And they sent forth deeds <note>The "deeds" here likely refer to bitter words or plots.</note> just like a chilling bow,
In order that they might shoot the innocent man in secret;
And they do not fear in their hearts when they strike him suddenly.
They established a shameless and hostile story firmly,
They considered how to hide a snare with their craftiness.
They said, "Who could see their dark deed with their eyes?"
And in their spirit they tracked down helpless sin.
They grew weak as they sought out boundless things.
A man with wise counsel shall approach from the depths,
So that he may exalt the life of the high-thundering <note>original: ὑψιβρεμέτοιο. A classic Homeric epithet usually reserved for Zeus, here transferred to the God of Israel.</note> King.
Thus they sent forth arrows as if they were a mere child <note>Suggesting that despite their malice, their attacks are ultimately as harmless as a child's toy against God's protection.</note>,
Speaking insolent things with their feeble tongues.
All those who looked upon them together were troubled.

<header>APOLLINARIS <note>original: Α Π Ο Λ Ι Ν Α Ρ. Referring to Apollinaris of Laodicea, the 4th-century scholar who rewrote the Psalms in the style of classical Greek epics like the Iliad.</note></header>

Who among mortals did not see, and seeing did not tremble in his heart,
and declare the much-troubling works of God?
Observing the skills of the immortal hand with his own mind,
let the righteous man rejoice in the help of the King.
And for all those who are upright, infinite praise shall increase.

->Psalm 64 <note>Septuagint numbering. In modern English Bibles, this is Psalm 65.</note><-

Sing for me, Ezekiel, and cry out, Jeremiah, <note>Apollinaris includes these prophets because the Greek Septuagint title for this psalm associates it with the words of Ezekiel and Jeremiah regarding the return from the Babylonian exile.</note>
how those in the land once came under the day of slavery,
how they once departed, leaving behind the temple of the King;
lament, mourning for the honored [dwelling] of the King.
The people of glory, for the sake of their own sins, [departed];
this sixty-fourth hymn was fashioned at that same time.

65.
Awaits you <note>original: Te manet. This Latin note refers to the opening of the Psalm in the Vulgate: "Te decet hymnus," or "To you praise is due/awaits."</note>
</margin>

For you, Blessed One, in Zion an infinite hymn is distinguished;
in Jerusalem you receive the returned vow.
All the race of mortals approaches you, and you, O Shepherd, listen;
hear our prayer; all human flesh entreats you.
We forgot righteousness when we heard lawless words,
but may you have a merciful heart toward our miseries.
Happy is he who, chosen by you, dwells in your house;
may he pitch his tent by your courtyard halls.
We shall fill our dear heart with the good things of your house;
your pure temple is wondrous in its righteousness.
Hearken, O Suppliant and King, to our shared voice;
you are the hope of all the ends of the earth and the distant sea.
Fashioning your shaded mountains in righteousness, O Blessed One,
girded with your strength as an unassailable belt,
you are the one who shook the greatness of the unmeasured sea.
Who could have the strength to bear the force of its surging wave?
The tribes of mortals were shaken, and they bore terrors in their hearts,

those dwelling at the furthest ends, for whom you fixed a sign.

let all people sing of the gladness of the King.

### Psalm 65. <note>Septuagint 65; this corresponds to Psalm 66 in modern Protestant and Jewish Bibles.</note>

If anyone should look upon the life of the dead who are rising, <note>Apollinaris often interprets the Psalms through the lens of the Resurrection.</note>
how the calling follows the commands of the King;
he would not marvel much; for the song knows this end;
this has become at once the sixty-fifth song.

<margin>66 Shout with Joy <note>original Latin: "Jubilate." This is the traditional title of Psalm 66 in the Western church.</note></margin>
**A**ll the earth, shout a song of victory to God the King;
and play the divine name on the clear-sounding lyre.
Sing God-pleasing glory for his fair-famed works.
Say: "For God, works of great wonder are brought to pass;
your enemies would speak lies to your exceeding strength." <note>This likely refers to enemies "cringing" or yielding feigned obedience out of fear.</note>
Let all the earth sing to God for me and fall on their knees;
let everyone sing of your sweet name, O King.
Come, friends, behold the famous works of the great God;

<header>A P O L L I N A R.</header>

He is irresistible in His counsels, far above the mortal race. <note>original: "meropēida phytlēn" – a Homeric epithet for humans, literally 'the race of voice-dividing ones.'</note>
Having commanded the sea, He fixed it into dry land,
and He made the sounding rivers into paths for feet.
There let us together rejoice in the immortal King,
who rules unceasingly by His own strength and might.
The eyes of the King watch over the tribes of mortals;
let those whose rush is full of bitterness not be arrogant toward me.
O people, celebrate God with auspicious words,
receiving His ever-sounding voice with your ears.
It is He who placed my heart so that it might be among the living,
setting the step of my feet unshaken among all people.
You took notice of us, O King, with wise counsel,
discerning us well, just as a smith tests silver in the fire. <note>The image of the smith (chalkeus) testing silver is a metaphor for spiritual refinement through trial.</note>
You drew us, even against our will, into a deceitful net;
You placed heavy sorrows upon our backs.
You set men to ride over our heads;
we passed through the life of water and of flaming fire;
but You led us out into the rest of a new season. <note>The "new season" refers to the refreshment or relief following the trial.</note>
May we come into Your house with parched offerings; <note>original: "thyeessin karphomenoisin" – referring to dried or burnt sacrifices.</note>
I shall pay the vows of my former promise,
which I uttered before while being tried by distress.
I shall bring fat, fire-blazing sacrifices to You:
with woolly rams and the fragrant smoke of ointment,
and I will offer strong-horned oxen along with goats.
Hear me, all you who fear the Lord,
while I tell all the good things He fashioned for my soul.
I kept crying out to the Immortal One, lifting Him up with my tongue;
but if I had seen lawless insolence in my heart, <note>original: "prapidessin" – a poetic term for the midriff, considered the seat of the mind and heart.</note>
then the King would not have heard my voice as I cried out.
Therefore, the All-Seeing One has heard our voice,

<margin>67 May God have mercy <note>original Latin: "Deus misereat"</note></margin>
May You have pity on us, O King, with merciful counsel;
and may You shine Your ever-manifest countenance upon us,
so that we may perceive Your path throughout the earth,
and Your evil-averting light among foreign nations.
The peoples shall hymn Your glory, O Blessed One, most high;
let Your much-shouted glory be the care of all peoples.
Let the nations rejoice, exulting in songs;
because You will judge the mortal race <note>original: "meropēida phytlēn" – a Homeric term for humans as 'voice-dividing ones.'</note> without unfairness,
and You shall lead the nations to travel across the friendly earth.
The peoples shall praise Your glory, O Blessed One, most high;
let Your much-shouted glory be the care of all peoples.
The life-giving earth has sprouted its fruit.
May our God grant us most graceful praise,
may the King grant us praise; and let the ends of the earth fear Him.

Psalm 67.
->A hymn of songs struck for the wise David,<-
->having the sixtieth and seventh place.<-

<margin>Let God arise <note>original Latin: "Exurgat ᴅᴇᴜꜱ"</note></margin>
Let the Immortal arise, and let Him send scattering upon His enemies,
and let the hostile ones flee far from His sight in fear;
thus may they utterly fail like helpless smoke;
as when wax melts, being consumed by a blazing fire,
so let the sinful perish from the eyes of the Admirable One;
but let the divine men, the interpreters of justice, be glad.

<page-num>82</page-num>
# APOLLINARIUS

Always needing the cause of divine eyes,
rejoicing from the soul with sweet gladness.
Sing to the King and celebrate His divine name in song;
prepare a graceful path for Him who journeys toward the setting sun. <note>original: "hypo dysios" — likely a reference to the Septuagint's "Him who rides upon the sunsets," often interpreted as the heavens.</note>
Ever-immortal, ever-immortal He was; "The Lord" <note>original: "anax" — meaning King or Lord.</note> was the name fashioned for Him.
And may one rejoice near His divine countenance.
Let all tremble through fear of His sight,
the leader of widows and the father of orphans.
The Pure One dwells in pure places.
He Himself grants the solitary a home to dwell in,
and He loosed the bound swarm from their wretchedness,
and He restores the light of life to those perished in tombs. <note>Apollinarius here interprets the "rebellious dwelling in a parched land" as a resurrection or restoration from death.</note>
Alas, how You, O King, led the people in times past;
how once You passed through the glorious ground of the desert.
The whole earth was shaken, and the blazing heaven dripped;
even the much-entreated ground of Sinai heard the voice of God,
even the voice of God the King, who watched over Israel.
O King, You willingly send forth a good rain upon Your land;
for You restored it when it thought it was perishing.
And the tribes of Your flock dwell within it;
You came as a ready helper of kindness for the poor.
The Lord shall direct the word to those who bring tidings with power,
leading the beauty of the well-born son <note>An epic phrasing for the "kings of armies" mentioned in the Psalm.</note>
of the son who shall divide the spoils of the house with the good man.
Though you should sleep in the midst of the sheepfolds, <note>original: "klērōn" — literally 'lots' or 'allotments', but here referring to the pastoral divisions.</note>
you shall be like the beautiful silver wings of a young dove,
whose back the golden-green bloom of gold has clothed,
when the Immortal sends His kings upon her.
In Salmon, the snow covers her body. <note>A reference to Mount Zalmon; the "snow" is often interpreted as the white bones of the defeated or a literal snowfall providing divine relief.</note>
A divine mountain, a mountain of many flowers was made very rich;
a rich mountain, it was made entirely like curdled cheese. <note>original: "tyrō" — cheese. A literal translation of the Septuagint's metaphor for a lush, fertile mountain (Mount Bashan).</note>

<summary>This page continues the epic paraphrase of Psalm 68, focusing on God as a father to orphans, His leading of Israel through the desert, and the metaphorical description of His holy mountain using pastoral imagery.</summary>
<keywords>Psalm 67, Psalm 68, Apollinarius of Laodicea, Sinai, Mount Salmon, orphans, widows, silver dove, divine mountain</keywords>

<meta>This page continues the poetic paraphrase of Psalm 67 (Psalm 68 in the English tradition) by Apollinarius of Laodicea. Having described God’s care for the poor and His march through the desert, the poet now turns to the majesty of Mount Zion and the triumphal procession of the tribes of Israel.</meta>

<page-num>83</page-num>

### PSALM 67

Why do you look askance at the fiery mountains?
This is the mountain where God chooses to dwell in a home dear to His heart.
There the Blessed One will fix His firm tent forever.
The chariot of God is ten thousand fold, and many are the dancers upon it. <note>The poet uses the term "choreutai" (dancers/performers) to describe the "thousands of angels" mentioned in the traditional Psalm, emphasizing a joyous, ritual procession.</note>
There the Blessed One, among the holy foundations of Sinai,
Rising on high, led away many spoils of war.
And He took gifts from the mortal race; <note>original: "meropēïdos genethlēs" — a classic Homeric phrase for the "articulate-speaking" race of men.</note>
For even those who were previously led into transgression had denied Him.
May the good and supreme King be praised forever;
The King shall be called "Praised" day after day.
He continually guides our light.
Grant help to me, the king, and to those who stand in need. <note>Apollinarius likely adopts the persona of David here, referring to himself as "the king."</note>
The King alone knows how to save even from death.
Yet truly He shall crush the heads of His enemies,
The long-haired crown of those whose hearts possess a sinful spirit.
The King said: "I will return from Bashan, trusting in My power, <note>Bashan was a region known for its high mountains and fertile soil; in the Psalm, it represents the heights from which God brings His people back.</note>
I will go back again from the unharvested sea; <note>original: "atrygetoio thalassēs" — a famous Homeric epithet for the sea, here applied to the Red Sea of the Exodus.</note>
So that you might dip your foot in pure blood,
And the tongues of your dogs in the blood of your enemies."
O Pure One, mortals saw Your paths openly,
The footsteps of the King upon His glorious seats.
The rulers went near those who were singing,
In the midst of maidens playing upon timbrels.
Praise the faithful, beloved King in the congregations,
The Immortal One from the fountains of gentle-hearted Israel.
There was Benjamin, whose youth is timeless,
And there were the leaders of those who formerly ruled Judah;
The champions of Zebulun and Naphtali were there together.
O Shepherd, command with Your song-worthy strength;
O God, establish for us the defense You have worked for our children.

<sig>F 2</sig>

<summary>This page concludes the description of God's victory over His enemies and depicts a grand liturgical procession of the tribes of Israel, utilizing Homeric epithets to describe biblical events.</summary>

<keywords>Mount Zion, Sinai, Bashan, Benjamin, Judah, Zebulun, Naphtali, Red Sea, Homeric hexameter, divine victory</keywords>

<page-num>84</page-num>

# APOLLINARIS <note>Apollinarius of Laodicea (c. 310–390 AD) was a scholar who rewrote the Psalms as classical Greek epic poetry to ensure Christian students could study high literature without pagan content.</note>

Rushing from the temple, O Pure One, to your city;
Kings honor the God of many names with gifts; <note>original: "polyklēenti theō" — "the God of many names or many prayers," a common epic epithet.</note>
Rebuke the reed-born beasts of the mountain-shaggy herd. <note>A poetic rendering of "Rebuke the beasts of the reeds" (Psalm 68:30), often interpreted as a reference to the crocodiles or hippopotami of Egypt, representing Israel’s enemies.</note>
The nations of bulls shall return with the calves of the people,
Until those for whom a price was openly paid are excluded.
Scatter the tribes of war-loving mortals, O Spotless One;
Coming as suppliants, they shall hasten from the cities of Egypt;
The race of Ethiopians shall stretch out their hands to the Immortal One.
Kingdoms of the earth, sing clearly of the King from every side;
Play the harp with a sweet-sounding lyre for the Shepherd of the World.
Sing to the One who has stepped from heaven to the heaven of the sheepfold. <note>original: "aipoliēs polon" — the poet uses "pole of the goat-pasture" to create a pastoral-cosmic image of God’s dwelling.</note>
Let them utter a dear hymn of praise with a life-sustaining voice.
Sing of boundless glory to the All-King;
Always over Israel He holds majestic pride,
And His strength is wondrous among the heavenly clouds.
The Immortal One, caring for His holy mortals; <note>original: "athanatos thnētoisi" — emphasizing the bridge between the divine and the human.</note>
May the King grant strength and noble power to Israel.
Always let the All-King be praised in unceasing song.

### Psalm 68 <note>Numbered as Psalm 69 in most English Bibles. This is one of the "Lament" psalms, traditionally seen by early Christians as a prophecy of Christ's suffering.</note>

->A hymn of David’s changing heart,<-
->holding the sixty-eighth part.<-

<margin>Save [me], 69. <note>original Latin: "Saluum 69." This is a later Latin reference to the Psalm number in the Vulgate Bible.</note></margin>
Save me from the waters, O King, for they have touched my heart;
Stuck in the woody abyss of life, I am failing; <note>original: "byssō en hylēenti" — "woody/slimy depth," referring to the "miry depths" where there is no foothold.</note>
A sudden storm has seized me as I go into the depths of the sea.
My throat has grown rough from crying out until I am parched.
I lost my sight, looking with my eyes for God the King;
Those who hate me without cause are more than the hairs of my head.
Those whom I unjustly feared as enemies are mighty.
In my foolishness, I paid for the plunder that I myself did not take. <note>Referring to the experience of being falsely accused of theft and forced to make restitution.</note>

<summary>This page concludes the triumphant victory song of Psalm 67 and shifts into the desperate plea of Psalm 68, where the speaker (David/Christ) describes drowning in a metaphorical sea of enemies and false accusations.</summary>

<keywords>Apollinarius of Laodicea, Egypt, Ethiopia, Israel, Shepherd of the World, David, lamentation, miry depths, Greek hexameter</keywords>

<meta>This page continues the poetic paraphrase of the Psalms by Apollinarius of Laodicea. Having concluded the triumphant imagery of the previous Psalm, the text moves into the lamentation of Psalm 68 (Psalm 69 in the English tradition). The poet continues to use elevated Homeric Greek to express the psalmist's deep distress and plea for divine rescue.</meta>

<page-num>85</page-num>

# PSALM 68 <note>Numbered 69 in the Hebrew Masoretic text. This is one of the "Lament" psalms, traditionally attributed to David, expressing deep suffering and a plea for God's deliverance.</note>

You know, O King, all that I have done in my foolishness, <note>original: "chaliphrosynēsin" — an epic term for light-mindedness or folly.</note>
nor were my reckless sins hidden from your sight.
Let not those who look to God's work ever hold shame because of me, O King,
nor let any man who longs for God hear himself blamed.
For your sake, O King, we have endured reproaches;
I will endure it, even if a flush of shame covers my face.
I have become a stranger, with no share among my own kin,
and a guest to the sons of my sweet and simple mother. <note>The poet uses "nēeos" (foolish/simple), a common Homeric epithet, to describe the mother, adding a classical flavor to the biblical "mother’s children."</note>
Because the zeal for your devouring house has seized me,
and I am bitten in my heart by the insults of your enemies.
With the labors of fasting I broke my own spirit,
and they fashioned that very fasting into a cause for reproach.
I set aside my graceful robe and put on a garment of mourning; <note>The poet translates the biblical "sackcloth" into the more classical "mourning garment" (penthimon eima).</note>
but even so, I became the idle chatter of their tongues.
Those sitting by the gates spoke myths about me,
and those heavy with wine sang of me to the lyre.
But as for me, I call to you with unceasing prayer;
it is time, O King, when you might willingly appear openly to me.
Hear me in the rushing abundance of your mercy;
may you grant me, O King, the great light of your truth.
Save me from the mud, so that I might escape through the swamp;
<margin>Ps. 68</margin> Rescue me, Blessed One, from my enemies and the deep currents.
Lest the watery storm-blast ever swallow me up,
nor the mournful deep of the abyss seize me from within,
nor the boundless pit close its mouth around me.
Hear me, you who carry a gentle and compassionate spirit;
in the fullness of your mercies, <insert>may you look upon me with your all-seeing eye;</insert>
never turn your gaze away from your own child; <note>In the Greek "paidos," which can mean "servant" or "child," emphasizing the intimate relationship between the sufferer and God.</note>
hear me quickly, O Most High Blessed One, in my distress.
Restrain my soul with your all-seeing eyes.

<meta>This page continues Apollinarius’s poetic rendition of Psalm 68 (Psalm 69 in English Bibles). The previous page ended with the psalmist’s plea for God to hear him. This section contains the famous "gall and vinegar" passage—often interpreted by early Christians as a prophecy of the Passion of Christ—followed by a series of curses (imprecations) against the psalmist's enemies.</meta>

<page-num>86</page-num>

->**APOLLINARIUS**<-

<meta>Pencil foliation 75 at top left</meta>

Because of our enemies, O King, may you save me;
For you know my disgrace, my reproaches, and my great pain.
Before you stand all those who have plotted my ruin.
My heart followed after blame and outrage within my breast; <note>original: "phresin"—referring to the midriff or heart, the seat of thought and emotion in Homeric Greek.</note>
Nor did any man come as a companion to me in my sufferings;
Nor did I find from where comfort for my ruin might come.
They cast spirit-destroying poison into our food, <note>original: "iòn" (poison or venom). This corresponds to the "gall" mentioned in the biblical text.</note>
And to me in my thirst, they gave death-bearing vinegar.
Let a many-braided net be prepared for them instead of a table, <note>The poet uses "linon" (linen or net) to adapt the biblical "snare" into a more classical hunting metaphor.</note>
A hunt, and a deceitful trap, and a reward in the field.
May you extinguish their evil-plotting sight,
And may you continually subdue their backs with toil.
Pour out your heavy-wrathful anger upon them forever;
And let the rush of your murky wrath surround them.
Let their many-nourishing homestead become a desert,
And let no one find a dweller within their halls.
Because they pursued with desire the one whom you struck,
Adding pains to our wounds and to our sorrows.
Stitch error upon their errors; <note>original: "amplakiēn" (an epic term for fault or sin). The poet describes adding sin to sin as "stitching" them together.</note>
Nor let them approach your throne of righteous suppliants.
May they be wiped away quickly from the books of the living;
Let them never be numbered among the righteous inhabitants.
I remain poor and I digest a myriad of cares; <note>original: "pessō" (to cook, digest, or ripen). In Homeric Greek, this is a metaphor for brooding over or enduring grief.</note>
But I have the champion-defense of the King's watchfulness.
I would hymn the praiseworthy divine name in the dance,
And I would exalt him continually, singing of his glory.
This would please the King more than a youthful calf,
One relying on new-grown horns and hooves.
Let the mind of the poor man rejoice in his heart as he looks on.
Seek, with a shepherd's care, the giver of life to your souls.

<header>PSALM 68</header>

For the immortal King heard the poor man;
nor did he thus abandon the man bound in evils.
Let the heavens and the earth dance for the King,
the boundless sea and whatever the swelling tide of the sea nourishes.
For the immortal King shall save Zion;
and he shall raise up the cities of lovely Judea.
And those dwelling there shall possess it all by lot;
and the generation of his servants shall hold it all.
Let it be a care to those who cherish and labor for him.

### Psalm 69 <note>In English Bibles, this is Psalm 70. This short psalm is a plea for God to hasten his help and shame the psalmist’s enemies.</note>

->Reminding God, who knows all things,<-
->David said, "O Master, save me now";<-
->This hymn happens to be the sixty-ninth.<-

<margin>70. O God, come to my aid <note>original: "Deus in adiutoriũ" (Latin). This is the standard opening for this psalm in the Latin Vulgate.</note></margin>
**O** Immortal One, look always toward our help.
May shame seize all those who hasten to destroy my heart.
Let the one who devises evil against me be turned back in shame.
Let shame immediately seize those who rejoice in my ruin.
Let joy and gladness surround the holy race,
as they always speak of the great-famed gifts of God,
all who find your light, which wards off evil, among the gods. <note>In this context, "gods" likely refers to the heavenly hosts or the "mighty ones" in the divine assembly.</note>
May you ward off the pain of the beggar and the poor;
may you never delay to be present when I call.

### Psalm 70 <note>In English Bibles, this is Psalm 71. The Greek tradition attributes this psalm to the "Sons of Jonadab," a group known for their faithfulness to ancestral tradition.</note>

->The sons of great-hearted Jonadab, whom in former times<-
->The inhabitants of Babylon led into a day of slavery,<-
->Arranged the clear-toned melody of the seventieth song.<-

<margin>71. In you, O Lord, have I hoped <note>original: "In te dñe speravi" (Latin).</note></margin>
**DO NOT** put me to shame, since you have become my hope;
O Blessed One, in your justice care for me and guard me.
Quickly save me, inclining your ear to hear me;

<meta>This page continues the hexameter paraphrase of Psalm 70 (Psalm 71 in English Bibles). The previous page ended with the introduction to this psalm, attributed to the sons of Jonadab. Here, the poet Apollinarius continues using Homeric language to express the psalmist’s plea for protection in old age.</meta>

<page-num>88</page-num>
<header>APOLLINARIUS <note>original: "ΑΠΟΛΙΝΑΡ." referring to Apollinarius of Laodicea, the 4th-century scholar who recast the Psalms into Homeric hexameters.</note></header>

Be to me a protective shield-bearer <term>shield-bearer (aspidiōtēs): a Homeric term for a warrior, here used for God’s protection</term> as is your duty,
firm as a house, where it is possible to escape from evil;
you yourself are my seat of much bronze <note>Homeric imagery denoting great strength and durability.</note> and my mighty defense.
O King, rescue me from the hand of the evil-doing man,
from the hand of the unjust and the one who longs for lawlessness.
For you, O Most Excellent, have always been my hope;
a hope ever-living, even from my fresh-blooming youth.
Trusting in you, I have lived from the womb that bore me.
You yourself were my shelter in the belly of my dear mother;
throughout my life, may my song-loving hymn be sung to you.
I have become like a portent <term>portent (teras): a sign or wonder, often viewed with awe or suspicion by others</term> to many in my life;
but you appeared as a mighty helper among them.
Let my mouth be full of the grace of songs from every side,
so that, O Blessed One, I may hymn your ageless glory,
singing your magnificent praise all day long.
Do not cast me off, O Pure One, at the evil threshold of old age; <note>The "threshold of old age" is a classic Homeric metaphor for the final stage of life.</note>
nor let me perish while my life is still dear; do not abandon me then.
For evil and hostile men have said these things against me,
and those who watch for my soul have conspired together,
proclaiming that the Immortal One has abandoned his servant:
"Hasten and seize him, for now there is no one to help him."
But you, O King of All, never turn yourself far from me,
nor neglect to keep your bright-eyed watch <note>original: "aglaoskopia."</note> over me in my need.
Let shame seize whoever strikes at my heart with evils;
let blame and reproach hold those who devise harm against me.
But as for me, I shall trust in the Kingly God,
and I shall sing to him a song of praise even more than before.
Our mouth shall proclaim your righteousness;
all day long I shall hymn your unperishing light,
though I am unskilled in the trade of numbers <note>A poetic rendering of Psalm 71:15, "for I know not the numbers thereof," meaning God's mercies are beyond calculation.</note> and free from ruin.

<meta>This page continues the verse paraphrase of the Psalms by Apollinarius of Laodicea. Having completed the plea for protection in old age from Psalm 70 (Septuagint numbering), the text transitions into Psalm 71, which is traditionally associated with the reign of Solomon and the themes of justice and royal succession.</meta>

<page-num>89</page-num>
<header>PSALM 71 <note>original: "Ψ Α Λ Μ. Ο Α." Following the Septuagint numbering; this corresponds to Psalm 71 in most modern Bibles.</note></header>

Full of courage, I shall enter the temple of the Immortal One;
I shall remember your righteousness alone, O King.
From my youth you taught me what is right, O imperishable Shepherd; <term>Shepherd (poimēn): a common Homeric title for a leader or king, here applied to God.</term>
therefore I shall proclaim your divine works even until now.
O Blessed One, do not leave me as I grow old and grey,
until I reveal your mighty arm to those born later,
announcing your strength and your lovely justice,
which you, O Shepherd, have performed even unto the boastful heights of heaven.
Who else is like you, who has brought such great sorrows upon me?
And yet, turning back, you gave me the boundless strength of life.
You called me from the deep, from the base of the dark earth.
You did not withhold the exceeding strength of your justice from me,
and turning back to me, you healed my soul-consuming sickness; <note>The Greek "periterpsinēsaon nouson" suggests a disease that withers joy or delight.</note>
you called me from the deep, from the base of the dark earth.
For I shall praise you, O Blessed One, with a song for all the people,
singing on the lyre to the all-pure God of Israel.
While singing of you, I would greatly rejoice with my lips,
and with my soul, which you, O King, have released from deadly diseases.
Moreover, let my tongue sing of your righteousness;
so that those who seek to cause me pain may be put to shame.

### Psalm 72 <note>original: "Ψαλμὸς οβ." This is Psalm 72 in the Septuagint/Greek tradition, corresponding to Psalm 72 in modern English Bibles. Note that the Greek verses below describe it as the "seventy-first" song, reflecting a variation in manuscript counting.</note>

->a sweet melody concerning the famous Solomon.<-
->It is the seventy-first song.<-

<margin>72 God, [grant your] judgment <note>original: "Deus iudic." Abbreviation of the Latin "Deus, judicium tuum regi da," the opening of this Psalm.</note></margin>
O most excellent Shepherd, may you grant your good justice to the king,
and send forth your righteousness to the son of the king.
So that they may judge your people with your justice,
and judge the cause of the poor and the needy with excellence.
Let firm peace be the care of the mountains and the hills.

<meta>This page continues Apollinarius of Laodicea’s poetic rendering of the Psalms into Greek hexameter, the verse form of Homer's Iliad and Odyssey. We are currently in the middle of Psalm 72 (Psalm 71 in the Septuagint), a prayer for the king—traditionally Solomon—celebrating his justice, his vast dominion, and his care for the poor.</meta>

<page-num>90</page-num>
<header>APOLLINARIUS</header>

To judge the common people among the poor with upright justice,
and to bring aid to the wretched children who are in pain;
may he cast down the lying man from his spirit-crushing pride, <note>The Greek "rhēxinoeiēs" is a compound word suggesting pride that breaks or overwhelms the mind.</note>
and may he shine continually, like the radiant sun.
Outshining all things, just as the moon leads the way,
may he come down quietly from on high, like rain falling upon a fleece; <note>This refers to Psalm 72:6, often interpreted by early Christians as a metaphor for the quiet incarnation of Christ.</note>
or as when the heavens silently cast snowflakes upon the earth.
That day shall then be the mother of righteousness,
and of lovely peace, as long as the strength of the moon shall shine.
He shall stretch his power from one sea to the other,
and from the sacred rivers to the myriad ends of the earth.
The tribes of the Ethiopians shall quickly fall on their knees before him,
and his enemies, subdued, shall lick the dust around him.
Bring gifts, O islands, and bring them together, O rulers of Tarshish; <note>original: "Tharsidos." Tarshish was a distant port, likely in modern-day Spain, representing the ends of the known world.</note>
may they bring much-prayed-for gifts from the Arabians and the Sabaeans.
All the kings of the whole earth shall kneel before him together;
all kinds of nations shall place their necks beneath his yoke.
For he released the poor man from the hand of powerful men,
and the one harmed by poverty, who had no protector near.
He shall spare the miserable and the needy,
and he shall release the souls of the wretched from destructive ruin.
He shall snatch them away from usury and from unjust violence;
and then his admirable name shall shine before him.
He shall live, and may he receive the prize of Arabian gold;
around him as king, may they call with unceasing prayers,
and soothe him all day long with songs of praise.
On the highest mountains there shall be a glorious seat for the land,
and may it bear honey-sweet fruit more fertile than Lebanon.
May the people of the city spring up like the grass of the whole field.
Praiseworthy is the divine name unto unending generations,

<page-num>91</page-num>
<header>PSALM 72 <note>This header uses the Septuagint numbering; in modern Bibles, this is the conclusion of Psalm 72 and the beginning of Psalm 73.</note></header>

Before the sun, the glorious name of God remains,
shining even before the light-bringing moon.
Concerning Him, the thrice-desired glory of the nations of the earth shall endure;
all nations shall forever call God blessed.
Blessed forever is the immortal God of Israel,
who alone always accomplishes wondrous works.
Blessed is the name of God, cherished in glorious renown,
both now, and hereafter, and for generations yet to be.
May the glory of God fill the whole earth; let it be so. <note>original: "hōde genesthō," translating the Hebrew "Amen."</note>

### Psalm 72 <note>This marks the beginning of Psalm 73 in the modern numbering system.</note>

->The divine mind of David, the son of Jesse,<-
->ceased from his songs due to his frailty.<- <note>A poetic way of saying David has finished his portion of the book as he aged.</note>
->But Asaph sprang up and cried out with a divine spirit,<-
->uttering the second hymn of the seventh decade.<- <note>The 72nd Psalm. In the Greek tradition, Asaph is the chief musician appointed by David.</note>

<margin>Truly he is good <note>original: "utique bonus ē [est]," a Latin shorthand for the opening of the Psalm: "Truly God is good to Israel."</note></margin>
How very good is God to the upright in heart of Israel!
And yet my own feet were shaken a little by necessity,
and my footsteps slipped here and there.
Because indeed, in my soul, I was envious of the lawless,
beholding the peace of those who live in wickedness.
For them, fate was always inescapable and unholy, <note>The poet uses "potmos" (fate/destiny), a term common in Homeric epic, to describe the life path of the wicked.</note>
and they felt no heavy lashes of the scourge.
They are always ignorant of human labors,
for they are not subdued by the trials that are the debt of men;
therefore the transgression of their heart has weighed them down,
and unholy lawlessness surrounds them
just as some overflowing, fat insolence. <note>A reference to Psalm 73:7, "their eyes stand out with fatness."</note>
They walked about, handling the anxieties of their own soul,
pondering lawless things in their breasts, they spoke;
from their wickedness, they struck the Most High with insults.

<summary>This page marks a major transition in the Psalter from the prayers of David to the songs of Asaph, concluding a hymn of royal blessing and beginning a meditation on the troubling prosperity of the wicked.</summary>
<keywords>Psalm 72, Psalm 73, Asaph, David, Septuagint, Greek hexameter, divine justice, prosperity of the wicked</keywords>

They set their greedy mouth even to the gates of heaven,
lifting their tongue across the expanse of all the earth.
Because of this, my people would turn back to this place,
and full days would appear to them in every way.
And I said: "How then shall God perceive these things in his mind?
Is the most high King truly knowledgeable?"
How do these exponents of error <note>The Greek term *uphētores* suggests those who speak for or represent a cause; here, it refers to those who justify their own sin.</note> prosper,
safely and continuously mastering their wealth?
Perhaps I said in vain that I kept a holy mind,
having washed my hands in pure, all-undefiled water,
subdued in body daily by a lash just so,
smitten in my heart from dawn by strong rebukes. <note>The speaker laments that despite his purity, he suffers "lashes" or discipline every morning, while the wicked do not.</note>
I thought how I might speak thus with such wisdom:
"I am consumed by the deeds of the generation of your children."
But I considered it in my prudent mind,
and yet this appeared as a labor in my eyes,
until I should enter the all-undefiled house of God
and search out the final end of their inner chambers. <note>In the biblical text, this "end" refers to the ultimate fate or destruction of the wicked.</note>
To them you gave a pain equal to their deceitful works;
having lifted them high, you brought them down to earthly ruins.
How suddenly were they touched by a dark fate!
Being weak because of their own error, they perished.
As a faint dream of those waking from their beds,
so would you destroy their image within your city.
Because of this, my heavy-angry spirit burned in my heart,
and my kidneys <note>original: "nephroi." In ancient thought, the kidneys or "reins" were considered the seat of deep emotion and conscience.</note> were changed by my helplessness.
But I, subdued by the lash, did not know in my soul,
how I became like a beast before you, O most high Shepherd,
even though I was cared for by your good eyes continuously.
You were holding my all-undefiled right hand,

By your counsels, Blessed One, you have guided me,
and with your glorious, manhood-honoring renown you have received me.
What fate is there for me now in the heavenly dwellings,
or what else beside you did I wish to hold upon the earth?
My heart and my body melted away, O Immortal Shepherd,
but you, my portion, are remembered forever when my life has ended.
For look, those who separate themselves from you shall perish utterly.
You have given over to a bitter destruction those who indulge in wantonness. <note>original: *machlosunēs*. This term usually refers to lust or lewdness, but in this biblical context, it refers to "spiritual adultery"—the act of being unfaithful to God through idolatry.</note>
But for me, it was a good thing to draw near to the King,
and to place my unceasing hope in the immortal God,
so that I may sing your praise in the gates of Sion, O King.

### Psalm 73 <note>Psalm 74 in modern English translations.</note>

-> An anthem like the God-pleasing wisdom of Asaph; <-
-> the third poem of the seventh decade is here performed. <-

<margin>74 Why, O God <note>original Latin: *utquid deu[s]*. This is the opening of the Latin version of this Psalm: "O God, why hast thou cast us off forever?"</note></margin>
Why have you cast us off forever, O Shepherd?
Why has your anger roared against the flocks of your own sheep?
Remember your creation, which you alone acquired of old;
uphold the scepter of your royal portion yourself.
Remember the glorious Mount Sion, where you dwell in famous houses.
Lift up your hands against their transgressions;
how much evil the sinner has plotted against the holy things,
and how your enemies have roared in the midst of your festival!
They did not understand, setting up their own signs as signals,
having already advanced beneath the narrow entrance.
Like men in a thicket of trees, they fell upon the doors with axes,
breaking them with the hatchet and the tools of the craftsman.
Having set fire to your whole God-pleasing temple,
they profaned your undefiled name upon the earth.
They said in their hearts—those unhonored kinsmen <note>original: *pēoi*. A Homeric term for kinsmen or relatives; here used to describe the enemies of Israel as a "tribe" or "people."</note>—these things:

They set their mouth to be greedy, raising their tongue even to the gates of heaven, reaching across the folds of the whole earth.

and washed my hands in pure, all-undefiled water? 
For I was thus subdued in body by the lash all day long; 
from dawn I was struck in my heart by harsh rebukes. 
I pondered how I might speak so wisely; 
for I am wasted away by the deeds of the generation of your children. 
But I pondered it with my prudent mind, 
and this appeared as a labor <note>The "labor" or "toil" here refers to the mental struggle of trying to understand why the wicked prosper while the righteous suffer.</note> in my eyes;

while they were being lifted on high, you brought them down to the foundations of the earth.

Failing in strength because of their own sin, they perished.
