I became like a beast before you, O all-highest Shepherd.

### Psalm 73 <note>In the Septuagint numbering used here, this is Psalm 73; in modern Bibles, it is Psalm 74.</note>

->A hymn like the prudent, God-pleasing song of Asaph,<-

<margin>74 Why, O God <note>original: *utquid deus*. This is the Latin opening of the Psalm, often used as a title in manuscripts.</note></margin>
Why, O Shepherd, have you cast us off continually? 
Your wrath is armed against the sheep of your flock. 
Remember your assembly <note>original: *klisiēs*. Literally "hut" or "dwelling," but here referring to the congregation or fold of Israel.</note>, which you alone acquired from the beginning; 
you yourself took up the scepter of your royal portion. 
Remember famous Mount Zion, where you dwell in glorious houses. 
Lift up your hands against their transgressions; 
see how many evils the sinner has devised against the holy ones, 
and how the captors boasted in the midst of your festival. 
They did not understand their own signs <note>original: *sēmeia*. Refers to the military standards or religious symbols the invaders set up in the Temple.</note> when they set up their standards, 
appearing like men who have passed beneath a wooded canopy. 
As if in a thicket, they fell upon these doors with axes, 
breaking them down with the hatchet and the tools of a craftsman. 
They have set fire to your whole God-pleasing temple; 
they have cast your undefiled name to the profane earth. 
They said in their heart, these kin gathered together:

<meta>This page continues the poetic paraphrase of Psalm 73 (Psalm 74 in modern Bibles) by Apollinaris of Laodicea. The text is written in Homeric-style Greek hexameters, transforming the biblical lament into a classical epic form. On this page, the poet concludes the description of the enemy's devastation of the sanctuary and transitions into a "hymn of memory," recalling God’s power over creation and his past victories (like the Exodus) to move Him to act in the present crisis.</meta>

<page-num>94</page-num>
<header>APOLLINARIS</header>

"Come here," [they said], "to put an end to the festivals of the Blessed One throughout the land."
For we have seen no sign of our own;
no longer is the Lord God our guardian, nor is there any prophet among us.
Tell us, Blessed One, how long shall the lawless man increase his insolence?
The enemy man unceasingly provokes your name.
Why have you turned back your hand, the very one that wards off evil?
Why do you keep your right hand tucked within the fold of your robe? <note>This is a poetic expansion of the biblical question "Why do you withdraw your hand?" It suggests God is keeping his power hidden or inactive.</note>
Yet God has reigned as King from the beginning of life;
He fashioned his light <note>original: *phengos*. In this context, it refers to God's "salvation" or "presence" among the people.</note> for us in the center of the earth.
The boundless sea was made firm by your power;
you crushed the heads of the venomous dragons in the rushing waves. <note>original: *iobolōn... drakontōn*. In the poetic style of Apollinaris, the enemies of Israel (specifically the Egyptians at the Red Sea) are described as mythological sea monsters or dragons.</note>
You broke the head of the hidden and deadly dragon;
you gave him as food for the tribes of the Ethiopians. <note>In the biblical text, the "people of the desert" eat the remains of Leviathan; Apollinaris uses "Ethiopians" to fit the geographical vocabulary of Greek epic.</note>
You split open the springs of winter-rushing rivers,
and you dried up the roaring stream of Etham. <note>original: *Ethamou*. This refers to the drying of the Jordan River or the waters in the wilderness of Etham.</note>
Day and night alike belong to your command;
you created the sun along with the tireless moon.
Your unwearying hand fashioned all the borders of the earth;
you established the seasons of spring and the toil of summer.
Remember these things, O King—the very works which you have made.
A foe, in his strife, has defied the royal strength of the Father,
and a wicked-minded people has stirred the Lord to anger with their lawlessness.
Do not abandon to the wild beasts the heart that is your care; <note>The Greek *heart* (hētor) here represents the "soul" or the "dove" mentioned in the traditional Psalm text.</note>
do not forever forget the souls of the poor.
Look upon the covenant of your servant, O Lord,
for the dark places of the earth are full of wrongdoing.
Do not send the lowly man away in anger;
let the destitute and the poor sing of your name, O Lord.
Arise, O Pure One, and judge our cause;
remember how the foolish man mocks God with his quarrels all day long.

### Psalm 74. <note>In the Septuagint numbering used here, this is Psalm 74. In modern English Bibles, it is Psalm 75.</note>

"Do not ever destroy" <note>original: *mēdepote phtheirēs*. This translates the Hebrew musical instruction "Al-tashheth" found in the psalm's superscription.</note> the good melodies of song,
the illustrious Asaph, wise of heart, has called out. <margin>618</margin>
The seventy-fourth hymn <note>original: *hepta sphais dekasi pros, tetratos*—literally "seven tens plus four."</note> was fashioned—

<margin>We shall give thanks 75 <note>original: *Confitebimur*</note></margin>
To the Shepherd of song we shall speak a God-fearing hymn.
We sing in prayer the glorious name of the King of All.
I shall speak clearly of your countless wonders.
"When your appointed time appears, O Blessed One, I shall judge rightly.
The earth melts away along with all its inhabitants.
I have established its pillars with my tireless hands.
I told the lawless to cease from their lawlessness,
and to the reckless not to lift up their <term>horn</term> <note>original: *keras*. In biblical and epic poetry, the "horn" symbolizes strength, power, or pride.</note> at all;
do not lift the horn on high against divine decree,
nor speak lawless things against the Immortal One."
For help comes neither from the glorious east nor from the west,
nor even from the tree-covered mountains far from the desert.
For God is always the judge over those who do not know him.
One he makes wretched, and another he lifts up on high.
In the hand of the Blessed One is a cup of well-mixed, unwatered wine. <note>The "unwatered wine" refers to the strength of God’s judgment, which is not diluted.</note>
It is poured out from one side to the other,
yet its dregs remain unemptied;
all the servants of recklessness shall drink from it together.
But I shall rejoice forever among those who are equal,
I play the lyre sweetly to the immortal God of Jacob.
"I shall shatter all the <term>horns</term> of the arrogant;
so that only the <term>horn</term> of the godly might be lifted up."

### Psalm 75. <note>In the Septuagint numbering; this is Psalm 76 in modern English Bibles.</note>

->The wise-hearted Asaph, laughing at the rulers of cities,<-
->cried out the fifth song of the seventh decade.<- <note>original: *hebdomatēs dekados ki pempton*—literally "seventy and five." The "laughing" or "rejoicing" is a poetic addition by Apollinaris.</note>

Known <note>original: *Notus*. This is the Latin opening of the Psalm in the Vulgate: *Notus in Judaea Deus*.</note>
76

**G**od alone is made clearly known in Judah;
His great name is poured out over the nations of Israel.
In peace <note>original: *en eirēnē*. This refers to Salem (Jerusalem), as "Salem" means "peace."</note> his strong place was built,
and in lovely Zion is his dear and pure house.
There he shattered the force of mighty bows,
the shield, the sharp sword, and the unshakable battle-cry.
You shine a wondrous light from the immortal mountains.
The foolish-minded were troubled in their hearts with all haste;
falling into a helpless sleep, they found nothing—
those rich men, now helpless with their own hands.
At the rebuke of the King of Jacob,
a deep, unwaking sleep seized all the horsemen.
O Blessed One, you are terrible; who could withstand your might?
From of old your wrath has carried immense weight.
You delivered justice from heaven into the ears of mortals;
the earth, trembling, was bound in stillness,
when the immortal King arose to judge,
to save the inhabitants of the whole earth with a gentle spirit.
The thoughts of men shall sing to you from their breasts,
and the sweet remnant of their thoughts shall keep a festival for you.
Make your vows to the Immortal One, and give him a gentle prayer.
Let all who surround God bring glorious gifts
to the Strong One, who cuts off the spirit of rulers,
to him who is terrible to all the illustrious kings of the wide earth.

<header>P S A L M. 77 <note>The Greek text uses LXX numbering 77, which corresponds to Psalm 77 in the King James Version, though the introductory verse describes it as the 76th.</note></header>

### Psalm 77.

->Long-haired Asaph, on the path of Jeduthun,<-
->sang the sixth song of the seventh decade.<- <note>original: *hebdome amphi hebē dekados melos iachen hekton*. This identifies the poem as the 76th in his series, which aligns with the Septuagint numbering for this Psalm.</note>

<margin>77 My voice <note>original: *Nouma*. Likely a corrupted OCR of the Latin *Voce mea*, the opening words of this Psalm in the Latin Vulgate.</note></margin>
I sent up a cry to our King in my time of need,
and He, having heard our voice, looked down upon me.
I prayed to the immortal God in the day of my labor,
<margin>---</margin>
reaching out my hands through the night, and I was not deceived.
My heart refused to listen to any who would comfort me;
remembering the King, I held a glorious delight.
Suddenly I burst into song, but my mind was weak with exhaustion.
My dear eyes were kept busy with the night-watches.
My heart endured in silence while it was being stirred.
I pondered in my mind the days of most ancient life,
and remembering former years, I sang of the generations gone by.
All night long, I sang loudly of many cares in my heart,
and my searching mind was always tracing them out:
Will He then forget the noble human race?
Will He no longer remember those whom He Himself planted?
Has He stopped his own flow <note>original: *rheethrō*. A poetic metaphor for the "stream" of God's favor or grace.</note> forever?
<margin>O</margin>
Has the merciful God completely forgotten to be compassionate?
In His great anger, will He cool His mercy?
Now, having barely begun to understand, I recognized the right things:
this change comes from the hand of the Most High.
I remembered the works of the immortal King;
from the beginning, I will remember the wonders of my Lord.
I will meditate continuously on His divine works;
in my mind, I would sing of the struggles of God-built virtues.
O Immortal One, Your path was always in the holy place.
What god is so completely like our Lord?

<sig>G</sig> <note>This "G" is a "signature mark" used by early printers and bookbinders to keep the folded sheets of the manuscript in the correct order.</note>

<summary>This page contains a Greek hexameter paraphrase of Psalm 77, where the speaker laments God's perceived absence before finding comfort in remembering God's past miracles. The poet, Apollinaris, uses high-classical Greek style to bridge the gap between biblical theology and Greek literary tradition.</summary>

<meta>This page continues the poetic paraphrase of the Psalms by Apollinaris of Laodicea. We are concluding the paraphrase of Psalm 76 (Psalm 77 in English Bibles) and beginning Psalm 77 (Psalm 78 in English Bibles). Apollinaris continues to use high epic language, casting the God of Israel in the role of a Homeric "Anax" (High King) who wields the elements of nature like Zeus, yet for the deliverance of His people.</meta>

<page-num>98</page-num>
<header>A P O L L I N A R I S</header>

You alone are the Kingly God who performs far-famed wonders;
You have revealed your life to all peoples alike.
With your far-famed arm, You rescued your people,
the children of Joseph and of great-hearted Jacob.
The waters beheld You and shuddered at the sight;
the soundless foundations of the deep currents were shaken,
and a terrible roar arose through the ethereal clouds.
For your arrows <note>original: *belemna*. A poetic term for missiles, used here to describe lightning bolts.</note> flew hither and thither,
with the rush of resounding thunder in the spinning wheel <note>original: *kyklō trochalō*. This likely refers to the "wheel" of the whirlwind or the celestial sphere mentioned in the Septuagint version of this Psalm.</note>;
the whole earth is illuminated by the flashes of the Immortal.
From its very roots, the thick-foliaged earth was moved and shaken.
In the roaring sea are your paths, O Blessed One,
and your tracks are found in many waters;
yet the footprint of the King shall not be clearly seen by men.
You guided the people as a shepherd tends beautiful sheep,
by the wise hand of Moses and Aaron the priest.

### Psalm 77. <note>Psalm 78 in Hebrew/English numbering.</note>

->The seventh song of the wise Asaph,<-
->the ever-breathing revel of the seventy-seventh verse.<-

<margin>
He attends. <note>original: *Attendit*. This is the Latin opening of the Psalm in the Vulgate: *Attendite, popule meus*.</note>
78
</margin>
O my people, look with your eyes upon the God-pleasing law;
incline your ears to the speech of our mouth.
Opening my lips, I will speak in dense riddles from the beginning,
I will sing out the paths of the generations from the ancient past.
We have known these things as they were heard before;
for our fathers recounted them to us.
The works of the ancients are not hidden from their sons,
as they shout clearly in hymns to the Immortal One,
telling of his great power and his wonderful deeds.
He established a testimony for well-horned Jacob, <note>original: *eükkerōs*. An epic epithet usually meaning "well-horned" or "fair-horned," used in Homer for cattle; Apollinaris applies it here to the patriarch Jacob, perhaps signifying strength or the "horns" of an altar.</note>

<meta>This page continues Apollinaris of Laodicea's poetic paraphrase of Psalm 77 (Psalm 78 in English Bibles). This page picks up with the instruction to pass the law to future generations and begins the historical recount of Israel’s rebellion and God's wonders during the Exodus.</meta>

<page-num>69</page-num>
<header>PSALM 77</header>
<sig>G 2</sig>

And He gave a glorious law to mighty Israel,
which the King established of old for our ancestors,
so that parents might reveal righteous things to their children,
so that the next generation might learn all these things thereafter.
Even the sons who should be born in the unity of the race,
so that they, in turn, might grow up and tell these things to their own children,
so that they might place their hope in the Immortal King,
and not forget the deeds of God, their King,
but might pursue the commands of the Blessed One within their hearts,
and might not become altogether like their fathers.
For that was a chilling and crooked generation of mortal men,
a race whose spirit was ever thick with evil in their hearts,
nor did their mind keep faith with their own God.
The sons of Ephraim, far-shooting with the long bow,
fled from the heat of battle, basely turning their backs.
They rejected the precious covenant of God,
nor were they willing to serve under the laws of the King;
instead, they forgot the good benefit of God,
and how He was moved by His own counsel to work so many wonders—
deeds which the King accomplished of old before all their ancestors,
throughout the land of Egypt, in the flowery fields of Tanis <note>original: *Tanis*. This is the Greek name for the Egyptian city of Zoan mentioned in the biblical text.</note>.
How He once tore the sea in two and led the people through;
making the thick currents stand like a solid wall <note>original: *askon*. Literally "a wine-skin," used here as an epic metaphor for the water being held back or contained as if in a vessel.</note> on either side,
and for them by day, He provided the sign of a path under a cloud,
while by night He guided them with the vast light of a torch of fire.
He split open the relentless, beast-nursing <note>original: *thērokomon*. A classical epic epithet for a mountain or wilderness that "nurtures wild beasts."</note> rock in the desert;
seeing it, you would have thought a drink was creeping up from the very abyss,
for He brought forth water from the holy rock for the children of Israel,
and a great stream like ever-flowing rivers.
Yet they forgot, and began to stitch error upon error,

-> # APOLLINARIS <-

They provoked the heavenly King with an unpardonable insult,
testing the immortal God from their chilling hearts,
demanding food to satisfy the cravings of their own delusion <note>original: *atē*. In Greek epic, *atē* refers to a temporary madness or blind folly sent by the gods that leads a person to ruin.</note>.
Speaking ill of the Immortal One before all, they said:
"Can He really prepare a feast in the wilderness?
For even if He brought water out of the crawling rock,
roaring in immeasurable rushing streams like a torrent,
from where would He provide bread for the needy,
or how could He set a table for the people?"
These things stirred the mind of the most powerful King to anger;
for which reason an unspeakable fire burned against Jacob,
and an unforeseen wrath fell upon Israel.
Because they did not trust the heavenly King,
nor did they place their full hope in His divine light.
From on high, the Shepherd <note>The "Shepherd" here is a poetic title for God, reflecting His care for His people as a flock.</note> commanded the clouds of the upper air,
and swiftly threw open the doors of the heavenly gates.
He rained down for them the well-known, sweet manna to eat;
He sent forth the bread of heaven for mortals to consume.
He gave the feast of angels to the human race;
God bestowed upon them an abundant feast for their journey.
He gathered the spirit of the south wind and made it blow,
and sent forth the rough blast of the sounding southwest wind.
He handed over meat to them like dust, falling of its own accord,
and He provided winged birds like the sand of the sea.
These fell suddenly into the assembly of men,
settling all around their many-scattered tents.
And feasting together, they were all filled with food;
for their pleasure, He granted the feast they desired.
Nor did He deprive them of what they longed for;
but the feast was not yet finished, though their hearts were filled to satiety...

# APOLLINARIS <note>original: *Α Π Ο Λ Ι Ν Α Ρ.* Apollinaris of Laodicea (c. 310–390 AD) was a scholar who rewrote the Psalms as a Greek epic poem to provide a Christian alternative to pagan literature.</note>

They provoked the wrath <note>original: *meiin*, a variant of *mēnin*, the famous first word of the Iliad</note> of the heavenly King,
testing the heart of the immortal, shuddering with fear,
demanding food to satisfy their own destructive folly.
Speaking evil of the Immortal among them all, they said:
“Can He truly prepare a banquet <note>original: *thaliēn*. A Homeric term for a rich feast or festivity.</note> in the wasteland?
For although He brought water from the unyielding rock,
with a roar like immeasurable rushing streams,
how could He grant bread to the needy?
From where could He set a table for the people?”
These words provoked the mind of the mighty King;
for this reason, an unspeakable fire blazed against Jacob,
and a terrible anger fell upon Israel.
For they did not place their trust in the heavenly King,
nor did they hold any faith in His divine light.
The Shepherd <note>original: *poimēn*. A common biblical metaphor for God, here used in an epic context.</note> commanded the ethereal clouds from above
and threw wide the doors of the heavenly gates.
He rained down the famous sweet manna for them to taste;
He sent forth heavenly bread for mortals to eat.
He gave the banquet of angels to the mortal race <note>original: *meropēïdi*. An ancient epic epithet meaning "articulate-speaking" humans.</note>.
God bestowed upon them a great feast for their journey.
He stirred the South Wind to blow,
and by His power brought the far-traveled blast of the Southwest Wind.
He delivered meat into their hands like dust,
and winged birds like the sand of the sea.
Suddenly these fell into the midst of the gathering of men,
surrounding their well-covered <note>original: *polystepheōn*. Literally "well-crowned," referring to the tops of the tents or the density of the camp.</note> tents on every side.
They all feasted together and were filled with food;
He granted them the feast their hearts desired.
Nor did He withhold what they craved.
But while the feast was not yet finished, and their hearts still lacked satiety <note>original: *korō*. A term for the point where one is full to the point of excess.</note>...

# PSALM 77 <note>In the Septuagint numbering, used by the early Church; this is Psalm 78 in most modern Bibles.</note>

And the flock of the Immortal followed Moses,
who brought an unexpected fate upon the heads of cities;
and he bound the strongest men of glorious Israel.
But despite these things, they did not forget their wickedness,
nor did they trust the King, even with such wonders.
But seeing their lives were empty and full of weakness,
their lives vanished quickly, just like words chased away by the wind.
**Once, close at hand**, when they were perishing, they began to long for the King;
from the break of dawn they went out, seeking Him as their helper,
calling to mind the multitude of their former hardships,
from which He had delivered them, granting them a day of freedom.
But they loved Him only with the very tips of their lips,
prattling with the flatteries of a deceitful tongue.
For they did not possess upright hearts or a steady mind,
nor did they keep faith with His covenant.
O King, You always hold a merciful purpose,
and You are gracious toward their sins and do not destroy them;
instead, subduing Your great-hearted anger that seeks to return,
You eagerly restrain Your kindling wrath.
For You know the nature of the race of weak mortals <note>original: *meropōn*. A classic Homeric epithet for humans, literally meaning "those who divide the voice" or "articulate-speaking."</note>,
whose life is like a wandering wind once death <note>original: *aidēs*. The poet uses the name of the Greek god of the underworld, Hades, to represent death or the grave.</note> restrains it. <margin>A small green decorative flourish, resembling a leaf or a stylized pen flourish, is drawn in the right margin.</margin>
Ah, how many times they provoked Him in the wilderness,
and stirred His spirit in those waterless regions,
testing the strength of the Most High God against what is right,
and irritating the Holy One of Israel with their words.
How they forgot the labors of His life-sustaining hand,
how He rescued them all from the slavery of the Egyptians.
How He performed unspeakable signs in Egypt,
and worked wonders in the plain of Tanis <note>Tanis is the Greek name for the Egyptian city of Zoan, mentioned in the biblical text as the site of God's miracles.</note>.
The water became blood, and it lost its former shape.

->### PSALM 77 <note>This corresponds to Psalm 78 in modern English Bibles, following the numbering of the Greek Septuagint.</note><-

And the shepherd-care of the Immortal followed after them with Moses,
bringing an unexpected fate upon the heads of cities;
and He secured the possessions of glorious Israel.
But even after these things, they did not forget their evil-stitching <note>original: *kakorraphiēs*. A Homeric term for weaving or plotting mischief.</note> ways,

Yet they loved Him only with the very tips of their lips,
wheedling Him with the flatteries of a lying tongue.
For they did not possess a straight-thinking heart or mind,
nor did they keep a faithful understanding of His covenant.
O King, You always hold a merciful counsel;
You atone for their failings and do not destroy them,
but instead, taming Your great-hearted anger, You turn it back,
and You eagerly restrain Your wrath as it begins to rise.

whose life is like a passing breath when Hades seizes it.

## APOLLINARIS

->### [CONTINUATION OF PSALM 77]<-

The springs were transformed, and the sweet stream was drained of its flow;
first the all-consuming **dog-fly** <note>original: *kynomyia*. In the Septuagint, this refers to the swarm of flies. Apollinaris uses a Homeric-style compound word meaning "dog-fly."</note> occupied the land;
then the frog, coming forth, ravaged the plowed fields.
And the **blight** <note>original: *erysibē*. This refers to mildew or grain-rot, though in many English Bibles it is translated as "caterpillar" or "locust larva."</note> gave over the fruit of the many-harvested crops;
and the sharp locust, reaping with toil, left nothing of the earth;
the cold, vine-bearing land perished by the hail,
and the sturdy sycamore withered under the joyless frost.
With a stroke of hail, He slaughtered the herds of four-footed beasts,
and a burning flame consumed their possessions.
Yet not even so did He cease, forever increasing His wrath against them,
wrath and terrible anger together with heavy-suffering labor.
Commanding with ungentle heavenly spirits, <note>A reference to the "evil angels" or "messengers of woe" mentioned in the Psalm (v. 49).</note>
He carved a path of much sorrow with a terrifying spirit,
nor did He spare the souls of the dying at all;
instead, He bound the herds and their keepers together in destruction.
Ah, how much the earth lamented the fate of the **firstborn**, <note>original: *prōtotokōn*.</note>
as it saw the first-fruit youth of the children being struck down.
But the Lord rescued His own people like fine sheep,
shepherding them like a splendid flock over the long mountains,
as they trusted in hope and the help of their Leader.
And the great depth of His sea covered their enemies.
They then reached the mountain of the Immortal’s decree,
the holy mountain which He had previously won with His own hand.
But the Lord drove out the former inhabitants from the land,
and bringing His people in, He divided the fields by lot,
rooting them by their tribes within His own dwellings.
Yet they still tested the patience of the King, provoking Him,
turning their spirits away from His testimonies,
doing the unlawful deeds of their ancestors of old;
they were like a useless bow, possessing a twisted and shameful deceit. <note>original: *strepsauchena lōbēn*. Literally "neck-turning shame," a vivid image for a bow that snaps back or a person who turns away from their duty.</note>

### PSALM 77 (78)

<margin>... [Fragmentary]</margin>
Provoking the Immortal One upon the mountain peaks;
and they distressed His heart with their **idols**. <note>The poet uses the term *eidoloisin* (idols), referring to the "high places" and graven images that turned Israel away from God after they settled in the land.</note>
Hearing this, God fanned His ill-working plan into a fury,
and immediately He held the steep pride of Israel in contempt.
He scattered the beloved, God-pleasing temple of **Shiloh**, <note>original: *Silōmē*. Shiloh was the major religious center of Israel before Jerusalem, where the Tabernacle was kept. It was destroyed or abandoned after the Philistines captured the Ark.</note>
where He had once lived as a housemate with miserable mortals.
They withdrew from their strength, and He surrendered them to plunderers,
and granted their beauty as a prey to the enemy.
The sword did not rest; they were slaughtered without ceasing;
those whom He once loved, He left without His dear help.
<margin>fire</margin>
A divine **fire** pitiably consumed the young men,
nor did any of the maidens raise a bridal song or lament; <note>The "fire" represents the heat of war. The lack of lamentation suggests a tragedy so absolute that traditional mourning and marriage rituals ceased.</note>
they did not save the priests from dying by the sword,
nor did any pity the widow <note>original: *polien* (the grey-haired woman).</note> of the perishing land.
Until at last, the Lord awoke as if from a sleep,
like a strong man whose head is heavy with wine.
Rising up, He struck the sinners on their backs, <note>A reference to the Philistines being struck with "emerods" or tumors (1 Samuel 5).</note>
and gave them a reproach for unending generations.
Then He rejected the bright tent of **Joseph**,
and immediately found fault with the lovely tribe of **Ephraim**; <note>The "tent of Joseph" and "Ephraim" refer to the northern tribes' loss of religious leadership.</note>
<margin>... [Fragmentary]</margin>
Instead, He chose the famous tribe of **Judah**,
and Mount **Zion**, which He cherished in His heart.
The King built His temple to be like that of the **unicorn**, <note>original: *monokerēos*. In the Greek Septuagint, this term (meaning "one-horned") is often used to symbolize great strength or height. The poet likens the stability of the Temple to this powerful creature.</note>
and fixed it in the earth for perpetual generations.
Then the King chose **David** as His servant,
and called him away from the many sheepfolds;
from behind the milking ewes He lifted him,
decreeing that he should shepherd **Jacob** His servant,
and hold great **Israel** as a famed possession.
And he shepherded them with the gentleness of a righteous heart.

### APOLLINARIS

and he led the wise company of his own people. <note>This line concludes the previous Psalm, describing God leading His people into the Promised Land.</note>

->**Psalm 78**<- <note>Numbered 79 in modern English Bibles. The Greek Septuagint, which the poet follows, numbers it as 78.</note>

->**The clear-voiced eighth song of Asaph,**<-
->**a labor-filled hymn concerning the seventh decade.**<- <note>original: *ebdomatēs dekados* (the seventh decade, i.e., the 70s). This is a poetic way of identifying the Psalm's number.</note>

<margin>
O God, they have come
79
</margin>

O Most High, the holy tribes of the heathen have invaded your portion;
they have defiled your pure temple with their lawlessness.
They have made **Jerusalem** like a shed for guarding fruit, <note>The poet uses the image of a flimsy temporary hut in an orchard (*opōrēs*) to describe the ruined state of the holy city.</note>
they have cast the remains of your servants to the birds of the air,
and set the flesh of your holy ones as food for the wild beasts.
As if it were water, the blood of your servants was poured out upon the ground;
around Jerusalem, it was poured out by those who should have been its guardians.
We have become a source of abuse to all the men outside our gates,
a laughingstock and a terrible plaything to those living around us.
Speak, Blessed One: how much longer will you be burdened with anger toward us?
Will your wrath continue to rise forever like a burning torch?
Pour out your fury upon the tribes that have not acknowledged your glory,
and upon the kingdoms that have neglected to seek your aid.
For they have devoured wise **Jacob** with evils, <note>Jacob here represents the whole nation of Israel.</note>
and finding his glorious place, they have made it a desert.
O Lord, forget our former foolishness and sins,
and quickly send down your mercies as helpers for us, O Blessed One.
For we have become utterly destitute among all people.
As the healer of our distress, O God, may you always be our help;
rescue your suppliants, O King, for the sake of your own honor.
May your name grant atonement for the errors of mortals,
lest the nations should ever say, "Where then has their God gone?"
May He be seen openly before the eyes of the nations,
avenging the blood of your servants which they poured out before.
The groaning of those in chains shall come before your eyes.

### PSALM 78

as great as the life of your immortal arm is;
so greatly care for the children who have perished unjustly.
Repay sevenfold into the lap of the outsiders <note>original: *thyraiois* (those outside the door/foreigners).</note>
 the terrible insults with which they reviled your infinite honor.
For we are your people, O King, and the sheep of your pasture;
we shall sing back a hymn of thanksgiving to you in our prayers,
and from generation to generation, we shall sing of your glory.

***

-> **Psalm 79** <- <note>Numbered 80 in modern English Bibles. This is a "maskil" or testimony of Asaph, written in the style of Homeric epic poetry.</note>

Singing a testimony for wise Asaph,
a noble song of deeds that are ever-changing,
governing the ninth after the seventh decade. <note>A poetic way of stating the number 79 (the 9th after 70).</note>

<margin>O Shepherd [of Israel] 80</margin> <note>original Latin: *Qui pascis* (You who shepherd).</note>
Look down, O Blessed One who shepherds honored **Israel**,
you who lead **Joseph** like a sheep and make him thrive.
Appear to me, seated upon your immortal **Cherubim**. <note>The poet uses the Homeric term *bebaōs* (stepping or standing) to describe God enthroned upon the celestial beings.</note>
Rouse your might before **Benjamin**, **Ephraim**, and **Manasseh**,
and come to save us.
Easily turning back, call us once more to prosperity,
and pour the light of your countenance upon our life.
O Lord of all powers, most kingly of all,
hear us, Blessed One: how long will you be angry at those who pray?
Granting us the bread of tears as our portion,
and making us drink a measured cup overflowing with grief.
You have made us a cause of strife among rival men,
and you have set forth frequent laughter for my enemies.
Turn us back, O Blessed One of powers, and call us to yourself;
send the light of your eyes upon our life.
You transplanted a flowering vine from **Egypt**;
clearing out the nations of the glorious land, you planted it.
Going before it, you prepared long paths through the fields,

# APOLLINARIUS

And you planted its roots, and they filled the whole field.
Countless mountains were covered by its shadowy bushes,
and its climbing vines sheltered the tops of the blessed cedars;
its branches stretched far toward the harvestless sea, <note>original: *atrygetoio thalasses*. This is a classic Homeric epithet for the ocean, used here to describe the Mediterranean.</note>
and its shoots went forth, rising above the rivers.
Why, O King, did you tear down its secure wall with your hands,
so that those who pass by the way now pluck its fruit?
A wild boar coming from the thickets has ravaged it,
and a solitary pig of the wilderness has grazed upon it.
O Leader of powers, turn your gaze toward me;
O King, stretch your all-seeing eye from heaven,
so that you may heal the pain of the green vine, <note>The "green vine" is a poetic metaphor for the nation of Israel.</note>
and prepare it and raise it up according to its destiny;
for it was made as the lovely work of your right hand.
Look upon the dear son of man, whom you yourself recognized as valiant,
though it is now dug around and burned with raging fire;
at the threat of your gaze, O all-seeing King, they shall perish.
Let your hand be poured out upon the man of your right hand,
upon the dear son of man whom you yourself recognized as valiant.
And we shall never depart from the immortal path.
Create the light of life, and may you listen to those who pray.
Turning easily, you will call us back to prosperity;
may you send the light of your eyes upon our life.

<meta>This page continues the poetic paraphrase of the Psalms attributed to Apollinarius of Laodicea. <header>### PSALM 81</header>

Strike a loud sound on the lyre, and with the holy psaltery.
As the song begins, dear men, sound the trumpet,
on that day when the ever-conspicuous beauty of the festival is present.
This is an ever-famous decree that Israel received,
to be kept for the righteous-judging King Jacob.
Joseph received it as a witness to the laws
when he came forth in days past from the gates of Egypt.
He heard a language that he had never known before. <note>original: *glōssan arizēlō aiōn hēs nēis etychthē*. This refers to the "strange language" heard in Egypt, often interpreted as the voice of God or the foreign tongue of the oppressors.</note>
His bright shoulders were released from painful labors—
he who formerly served with weary hands at the heavy basket. <note>The "basket" refers to the containers used by the Israelites to carry clay and bricks during their enslavement in Egypt.</note>
Then indeed you cried out, and I came as your defender;
I answered you from the secret place, hiding fire within a dark storm;
and I tested you at the waters of thirst. <note>original: *dipsaleē ērysō en hydati peiran*. A poetic reference to the "waters of Meribah" where Israel tested God's patience.</note>
Hear, O my people, for I shall speak fitting words;
never forget my commands, O Israel.
No new god shall appear among you,
<margin>The First Commandment</margin>
nor shall you fall on your knees to pray to a foreign god.
For I am your God, the ageless source of all good things,
who saved you and led you up from that ruthless land.
If you lack anything, open wide your mouth and I shall fill it.
But my foolish people did not listen to my words,
nor did Israel wish to behold me with their eyes.
Therefore I let them go to follow the desires of their own hearts,
following after their own foolish ways.
For if my people had listened to my voice,
if only Israel had walked in my footsteps!
I would have brought their enemies to the ground without any toil,
and I would have destroyed those who hated them with my own hand.
The enemies would have cringed before the Almighty King,
while for my people, the time of their lives would be forever pleasant.

# Psalm 82
<note>Numbered as Psalm 81 in the Greek Septuagint. In modern English Bibles, this is Psalm 82.</note>

The first noble song of the eighty-first, by Asaph.

The Undying One stands in the assembly of the gods, most high,
and sitting in their midst, the King judges among the blessed ones.
“How long will you fail to judge by the laws of pure justice,
and show favor to the faces of the arrogant and the wicked?
Restrain the violence against the orphan and the lowly,
and for the humble and the needy, make straight the laws of justice.
Deliver the destitute and the poor from their helplessness,
and snatch them without grief from the hand of the shameless.”
The foolish—they did not consider in their minds, nor did they understand,
for which reason they ignorantly walked in destructive darkness.
Therefore, let the foundations of the whole earth be shaken.
I myself willingly said before: “You are blessed ones,
and children of the glorious Most High King.”
But you shall die like mortals upon the earth,
falling like any one of the dying rulers.
Arise, O All-King, and judge for me your earth,
because you shall possess all the nations as your inheritance.

O Undying One, surely there shall be a voice heard by all;
never be silent, nor be mild in your heart.
Because the roar of your enemies has risen up thickly,
and those who hate you raise their heads.
They have devised a harmful plot against your people...

<header>PSALM 82</header>
<note>In modern English Bibles, this is Psalm 83. The header uses the Septuagint numbering.</note>

nor be silent; for they have plotted destructive things against your holy ones.
“Come now, dear friends, let us destroy them utterly,
and let us erase the name of Israel from the earth.”
Immediately, the hateful, mischief-plotting ones conspired;
they crafted a covenant against you, O Blessed One:
the tribes of the Edomites and the sons of Ishmael.
Together with them were Moab and the arrogant Hagrites,
the Ammonites and Gebalites, and the sons of Amalek,
and neighbors coming together with the people of Tyre.
Nor was the vast people of the Assyrians left behind,
but putting on their armor, they went as champions for the sons of Lot.
<note>The "sons of Lot" refers to the Moabites and Ammonites, traditionally descended from Lot.</note>
Do to them as you once did to Sisera and Midian,
or to the followers of Jabin by the storm-swollen stream of Kishon,
who were previously destroyed at Endor in grievous toil,

like dust which the wind quickly drives across the land.
Make their leaders, O most high King,

<note>These are names of Midianite princes and kings defeated by Gideon in the Book of Judges.</note>
all those who are commanders among them.
Who previously conspired to seize the most beautiful altar of God.
O King, make them all like a rolling wheel,
or like withered chaff before the storm,
or like fire feeding upon thick meadows and woods,
or like a flame rushing over parched mountains;
even so, O Blessed One, pursue these men with your whirlwind,
and terrify them all with your irresistible, harsh wrath.
Enclose their faces in shame, all of them,
so that, being ashamed, they may seek your name.
Let them be constantly reproached with hateful rebukes.
Let disgrace seize them all, and let the proud perish together.

### Psalm 83

<note>In modern English Bibles, this is Psalm 84. The numbering here follows the Septuagint, the ancient Greek translation of the Old Testament.</note>

->A direct hymn for the sons of high-spirited Korah,<-
<note>The "Sons of Korah" were a group of Levites to whom several Psalms are attributed; they served as temple singers and gatekeepers.</note>
->it followed as the third of the eighth decade.<-

How lovely are your dwellings, O Lord of powers, forever;
from my soul I long to dwell around the courts of the Blessed One;
my soul and my limbs exult in the living God.
For even the sparrow has found a home to her liking,
and the turtle-dove has found a safe nest for her young;
your altars, O Blessed One, are and shall be my home, O God of powers.
Blessed indeed are all those who dwell in your houses;
they shall sing your glorious praise in their songs.
Blessed is the man who has found noble strength in you;
obeying in his heart and driving himself toward heaven itself
through the valley of weeping <note>original: "κοίλῃ κλαυθμῶνος" — This refers to the "Valley of Baca," a place of sorrow transformed by faith.</note>, where he found a famous place.
The giver of the law will grant blessings in due measure;
they shall go from strength to strength as they set out,
the God of gods shall be seen openly by them in Zion.
<term>Zion</term> <note>The hill in Jerusalem where the Temple stood; used here as the destination of the spiritual pilgrim.</note>
Hear me, O Blessed One of powers, for I pray to be your servant;
O Ruler of Jacob, receive now my voice with your ears;
look kindly with your eyes upon my defense;
do not pass by the face of your anointed one.
A single day spent in your court is better than thousands elsewhere.
It is better to be called a dweller in the halls of God
than to be a powerful inhabitant among the tents of sinners;
since God dearly loves mercy and truth,
He himself will always grant glory and his grace;
and he will never leave the innocent apart from his own good things;

### Psalm 84

O King, you have shown love to your land again after our sorrows.

You have released the boundless lawlessness of your people.
You have covered all manner of their sins from your sight.

Turn back to us and bring your light to me, be merciful to your own;
ward off your much-suffering wrath from us.
Do not be angry with your servants forever,
nor increase your heavy-hearted wrath from generation to generation.

so that your people might rejoice, exulting in you.
Alas, reveal your divine mercy to us;
give your evil-averting light to those who look to you.
I will hear what God himself shall speak to me,
planting peace for all his people,
and for the minds of his holy ones, and those who turn back to him.
Truly his light attends those who fear him,
so that famous glory may extend throughout our land.
There, truth and mercy meet one another,
and peace has kissed lovely righteousness.
Gracious truth has sprung up from the earth,
and righteousness, flowing from heaven, has looked down.
For indeed the King of gentle things shall give life-sustaining gifts,

### Psalm 85
<note>Following the Septuagint numbering; this is Psalm 86 in modern English Bibles.</note>

->I sing a noble song, the eighty-fifth;<-
->great David offered up this prayer to the King.<-

Incline your ear, O King, and listen to my voice,
for I have become a man without possessions and poor.
Guard my heart, O King, for I am holy to you. <note>In this context, "holy" or "devoted" (hosios) implies someone who is faithful to God's covenant.</note>
Save your servant, since you are my undying hope.
Be merciful to me as I cry out to you all day long in prayers.
Gladden the heart of your strong-spirited servant,
for I have sought after you for my life and my soul.
For you yourself are very gentle, doing what is kind,
nor do you fail to increase your bounty for those in need.
Hear my prayer and look upon the sound of my voice.
In my distress I cried out, and you listened to my prayers.
There is no other among the immortals <note>The poet uses the term "immortals" (athanatoisi), common in Greek epic for gods, to translate the Hebrew concept of heavenly beings or false gods.</note> entirely like God.
For no works appear altogether like your works.
All the nations of men, whom you fashioned with your hands,
shall come before you, Most Blessed One, and shall pray.
They shall glorify your name and your ageless strength,
since you are great and perform undying wonders.
You alone have always been the supreme King.
Guide me in your way, and let me find the truth;
let my soul be gladdened as it trembles at your divine name.
With an unceasing heart I will sing of you, O Blessed Supreme One.
May I glorify your famous name continually,
because your mercy, O King, covers me round about.

--- Page 157 ---
<meta>This page continues the transcription of the "Paraphrasis Psalmorum" (Paraphrase of the Psalms), a poetic reworking of the Psalter into Homeric Greek hexameters. This page contains the beginning of Psalm 87 (Psalm 88 in modern Bibles), which is traditionally regarded as the most sorrowful of all the Psalms. The poet, likely Apollinaris of Laodicea, uses epic vocabulary to describe the despair of one who feels abandoned by God in the realm of the dead.</meta>

<header>APOLLINARIS</header>

### Psalm 87 <note>In modern Bibles, this is Psalm 88. The Greek Septuagint, which the poet follows, numbers it as 87.</note>

-> This too was given to Mahalath <note>original: "Μαελέθ" (Maeleth). This is a transliteration of a Hebrew musical term, possibly referring to a specific tune or a flute-like instrument.</note> for the wise sons of Korah, <-
-> the eighty-seventh sweet song. <-

O most supreme, good strength of our light,
by day I cry out and all through the night to you in my prayers;
let my prayer come before your eyes,
to my supplications, O King, may you incline your ear;
because my helpless soul is weighed down with sorrows,
and my dear life has come near the lowliness of Hades. <note>original: "ἀίδαο" (Aidao). The poet uses the name of the Greek underworld to represent the Hebrew 'Sheol' or the grave.</note>
I have been reckoned as equal to those rushing down into the pit;
I became like a mortal man for whom no helper appears,
and yet among the dead I am like one who is free,
like the neglected ones who, having been slain,
sleep around tombs, of whom you no longer take heed, nor do you care for them.
For indeed they were driven out from your glorious hand.
They placed me in the lower pit that destroys men, <note>original: "φθισήνορα" (phthisēnora). A classic Homeric epithet meaning 'man-destroying' or 'wasting away.'</note>
beside the shadowy place of murky death.
Your heavy-pained anger is set firm around me,
and all your waves toss me high in the air.
You have removed all comfort of companions far away;
they loathe me like some dismal thing.
Ah, how then they lived!—and I follow unwillingly, <note>The poet interprets the "imprisonment" of the Psalmist as being forced into a life he does not choose.</note>
and from weakness, even the light of my eyes was dimmed.
But I called upon you as a helper in such great distress,
and toward you my hands were raised all day long.
Will you work your wonders among the neglected dead?
Or shall physicians rise up as singers for you? <note>original: "ἰητῆρες" (iētēres). In the Greek version the poet follows, the word for 'the departed' (Rephaim) was interpreted as 'physicians' or 'healers.'</note>
Shall someone, O King, sing of your mercy beside the tomb,
and of your lovely truth in the place of destruction?

<summary>A poetic Greek paraphrase of the first half of Psalm 88, depicting a soul in deep despair and physical decay, using the language of Homeric epic to describe the grave and God's abandonment.</summary>
<keywords>Psalm 88, Apollinaris of Laodicea, Hades, sons of Korah, Mahalath, Greek hexameters, lamentation, underworld</keywords>

--- Page 158 ---
<meta>This page continues the Greek hexameter paraphrase of the Psalms, traditionally attributed to Apollinaris of Laodicea. It concludes Psalm 87 (the modern Psalm 88), a prayer of profound distress, and begins Psalm 88 (the modern Psalm 89), which celebrates God's covenant with David.</meta>

<header>PSALM 87 <note>The Septuagint numbering; this corresponds to Psalm 88 in modern Bibles.</note></header>

Shall your divine works be revealed to mortals in the darkness?
Or is your justice not forgotten in the land of oblivion?
Therefore, while living, I pursue you and beg for your help,
and waking at dawn, I shall call upon you with my prayers.
Why do you send my gentle-minded prayer so far away,
and turn your face back away from your suppliant?
Poor from my youth and well-acquainted with labors,
from my heights I fell low and remained helpless.
Around me, O blessed one, your thick floods have surged,
and the chilling terrors of your life-ending wrath drive me away.
They surround me like deep-flowing water,
casting around from every side, all day long, here and there.
You have removed my neighbor and my faithful companion,
and no consoler from the outside came to me in my grief.

## Psalm 88 <note>In modern Bibles, this is Psalm 89.</note>

-> A song worthy of the heavenly wisdom of the graceful Ethan, <-
-> the eighty-eighth melody. <note>original: "ὄγδοον ὀγδοάτῃ ἀμφὶ μέλος δεκάδι" (ogdoon ogdoatē amphi melos dekadi). The poet uses a complex phrasing to indicate the number 88.</note> <-

Ceaselessly and forever, O King, I shall sing of your mercy,
from generation to generation, O blessed one, telling your truth with my speech.
For you said that mercy is built up for all time,
and your truth is established in the very heavens.
"I have made a covenant with my chosen ones;
I swore to David, who was ever my servant:
Ceaselessly I shall prepare your offspring for their thrones,
and I shall build your most glorious throne for all generations."
The heavens shall hymn your divine works, O blessed one,
and your truth shall be praised in the assembly of the holy ones.
For who among the dwellers of the clouds shall be equal to the Lord?
Or who will ever be like you among the sons of God? <note>original: "υἱάσιν ἀθανάτοιο" (huiasin athanatoio). Lit: "sons of the immortal." The poet uses epic language to describe the heavenly host or angels.</note>

<sig>H 2</sig>

<meta>This page continues the poetic paraphrase of Psalm 88 (Psalm 89 in modern Bibles) in Greek hexameter, attributed to Apollinaris of Laodicea. The text continues to praise God's sovereignty over nature and history before transitioning to the specific covenant made with King David.</meta>

### APOLLINARIS

God has always been glorious in the councils of the holy ones,
how great, how fearsome; he encompasses all those who dwell around him.
O leader of the powers, who could rival your might?
You yourself, armed with truth, perform valiant deeds.
You yourself rule over the force of the loud-crashing sea,
calming the waves when the winds stir them into a crest.
You struck down the arrogant man like one mortally wounded; <note>This likely refers to Pharaoh or the mythical sea monster Rahab, symbolizing chaos and opposition to God.</note>
heavy with power, you scattered the wretched transgressors.
The heavens and the earth are possessions of your command;
with your well-rounded hands you laid the foundations of the world,
and you yourself created the north and the boundless sea.
Both Tabor and Hermon <note>Mount Tabor and Mount Hermon are prominent mountains in the region of Israel/Palestine, used here to represent the whole of the land praising God.</note> dance around you,
<margin><image-desc>A small green decorative branch with leaves and a single red tip.</image-desc></margin>
always unshaken by the strength of your arm. <note>original: "ἀστυφέλικτος" (astyfeliktos) meaning immovable or firm.</note>
Let your right hand be magnified with powerful courage;
justice and judgment stand around your throne;
truth and mercy run before your face.
Blessed is the people that knows how to shout for joy;
truly your ageless light shall lead them,
those who always rejoice, for whom your name is a delight,
and their fame is celebrated under your righteous ways.
For you declared yourself to be their boast of exceeding strength,
and in you, emboldened, they shall lift up their horn. <note>The "horn" is a standard biblical metaphor for strength, dignity, and military victory.</note>
For the immortal's protection of help is always over the land—
the protection of the pure King, the glorious one of Israel.
Then you spoke these things, addressing your children in speech:
"I have bestowed strength upon a powerful one and help upon a wide-ruling one.
Choosing the most excellent from the people, I exalted him for the nations.
I found David, my dear servant and companion;
I anointed your beautiful body with holy oil;
for indeed my hand shall delight to be his helper..."

<summary>This page contains a Greek poetic rendering of Psalm 89, celebrating God's power over the sea and mountains, and recounting the divine choice and anointing of King David as the protector of Israel.</summary>
<keywords>Apollinaris of Laodicea, Psalm 89, Davidic Covenant, Mount Tabor, Mount Hermon, Greek hexameter, Divine Sovereignty, Anointing</keywords>

<meta>This page continues the Greek hexameter paraphrase of Psalm 88 (Psalm 89 in modern numbering), likely written by Apollinaris of Laodicea. Having established God’s power over creation, the text now focuses on the "Eternal Covenant" with King David, promising him divine protection and a perpetual kingdom, while also warning of the consequences if his descendants stray from the law.</meta>

<page-num>117</page-num>
<header>PSALM 88</header>

And my arm shall always strengthen him.
Never shall the enemy find delight <note>The Greek "ἡδὸς" (hedos) suggests the enemy will gain no pleasure or advantage in their assaults.</note> in prevailing over him,
nor shall the son of lawlessness bring him to ruin through suffering.
I will cut down his enemies before his eyes with my own hands,
and I will expose those who hate him to public shame.
And my truth and my mercy shall run together with him,
and through me his horn <note>In biblical and Homeric-style poetry, the "horn" represents strength, dignity, and kingly power.</note> shall be raised in great glory.
I will place his hand upon the unharvested sea, <note>original: "ἀτρυγέτοιο θαλάσσης" (atrygetoio thalasses). This is a classic Homeric epithet meaning "barren" or "unharvested," here used to describe the vast extent of David's dominion.</note>
and his right hand shall rule over the rivers.
He himself, with eternal longing, shall call me "Father,"
my Savior, the light of his salvation, and the Most High God.
And I will make him my firstborn, my beloved son,
high above all the kings who labor in war.
I will keep my gentle mercy for him unceasingly,
and I will confirm the glorious faithfulness of my covenant with him.
His beloved seed shall remain firm from generation to generation,
and his radiant throne as long as the days of heaven endure.
But if his children should forget my laws,
and do not walk together in my paths,
if they should treat my justice as a profane thing,
and forgetfully disregard my commandments,
I will visit their hateful deeds with a rod and a scepter,
punishing their reckless acts with scourges.
Yet I will not utterly scatter my former mercy from him,
nor will I deal unjustly with my truth,
nor will I profane my covenant,
nor will I take back the words that have passed from my lips.
I have sworn to David; I will not lie with my firm oaths:
his line shall be blessed forever among all people,
and his throne shall be radiant, just like the sun.

Just as the full moon is by its nature when at its peak,
and a faithful witness to him stays in the great heaven. <note>This refers to the promise in Psalm 89:37 that the Davidic throne would be as enduring as the moon.</note>
But you, O King, have shifted him with all kinds of rebukes;
you have passed by the one anointed with your own oil. <note>original: "χρισθέντα" (christhenta). While this refers to King David as the "Anointed One," for a Christian writer like Apollinaris, it carries a heavy resonance with Christ.</note>
You have overturned the covenant of your strong servant,
and you have made his sanctuary a profane thing upon the earth for all.
You have broken all his glorious battlements, <note>original: "κάρηνα" (karena). Literally "heads" or "summits," used here in a Homeric sense for the tops of city walls or towers.</note>
by which he was previously guarded as if they were his greatest defenses.
And wayfarers passing by plundered him;
he became a thing of shame before his own neighbors.
You raised up the right hand of those eager to destroy him;
you gave glory to his shameless plunderers.
You turned back the strong edge of his sword in battle,
and you did not come as a helper to him when he was in need during the fray.
But from the thickets of battle, my glory is stained;
ah, how his unique seat <note>The "unique seat" refers to the throne of David.</note> is cast down from on high!
Ah, how his throne perished in such a short and fleeting time!
And around his crowns, shame held him back on all sides.

<margin><image-desc>A small ink drawing of a plant with thin leaves and stalks, possibly a botanical study or marginal doodle.</image-desc></margin>

Tell me, Blessed One, how long will you be angry and harsh,
fanning your soul's anger into a burning fire?
Remember, All-King, how short human life is made.
For surely you did not create the race of mortal men in vain?
What mortal is there who shall escape the bond of death in his life,
calling back his dear soul to return from the house of Hades? <note>original: "ἐξ ἀΐδαο" (ex Aidao). Apollinaris uses the classical Greek name for the underworld, Hades, to translate the biblical concept of Sheol.</note>
Where for me is the boundless radiance of your former mercy,
which you promised to David, nodding your head in confirmation? <note>In Homeric poetry, Zeus's "nod" was the ultimate sign of an irrevocable promise; Apollinaris applies this image to God's promise to David.</note>
Do not forget your servants who have been reproached,
whose evil quarrels we previously bore in our own bosoms,
just as the enemies formerly inflamed you with insulting words,
they who disgraced the image of your Anointed One. <term>Anointed One: (Christos). The psalmist refers to the earthly king, but the poet likely intends the reader to think of the suffering of Christ.</term>

Blessed shall be the Lord, the God of Israel. <note>original: "αἰνετὸς ἔσσετ᾽ ἄναξ θεὸς ἔσσεται Ἰσραήλο." This line serves as a formal "Amen" concluding the third book of the Psalms (Psalms 73–89).</note>

### Psalm 89. <note>In the Septuagint numbering used here, this is Psalm 89; in modern English Bibles, it is Psalm 90.</note>

If it is lawful for a mortal to speak of the Heavenly One, he was ever made
<margin>Moses.</margin> Moses, the most faithful servant of the Immortal God.
He who once offered up a prayerful cry, which was composed
as the eighty-ninth song. <note>Apollinaris often includes these "metrical introductions" to identify the speaker and the Psalm number within the poem itself.</note>

To us, O Blessed One, you are sweet in our incurable generations.
Before the seats of the mountains, before even the birth of the earth,
I sing of you as King from of old and unto eternity.
Do not turn me away and make man low again,
commanding mortals to see a life that returns to the dust. <note>original: "παλίνδροσον" (palindrosos). Literally "back to dew," a poetic way of describing the body returning to the earth.</note>
For in your eyes, a thousand years are as yesterday,
which, having passed away, was as a watch in the night.
Their works shall become a thing of grief to them in their years.
<image-desc>A small green decorative flourish in the right margin resembling a stylized sprout or flame.</image-desc>
Like a flower, the dewy morning shall pass away;
the morning might bloom, and having bloomed, pass away.
But at evening, it might fall and dry up in its falling.
For we have become weak when you are angry,
troubled by the terrible wrath of the King.
You have set our lawlessness before your ready eyes;
**our life is revealed in the light of your countenance.** <note>This line is underlined in green ink in the original manuscript.</note>
We have become like smoke because of our frailty,
and as you are angered, our precious strength melts away.
Our years are exactly like a spider's web. <note>A striking simile used by Apollinaris to emphasize the fragility and intricacy of a short life.</note>
The time of human life is seven decades.
And if we should live even more, up to eighty years,
that life is always excess toil and labor.
Because he gently subdues the race of mortals.
Who has recognized the great strength of your wrath in his heart?
Who could number the joyless terrors of your soul?

O King, may you show your precious season to their right hand, <note>Apollinaris uses epic, Homeric Greek to describe God's favor and timing.</note>

with delight and gladness in our hearts all our days;

and now guide their dear children with a returning spirit.
Let the celebrated splendor of the King appear to us,
directing the works of our inner hands. <note>The "inner hands" is a poetic way of describing the intentions or spiritual efforts of the soul.</note>

Since he himself would rescue me from the snare of the hunters,
and from the reckless word of a full-to-bursting disturbance.
The King himself, covering you with his shadowy shoulders; <note>original: "μεταφρένῳ" (metaphrenō). Apollinaris uses the anatomical term for the back/shoulders to mirror the "pinions" or "feathers" of the original Hebrew/Greek Psalm.</note>

never shall you fear because of nightly terror,
nor the arrow flying with a whirring throb by day,
nor the thing crawling in the dark through the depths,
and under the demonic influence of the one crying out at midday. <note>This refers to the "Noonday Demon" (daimoniou mesembrinou), a famous figure in early Christian ascetic thought representing sloth or sudden spiritual attack.</note>

A myriad <note>original: "μυριὰς" (myrias). In Greek, this means ten thousand.</note> shall fall right there from your right hand.
Truly, no one shall ever be able to come near you to harm you.
Nevertheless, looking on with pure eyes,
you shall see the just reward for evil-doing. <note>original: "ἀμπλακίης κακοεργέος" (amplakiēs kakoergeos). A poetic phrase for the consequences of sinful deeds.</note>
Since you yourself were made my life-giving hope,
you indeed hold your brave confidence with the Most High.
Never fear the encroaching necessity of evils,
nor be distressed that a scourge might strike your body;
He will command heavenly armies to surround you,
that they might guard you in all your paths;
And they, O blessed one, shall lift you in their palms and carry you,
lest you ever strike your precious foot against a stone.
You shall boldly step upon the asp and the basilisk, <note>The "basilisk" in ancient texts refers to a mythical king of serpents, though here it represents a deadly poisonous snake.</note>
you shall trample the venomous dragon and the beast underfoot.
"I shall rescue him whose soul has yearned for my hope;
I will protect him well, for he has truly learned my name.
When he prays, I myself will hear him swiftly,
and I will be near him in his distress all his days;
Having rescued you, I will grant you everlasting and magnificent glory,
and I shall fill his life with endless generations;
I will unceasingly reveal to him my ageless light."

How I greatly rejoice, O Blessed One, enchanted <margin>(chtheis)</margin> <note>original: "θέλχθεις" (thelchtheis). The margin provides the second half of the word split by the line break.</note> by your deeds, and I shall delight my mind in your divine works. 
Who could sing of your boast-worthy art? 
Who could sing <gloss>e</gloss> <note>The scribe inserted the letter "e" to correct the spelling of the verb "to sing."</note> with a wise mind of its deep wisdom?
If any man ignorant of these things shall ever know them in his heart,
or if one who is preoccupied with folly should perceive them:
he shall not see the unjust flourishing like grass,
those prophets of lawlessness looking down from on high,
only so that they might forever reap destruction for their own evil.
But you alone were created the immortal, highest King.
Let the tribes of your enemies, those who work evil, perish.
The wanderers shall be scattered in all directions by the winds,
whereas, as if with the horn of a unicorn <note>original: "μουνοκέρηος" (mounokerēos). In the Septuagint tradition, this term refers to a powerful wild ox, symbolizing strength and victory.</note>, my horn shall rush upward,
and my gray old age shall flourish with rich oil.
I have seen with my own eyes the much-suffering fate of my enemies,
and I shall be gladdened by news of bitter and unaccustomed things.
Then the holy mortal shall bloom like a palm tree <note>original: "φοῖνιξ" (phoinix). The date palm was an ancient symbol of longevity and endurance because it thrives in the desert.</note>,
just like the verdant cedar of Lebanon that is heavy with spirit.
All those who are planted in the divine houses
shall bloom in the immortal courtyards of God.
Abundant and flourishing even in the season of old age,
they shall proclaim each thing, as is right, having experienced good things;
because the King, God, was always created upright in mind,
and he covers over the unjust thought with his wise understanding.

<header>PSALM 92 <note>This is Psalm 93 in the Masoretic/modern English numbering.</note> 123</header>

Firmly you set the unshakable, boundless world.
O Immortal One, your throne stands ever most ready;
From the ancient beginning of the world, you alone were most blessed.
From all sides the shouting rivers roar with their streams,
Longing to murmur with a surging rush,
Rising from the din of many gathered waters.
All the lofty works of the sea are mortal;
But the highest King alone was established in the heights.
Your testimonies, O King, possess a harmonious faith.
Within the undefiled royal temple, it stands forever firm,
For a time without end and through long-stretching days.

### Psalm 93 <note>This is Psalm 94 in modern English Bibles.</note>

->On the fourth day of the week, David sang a sweet melody<-

> The Immortal One has ever been an avenger <note>original: "ἔκδικος" (ekdikos). The Greek verse emphasizes God's role as the one who brings justice or "venges" the oppressed.</note> for all;
> The King, blushing at no one, shall judge with punishments.
> Ruling with noble laws, rise up, O Ruler of the earth,
> And pay back a reward for transgression to the arrogant.
> Hear, O Blessed One, how long shall the leaders rule by force?
> How long shall the spokesmen of error boast,
> Intending with their tongues the lawless insolence of their hearts?
> Workers of wickedness speak lifeless things without ceasing,
> Those who, O King, have laid your people low to the ground by force,
> And have ravaged your inheritance with evil, destroying it.
> They have slain the orphan, and they have defiled the widow,
> And they have overpowered the newly-arrived stranger, deprived of a helper.
> Nor did they regard the royal vengeance of the heavenly ones.
> For they did not think that the God of Jacob <note>The "God of Jacob" is a traditional title for the God of Israel, emphasizing the covenant with the patriarchs.</note> would know their insolence.
> But come, consider these deeds in your hearts, O foolish ones,

<meta>This page continues the Greek hexameter paraphrase of the Psalms, traditionally attributed to Apollinaris of Laodicea (4th century AD). This specific page continues Psalm 93 (Psalm 94 in modern Bibles), picking up from the middle of the poem where the psalmist addresses the foolish and describes God's role as the supreme judge and comforter.</meta>

<page-num>124</page-num>
<header>APOLLINAR[IS].</header>

<image-desc>The initial letter 'O' of the first line is a drop cap, surrounded by an ornamental vignette printed in green ink. The decoration consists of delicate foliate scrolls and a stylized creature or grotesque head extending into the left margin.</image-desc>

**O** <note>The drop cap 'O' begins the word "Οἷς" (To those).</note> you whose lives were previously spent in folly, gather your wits.
Does he who gives hearing to others not hear even more himself?
Or shall the eyes of others not be seen by the eye of light?
Can he who instructs the nations not know how to rebuke evil,
he who alone plants every thought in the minds of mortal men? <note>original: "μερόπεσσι" (meropessi). A classic Homeric term for 'articulate-speaking' humans, used here to give the Psalm an epic, heroic tone.</note>
The King has perceived the vain counsel of mortals.
Blessed is the one who is disciplined by your instructions,
perceiving the designs of your law in his own heart;
planning gently so that he may escape the season of evil,
until an inescapable pit might appear for the reckless.
For he would never cast his people away from his sight,
nor will he abandon them to their perishing fate.
Until justice, having given birth to judgment, returns once more,
suspending all the upright-minded from its cherished branches. <note>This is a vivid poetic metaphor unique to this paraphrase, suggesting that the righteous are like fruit or blossoms supported by the "branches" of divine justice.</note>
Who would raise me up to be a destroyer of the ruinous ones?
And who would arm me and rouse me to battle against the lawless,
if the ruling Lord were not my protecting helper?
My living soul would have flown away to Hades. <note>original: "ἄϊδι" (Aidi). The poet uses the classical Greek name for the underworld to translate the Hebrew 'Sheol'.</note>
For whenever I received flight in my sinking mind,
immediately your mercy, appearing, O King, saved me;
however much immeasurable pain I bore amidst my sorrows,
I was comforted in my heart by your consolations.
Let no lawless throne, O King, be near to you,
which fashions misery through a fragile spectacle, <note>original: "γεδανῷ θεατίσματι" (gedanō theatismati). This refers to the fleeting, theatrical nature of worldly power that opposes God.</note>
longing to destroy the holy soul of a man;
devising doom for innocent blood without cause.
But the most high King himself became a fortress for me,
God came as a ready helper for my hope.
He shall deal to those ones a recompense equal to their own outrage,
and the King shall destroy them for their own deeds.

<header>P S A L M. 94.</header>

### Psalm 94. <note>In modern English Bibles, this is Psalm 95. The numbering difference arises because the Greek Septuagint, which the author followed, combines or divides certain psalms differently than the Hebrew Masoretic text.</note>

->Of David, the wise singer of the beautiful-choired lyre,<-
->the ninety-fourth eloquent poem.<-

Come, let us rejoice and be glad in the King;
let us sing a song of praise <note>original: "παιήονα" (paieona). A 'paean' was originally a Greek hymn of thanksgiving or triumph, usually dedicated to Apollo.</note> to the immortal Shepherd of the world.
With kindness let us seek his blooming countenance,
and rejoicing with psalms, let us raise a shout of praise together.
For indeed, among all things, God increases great strength,
and he himself was made the most kingly of all the blessed ones. <note>original: "μακάρων" (makaron). In Homeric Greek, this refers to the 'blessed gods,' but in this Christian context, it refers to the angels or the 'gods' of the nations over whom God reigns supreme.</note>
He will never cast away his dear and gentle people,
holding the famous boundaries of the earth in his immortal hand,
ruling supreme over the high-crested mountains.
He himself fashioned the sea and holds it by his sovereignty,
and he built the much-nourishing earth <note>original: "πουλυβότειραν" (poulyboteiran). A classic Homeric epithet for the earth that provides food for all.</note> with his own hands.
Come, praying, let us fall about his knees;
let us weep before God, and he will have pity on his own handiwork,
for he is our King and our God at once,
and we are his people and the nurslings of his pasture.
Today, if you would hear the voice of the King with your ears,
let no one have a heart of bronze <note>original: "χαλκείη κραδίη" (chalkeie kradie). A poetic metaphor for a stubborn or unfeeling heart.</note> or be unyielding,
as when you provoked the heart of the Immortal with your deeds,
as of old when you were before God in the rugged desert,
where our ancestors of old put him to the test;
who for forty years saw my works and my might.
Therefore, I loathed that destructive generation,
and I said: they wander continually in their hearts,
and they have not at all considered our paths.
I swore in my wrath that they should not see my place of rest.

<note>This list provides the Greek terms used in the poem above: Psalm, David, lyre/harp, King, paean (hymn), shepherd, most kingly, he will show mercy, desert/wilderness, generation, rest.</note>

<summary>A poetic Greek paraphrase of Psalm 95, calling the faithful to worship God as the creator of the natural world while warning them not to be hard-hearted like the Israelites in the desert.</summary>
<keywords>Psalm 95, David, Creation, Shepherd, Wilderness, Hardness of Heart, Epic Poetry, Hexameter</keywords>

-> ### Psalm 95. <-

-> To the infinite King of the radiant house, <-
-> King David sang after his portion of the spoil, <note>original: "μετ’ ληΐδος αἶσαν" (met’ lēïdos aisan). This phrase likely refers to the dedication of the house of God after captivity, a theme found in the Septuagint title for this psalm.</note> <-
-> the ninety-fifth clear song. <-

Sing to the King for me with newly-born songs;
let all the earth shout with great strength and sing back to the Shepherd.
Resound the honored name of the All-King;
proclaim his light day after day.
Announce his honor to nations of every kind;
declare the honored wonders of God to the peoples.
Because he is indeed great and was always fashioned to be praised,
and he is a most powerful terror <note>original: "δέος" (deos). While often meaning 'fear,' in this epic context it refers to the religious awe or dread inspired by a supreme deity.</note> among all the gods.
For all the gods of the nations were but idols of demons; <note>original: "δαιμονίων εἴδωλα" (daimoniōn eidōla). This reflects the early Christian interpretation of the Septuagint, where the 'worthless idols' of the nations are identified as demonic spirits.</note>
but God created the bronze-clad <note>original: "πολύχαλκον" (polykhalkon). A classic Homeric epithet meaning 'brazen' or 'firm as bronze,' here used to describe the enduring strength of the heavens.</note> heaven by his own counsel.
Praise and great beauty are before his face;
purity and ever-befitting glory are joined in his temple.
Bring honor and glory to God, the All-King;
bring glory that bears his name to the honored Lord.
Having received your sacrifices, go unto his courts;
pray to the Shepherd of the world in the holy, sacrifice-receiving court;
let all the earth tremble before God the King.
Speak to the peoples: God reigns among all.
For he established the circle of the world, which time knows not how to shake.
May he judge the peoples with laws that walk a straight path.
Let the heavens be glad, and let the earth rejoice together;
let the sea be stirred, and all that the swell of the sea nourishes.
Let all things together rejoice in the fruit-bearing plains.
Then indeed let the thickets and the forest be glad together,
before God the King as he hastens toward the earth;
for he has come to judge the wretched mortals, <note>original: "μερόπεσσιν ὀϊζυροῖσι" (meropessin oizyroisi). 'Meropes' is the Homeric term for 'articulate-speaking men,' and 'oizyros' is a common epic adjective for the 'miserable' or 'toiling' condition of humanity.</note>

# Psalm 95.

-> Of the bright palace of the eternal King, <-
-> David’s song rang out after the fate of the spoils, <- <note>original: "ληΐδος αἶσαν" (leïdos aisan). This refers to the historical context of the Psalm, often associated in the Greek tradition with the restoration of the land or the temple after conflict.</note>
-> the ninety-fifth clear-sounding song. <-

Sing to the King for me with newborn songs;
Let all the mighty earth sing back to the Shepherd.
Sing out the honored name of the All-King;
Day after day, announce his auspicious light. <note>original: "αἴσιον ἦμαρ" (aision emar). The poet uses the Homeric term for a "favorable day" to describe the biblical concept of salvation.</note>
Proclaim his honor to all various nations;
Remind the peoples of God’s honored wonders.
For he is truly great and has always been worthy of praise,
And he holds the most powerful awe among all the gods.
All the gods of the nations were but idols of demons; <note>original: "δαιμονίων εἴδωλα" (daimonion eidola). In the early Christian worldview, the gods of neighboring nations were often interpreted as literal fallen spirits or demons.</note>
But God created the bronze-heavy heaven by his own will. <note>original: "πολύχαλκον" (polychalkon). A classic Homeric epithet for the sky, suggesting it is solid, enduring, and bright like polished metal.</note>
Praise and great beauty are before his eyes;
Purity and ever-fitting glory belong in his temple.
Bring honor and glory to the All-King God;
Bring glory worthy of his name to the honored Lord.
Take up his sacrifices and enter into his courts;
In his holy court, pray to the Shepherd of the world.
Let all the earth bow down before the presence of the King.
Say to the nations: God reigns over all.
For he established the world’s circle, which time cannot shake. <note>original: "κύκλον ἔρρεισε" (kyklon erreise). This reflects an ancient view of the world as a stable, fixed sphere supported by divine power.</note>
May he judge the peoples with laws that walk a straight path.
Let the heavens be glad, and let the earth rejoice at the same time;
Let the sea be stirred, and all that the swelling ocean nourishes.
Let all things dance together in the fruit-bearing fields;
Then let the thick thickets and the woods rejoice together
Before the presence of the King as he hastens across the earth,
For he has come to judge wretched mortals, <note>original: "μερόπεσσιν ὀϊζυροῖσι" (meropessin oizyroisi). A common Homeric formula for "mortals who speak," here emphasizing human frailty before the judge.</note>
