### Psalm 96. <note>While the header reads 96, the poem itself identifies as the ninety-fifth song. In modern English Bibles, this is Psalm 96.</note>

From the radiant house of the exalted King,
David the lord sang with his portion of the spoils,
the ninety-fifth clear song.
Sing to me the King with new-born songs,
let all the mighty-strengthened earth sing to the Shepherd.
Celebrate the name of the King of all;
from day to day, proclaim his auspicious light.
Announce his honor to all various nations.
Tell of the wondrous works of God to the peoples;
for he is indeed great and has been made forever praised,
and he holds a most powerful dread among all gods.
The idols of the demons were the gods of all the nations;
but God by his own counsel created the bronze-vaulted heaven. <note>original: "πολύχαλκον" (polychalkon). A Homeric epithet for the sky, suggesting it is as solid and brilliant as forged bronze.</note>
Praise and great beauty are before his eyes;
purity and ever-fitting glory belong in his temple.
Bring honor and glory to God, the King of all;
bring the glory that belongs to his name, to the Lord;
receiving your sacrifices, enter into his courts;
pray to the shepherd of the world in his holy court.
Let all the earth tremble before God the King.
Say to the peoples, God reigns over all.
For he fixed the circle of the world, which time knows not how to shake.
He judges the peoples with laws that always lead straight.
Let the heavens be glad, and let the earth rejoice together;
let the sea be moved, and all that the swelling of the sea nourishes;
let all things together rejoice in the fruit-bearing fields.
Then let the dense thickets and the woods be glad,
before God the King as he hastens across the earth,
for he has come to judge the sorrowful mortals, <note>original: "μερόπεσσιν ὀϊζυροῖσι" (meropessin oizyroisi). The author uses "meropes," a classic Homeric term for humans as "articulate-speaking," combined with "oizyros," meaning "woeful" or "miserable."</note>

### Psalm 96. <note>In modern English Bibles, this is Psalm 97. The numbering here follows the Greek Septuagint tradition.</note>

-> Of David, when his land was restored to him, <-
-> concerning the ninety-sixth beloved song. <-

Rejoice, O earth! He has begun his reign, he who rules the heavenly ones;
rejoice in splendor, you islands of the grey sea-brine. <note>original: "πολιῆς ἁλοσύδνης" (polies halosydnes). A classic Homeric phrase describing the salt-foam of the sea.</note>
The Lord is near, shrouded in cloud and storm,
encompassing his throne on all sides with the fair justice of his judgment.
Before his mighty sovereignty, a soot-burning fire shall go forth,
so that it might consume sinners on every side.
The earth shone under the flashes of his lightning;
and as it saw them, the very foundations of the world were shaken.
The mountains melted like wax around the Lord,
before the King of all the diverse earth.
The starry heavens announced his divine law,
and all people clearly saw the glory of the heavenly King.
Let them be ashamed as they fall prostrate before carvings,
all those who offer glory to their own idols.
You bright angels, likewise bend your knees to him.
Let there be joy in the hearts of the mountains of Zion,
and the dancing <note>original: "ποσίκροτ’" (posikrot). Literally "foot-striking," referring to the rhythmic stamping of choral dancing.</note> daughters of Judah rejoiced in their souls,
by the will of your great-strength justice, O High-Ruler;
because you alone are most supreme to the very ends of the earth;
since you have been lifted so high, O God famous for your wisdom, as no other can be.
You who love the one God from your heart, flee from evil deeds;
for the All-Overseer <term>All-Overseer: A translation of "πανεπίσκοπος" (panepiskopos), emphasizing God's role as a guardian who sees everything.</term> loves the souls of his servants above all,
and he will always rescue them from the hands of the avenging demon.
A rising light has shone around the eyes of the righteous man...

### Psalm 97. <note>In modern English Bibles, this is Psalm 98. The numbering here follows the Greek Septuagint tradition.</note>

-> And David sang this ninety-seventh song as well. <-

Sing to me of the King with new-born songs,
him alone, since he made all things with his hands; <note>original: "θνητὰ" (thneta). While the traditional Psalm text says "wonders," Apollinaris uses a poetic term often referring to the creation of mortal things or earthly wonders.</note>
with his own life-preserving right hand he saved all things,
and with his glorious arm he guards all.
He revealed to mortals a deliverer from all kinds of evils,
the Immortal One standing before the generations of many troubles;
he caused lovely righteousness to rise for his own people.
For he remembered in his heart the house of Jacob,
and the truth concerning the dwelling of Israel;
the famous ends of the whole earth clearly saw
the evil-averting light of the mighty King. <note>original: "φέγγος ἀλεξίκακον" (pheggos alexikakon). "Alexikakos" is a classic Greek epithet, often applied to deities like Apollo, meaning "averter of evil."</note>
Let all the earth shout for the glorious Shepherd,
sing psalms joyously, and sing out in clear revelry,
play the lyre for the King of All with the sweet-sounding cithara,
with beaten trumpets and the horn that sounds a booming noise,
shout a song of praise to the immortal King of All.
Let the sea be stirred, and all that the swelling sea-wave nourishes,
the whole circle of the world, and all who inhabit the earth;
let the rivers clap together with tireless hands; <note>original: "ἀχρειώτων" (achreiōtōn). This is a poetic choice for "unwearied" or "ceaseless," personifying the rivers' constant flow as hands clapping in praise.</note>
let the mountains now rejoice before his face;
for he comes—or rather, he has already come—so that he might judge the earth fairly,
calling everyone under the truthful law of justice,
setting forth an unwavering tongue for the eager peoples.

### Psalm 98. <note>In modern English Bibles, this is Psalm 99. The numbering here follows the Greek Septuagint tradition.</note>

-> For the ninety-eighth is a sweet melody, <-
-> for David singing with a clear-toned lyre. <-

The immortal God has reigned; let the peoples not rejoice in pride; <note>The Greek "μὴ χαίρετε" (me chairete) suggests a warning to the nations that God’s sovereignty is a cause for awe or trembling rather than worldly celebration.</note>
seated upon the Cherubim he came, let the foundations of the whole earth be shaken.
The glorious King is great among the peoples and admired among the gods; <note>original: "θεοῖσί" (theoisi). In this context, "gods" usually refers to heavenly beings or angels.</note>
let everyone together sing of your ageless name;
for indeed it was made fearful and unapproachable,
and the honor of the King always embraces divine justice.
You yourself gave to Jacob a soul-pleasing life,
and provided a decree of right-judgment for Israel.
Lift up our immortal King of All,
worshipping at his footstool; <note>original: "θρῆνυν" (threnyn). Literally a footstool, symbolizing the earthly dwelling of God's presence.</note> for he was made holy.
Moses was great among the priests, and famous Aaron,
and among those who pray was steadfast Samuel; <note>original: "μενέχαρμος" (menecharmos). A classical epic epithet meaning "staunch in battle," here applied to Samuel’s spiritual persistence.</note>
they entreated him; and he in turn spoke with a voice,
and he answered them from a thick cloud with a word.
They became good guardians of the testimony of God,
and they did not neglect the many commands they learned.
Immortal One, you gave them a ready ear from heaven,
and your heart was always gracious toward them;
whatever things they did, you directed them with help.
Lift up our immortal King of All,
and bow forever at his holy mountain,
because among all, the King was made holy.

**All** the earth, shout aloud to the greatly-famed Shepherd;
rejoicing, place your necks beneath the yoke of the Immortal One.
Hasten joyfully before the divine countenance; <note>original: "ἐνώπιον ὄσσαν" (enopion ossan). The poet uses Homeric phrasing to describe appearing before the face or "voice" of God.</note>
perceive Him always as the King of All in your service.
He himself fashioned life for mortals, not we ourselves;
He fashioned us, and we were not the ones who knew how. <note>original: "ἴδριοι" (idrioi). A classical term for 'skillful' or 'expert,' emphasizing that humans did not design their own existence.</note>
We, in turn, are His people and the sheep of His pasture.
Hasten with gentle spirits within the gates of God;
and in His courts, entreat God the King with song.
Sing of the gentle name of God always upon the earth,
He unceasingly keeps watch over all generations.
From generation to generation He carries His all-true counsel.

---

### Psalm 100. <note>In modern English Bibles, this is Psalm 101. It is a vow of kingly integrity.</note>
->David sang this hundredth song.<-

**Within** my song to you, O King, I shall sing of mercy and judgment;
playing the lyre, may I walk in blameless paths.
Waiting for when you shall come to me, O deathless Shepherd.
I walked with a kindly heart through the middle of my house,
observing nothing at all lawless with my eyes;
and moreover, I loathed sinful and insolent men.
Never did a wavering counsel stand before me; <note>original: "ἀμφιπρόσαλλος" (amphiprosallos). A rare epic epithet meaning 'fickle' or 'leaning both ways,' famously used in the Iliad to describe the god Ares.</note>
let the terrible serpent flee from me, for I remained ignorant of deceitful things.
My ear was barred from hearing one who speaks evil;
like a destroyer of friends, I drove such a one away from my companions.
With an insatiable heart and arrogant eyes,
I was unwilling to be a friend and guest at the feast.
My eyes were stretched toward holy men,
so that they might hold a common seat, sitting together with me;
I held as a servant one concerned with holy paths.

No arrogant man dwelt within my halls;
nor did one speaking injustice come before my eyes.
At dawn, I slew the spokesmen of recklessness, <note>original: "ἀτασθαλίης ὑποφήτας" (atasthalies hypophetas). A very Homeric phrase. In the Odyssey, *atasthalia* refers to the reckless folly of the suitors; here, the poet uses it to describe those who willfully ignore God's law.</note>
eager to subdue the servants of lawlessness with destruction.

### Psalm 102. <note>In the Septuagint numbering used here, this is Psalm 101. In modern English Bibles (KJV/NIV), it is Psalm 102. It is known as one of the seven "Penitential Psalms."</note>

> The prayer of a poor man weighed down by evil ruin,
> when he pours out his heavy grief before God;
> this is truly the hundred and second song.

Hearken to our prayer, O most excellent Shepherd;
and receive my voice as I make my plea.
Do not turn your graceful countenance away from me;
lean your keen ear to me, O King, in my distress.
When, O Blessed One, I call upon you, quickly incline your dear ear;
for my life has melted away like smoke;
and my bones are burned up like dry firewood. <note>original: "φρυγίῳ" (phrygio). Refers to dry sticks or kindling, emphasizing the physical sensation of being "burnt out" by fever or sorrow.</note>
I was struck down like grass, and my heart withered away;
and in my weakness, I forgot to give my stomach its dear bread;
from our groaning, my bones are pressed close to my flesh; <note>The poet describes the emaciation of the speaker, whose skin clings to his bones due to fasting and grief.</note>
you might say I look like a pelican dwelling in the desert,
or an owl of the night, an inhabitant of bare ruins,
or a sleepless bird alone upon a roof.
Enemies reviled me with words all day long,
and those who once honored me swore a vain oath against me;
because in my suffering I ate ashes like bread,
and I drank my drink mixed with tears,
because of your destructive wrath and anger, O Most High,
because having lifted me up, you cast me down again from the heights. <note>A classic expression of the "vicissitudes of fortune," suggesting that the pain of the fall is greater because of the previous height of success.</note>
The days of our life pass away like a shadow,
and I have become just like parched grass.

### Psalm 100. <note>This is Psalm 101 in modern English Bibles. It is a "Royal Psalm" where the king pledges to rule with justice and keep his court free from corruption.</note>

-> Psalm 102. <- <note>This is Psalm 102 in the Septuagint numbering, but Psalm 102 in most English Bibles. It is one of the seven "Penitential Psalms."</note>

A prayer of a poor man weighed down by evil ruin,
whenever he pours out a prayer before God;
this was indeed the hundred and first song.

Hear our prayer, O most excellent Shepherd;
receive into your ears the voice of one in need.
Do not turn your gracious face away from me;
incline a sharp ear to me in my day of distress.
Whenever I call upon you, Blessed One, quickly lean your dear ear;
for my life has melted away like smoke;
and my bones are parched like dry firewood.
I was struck like grass, and my heart withered;
I forgot to give my stomach its dear bread out of necessity;
because of my groaning, my bones cling to my flesh.
You might say I look like a pelican dwelling in the desert,
or a night-raven <note>original: "κόρακα" (koraka). While usually a crow or raven, in the context of the Septuagint and this poem, it refers to an owl or bird that haunts ruins.</note> inhabiting desolate places,
or like one who is sleepless and alone upon a house.
My enemies taunted me with words all day long,
and those who once honored me now swear against me in vain;
because in my distress I ate ashes like bread,
and mixed my drink with bitter tears,
because of your deadly wrath, O Most High, and your anger,
for you lifted me up and then cast me down again from the heights.
As a shadow, the days of our life come to an end,
and I myself have become exactly like withered grass.

->Psalm 101.<- <note>Psalm 102 in modern English Bibles.</note>

and receive the voice of one in need.

because my life has melted away like smoke,
and my bones are burned like parched firewood.
I was struck like grass, and my spirit was withered;
out of necessity, I gave my dear bread to my crouching belly <note>original: "γαστέρι δ' ὀκλαδίῃσι" (gasteri d' okladiēsi). This suggests the physical posture of one hunched over in grief or hunger</note>;

My enemies insulted me with words all day long,
and those who once honored me swore an oath against me in vain;
because in my distress, I ate ashes like bread,
and I drank my drink mixed with mournful tears,
because of your destructive wrath and anger, O Most High,
for after lifting me up, you cast me down again from the heavens.

and I myself have become altogether like parched grass.

How much of the lawlessness of mortals <note>original: "μερόπων" (meropōn). An ancient Homeric epithet for humans, often interpreted as "articulate-speaking."</note> he has allowed to pass away!

you who have cared for his immortal aid.

which always hold their far-reaching power throughout his domains.

<header>PSALM 103 <note>In the Septuagint numbering; this is Psalm 104 in most English Bibles.</note></header>

Covering the glorious upper chambers with the highest waters,
making the clouds a support for his immortal footsteps,
walking far upon the wings of the passing winds,
making the straight-running spirits his messenger-army,
and transforming them into the rushing impulses of a life-giving, blazing fire.
You fixed an all-bronze <note>original: "παγχαλκέον" (panchalkeon). An epic Homeric epithet applied here to describe the indestructible foundation of the earth.</note> foundation for the earth,
so that it might always remain unshaken throughout its life.
You placed the abyss around it like a divine robe;
great waters stood even above the high-crested mountains.
But they turned to flight at the King’s command,
and they shall dread the sound of your loud-thundering voice.
They go up the mountains, and the low earth appears,
to the place where you fixed the foundations for those regions.
You fixed a boundary, O Blessed One, which the rain shall not pass,
nor shall they ever return to cover the beloved earth.
You pour out springs among the clefts of the rugged earth,
waters shall flow through the middle of the high-crested mountains.
Drink appears for the mountain-dwelling beasts through the thickets;
the thirsty nation of every kind of wild donkey <note>original: "ὄναγρων" (onagrōn). Wild asses, known for their independence in the wilderness.</note> seeks you alone.
The winged birds of the air stand near them;
in the midst of the strong rocks, they shall give their cry.
Fattening the mountains from your upper chambers,
the life-bearing earth shall be filled with your fruits,
causing honey-sweet grass to spring up for the four-footed beasts,
and green herb for the service of articulate-speaking <note>original: "μεροπήϊδα" (meropēida). A classical Greek epithet for humans, referring to the power of speech.</note> men,
so that he may bring forth grain, the fruit of glorious counsel, from the ground,
and wine, a much-rejoicing gift for the hearts of mortals;
making the eyes shine with the white-skinned blood of the olive <note>original: "αἵματ᾽ ἐλαίης" (haimat' elaies). A poetic metaphor for olive oil.</note>,
and strengthening the ruddy heart of man with bread.
The trees of the fruit-bearing plain shall be filled with plenty,

> and in the steep rocks, the hares <note>original: "λαγωοί" (lagooi). The Greek Septuagint and this poem use "hares" to represent the Hebrew 'shaphan' or rock hyrax.</note> are full of courage.
> You yourself, O King, fashioned the moon to mark the seasons.
> The radiant sun has observed its own setting.
> Unsmiling darkness became the sign of night,
> and the night is the tender of beasts; for the wild creatures love the night,
> even the young lions, eager for the necessary hunt,
> always seeking their feast from God.
> When the dawn gathers over the shining earth,
> they seek their beds within their own thickets.
> But man goes back again to his own work,
> waiting for the end of his labor at the evening hour.
> How your much-renowned works are magnified, O Blessed One!
> You fashioned them all together with divine wisdom, O Shepherd.
> The holy earth is weighted down with your unmeasurable works,
> and so is the hollow of the boundless and wide-pathed sea.
> Ah, how many creeping things of the roaring sea are raised for you—
> living things you might behold, the greater mixed with the small.
> There the path was made for many-oared ships.
> There is the dragon <note>original: "δράκων" (drakōn). This refers to Leviathan, described here as a "plaything" or "toy" of God, emphasizing the Creator's absolute power over even the most terrifying sea monsters.</note> whom you fashioned long ago to be your plaything.
> All things look to you to give the proper food to each;
> and may every creature receive a good feast from you.
> All things greatly rejoice when you spread out your palms,
> but a dread trembling seizes them when you turn your face away.
> When you take away their spirit, they die, overcome,
> and they perish straightway, returning to Mother Earth.
> But if you grant their spirit to return again...

<summary>This page contains a poetic Greek paraphrase of Psalm 104:16–30, describing how God provides for all creatures—from mountain goats and lions to the sea-monster Leviathan—and governs the cycles of day and night.</summary>
<keywords>Apollinaris of Laodicea, Psalm 104, creation, nature poetry, Leviathan, divine providence, Greek hexameter</keywords>

# PSALM 104 <note>numbered as 103 in the Greek Septuagint</note> 137

...once again with His bounties, and the whole earth is illuminated.
The glory of the King shall be forever, it shall be entirely unaging;
He rejoices with a noble heart in the splendor of His own works.
The earth trembles under the flashing of Your gaze;
when You touch them, the mountains roar with smoke. <note>original: "καπνῶ" (kapno). This refers to the volcanic or smoky appearance of Sinai or mountains in the presence of God.</note>
As for me, I shall sing hymns to the King continually.
I would chant to the Immortal, much-entreated one, as long as I walk the earth.
Delighting in the sweet cares of my song, may You receive my melody;
I shall dance with unending joys in the presence of the King.
May the ministers of wickedness cease entirely;
and may the whole lawless race perish from the earth.
O my soul, sing praise to the King continually.

---

->Psalm 105.<- <note>numbered as 104 in the Greek Septuagint</note>

->The Hallelujah of the fourth book, of the voices that bring aid;<- <note>The "fourth book" refers to the fourth division of the Hebrew Psalter (Psalms 90–106).</note>
->this is the one hundred and fifth psalm.<-

Sing, as you entreat the unaging name of the King;
declare the works of God to all kinds of peoples;
sing, and play the lyre clearly for the King with psalms,
and with wise understanding, speak of His divine deeds.
Let our praise be fixed upon the immortal King.
Let the heart of those who always seek the King be gladdened;
seek the King, from whom your strength was fashioned.
And seek His divine countenance at all times.
Remember the wonders which He wrought with His hands,
and the followers <note>original: "ὀπηδούς" (opedous). A Homeric term for attendants or companions, used here to describe the judgements or "words" that follow from God's mouth.</note> of the righteous decrees of His tongue.
His servants are the beloved race of Abraham;
He found the children of Jacob to be His chosen people.
The King Himself reveals His justice among all people.

<meta>This page continues the poetic paraphrase of Psalm 104 (Psalm 105 in the English tradition). The previous page ended with an exhortation to seek the Lord. This page recounts the history of God's covenant with the Patriarchs (Abraham, Isaac, and Jacob) and the providential journey of Joseph into Egypt. The author, Apollinaris, uses the language of Homeric epic—such as the "day of slavery" and the "divine nod"—to translate Hebrew concepts into a classical Greek framework.</meta>

### APOLLINARIS <note>original: "ΑΠΟΛΙΝΑΡ." This identifies the author as Apollinaris of Laodicea, a 4th-century bishop who rewrote the Psalms in the style of Homeric epics like the Iliad.</note>

He unceasingly remembered His own covenants;
the word which He commanded to thousands of generations,
the decree He eagerly confirmed with a nod <note>original: "κατένευσεν" (kateneusen). In Homeric epic, the "nod" of a deity represents an irrevocable and divine decree.</note> to Abraham long ago,
swearing a great oath to Isaac, His dear servant,
which He previously ordained for the wise Jacob to fulfill;
and He promised a holy covenant to Israel,
pledging to give him the whole land of Canaan,
measuring out the portions of your inheritance with a line. <note>This refers to the ancient practice of using a "measuring line" or rope to survey and divide land among tribes.</note>
In that place, their number was very small, and they themselves were few,
dwelling as migrants in a foreign land;
often they traveled from one nation to another;
from one kingdom they passed to a different people.
He allowed no one to raise a harmful hand against them;
rather, in His wrath, He subdued kings to rebuke them, saying:
"Keep away from My holy ones; do not harm My prophets." <note>original: "ὑποφήτας" (hypophetas). This is a poetic rendering of the biblical verse "Touch not my anointed, and do my prophets no harm" (Psalm 105:15).</note>
He called a bitter, ruinous famine upon the soil;
He broke the life-giving strength of the fertile grain.
But He sent ahead for them a good and prudent guardian:
Joseph, who entered into the day of slavery. <note>original: "δούλιον ἦμαρ" (doulion emar). This is a famous Homeric idiom used to describe the moment or state of being enslaved.</note>
Ah, how he suffered then, bound in inescapable shackles,
and how his heart was pressed down, subdued by iron;
until the noble fulfillment of his dreams arrived.
The Most Blessed One Himself tested His servant with His word.
The Lord of the Nile <note>Refers to the Pharaoh of Egypt.</note> commanded and released him from his bonds;
the ruler of nations set him free from his distress,
entrusting his house to be guided by Joseph’s command,
and making him the renowned leader of the kingdom,
so that he might teach his laws to the champions of the Nile,
and make their elders wise.
Then, Israel came into the land of Egypt;

-> ### PSALM 104 <-
<note>In many English Bibles, this is labeled as Psalm 105. The numbering difference stems from the Greek Septuagint tradition.</note>

Jacob dwelled in the glorious land of Ham; <note>original: "Χαμοῦ" (Chamou). In biblical poetry, Ham is the ancestor of the Egyptians, and Egypt is often called the "Land of Ham."</note>
but the King <note>Referring to God.</note> increased His people to be marvelous,
and made them much stronger than their enemies.
Then they <note>the Egyptians</note> turned to harm the people pleasing to God,
exchanging their customs for divine-stirred strife.
To them God sent His wise servant Moses,
and the desired Aaron, whom He had chosen.
He entrusted to them the shining signs of His counsel,
and the power of wonders to be accomplished in the land of Ham.
He sent forth darkness over the pitiless land;
but they resisted the bitter words of God.
He turned their water into dark red blood,
and killed the fish of the black-dyed river.
The earth brought forth a swarm of water-bred frogs;
they even swarmed into the chambers of the contentious kings.
He spoke, and the dog-fly entered the palace of the ruler,
and gnats <term>original: "σκνίφες" (skniphes). Small stinging insects, often translated as lice or mosquitoes.</term> destroyed the famous houses of the whole land.
Cold hail rattled down from the rain-bringing clouds,
and a flame <note>Referring to lightning.</note> entered the earth to accompany the hail.
They destroyed the vines, and the fig trees perished,
and He broke the flowering trees of the land.
He spoke, and the sand-swarming, thick locust moved in,
and the much-feared caterpillar <term>original: "βροῦχος" (bruchos). A wingless locust or a destructive larva.</term> in unmeasurable numbers;
they stripped the whole flowery grass of the earth.
Oh, how each one lamented the fate of the firstborn,
the early youth of the departing generation of children!
He led out His people heavy with gold and silver,
and they had no weak comrade among their tribes.
Oh, how the pitiless land rejoiced as they departed;
for all were struck with incurable fear in their souls.

<summary>This page recounts the biblical plagues of Egypt through the lens of Homeric epic poetry, detailing the transformation of nature into a weapon of divine judgment. It concludes with the Israelites departing Egypt enriched with gold and silver while the Egyptians remain paralyzed by fear.</summary>

# APOLLINARIS

He spread a shading cloud to lead the people,
and with fire He scattered the pervasive darkness of the whole night.
At their request came a countless flight of quails; <term>original: "ὀρτυγομήτρα" (ortygomētra). Literally 'quail-mother,' referring to the birds provided to the Israelites in the wilderness (Exodus 16).</term>
and He filled their souls with heavenly bread. <note>Referring to the Manna.</note>
He split the rock, and a stream of rushing water led forth,
and rivers flowed with force through the waterless plains.
For He remembered in His heart the lovely promise,
which the Lord had long ago granted to His servant Abraham.
Therefore He brought out His honored people in gladness,
gathering His servants together with increasing joy.
And He gave to them the lands of many-pathed generations,
and they shared in the labors of other peoples,
so that they might always remember the covenant of righteousness,
and uphold the law of the heavenly King.

### Psalm 105
<note>In English Bibles, this is Psalm 106. It is a psalm of confession, recounting Israel's history of rebellion and God's mercy.</note>

-> This is the fifth [section] of soothing, helpful chants. <-

<image-desc>A small decorative floral flourish or vine is printed in the left margin next to the initial letter of the following verse.</image-desc>
O peoples, sing of the kindness of the King of All,
for He unceasingly increases His enduring bounty.
Who shall sing of my King’s unshakable strength,
or bring His praise to the ears of all kinds of mortal men?
Blessed are the men who remember justice,
for whom righteousness was fashioned for all time.
Remember Your suppliants, Lord, with the justice shown to Your people,
and grant to us Your famous, watchful light,
that we may look upon Your divine songs,
and our hearts shall rejoice in the gladness of Your nation,
holding a shared praise for the immortal inheritance.
We have been sinners just like our fathers,
acting lawlessly with most unholy deeds.

# PSALM 106

They were so ignorant that they forgot Your wonders,
nor did they consider in their minds the vastness of Your mercy.
Breathing bitter thoughts in their hearts, they stood beside <term>kt’</term> <note>original: "κτ’", a poetic shortening of "kata," meaning along or by.</note> the Red Sea;
yet He saved them nonetheless through the help of their King,
so that mortal men might know the great strength of the Immortal One.
With a command, the Lord dried up the Red Sea,
and He led them through the depths as if through a sandy land.
He saved them from the hands of those who sought their ruin,
and He delivered them from their enemies, granting them a day of freedom.
The deep-flowing surge of the sea covered their oppressors with its heights,
and not a single inhabitant of those hostile lands returned to bring them word.
Then they believed for a time in the life-sustaining word of God,
and they sang a hymn of praise to Him with their songs.
But they quickly forgot the King’s words in their haste,
and they were false to the counsel of the immortal God.
They felt a wicked desire in the desert place <term>kt’</term> <note>Again, the poet uses the shortened "kata" to maintain the epic meter of the verse.</note>,
and they put the King to the test in the waterless ground.
But even so, He granted the desire of their souls,
filling their hearts with the abundance they themselves had craved.
Then, in their resentment, they spoke words of rebuke against Moses,
and they continually strove against Aaron, the holy one.
The shameless earth gaped open and swallowed Dathan,
and it covered the entire camp of the wicked Abiram.
A burning flame consumed their tents,
a fire that burned the people who were steeped in sin.
And then, at Horeb, they fashioned a horned calf,
and they fell down in vain before a mere idol.
Fools! They traded their own glory
for a calf that feeds on honey-sweet grass.
They forgot the strength of the King who stands firm in battle,

<meta>This page continues the poetic paraphrase of Psalm 106 (LXX 105) by Apollinaris of Laodicea. The previous page detailed the miraculous crossing of the Red Sea and the subsequent rebellions in the wilderness, specifically the earth swallowing Dathan and Abiram. # APOLLINARIS

He who performed great and glorious deeds in Egypt of old—
deeds that a mortal man would boast of—was renowned throughout the land of heavy-hearted <term>Ham</term> <note>original: "Χαμοῦ"; Ham was the son of Noah, traditionally considered the ancestor of the Egyptians.</note>,
bringing terrors upon all mortals by the Red Sea.
And indeed, the Lord would have destroyed them all, for He spoke of their total ruin,
had not His servant Moses, tireless in his toil, stood before Him,
pleading with outstretched hands to turn Him away from their destruction.
They held the glorious land in contempt, behaving wickedly;
they never trusted in the righteous command of the Blessed One.
Instead, they constantly muttered within their tents,
refusing to listen to the voice of God as He spoke to them.
Therefore, the King raised His right hand to strike them down,
intending to cast them all across the desert wastes,
to destroy His people through foreign tribes,
or to scatter them among many distant cities.
Yet they gave themselves over to the rites of the frenzied <term>Baal-Peor</term> <note>original: "Βεελφεγόροιο", a Moabite deity whose worship involved ritual immorality and eating food sacrificed to the dead.</note>,
eating sacrifices offered to the dead from a hundred-fold offering,
boiling with unholy desire for the master of love-madness.
For them, this insolent behavior brought forth a lethal and divine wrath.
Then <term>Phinehas</term> <note>A priest who intervened to stop the plague by executing those who were unfaithful.</note> appeased the King of All with his prayers;
he interceded, and through his pleas, the plague withdrew.
For this, he received a glorious reward of justice from God,
a blessing to remain through every generation for all time.
You know, too, of the waters of <term>strife</term> <note>original: "ἀμφήρειστον ὕδωρ", referring to the incident at Meribah where the people complained about lack of water.</note>, and how they provoked Him;
Moses was brought to suffering by the sins of the people,
his heart constantly worn down by bitter sorrows,
so that he was forced to speak rashly and waver in his faith.
For they spared the nations that the Lord had commanded them to destroy.
Mingling with those peoples, they learned their lawless ways
and bowed their necks before lifeless idols.
Yet they were not immediately destroyed, but they fell under the weight of a cruel necessity.

It highlights how the people's disobedience led to both divine wrath and the eventual corruption of their faith through foreign idols.</summary>

# PSALM 106

->### <-

They sacrificed their own daughters and sons to demons. 
The suffering earth groaned, weighed down by innocent blood—
the blood of those they bore, for whom they became murderous destroyers, 
offering them to the idols of arrogant <term>Canaan</term> <note>original: "Χαναάνου"; the land promised to the Israelites, whose inhabitants practiced child sacrifice to deities like Moloch.</note>. 
The earth was reddened on every side with the blood of the slain, 
wantonly defiled by these hateful deeds. 
They were consumed by a destructive lust, for such were the things they cared for. 
But the King was utterly enraged at His evil-doing people, 
hating His own <term>inheritance</term> <note>original: "κλῆρον" (klēron), meaning a lot or portion assigned by God.</note> because of their arrogant hubris. 
He delivered His servants into the hands of their enemies, 
giving them over to be ruled by those who hated them. 
They were subdued by the harsh necessities of those who live for dread, 
falling as weaklings beneath the hands of both their oppressors. 
Many times He rescued them; yet they piteously chose destruction; 
with hateful minds, they grieved the King even more than before, 
their necks bowed down by their own lawlessness. 
When He saw them suffering, He spared His own people; 
He heard them begging and listened to their prayers. 
For He immediately remembered His <term>covenants</term> <note>original: "συνθεσιάων" (synthesiaon); formal agreements or treaties between God and His people.</note>; 
in the mercy of His kindness, He turned away from His former plan of ruin. 
A wonder takes me, how the King softened His heart, 
and how those who were previously their plunderers were moved to pity them. 
Rescue us, O most high King, with Your finest help, 
gathering us from every side, wherever we have been scattered among the nations; 
so that we may piously honor Your noble and divine name, 
taking great pride in singing Your most glorious songs. 
Blessed forever is the King, the renowned God of Israel, 
from generation to generation, continually for all days. 
And all the people shall say: <gloss>"Amen"</gloss> <note>original: "Γένοιτο" (Genoito); literally 'let it be'.</note>, let it truly be so. <margin>A vertical green floral ornament is positioned in the right margin, featuring stylized curving vines, leaves, and a central circular motif.</margin>

<meta>This page marks a transition in the manuscript. The previous page concluded the poetic retelling of Psalm 106 (LXX 105), which focused on Israel's failures in the wilderness. # APOLLINARIUS

->### Psalm 106 ###<-
<note>In the Septuagint numbering followed here, this is Psalm 106; in modern Bibles, it is Psalm 107.</note>

This is the sixth book of dawn-light, help-bringing verses.
<note>original: "Εκτος ὅδ' ἀμφιλύκεια βοηθίων στραπάων"; Apollinaris often begins new sections of his Psalter with a metrical header. "Sixth" refers to the division of the 150 Psalms into smaller books.</note>

O peoples, sing of the kindness of the Universal King,
because He increases His mercy for His own forever.
Let those speak to whom God granted the day of freedom,
whom He once released from the powerful hand of their enemies.
He gathered them here from far-flung cities—
some from the East, some from the land of the West,
and others from the North and the <term>barren sea</term> <note>original: "ἀτρυγέτοιο θαλάσσης" (atrygetoio thalasses); a classic Homeric epithet for the sea, meaning "unharvested" or "yielding no grain."</note>.
They were wandering through the vast, parched region of the desert,
nor did they have a path to find their own city.
They were wasting away with thirst and pitiless hunger together,
and their soul within them was melting away from weakness.
But they cried out to God, their hearts stirred by their troubles,
and the King rescued them from all their distress.
He revealed a straight path, leading them on,
so that they might reach the beloved city for which they yearned.
Let the King’s mercy be sung with vigor,
and His wondrous deeds among the <term>race of mortal men</term> <note>original: "μεροπήϊδι φύλῃ" (meropeidi phyle); an epic phrase referring to "articulate-speaking" humans.</note>,
because He provided a ready feast for the needy soul,
and sustained the hungry heart with good things—
for those sitting in the shadowy gloom of death,
trapped in poverty and iron chains.
They had grieved God with a hateful mind, ignoring His commands,
mockingly provoking His restraining counsel.
Therefore they were subdued by unbearable toil and ruin,
and no helper came to them in their weakness.
But they cried out to God, their hearts stirred by their troubles,
and the King rescued them from all their distress.

# APOLLINARIUS

->### PSALM 106 ###<-

He brought them out of the gloom and from the pits of death,
breaking the strong and endless bond of the bars.
Let the life of the King’s mercy be sung aloud,
and let His divine works be a concern to the <term>race of mortal men</term> <note>original: "μεροπήϊδι φύτλῃ" (meropeidi phytle); a traditional epic expression for humanity, literally meaning "articulate-speaking" or "mortal" lineage.</note>.
Because He shattered the strength of the bronze gates,
and suddenly broke the <term>iron bolts</term> <note>original: "ἀδάμαντος... ὀχῆας" (adamantos ocheas); literally "bolts of adamant" or "hardest metal," commonly translated as iron in biblical contexts.</note>,
snatching all away from the path of wretched evil.
Because of their lawlessness, they found blame for their transgressions,
spitting out from their hearts all life-sustaining food,
and they descended even to the gates of cold-passing death.
Again they cried out to God, their hearts stirred by their labors,
and the King rescued them from all their distress.
He healed those who were suffering, soothing their spirits,
and released those perishing from soul-devouring pain.
Let the life of the King’s mercy be sung aloud,
and let His divine works be a concern to the race of mortal men.
Let them offer to the King a sacrifice that dwells in songs;
let them tell of His works, rejoicing with a good mind.
Sailors who attend to the force and the ships of the sea,
for whom labor arises through the waves and the immense water,
they have seen with their eyes the works of the wise God,
and all His wonders among the seafaring abysses.
At the King’s command, an unspeakable squall arose,
and the swelling, high waves of the sea rushed forth;
you would say the wide heaven was suddenly mixing with the abyss.
Their soul was melting with loud-crashing pain;
a confusion seized them all, like one <term>fettered by wine</term> <note>original: "πεπεδημένον οἴνῳ" (pepedemenon oino); a poetic way of describing a drunkard's staggering.</note>;
they forgot their seamanship, and their skilled craft was nowhere to be seen.
Again they cried out to God, their hearts stirred by their labors,
and the King rescued them from all their distress.

# APOLLINARIUS

### Psalm 107

->And the sixth is the harshness of the helping hosts.<-

O people, sing of the kindness of the All-King,
because He has unceasingly raised up His own greatness;
let those speak to whom God has granted a day of freedom,
whom He once released from the strong hand of enemies.
He gathered them here from many-winged cities,
some from the rising sun, some from the evening land,
and others from the North and the <term>unharvested sea</term> <note>original: "ἀτρυγέτοιο θαλάσσης" (atrygetoio thalasses); a classic Homeric epithet for the sea, meaning barren or impossible to harvest like a field.</note>.
Wandering through the boundless, dry space of the desert,
they could not find the path to their own city.
They wasted away with thirst and pitiless hunger,
and their spirit melted away within them from weakness.
Again they cried out to God, their hearts stirred by their labors,
and the King led them out of every necessity;
He showed them a straight path to tread, leading them
so that they might reach the beloved city they desired.
Sing of the mercies of the King and His rush of power,
and His divine works among the <term>race of articulate-speaking men</term> <note>original: "μεροπήϊδι φύλῃ" (meropeidi phyle); an ancient poetic term for humanity, distinguishing humans from animals by their ability to speak.</note>,
because He provided a ready feast for the lacking soul,
and supported the hungry spirit with good things.
Those sitting in the shadowy darkness of death,
constrained by poverty and iron bonds—
because with a hateful mind they grieved the heart of God by their commands,
foolishly provoking His restraining counsel;
therefore they were subdued by unbearable toil and ruin,
and no helper came to them in their weakness.
Again they cried out to God, their hearts stirred by their labors,
and the King led them out of every necessity.

# APOLLINARIUS

### Psalm 107

He led them out of the gloom and the pits of death, 
breaking the strong and endless chains that bound their limbs.
Let the mercies of the King exalt life,
and let His wondrous works be known to the <term>articulate race of men</term> <note>original: "μεροπήϊδι φύτλῃ" (meropeidi phytle); a classical Homeric epithet for humanity, referring to our ability to divide sounds into distinct, rational speech.</note>;
for He shattered the strength of the bronze gates,
and swiftly broke the well-wrought bars of <term>iron</term> <note>original: "ἀδάμαντος" (adamantos); though often translated as 'adamant' or 'diamond' in mythology, in this biblical context it refers to the hardest of metals used for prison bars.</note>.

He snatched them all from the path of wretched misery; 
because of the stain of their impiety, 
they loathed all life-sustaining food in their hearts, 
drawing near to the very gates of cold-traversing death.
Again they cried out to God, their minds distressed by their labors, 
and the King led them out of every distress.
He healed them in their trial, soothing their spirits, 
and released those wasting away from soul-devouring sorrow.
Let the mercies of the King exalt life,
and let His wondrous works be known to the articulate race of men;
let them offer to the King a sacrifice of song, 
and let them proclaim His works with a joyful heart.

Sailors who tend to the power of the sea and its ships, 
whose labor rises through the waves and the immense waters—
these have seen with their own eyes the works of the all-wise God, 
and all His wonders in the deep sea-traversing abysses.
At the King’s command, an unspeakable storm-blast arose, 
driving the high-arched waves of the sea; 
one would think the wide heavens were suddenly mingled with the abyss. 
Their courage melted in loud-crashing sorrow; 
they were seized by a trembling, like a man overcome by wine; 
they forgot their seamanship, and all their clever skill vanished. 
Again they cried out to God, their minds distressed by their labors, 
and the King led them out of every distress.

Κ

# APOLLINARIUS

### Psalm 107 (Continued)

Straightway He changed the storm into a gentle breeze;
and once again the waves were soothed into a steady silence.
Gladness took hold of them all when they saw the calm water,
as they hastened toward the harbor under the guidance of God.
Let the mercies of the King exalt life,
and let His divine works be a care to the <term>articulate race of men</term> <note>original: "μεροπήϊδι φύτλῃ" (meropeidi phytle); a Homeric epithet emphasizing human speech and reason.</note>.
Let the hymns of the ever-fitting assemblies of the people be raised up,
and sing His praise in the sacred seats of the elders.

He sent forth a great stream from a fair-flowing river;
but soon the watery path was parched by joyless thirst.
He turned the once-nourishing land into a <term>salty waste</term> <note>original: "ἀλμὴν" (almen); referring to a barren, salt-encrusted desert.</note>,
withered by the wickedness of those who dwelt nearby.
But then He turned the wilderness into a pool of many waves,
and waters flowed like rivers into the parched earth.
There He settled the hungry who once suffered many griefs;
and those noble people established cities to dwell in.
They planted vineyards and sowed the fields,
rejoicing in the lovely fruitfulness of their lives.
God praised their work, and the people flourished,
and herds of four-footed beasts were nourished in their pastures.

But then, becoming feeble, they were brought low by distress,
<margin>D</margin> worn down by sorrows and unutterable pains.
A terrible contempt was poured out upon the rulers,
<insert>and having left the path, they wandered through untrodden wastes;</insert> <note>This line, underlined in green in the original manuscript, mirrors the Psalm's description of leaders lost in the wilderness as a sign of divine judgment.</note>
yet He came as a helper to the poor in their helplessness,
establishing their families like the beautiful flocks of a shepherd.
The upright in mind, seeing these things, shall rejoice in gladness;
but wickedness shall be afraid to open its mouth ever again.
Whoever is wise will know to guard all these things in his heart,
and to perceive the mercy of the Immortal One within his breast.

# Psalm 107.
## The one hundred and seventh hymn of David.

My heart is ready, O All-Watcher, my heart is ready;
In Thy glory I will sing to Thee and chant with psalms.
Awake for me, O <term>lyre</term> <note>original: "φόρμιγξ" (phorminx); a stringed instrument often associated with Homeric epic poetry.</note>, and my psalter of hymns;
Promptly at dawn I will sing as I awaken.
I will proclaim Thy power, O King, which is cried aloud among the peoples,
And I will sing a clear-sounding praise among the many nations.
Thou hast Thy greatness high above the heavenly gates,
And Thy truth flies even unto the clouds.
Arise above the heavenly gates, O most high Shepherd,
And let Thy boundless glory reach over all the earth,
So that Thou might snatch Thy beloved ones from the grip of distress.
Save me with Thy right hand, and hear my voice.
The King spoke a wise word to His holy one:
"Having been exalted, I shall now divide Shechem,
And I will measure out the hollow valley of <term>tents</term> <note>original: "κλισιάων" (klisiaon); referring to the Valley of Succoth.</note>.
Gilead is mine, and Manasseh is mine;
Ephraim was made the strength of my head.
Moreover, Judah is my glorious king,
And the land of Moab is my blameless washpot.
I will stretch out my shoe over Edom;
The hostile tribes are subdued under our feet."
Who will lead me to the famous tower of the city?
Or who will be my guide among the people of Edom?
Will You not, O King, who formerly cast us off?
Will You not go forth as our helper when we are armed for battle?
May You appear openly, O King, as an ally to us in our weariness;
For the light of <term>mortal men</term> <note>original: "μερόπων" (meropon); a classicizing term for humans, literally 'those with divided speech'.</note> is but a vain shadow for us.

<header>A P O L L I N A R.</header>

May we fulfill our life in the mighty King <note>Referring to God as the sovereign ruler.</note> with readiness, 
and may He ward off the terrible insolence of our enemies.

### Psalm 108.

->David’s clear-sounding song, the hundred and eighth.<-

O Blessed One, do not pass over my praise in total silence; 
for the mouth of a terrible sinner gapes deceitfully against me; 
having sharpened their tongues, they spoke evil of me with bitterness, 
and trusting in hateful words they gathered together, 
and idly they armed themselves for a deadly battle. 
Those whom I sought to love wove evils against me instead. 
<margin>108</margin> But I continued to pray, stretching my eye toward heaven. 
Instead of my good deeds, they gave me destructive sorrows, 
and in place of our friendship they chose a wicked hatred. 
Appoint a wicked man as a leader over him; 
and let an <term>accuser</term> <note>original: "διάβολος" (diabolos); in the Greek of the Septuagint, this term often refers to a legal adversary or slanderer before it became a proper name for the Devil.</note> stand firm at his right hand. 
When he is judged, may he be overcome by the votes of justice; 
and let his reckless prayer quickly become a sin. 
Let his days be very few and full of grief, 
and let an enemy seize his illustrious <term>office</term> <note>original: "ἐπωσύνην" (eposynen); a poetic form of "episkope," meaning a position of oversight or leadership. This verse is famously cited in the New Testament regarding the replacement of Judas Iscariot.</note>. 
May his sons be left as fatherless orphans, 
and may a cruel widowhood seize and bind his wife. 
Let his children be beggars, wanderers among foreign people; 
let them be driven from their own dear soil by harsh necessity. 
Let a creditor track down all that he possesses, 
and let strangers be plunderers of his hard work. 
Let no helper come to him from the enemy’s side, 
nor let anyone take pity on his orphaned offspring; 
let his dear children be the plaything of a wretched destruction; 
may he utterly lose his name, obscure and unheard of, in a single day.

<summary>This page concludes Psalm 107 and begins Psalm 108 (LXX numbering), a dramatic imprecatory poem where the speaker calls for severe divine judgment and social ruin upon a deceitful adversary.</summary>
<keywords>Psalm 108, David, imprecation, judgment, accuser, orphans, Apollinaris of Laodicea, justice</keywords>

--- Page 202 ---
<meta>This page continues the poetic paraphrase of Psalm 108 (Septuagint numbering; Masoretic 109). The previous page ended with the curses directed at the enemy's children, describing them as wandering beggars.</meta>

<header>P S A L M  108</header>

May the wickedness of his fathers be remembered before God, 
and may the sin of his hateful mother never be blotted out. 
May those outcasts remain always before the face of the King <note>Referring to God as the sovereign Judge.</note>, 
and let the memory of them perish from the good earth. 
For he did not remember to perform acts worthy of <term>kindness</term> <note>original: "ξυνητύος" (ksynetyos); a poetic term for fellowship, social bonds, or shared humanity.</note>. 
Instead, that reckless man pursued the needy and the poor, 
longing to slaughter the man already burdened by heavy sorrow. 
He loved the curse too much; let it come quickly upon him! 
He loathed blessing; let it flee far away from him. 
He clothed himself in the curse he chose as if it were a robe; 
it entered into his inner parts like water to a thirsty man, 
and soaked into his bones like fattening oil. 
May the curse be to him like the necessary garment upon his skin, 
and like the belt he wears, let him be girded by it forever throughout his life. 
This is the reward for those who make it their business to speak evil of me to the King, 
and for those who labor to create infinite sorrows for my soul. 

But you, O All-King, provide a remedy for me for your own sake, 
because you increase your <term>kindness</term> <note>original: "ξυνητὺν" (ksynetyon); the poet repeats this word to contrast the enemy’s lack of fellowship with God's abundance of it.</note> with gentle deeds. 
I am needy and poor; do not let me go! 
My heart is troubled within by my own anxious thoughts. 
Like a fading shadow, I wandered, my mind stirred with agitation; 
I was shaken off like locusts tossed in the wind. 
My dear limbs were subdued, trembling under the weight of weak-making hunger, 
and my body wasted away for lack of shining oil. 
Then I became a reproach, an object of blame among all; 
as they saw me, each one shook their deceitful head in mockery. 
But come quickly as a helper to the one calling upon you, O Blessed One, 
and grant me your kindness as an aid, O Lord. 
So that they may recognize the good help of your hand, 
which you, O Lord, intended to perform for your servant.

<header>A P O L L I N A R I U S</header>

Let those men curse, but may you grant me blessings.
May shame come upon my enemies as they approach,
but let your servant, who sings your praise, rejoice in his heart in every way.
Let those who are always speaking ill of me be clothed in wretched blame,
wrapped in a chill <note>original: "ψῦχος" (psychos). While the standard Biblical text uses "shame," the poet uses the word for "chill" or "cold" to describe the shivering, physical sensation of public disgrace.</note> like a double-layered cloak.
I will sing to the All-King <note>A common title for God in this paraphrase, emphasizing sovereignty.</note> with songs from my mouth,
and in the midst of many people, I will celebrate His glory in song.
For He came as a helper at the right hand of the poor man,
so that He might deliver my soul from the hands of hostile men.

->Psalm 109<-
<-The ninth song of David’s clear-toned melody following the hundredth.->

<margin>The Lord Said</margin>
"Sit at my right hand upon the throne," God said to my God <note>This opening reflects the traditional Christian interpretation of this Psalm as a conversation between God the Father and God the Son.</note>,
"until I subdue your enemies beneath the footstool of your feet."
May the Immortal One send forth your blameless scepter from Zion,
so that you, O King, may rule in the very midst of your enemies.
Power is yours in the day of your beginning, even as you possessed it before;
on the day of splendor among the holy ones, you shall hold firm glory.
You were brought forth as the fruit of my own womb before the morning star.
The faithful King has sworn an oath, and He will not be false to His word:
"You shall be a priest of the same kind as <term>Melchizedek</term> <note>Melchizedek was a mysterious figure from the Book of Genesis who was both a King and a Priest; here, he serves as a model for the Messiah's eternal priesthood.</note>."
O Pure One, you have a shield-bearer near your right hand,
so that in His wrath He might subdue the kings of the earth.
The Blessed One shall judge the nations; He shall fill up the ruins of the enemy,
and throughout the wide earth, He shall crush the heads of many.
He shall drink water from the mountain-stream as He travels the famous path;
therefore, being exalted on high, the King shall lift up His head.

### PSALM 110 <note>This follows the Septuagint numbering; it is Psalm 111 in modern Bibles.</note>

With an unceasing heart, I will sing your praise to the King,
in the assembly of the upright and in the radiant gatherings of the holy.
The works of the immortal King are always extraordinary,
cared for by His divine and manifold counsels.
The works of God are forever a cause for praise and magnificent glory;
His righteousness remains from generation to generation.
The King has established a memorial of His wonders in His heart.
God is compassionate and merciful toward all people.
He gave a glorious feast to those who serve Him as friends.
He will unceasingly remember His sacred covenants <note>original: "ἁρμονιάων" (harmoniaon). While the biblical text refers to a "covenant," the poet uses a word meaning "harmonies" or "unions," suggesting the divine order established between God and man.</note>.
He declared the goodness of His holy works to the people,
desiring to give them the ever-enviable inheritance of the nations,
where He always executes justice and truth.
The commandments of the King hold a glorious faithfulness;
they are firmly established forever from generation to generation,
built upon solid truth and straight laws.
He bestowed a true redemption upon all His people;
the Shepherd has commanded His covenants to last forever.

<image-desc>A hand-drawn green horizontal flourish consisting of a central stem with symmetrical leafy scrolls and vine-like patterns extending to the left and right.</image-desc>

God’s famous name was made pure and fearful.
<margin>The fear of God is the beginning of wisdom—the first principle.</margin>
The fear of God is the beginning of heavenly wisdom,
and there is a right understanding for all mortals who serve as ministers of His wisdom.
The undying praise of God remains from generation to generation.

-> Psalm 111 <- <note>Psalm 112 in modern Bibles.</note>

Blessed is the man who trembles before the King in his heart,
caring deeply for the powerful <note>original: "κεραῇσι" (keraēsi). Literally "horned," a Homeric epithet used here to denote strength or sharp authority.</note> commandments of the Immortal One.
The blood of his offspring shall remain mighty upon the earth,
blooming with wealth along with the generations of the upright.
Abundance and glory shall never fail his house,

# APOLLINARIS

But there is need in unceasing righteousness.
From the darkness a great light dawned upon those of upright mind.
God is compassionate, holy, and merciful.
Gentle is the man who, rejoicing, lends to others,
guiding the assembly of his kindly words with care.
Never shall the turmoil of a painful life overtake him.
Unceasingly his holiness shall be a praiseworthy memorial.
He shall never fear an unlawful rumor reaching his ears.
He always keeps his heart ready in hope upon the King.
Straightforwardly he has fixed his unshaken intent,
until he looks upon the fate of his enemies with his own eyes.
He has scattered his wealth, showing grace to the poor from his store.
His righteousness remains from generation to generation.
He shall have his horn <note>In biblical and Homeric Greek, the "horn" (keras) symbolizes power, dignity, and victory.</note> raised up in honor and strength.
Looking upon these things, the wicked man shall be vexed,
groaning in wretched wasting-away with envious teeth;
the desire of the wicked man shall perish unfulfilled.

The King of all sorts of people dwells in the highest,

# PSALM 113

...surveying the earth and the heavens with His eyes,
He has learned how to raise the destitute alone from the ground,
and from the dung-heap He lifts up the poor man in turn;
so that he might go to sit among leaders in their assembly,
a companion to the rulers of the divine people;
<insert>He who gave the barren woman a glorious home to dwell in,</insert>
making her a mother of sons, rejoicing over her children.

## Psalm 113
<note>In the Septuagint numbering used here, Psalm 113 combines two poems that are separated in modern Bibles: the story of the Exodus (Psalm 114) and a plea for God’s glory over idols (Psalm 115).</note>

->The third song after the eleventh decade; Hallelujah.<-
<note>original: "Ἐνδεκάτης δεκάδος σὺν καὶ τρίτον" — This is a poetic way of saying 113 (11 x 10 + 3).</note>

**W**hen strong Israel went forth in their might,
and the people of Jacob left the foreign-tongued <note>The Greek term "barbarophonon" refers to the Egyptians, whose language sounded strange or unintelligible to the Hebrews.</note> home,
the Jewish temple became the sanctuary of God,
and the Immortal One ruled over glorious Israel.
The sea fled as soon as it perceived Him,
while the Jordan River was driven far back;
the mountains skipped like rams,
and the peaks of the shaggy mountains became like playful lambs.
Tell me, sea, why did you part in flight?
And why, Jordan, were you driven so far back?
Why, mountains, did you skip like rams,
and you peaks of the shaggy mountains like playful lambs?
The earth shook before the immortal God,
who always rules over great-hearted Jacob;
He who turned the rock into a likeness of pools of water,
commanding the sharp cliff to bubble up with springs.
Do not do these things for our sake; for what strength have we gained?
<insert>But by Your wisdom may You grant the highest glory,</insert>
by Your mercy, O King, and Your true counsel,
lest the tribes should ever say, "Where then has their God gone?"

### APOLLINARIS

Our God, on the other hand, reigns forever in heaven;
He is established in heaven, yet He shepherds all things on the earth,
fashioning whatever He desires in His ever-living spirit.
The idols of the nations are but gold and silver,
the lovely handiwork of the hands of mortal craftsmen.
They have no voice, even though mouths were fashioned for them;
they carry no light within their polished eyes;
they possess ears only, yet they do not hear a cry;
no scent of air enters into their nostrils;
they have no strength for touching, even if they are equipped with hands;
they have never set a footprint upon the nourishing earth;
nor can a voice be heard coming from their throats;
for they do not even breathe with a rush of spirit through their mouths.
May the builders of idols become just like them,
as well as all those who are overbold in their minds for such things.
Glorious Israel set their hope upon the King;
He is their helper and their undying shield-bearer. <note>original: "aspidiotes" — a classical Greek term for a shield-bearing soldier, here used as a poetic epithet for God's protection.</note>
The glorious house of Aaron set their hope upon the King;
He is their helper and their fearless shield-bearer.
Those who fear the Lord set their hope upon the King;
He is their helper and their valiant shield-bearer.
The Immortal One, mindful of us, has blessed us all.
He blessed the glorious house for peaceful Israel;
He blessed the pure house of Aaron the priest;
He blessed all the people who care for the King of All,
together with everyone of every age in greater honor;
He increased them all greatly along with our children.
May you be blessed in the mind of the immortal King of All,
who created the green earth and the wide heaven.
The heaven belongs to the heavenly, radiant King;

# Psalm 116
### The fourth after the eleventh decade; Hallelujah. <note>This numbering refers to its place in the Psalter: the 114th Psalm (110 + 4). The Greek numbering in the header, 116, follows a different manuscript tradition.</note>

I have loved God because he heard my supplication,
because he inclined his divine and good ear to me;
and I shall sing of him continuously as my noble helper.
The pangs of death held me fast in a silence-bringing grip,
and the turmoils of silent Hades <note>The Greek term "aidao" refers to the underworld or the grave.</note> took hold of me.
I found chilling pain and boundless toil;
but I called upon the much-entreated name of the Immortal King:
"O Incorruptible One, quickly save my terrified soul!"
Our King is holy and merciful,
increasing his mercy toward the human race;
God has always known how to guard the simple-hearted;
and he saved me, though I was overcome, with his life-sustaining hand.
O my soul, why do you delay to return to your rest,
from your need, since God has snatched you from painful ruin?
For he has rescued my heavy-hearted soul from death,
my eyes from weeping, and my feet from slipping;
may I appear well-pleasing to the King in the land of the living.

### PSALM 117

...because he unceasingly increases his mercy toward his own.
In my distress, I called upon the mighty King, <note>original: "eridhenetō" — a high-epic epithet for someone extremely powerful or flourishing.</note>
and leading me out of misery, he urged my paths forward. <note>This renders the biblical phrase "he set me in a large place."</note>
The pure Lord is forever the helper of my need;
I shall never fear what a mortal may do to me.
The pure Lord is forever the helper of my need;
and I shall look upon the wretched fate of my enemies. <note>original: "dēiōn" — an epic word for foes in battle.</note>
It is better to live trusting in the immortal King,
than to vainly hold a bold mind toward mortal man,
or to pour out a helpless hope upon leaders.
All the nations surrounded me on every side;
but trusting in the King, I overcame them all.
They held me in a circle, walled up on all sides;
but trusting in the King, I overcame them all.
Like bees, they swarmed around me from all directions like a hive, <note>original: "simblon" — a beehive. The poet expands the biblical metaphor of bees with epic detail.</note>
and they were scorched like fire among thorns in the courtyards;
but trusting in the King, I overcame them all.
I was pushed and about to fall, but a good champion came to my aid. <note>original: "promachos" — a front-line warrior or defender.</note>
My praise and my strength reside in the immortal King,
and a ready light that wards off evil appeared to me. <note>original: "alexikakon" — a classical title for a protector god (like Apollo), here applied to God’s salvation.</note>
The cry of joy and light is in the tents of the righteous.
The right hand of the King has wrought glorious power;
his right hand has made my head very famous;
the right hand of the King has wrought glorious power.
I shall never die, but it shall be my time of life,
and with great understanding I will proclaim the royal works.
The immortal Shepherd disciplined me with his beloved teachings;
but he did not deliver me over to be conquered by an evil death.
Come now, open for me the faithful gates of righteousness,
and entering in, I will sing a much-entreated hymn to the King.

This is the gate of the King; let the righteous care for this path.
Immortal One, I glorify you, because you heard my voice,
and you appeared to me as a ready light that wards off evil. <note>original: "alexikakon"—a classic epithet meaning 'averter of evil,' often used for Apollo or Heracles in ancient Greek religion.</note>
The stone which the builders quarreled over and rejected,
that very one appeared as the straight head of the walls of the houses; <note>This refers to the "chief cornerstone" of the Psalm.</note>
God built this precious thing with his own hands,
and it was a wonder in the eyes of mortals.
God fashioned this day with his own hands,
so that it might appear as gladness and joy to articulate-speaking men. <note>original: "meropessin"—a standard Homeric term for humans, referring to their ability to speak with a divided or articulate voice.</note>

we bless you, having come to the halls of the Blessed One.

He is our God, and I will lift you on high.
Immortal One, I glorify you, because you heard my voice,
and you appeared to me as a ready light that wards off evil.

because he increases his mercy unceasingly.

### [Page] 159

### PSALM 117 <note>Though the header says 117, this is the continuation of Psalm 118 (Hebrew 119), the longest psalm in the Bible, known for its focus on the Law of God.</note>

I have trodden the paths in the footsteps of the Immortal One.
You have firmly commanded the people to keep your instruction. <note>original: "ennesiēs"—a poetic term for a command or suggestion, often used in Homeric Greek for divine guidance.</note>
O that all my paths were guided very well,
so that I might always be mindful of your righteous judgments.
And in my mind I would escape heavy and shameful blame,
if I fix my eyes upon your god-fearing instructions.
With a straight-aiming heart, O King, I will sing your praise,
whenever I learn the works of God-pleasing justice.
In my heart, O Spotless One, I will keep your righteous laws; <note>original: "prapidessi"—literally the midriff or diaphragm, considered the seat of the mind and emotions in ancient Greek poetry.</note>
do not utterly forsake me, O most gentle shepherd.

<margin>A green ink manicule (a hand with a pointing index finger) is drawn in the right margin, pointing towards the first block of text.</margin>

How shall a young man, flying on the wings of youth, keep his way straight,
unless he keeps the instructions of the Blessed One in his heart?
With every thought, O King, I have sought you;
do not thrust me away from your instructions.
I have placed your words in my breast, O Blessed One,
so that I might not be overcome by destructive follies.
Blessed are you, O Blessed One; teach me your laws.
With my lips I have declared the laws of your mouth.
I have rejoiced in your path, O Most Blessed of ten thousand, <note>original: "myrielaōn"—a compound meaning 'from ten thousand,' used here to emphasize God's supreme status above all others.</note>
as one who exults in his heart over great wealth.
I will meditate thoughtfully upon your instructions,
and I would track out your precious path.
I will meditate continually upon your righteous judgments,
nor will I ever forget your holy words.

<margin>A green decorative flourish, resembling a stylized vine with multiple leafy extensions, is drawn in the right margin below the second block of text.</margin>

In return for his labors, O Blessed One, grant your servant a reward;
I shall live, increasing the profit of your immortal words in my mind.
Scatter the mist from my eyes, O Blessed One, which was there before,
so that I may clearly see the all-famous wonders of your laws.

### [Page] 160

### APOLLINAR[IUS]

I dwell upon the short-lived earth of a foreign life; <note>original: "ammodapou biotoio"—a poetic way of describing the psalmist as a stranger or sojourner on earth (Psalm 119:19).</note>
do not hide from me the grace of your good instructions. <note>original: "ennesiaōn"—a Homeric term for suggestions or divine commands.</note>
My mind has longed for your righteous judgments all my days.
From heaven, you rebuked the command of the arrogant;
wretched indeed are those who have forgotten your instructions.
O King, drive far from me blame and much reproach,
because I have followed the tracks of your venerable testimonies.
Slanderous rulers chattered all about me,
but I was always occupied with your righteous judgments.
the testimonies of the King are the concerns of my heart,
and your laws support my every counsel.

My soul is brought low to the most shameful ground; <note>A poetic rendering of Psalm 119:25: "My soul cleaves to the dust."</note>
nevertheless, O Spotless One, let me live by your words.
You heard me as I declared our paths;
O most blessed one, teach me your righteousness swiftly.
Lay the glorious path of justice beside my soul,
and I will sing skillfully of your wonders.
My soul sank, struck by evil ruin, <note>original: "atē"—a classical Greek term for a delusion or divine curse that leads to ruin.</note>
but sustain my heart once more with your words.
O King, keep the lawless path far from me,
and have mercy on us with your lawful oracles.
I have always found the path of truth pleasing to my heart,
nor did I forget your God-befitting righteous judgments.
Do not utterly shame me, who trusts in your testimonies;
I ran the tireless path of your guidance <note>original: "aeideiketon"—usually meaning "unseemly" in Homer, but here used by Apollinaris to mean "boundless" or "continuous" in the context of a long path.</note>
when you greatly broadened the heart within my breast.

O Blessed One, teach me the track of your good laws,
and I shall track it through continuous generations.

### [Page] 161

### PSALM 118

Give me, O King, wisdom, so that I might search out your law,
and I shall always guard it within my heart. <note>original: "sternoioin"—literally "breasts" or "chest," used in epic poetry as the seat of thought and emotion.</note>
If you guide me by your good laws,
I long to wrap them all about my mind. <note>original: "prapidessi"—a Homeric term for the diaphragm or the seat of the intellect.</note>
May my heart incline toward your testimonies,
and may I not hold onto insatiable, wealth-loving insolence. <note>A poetic expansion of Psalm 119:36, which warns against covetousness or selfish gain.</note>
Drive away from my eyes the empty-boasting outrage; <note>original: "keneauchea lōbēn"—referring to the "vanity" or "worthless things" mentioned in the biblical verse.</note>
let me live, O Blessed One, in your life-bearing paths.
For your servant, may you make firm your word in fear.
Bring to light the reproach I formerly feared,
because your judgments are tempered with kindness.
I hurry in my heart with a desire for your commands; <note>original: "ennesiaōn"—suggestions or divine instructions.</note>
may I have your righteousness as a partner for my life.

May your divine mercy, O King, cover me;
let the light of your words, which ward off evil, <term>alexikakoi</term> <note>A classical epithet often applied to gods like Apollo or Hercules, meaning "averting evil." Here, it describes the protective power of God's word.</note> follow.
So that I may answer the insolent as is right,
because I have placed my hope in your words.
Do not utterly withhold the truth of your mouth from me.
For I hold a longing for your well-ordered righteousness.
I will guard your radiant law well within my mind,
from generation to generation, throughout all days.
Therefore I always ran forward on broad paths,
because I followed the royal command;
nor did I feel shame as I spoke of your testimonies.
And how those kings then greatly marveled at my tongue;
I nourished my heart with the cares that you loved in abundance;
and I stretched out my hands toward your lovely command,
longing to feed my mind under your good laws.

L <note>This "L" likely serves as a signature or a gathering mark for the manuscript's production.</note>

<summary>In this section of the Psalm 119 paraphrase, the poet-theologian Apollinaris uses Homeric language to plead for divine wisdom, the rejection of vanity, and the courage to speak God's laws before kings.</summary>

### [Page] 162

->**APOLLINARIS**<-

**R**emember your word to your servant, upon which you caused me to hope;
this became a noble comfort for my weakness,
because your word has always been my beautiful life.
Always the arrogant <note>original: "hyperphialoi"—a common Homeric epithet for the "overbearing" or "insolent" suitors in the *Odyssey*.</note> prepared lawless deeds;
but my mind was always fixed firmly upon your decrees.
I shall find relief, O King, by unfolding your decrees.
Truly, my heart is struck and fluttered by weakness,
seeing those who ruin your good laws.
I have often sung a melody of your dear laws,
even while being a wanderer in foreign lands.
Through the dark night I remembered the King,
and I guarded your immortal law within my dear breast.
I hold this as a worthy reward for your good justice.

<margin>A hand-drawn green sprig or branch appears here.</margin>

**Y**ou yourself said you were my portion, and I promised to guard your laws.
With an unceasing heart I entreated the King’s face;
be merciful to me, O King, and I shall do things worthy of your words.
For I considered your paths within my heart;
and I turned my feet close to your testimony.
From there I became most ready, being one who is unshaken,
having a heart that cares for your instructions. <term>ennesiaōn</term> <note>A poetic term for divine suggestions, commands, or promptings.</note>
Though I was bound by the deceitful ropes of the sinful,
I did not forget the commands of your laws in my mind.
Instead, I hastened to rise up in the middle of the night
to sing of the laws of your justice with my thoughts. <note>original: "prapidessi"—referring to the internal organs or the diaphragm, considered the seat of the intellect in early Greek poetry.</note>
I was a companion to all your servants, O Blessed One,
to as many as have guarded the instructions of God.
The earth is heavy with the good kindness of the King.
May you teach us your glorious righteousness.
