Apollinaris continues to use Homeric vocabulary to frame the Psalmist’s devotion as an epic journey of the soul.</meta>

### PSALM 119

**L 2**

With a gentle word you have granted gifts to your servant;
may you, O Blessed One, teach me gentle wisdom,
<margin>(teach),</margin> because I have trusted in your divine promptings, O Blessed One. <note>The Greek text here uses "ennesiēsi," a poetic term for divine suggestions or commands.</note> <image-desc>decorative green branch at the end of the line</image-desc>
Before I was brought low, I committed a reckless folly;
therefore I have guarded your words within my dear breast.
You are gentle, O Blessed One, and may you teach me your righteous judgments.
The unspeakable insolence of the overbearing <note>original: "hyperphialōn"—this is a classic Homeric epithet for the "arrogant" or "insolent," most famously used to describe the suitors in the Odyssey.</note> has been spread out against me;
but I will ponder your concerns in my spirit.
Their own heart was thickened like white milk; <note>This is a literal translation of the Greek "leukō ikelon epepēkto galakti," which mirrors the Hebrew idiom for a heart that has become "callous" or "fat."</note>
but as for me, your law has always been the care of my mind.
How good it was for me to be made low and weak,
so that I might learn your glorious righteousness.
How good the royal law of your mouth has become, <image-desc>decorative green branch at the end of the line</image-desc>
better for us than thousands of gold and silver pieces.

---

By your hands, O Blessed One, I was fashioned and molded;
give me then understanding, so that I may learn your counsels.
Your servants, seeing me, rejoiced in their souls;
because I have bound my cares to your words.
I have clearly perceived the laws of your righteousness,
and indeed by your will I was made low and strengthless.
May you grant your mercy to us as a consolation, <image-desc>decorative green branch at the end of the line</image-desc>
and provide a wise word to your servant.
If only you would take pity on me, so that I might live with confidence,
because the royal law has been my meditation. <image-desc>decorative green branch at the end of the line</image-desc>
Shame to the overbearing, who acted against the law toward me;
but I will sing aloud concerning your instructions.
May your servants who fear your life turn back to me,
those who have known the testimonies of the King who gives help.
May the thoughts of my mind be blameless in your instructions,

<image-desc>In the lower right margin, there is a cluster of three small green decorative branch flourishes.</image-desc>

<summary>This page contains the poetic Greek paraphrase of Psalm 119, verses 65–80. The poet emphasizes the value of divine law over material wealth and the necessity of suffering for spiritual learning.</summary>

<meta>This page continues Apollinaris of Laodicea’s hexameter paraphrase of Psalm 119 (Septuagint 118). The text transitions from the end of the previous section into two new stanzas, marked by the Greek letters Theta and Epsilon (which Apollinaris uses to structure his poetic acrostic). Apollinaris continues to blend the Davidic voice with the vocabulary of Homeric epic, casting the Psalmist as a seeker of divine light amidst the "thoughtlessness" of the arrogant.</meta>

# APOLLINARIS

...so that I might escape from evils and be driven far from shame. <note>This line concludes the previous stanza, where the speaker asks for an upright heart to avoid being put to shame (Psalm 119:80).</note>

<insert>T</insert>oward a divine radiance my mind has been stretched,
longing to hear your word together with good instruction.
My eyes were wasting away for your word;
you ask, "When will you come to be a comfort to me?"
I became like a wineskin near the snowy frost; <note>original: "askos hopōs... chionōdeos"—a poetic rendering of Psalm 119:83 ("I have become like a wineskin in the smoke"). Apollinaris replaces "smoke" with "frost" or "ice" to maintain the epic, elemental imagery of his verse.</note>
but I never forgot your righteousness in my heart.
Tell me, how long is the life of your servant?
When will you execute a worthy judgment to ward off this insolence?
Insolent men mixed together in their thoughtlessness;
they did not follow your law, but held to a path of lies.
The commands of the King are undying and all-seeing;
others persecuted me unjustly, but may you be my helper.
They nearly brought me to the earth, having completely overpowered me;
but I remained, trusting in your instructions.
Immortal King, I shall live beautifully by your word,
and I shall guard the testimonies of your mouth within my breast.

**F**rom generation to generation you dispense your all-commanding counsel; <note>original: "panomphea"—a Homeric epithet usually reserved for Zeus as the source of all voices or oracles.</note>
your word remains forever in the starry heaven.
The blessed earth remains, rooted by your hands;
the dawn and the suns command your light and radiance.
For all things serve as attendants to what you have crafted with your hands.
If your law were not the object of my care and concern,
I would have perished in a cold and disobedient fate.
Never shall I forget your divine statutes;
since from them I have found a gracious path for my life.
I am your servant, so may you ward off an evil fate from me;
because I have always trusted in your instructions.

# PSALM 118
L 3

Vainly the lawless waited to destroy me in their recklessness,
but I discerned the grace of your testimonies.
I have perceived the limit of this life with every kind of understanding;
but I have learned that your commandment, O King, is broad. <note>original: "basileu" — though literally "King," Apollinaris uses this Homeric title to address God, emphasizing His sovereignty over the cosmos.</note>

---

How very much I cherished your royal law!
And taking it into my heart, I shall sing of it all day long.
Through your instructions, you have taught me more than my enemies;
this is my lasting inheritance, and it warms my soul continually.
Yield to me, you who are the arrangers of ancient wisdom, <note>This refers to the "teachers" mentioned in the biblical text (v. 99). Apollinaris uses the epic term "kosmetores," often used by Homer for leaders or commanders.</note>
for I possess the meditations of the King’s own mind in these instructions.
I have found a counsel far greater than the prudence of the elders,
by tracking the testimonies of the King within my spirit.
I have held back my footsteps from every kind of evil path,
so that I might guard your beloved word within my mind.
Nor did I turn away from your steadfast counsel,
fearing always the help found in your instruction.
I found your law of words to be sweet to my throat, <note>original: "leukanio" — a poetic term for the throat or windpipe.</note>
being more delightful to us than sweet honeycomb.
I have understood all things well by the grace of your instructions;
therefore, I have come to hate every lawless path.

---

Your law, O King, was made a lamp for my feet forever,
an undying and ageless light for my footsteps.
I have sworn, O Pure One, to guard the decree of your justice.
I became weak and humbled, but I lived by your words.
May you look with favor upon the offerings of my tongue, <note>original: "katathumia" — things pleasing to the heart or desires; here referring to the voluntary offerings of prayer.</note>
and teach me your most glorious justice.
I hold my soul in my hands for you continually, <note>A poetic rendering of "my life is continually in my hand" (v. 109), an idiom meaning to be in constant danger.</note>
nor would I ever forget your law within my mind.

<meta>This page continues Apollinaris of Laodicea’s hexameter paraphrase of Psalm 119 (Septuagint 118). The previous page ended with the beginning of the Nun section, comparing God’s law to a lamp for the feet. This page continues that section and moves into the Samekh and Ayin sections.</meta>

### APOLLINARIS

<meta>Marginal bracket groups the first six lines of the page.</meta>

Evil-devising men stretched out a snare <note>original: "linon" — literally "flax," used here in the Homeric sense of a hunter's net or a snare.</note> for us,
yet I did not ignore your command.
I held the life-sustaining inheritance of the King’s testimonies,
because they have become the joy of my heart.
I have yearned in my heart to proclaim your justice, O Blessed One,
so that you might refresh my heart with your rewards.

---

I loved your law, but I loathed those who are lawless;
you are my day <note>original: "ēmar" — in epic poetry, this can mean one’s "day of destiny" or "protection," suggesting God is the author of the poet's life-light.</note> and the helper of my journey.
I placed my glorious hope in your words at all times.
Depart from me, you who suggest hateful sins,
so that I might pursue the commands of God the King.
Stretch out a helping hand for your words,
and do not bring me to shame as I wait for your guidance.
Help me as I am tested, O Most Blessed, and you will save me;
I shall meditate on your justice without ceasing.
You have destroyed those who hate you and disregard justice,
for the intention of the ungodly is sinful.
I considered all who sin against the law to be as dross;
therefore, I have cherished the grace of your testimonies.
Fix my body with your fear as if with nails; <note>This is a poetic rendering of the Septuagint's "pierce my flesh with your fear," using the epic term "hēlois" (nails).</note>
for I tremble before the mighty glory of your statutes.

---

I have practiced good law and lovely righteousness;
do not surrender me as prey and plunder to those who use force against me.
Remain with your servant for his good, O Most High,
and let not lying men defeat me in justice.
My eyes have longed for your light that wards off evil,
and for your magnificent and glorious word of life.
Deal with your servant, O Blessed One, in a manner worthy of your kindness,
and teach me your most glorious righteousness.

# PSALM 118

I am your servant; grant me wisdom,
so that I may know the foundations of your testimonies.
It is time for the Great King <note>original: "pambasilēï" — literally "All-King," a title for God mirroring the Homeric style for Zeus.</note> to accomplish a work much prayed for;
for the evildoers have scattered your life-sustaining law.
Therefore I have longed for the great glory of your commands,
far more than shimmering gold or topaz. <note>original: "topazio" — a precious gemstone; the poet uses the epic hexameter to elevate the biblical comparison of God's word to physical wealth.</note>
I have directed my mind toward your command as its guardian;
I have come to hate every path of a lawless life.

<margin>A light green sketch of a leafy vine or branch appears in the right margin beside the following paragraph.</margin>

The testimonies of the King are always wondrous;
therefore, I have tracked them all with the movements of my mind.
May the grace of your words grant us life and light,
always instilling wisdom in the hearts of the simple. <note>original: "nēpiachōn" — literally "infants" or "simple ones," those without guile who are open to God's teaching.</note>
I opened my mouth and drew in the spirit <note>original: "pneuma" — can mean breath, wind, or the Holy Spirit; the poet depicts the act of longing for God as a physical gasping for air.</note> of the Most High,
because I always longed for the glory of your commands.
Extend your gaze toward us, O King,
as is right to perform for those who cherish your names.
Direct my steps as they run toward your words,
and let no incurable wickedness enslave me.

<margin>A second, smaller green botanical sketch is drawn in the right margin near the bottom of this stanza.</margin>

Save me from the deadly lies of mortal men,
and I shall guard your good commands within my heart.
Show your lovely face to your needy servant,
and teach me the fame of your justice.
My eyes poured forth tearful streams
because men overran the character of your divine laws.

Truly, you are a holy King, and you judge all things rightly.
You have commanded your justice through your testimonies,
and the unshakable strength of lovely truth.
Grief for your divine words <note>original: "theophradeōn" — an epic compound meaning "spoken by God."</note> consumes my heart,
because the evil-minded have forgotten your commands.

L 4

# APOLLINARIS

...your word is like a blazing fire; <note>original: "pyriphlegethonti" — a fiery Homeric epithet, here describing the purifying intensity of God's word.</note>
but let your servant cherish it in his heart.
I have become small and despised,
yet I always sing of your righteousness in my mind.
Your righteousness is ageless for all days,
and your truth follows your wise laws.
Hateful toil and helpless necessities have seized me,
but even so I did not forget, singing aloud of your concerns.
You bear the fruitful testimonies of holy deeds;
grant me wisdom, O King, and let my life be made whole.

<margin>A hand-painted green botanical flourish with red floral accents is placed in the right margin between the first and second stanzas.</margin>

From my heart I cried out, O Pure One, and may you save me,
and I shall guard your good testimonies in my breast.
I cried out, rising early in the darkness of the night,
unfolding my words to the King at dawn.
My eyes looked out swiftly before the morning hour,
so that I might cry out the beautiful meditations of your words.
Hear our voice with a merciful heart;
guide my path according to your righteous judgments.
Pursuers of lawlessness held me in their company; <note>original: "synerithon" — implies being a companion in labor or a partner, here used for those joined in wickedness.</note>
they fled far from you and your glorious law.
You are near, O King, and all your paths are all-sufficient.
From the beginning I have known of your testimonies,
that you have unceasingly fixed their faithful foundations for all.

Look upon my weakness, O King, and may you save me;
for I keep your law, remembering it in my mind.
Having rescued me, O King, and having judged my cause,
let your word be the source of life for me.
Far from the impious is your light that wards off evil; <note>original: "alexikakon" — a classic Greek epithet used for a deity who protects from or averts misfortune.</note>

<summary>This page concludes the Tsade and Qoph sections of Psalm 119, emphasizing the purity of God's word and the psalmist's devotion during the night watches, before beginning the Resh section's plea for rescue from affliction.</summary>

<meta>This page continues the hexameter paraphrase of Psalm 119 (Septuagint 118) by Apollinaris of Laodicea. It finishes the sections corresponding to the Hebrew letters Resh (Deliverance) and Shin (Peace/Prudence), and begins the final section, Tav (Supplication). # PSALM 118

...for they have forgotten your divine righteousness.
The mercies of the King are infinite;
guide my path according to your fair judgments.
Countless persecutors weigh me down with labors,
yet still I have not turned away from your testimonies.
Seeing the servants of folly, my heart melts away—
those whose minds refused to keep your word, O Lord.
Consider how I have cared for your commands;
may I choose the life-giving <note>original: "zooren" — a Homeric epithet for something vital or life-sustaining.</note> path of life.
You have made truth the mother of your words,
upon which you have established your law of justice forever.

Shameless rulers persecuted me without cause; <note>original: "mapsidios" — a Homeric term for "vainly" or "without reason," translating the Hebrew 'hinnam'.</note>
but my heart trembled only at the terrors of your words.
I shall rejoice in my heart over your words,
like one who takes great spoil from the enemy. <note>original: "apeinymbros" — an obscure poetic term for one who despoils or carries away booty.</note>
I have hated injustice, while deeply loving your law.
Seven times a day, O King, I have praised your glory,
singing of the great and boast-worthy works of your righteousness.
May peace belong to the servants of the King,
and may no strife seize them as they set forth.
I have looked for your light, and longed for your commands;
I have kept all your testimonies and loved them in my soul.
I have kept your commands and your lovely harmonies; <note>original: "harmonias" — here used to translate the 'ordinances' or 'statutes' of the Law, suggesting a cosmic order.</note>
for you have known the goal of our paths before us.

Let my supplication come before the King,
and send us wisdom worthy of your words.
O Blessed One, the grace of our prayers shall reach you;
may your word always be my rescuer from evil.

--- Page 225 ---
<meta>This page concludes the monumental hexameter paraphrase of Psalm 118 (Septuagint numbering) and begins the "Songs of Ascents" (Psalms 119 and 120). Apollinaris continues to blend the language of the Greek Septuagint with the heroic vocabulary of Homer, casting the Psalmist as a wandering epic figure seeking the protection of the "King" (God).</meta>

<header>APOLLINARIS</header>

With lips that raise no worldly clamor, I shall sing a song-loving melody,
since you have taught me your glorious righteousness.
May my tongue utter your dear, life-sustaining <note>original: "zoarkea" — a compound word meaning "sufficient for life" or "sustaining life."</note> word,
because you have carved out righteousness within your commands.
Always stretching out your right hand, may you save me;
for I long for your instructions with all my heart.
From my soul I have yearned for your light that wards off evil, <note>original: "alexikakon" — a classic Homeric epithet often used for deities who protect humans from harm.</note>
and with my tongue I shall sing of your royal law.
May my soul live and sing your glorious praise;
and may I always be watched over by your judgments.
I have wandered like a woolly-fleeced <note>original: "eiropokou" — a very specific Homeric adjective describing sheep.</note> and lost sheep;
seek out your servant, who remembers your bidding.

### PSALM 119

-> This is the eleventh decade toward the nineteenth imperishable song, <-
-> a ladder-like song of life-bearing ascents. <note>The "Songs of Ascents" (Psalms 120–134 in modern Bibles) were traditionally sung by pilgrims going up to Jerusalem. Apollinaris calls them "ladder-like" (klimakoessa), evoking Jacob's ladder.</note> <-

In my distress I cried out, and the King heard my prayers.
Rescue my soul from unjust lips,
O Blessed One, do not abandon me to the ruin of a deceitful tongue.
What could a treacherous tongue provide for you, or what could it add?
It is like the sharpened arrows of a hunter,
or the burning coals of the desert wilderness. <note>original: "rheemou" — referring to the desert or a desolate place.</note>
Woe is me, for I have dwelt too long as a stranger on the earth,
pitching my dwellings among the tents of Kedar. <note>Kedar was a nomadic tribe; in the Psalms, it symbolizes living far from the holy city among hostile people.</note>
Long have I lived as a resident in foreign lands.
Though I pursued peace with those who love war,
when I spoke, they were vainly driven toward the strife of battle.

### PSALM 120

-> This is the twelfth decade, of life-bearing ascents. <-

<meta>This page continues the "Songs of Ascents" (Psalms 120–134). Having just finished Psalm 119, Apollinaris now paraphrases the famous "I lift up mine eyes" (Psalm 120/121) and "I was glad when they said unto me" (Psalm 121/122). He continues to use Homeric epic language, describing God as a "King" and the moon as "horned."</meta>

<header>PSALM 120</header>

I have stretched my eyes over the tree-covered mountains,
looking for the strength of our help from that place.
May my power be established by the dwelling King,
who fashioned the green earth and the wide heaven.
But, dear friend, do not allow your foot to be shaken,
nor let sleep seize the mind of your sleepless guardian.
For sweet sleep would not take the nodding guardian <gloss>down</gloss> <note>original: "kata" — used here to emphasize the weight of sleep pressing down.</note>
of the famous and wise Israel.
May God protect and shield your right hand;
the fury of the sun shall not burn you throughout the day,
nor shall the horned <note>original: "kerae" — a poetic description of the crescent moon's "horns."</note> moon harm you throughout the night.
May the King keep you far from every kind of evil,
caring for your soul, so that pain may never find it.
May the Immortal One keep you warm as you enter and as you depart,
now and hereafter for all continuous generations.

## Psalm 121 <note>Numbered as Psalm 122 in modern English Bibles.</note>

-> This twelfth decade rushes back into a single song. <note>Apollinaris organizes the Psalms into "decades" or groups of ten. This marks the start of a new grouping within his epic retelling.</note> <-

When I learned from men, my heart was greatly, truly warmed,
those who told me to go as a companion to the house of God.
There we stand within the sacred courts of Jerusalem;
this city was built as a place of honor;
and around it were gathered all those who are neighbors from outside.
For there the twelve tribes were urged to come,
a witness for the people of God-pleasing Israel;
so that they might sing of the pure name of God.
There the seats were fixed, softened with justice,
the thrones in the houses of the breaker-of-ranks <note>original: "rhexitheentos" — a classic Homeric epithet usually reserved for epic heroes who break through enemy lines, here applied to King David.</note> David.
Seek deep peace for Jerusalem;
and for those who love you, O blessed one, may boundless prosperity flow.

<header>APOLLINARIS</header>

Life-giving prosperity is found neither in walls nor in towers. <note>original: "erkesin ou’ purgoisi" — referring to the fortifications and defensive towers of Jerusalem mentioned in the previous lines.</note>
But for the sake of my brothers and my companions,
<margin>A small green decorative branch with leaves in the left margin</margin> I have obtained peace because of your counsel;
longing for your house and all your blessed things.

# Psalm 122 <note>Numbered as Psalm 123 in modern English Bibles.</note>
## This is the twelfth decade, and it brings with it a second hymn. <note>Apollinaris organizes his poetic version of the Psalms into "decades" (groups of ten). This is the second poem in the group beginning with Psalm 121.</note>

Lift up your eyes to the heavenly King;
as when the light <note>original: "phaos" (light) — here used poetically to refer to the eyes or the attentive gaze of a servant.</note> is toward the hands of masters for their servants,
as when the light is toward the hands of a mistress for her handmaiden,
so our blessed eyes seek out your strength,
until you extend your mercy to us who are in need.
Have mercy on us, O Most Blessed, and do not cease,
for we have all been filled with shameful insolence.
Malice has overflowed within the hearts of each of us; <note>original: "bebelthen" — a vivid verb suggesting that the evil of their enemies is bubbling over or surging like a flood.</note>
let bitter reproaches be left for the wealthy,
and let all disgrace be for the arrogant.

# Psalm 123 <note>Numbered as Psalm 124 in modern English Bibles.</note>
## And the twelfth decade bringing a third hymn.

If there had not been an excellent guard to rescue our city,
if there had not been a guard—let great Israel speak—
when enemies were arming themselves against our people for battle,
they would have seized and swallowed us while we were still alive;
when they were enraged with a fiery and cruel heart,
then the swift, deep water would have held us back.
In our spirit we have passed through the rush of the winter water;
our soul has passed through the unstable water.
Blessed is He who did not give us as a prey to their teeth;
our spirit flew off, entirely like a sparrow,

--- Page 228 ---
<meta>This page continues the hexameter paraphrase of the Psalms attributed to Apollinaris of Laodicea (4th century AD). The text completes the "Song of Ascents" begun on the previous page—Psalm 123 in the Greek numbering, which corresponds to Psalm 124 in modern English Bibles—and provides the next two hymns in the series.</meta>

<header>PSALM 123 <note>Numbered as Psalm 124 in modern English Bibles.</note></header>

...having escaped the pure nets of mountain-bred hunters. <note>original: "oresitrophon... lina" — Apollinaris uses epic hunting imagery to describe the soul escaping a trap like a bird.</note>
The net was broken, and we escaped the unavoidable necessity.
Our strength is found with the immortal King,
who created the green earth and the wide heaven.

## Psalm 124 <note>Numbered as Psalm 125 in modern English Bibles.</note>

->This is the twelfth decade, and it carries with it the fourth hymn.<-

Those who trust in the immortal mountain <note>original: "Ourei athanato" — referring to Mount Zion, described here with the epithet "immortal."</note> are like Zion;
no terrible turmoil should reach the dweller of Jerusalem.
Mountains are around it, and around the people,
now and hereafter for continuous generations.
For the King will never permit the scepter of the wicked <note>original: "atasthalontos" — a Homeric term for the reckless or lawless, here referring to oppressive foreign rulers.</note>
to be numbered <note>The Greek "εἶ" is an abbreviation for "einai," meaning "to be."</note> among the portion of the ever-graceful holy ones,
so that the righteous do not stretch out their hands toward sin.
Do good, O Lord, to the good and to those of an upright heart;
but for those turning aside to the crooked counsels of pride,
the Pure One shall lead them away suddenly into lawlessness.
Gracious peace be with glorious Israel.

## Psalm 125 <note>Numbered as Psalm 126 in modern English Bibles.</note>

->This is the twelfth decade, having also the fifth song.<-

When the King gathers the returning captives of Zion,
it was a consolation for us from much-groaning sorrows.
Ah, then being filled, we may bear joy upon our mouths,
and a tongue pregnant <note>original: "egkumona" — a vivid metaphor suggesting the tongue is "heavy" or "swelling" with praise.</note> with the sound of total delight.
And some foreigner, hearing from afar, might say:
"These were the boast-worthy works of God the King."
May we exist in gladness and splendor.
Turning back the captive people to our flock,
as the terrible winter torrent is turned by the breath of the south wind. <note>original: "Notou pnoie" — in the Mediterranean, the south wind brings warmth that melts snow, turning dry beds into rushing streams. This adapts the biblical "streams in the Negev" for a Greek-speaking audience.</note>

<summary>This page contains the conclusion of Psalm 124 and the entirety of Psalm 125 and the beginning of 126 (modern numbering) rendered into Greek epic verse. The poems emphasize God's protection of Jerusalem and the joy of the exiles returning to Zion.</summary>
<keywords>Songs of Ascents, Mount Zion, Jerusalem, Captivity, Apollinaris of Laodicea, Hexameter, Divine Protection, Restoration</keywords>

The author continues to use high epic style, borrowing vocabulary from Homer to express biblical truths.</meta>

<header>APOLLINARIS</header>

...nourished by the breath of the south wind, the dread mountain-torrent grows. <note>original: "chimarrous ainos" — Apollinaris uses the image of a winter-flowing torrent to translate the "streams in the Negeb" mentioned in the Psalm.</note>
Those who sow with tears shall reap with dancing.
The reapers groaned as they carried out their seed,
but they shall carry back their sheaves with joy to the threshing floor.

# Psalm 126 <note>Numbered as Psalm 127 in modern English Bibles.</note>
->This is the twelfth decade, and it holds the sixth wonder.<-

Unless the All-King <note>original: "pambasileus" — a grand epic title for God frequently used by Apollinaris.</note> willingly builds the house,
how greatly do the building men labor in vain.
Unless the All-King guards the city,
pointlessly does the gatekeeper keep a sleepless watch.
From the dawn, a vain watch arises for everyone.
Wake up, and sit thus as you have been commanded,
you who eat the honey-sweet yet sorrowful bread of toil.
For when very sweet sleep comes to His friends,
behold the inheritance of the King: the sons,
the dear and glorious reward of the fruit-bearing womb.
For just as arrows are in the hands of the strong,
so the sons of those who are shaken will be mighty. <note>original: "tinachthentōn" — this likely refers to the "sons of one's youth" in the original Psalm, here rendered with an epic verb suggesting vigorous movement or battle.</note>
Blessed is he who fulfills his own dear desire from them. <note>The "desire" here refers to the "quiver full of arrows" mentioned in the biblical text.</note>
They shall not be seized by the lies of enemies insulting them at the gates.

# Psalm 127 <note>Numbered as Psalm 128 in modern English Bibles.</note>
->The twelfth decade, attending to the seventh hymn.<-

Blessed are those who care for the fear of God within them,
whose feet are always ready on the path of the King.
You shall delight in what you labored for, eating the honey-sweet fruit.
All things shall be well for you, and you shall be called the most blessed of mortals.
Your wife, like a cultivated vine, shall grace your house,
and your sons shall be like young olive trees,

<header>PSALM 127 <note>Numbered as Psalm 128 in modern English Bibles.</note></header>

...flourishing, they shall come around the circle of your table.
How greatly blessed are all those who tremble before the King.
May you have imperishable royal praise from Zion;
and you shall behold the wealth of divine Jerusalem,
through all the days of an untiring life;
and you shall see children for your dear ones, as many as you can perceive;
peace having anointed the glorious Israel.

### Psalm 128 <note>Numbered as Psalm 129 in modern English Bibles.</note>

->The twelfth decade flashes its eighth hymn.<-

"Often from my youth they raised a shameless battle-cry against me,"
let echoing and famous Israel now speak.
"Often from my youth they raised a shameless battle-cry against me,
yet they were not strong enough to prevail against my songs."
The impious fashioned evil sorrows upon my back,
stretching out their own fruitless transgression. <note>original: "amplakiēn... apophōlion" — a poetic way to describe the "plowing" of the wicked mentioned in the biblical text, using epic terms for failure and error.</note>
But the holy King broke the neck of the sinners. <note>original: "auchen' alitrōn" — Apollinaris uses the image of breaking a neck where the Hebrew text speaks of cutting the "cords" or "yoke" of the wicked.</note>
May shame cover those who hate Zion forever.
Let them become withered like the grass upon the rooftops,
which dries up in the joyless season before the reaper comes.
The binder of sheaves comes to it with an empty hand;
nor does he fill his lap while gathering the yearly handfuls,
nor does any passer-by offer praise to the men there;
men whom they used to greet, rejoicing in the divine season. <note>The poet is paraphrasing the traditional blessing: "The blessing of the Lord be upon you."</note>

### Psalm 129 <note>Numbered as Psalm 130 in modern English Bibles.</note>

->The twelfth decade and its ninth hymn are seen.<-

From the depths of the pit I sang a cry in my need,
hearken to our voice, O most high Shepherd; <note>original: "panypertate... poimēn" — a classicizing title for God as the supreme pastor.</note>
listen to my supplication, and receive my voice.

<meta>This page continues the Greek hexameter paraphrase of the Psalms by Apollinaris of Laodicea. The previous page ended with the middle of Psalm 129 (modern 130), a "Song of Ascents" expressing deep repentance and hope in God’s mercy.</meta>

# APOLLINARIUS

For if you should keep the divisions of men within their breasts,
what mortal shall be able to stand against the immortal’s wrath? <note>original: "dichonomias meropōn" — a poetic way of describing human sins or "iniquities" as things that divide or distinguish people before God’s judgment.</note>
Always, O King, you keep mercy in your heart;
for the sake of your wisdom I have endured royal virtue. <note>Line highlighted in green. The original Psalm says "that you may be feared," but the poet here links God's forgiveness to the pursuit of wisdom and virtue.</note>
With my soul I hastened to choose the God-pleasing law,
and in my heart, O Blessed One, I longed to place my hope in you,
from the morning watch even until the paths of night,
from the morning watch let Israel care for God;
because the King has a living and free day,
rescuing all of glorious Israel from evil.

## Psalm 130 <note>Numbered as Psalm 131 in modern English Bibles.</note>

->This is the thirteenth hymn of the decade.<- <note>The poet numbers the psalms by "decades" (tens); this is the 130th poem.</note>

**N**ever was my heart lifted up arrogantly within my mind,
nor did haughty insolence turn my eyes away;
nor did I ever keep a fluttering heart among great matters.
Nor did I stretch myself out toward wonders too faint for me, <note>Line highlighted in green. This refers to the psalmist not meddling in things "too great or too wonderful" for him.</note>
but always thinking the lowliest thoughts with gentleness;
and I stilled my voice into a profound silence, like an infant
child yearning for milk wails for its mother; <note>Line highlighted in green. This reflects the famous image of the "weaned child" in Psalm 131.</note>
so my heart shall follow in return.
Let Israel look to God the King with their minds,
both now and hereafter through continuous generations.

## Psalm 131 <note>Numbered as Psalm 132 in modern English Bibles.</note>

->From the thirteenth decade, calling out the first<-
->sweet and clear, soul-reviving song of the steps.<- <note>Line highlighted in green. The "steps" or "ascents" refers to the traditional grouping of Psalms 120–134.</note>

**R**emember David, and all of his gentleness.
He swore to the Immortal One, and made a vow to the King of Jacob:
"If ever I shall enter within the roofed house; <note>The poet uses the Homeric term "hymeentos" for a roofed or vaulted house.</note>

<meta>This page continues the poetic paraphrase of the Psalms by Apollinaris of Laodicea. Having concluded the brief Psalm 130 (English 131) on the previous page, the poet now begins a more expansive rendering of Psalm 131 (modern 132), which recalls David’s vow to find a home for the Ark of the Covenant.</meta>

# PSALM 131 <note>Numbered as Psalm 132 in modern English Bibles.</note>

...if I shall climb into the bed of most beautiful couches,
if ever I shall receive honey-sweet sleep upon my eyes,
if I shall allow my dear eyelids to slumber,
or choose rest for my temples,
until I find a precious place for the King,
so that I might find an eternal temple for the God of Jacob. <note>In this passage, David vows not to rest until he has established a permanent dwelling place for God in Jerusalem.</note>
We heard of this, as the report is sung throughout Ephrathah; <note>original: "Ephratha" — the ancient name for the area around Bethlehem.</note>
we found it, as the rumor goes, among the woods and glens. <note>original: "dryma" — referring to the "fields of the forest" or Jaar, where the Ark was kept before being brought to Jerusalem.</note>
Come, friends, let us go into the dwellings of the Immortal One,
and let us draw near the majestic floor where he set his foot.
Awake, All-King, and reach your place of rest;
and may you yourself raise up the chest of all-purity. <note>original: "phōriamon" — the poet uses this Greek word for a chest or coffer to describe the Ark of the Covenant.</note>
May your priests, Blessed One, be clothed in righteousness,
and may many holy ones rejoice in gladness.
For the sake of David, your mighty servant,
do not ever turn away your face from your Anointed. <note>original: "christou" — meaning the "Anointed One," referring here to the King, but often interpreted by early Christians as a reference to Jesus.</note>
The King swore to David to perform for all the people,
He swore and nodded assent, and he will not lie in his oaths:
to set the fruit of your womb upon the royal throne.
If your offspring shall keep this covenant of mine,
and the good testimonies which I myself shall teach their minds,
they shall never cease to sit upon your lofty seat;
for God has chosen the land of Zion,
since he desired it as a lovely and delightful home.
“This is my rest among endless generations;
here let me dwell, bound by a noble desire.
I would fill the houses of the needy widow with good things, <note>The poet adds "widows" to the traditional text, which speaks generally of the poor being satisfied with bread.</note>
and to the poor again I would grant a satisfying feast of life;
there may light surround the priest,
and may many holy ones rejoice in gladness.”

->M<- <note>A signature or printer's mark.</note>

<meta>This page continues the poetic paraphrase of the Psalms by Apollinaris of Laodicea. We are finishing Psalm 131 (modern 132), a song describing God’s promise to King David. Apollinaris uses Homeric-style Greek hexameters to elevate the biblical text into the form of a classical epic.</meta>

->APOLLINARIS<-

...fruit of your loins upon your throne. <note>This completes the sentence from the previous page, referring to God’s promise that David’s descendants would rule.</note>
Here I shall make an undying horn <note>original: "keras" — a symbol of strength and kingly power</note> sprout for David.
I have prepared a ready lamp for my Anointed One, <note>original: "christō" — refers to the King as the "Anointed," but carries messianic weight for the poet.</note> a guiding path;
I shall kindle shame and reproach upon his enemies,
but upon him, my holiness shall flourish.

### ->Psalm 132<- <note>Numbered as Psalm 133 in modern English Bibles. It is a famous song celebrating unity.</note>

This is the second hymn after the thirteenth decade, <note>Apollinaris numbers the psalms by "decades." The 13th decade is 130, so this is Psalm 132.</note>
and let there be an undying flow of fair-spoken wisdom.

What thing is beautiful for mortals, and what is made pleasant,
if not for brothers to dwell together in a dear home?
It is like the fragrant oil upon the head,
creeping down all the way to the beard—
the beard of the venerable Aaron— <note>Aaron was the first High Priest; the oil refers to the "anointing oil" used to consecrate him.</note>
and from his chin, running down to the very edge of his robe.
It is like the heavenly dew of many-flowered Hermon, <note>Mount Hermon is a high, snow-capped mountain known for its heavy dew.</note>
coming down upon the goat-pastured mountains of Zion.
For there the Shepherd <note>original: "poimēn" — a poetic title Apollinaris gives to God.</note> commanded every blessing:
to remain unceasingly throughout the days of life.

### ->Psalm 133<- <note>Numbered as Psalm 134 in modern English Bibles. This is the last of the "Songs of Ascents."</note>

Let this be the third hymn from the thirteenth decade.

All you servants of the Lord, sing to the Shepherd of the world,
you who stand in the house of the very-glorious King,
who attend to the house of God and his wide courts.
Throughout the night, lift up your hands in the holy dwellings,
and sing to the all-powerful All-King.
May the King grant you a ready praise from Zion,
he who made the green earth and the wide heaven.

### ->Psalm 134<- <note>Numbered as Psalm 135 in modern English Bibles.</note>

Ever may the journey go toward the fourth glorious hymn,
the "Hallelujah" song for the thirteenth decade.

--- Page 234 ---
<meta>This page continues the poetic paraphrase of the Psalms, transitioning into Psalm 135 (numbered as 134 in the Septuagint tradition). The previous page concluded the short "Song of Ascents" regarding brotherly unity. Here, the poet continues using Homeric hexameters—the meter of the Iliad and Odyssey—to frame the history of Israel as a grand epic.</meta>

->PSALM 135<- <note>Numbered as Psalm 134 in the Greek Septuagint. It is a hymn of praise for God’s sovereignty in nature and history.</note>
->M 2<-

Sing the name of the All-King; <note>original: "pambasilēos" — a majestic title used by this poet to emphasize God’s total sovereignty.</note>
Praise the singing Shepherd in song, O you His children, <note>original: "paides" — can mean children, servants, or disciples.</note>
You who stand in the house of the mighty King,
And who attend the well-fenced courtyards of God’s dwelling.
Sing to me of the King, for God is truly good;
Play the lyre to His name, for it is beautiful to sing this.
Because the Shepherd chose wise Jacob for Himself,
And made glorious Israel His own possession.
I know in my own heart how great the Shepherd is;
Our King surpasses all other blessed ones. <note>original: "makarōn" — a term often applied to the Greek gods; the poet uses it here to show God is greater than any "blessed" deity of the pagans.</note>
Doing all things that He desires in His most-excellent heart,
In the starry heaven, and on the much-nourishing earth,
In the deep foundations and in all the seas.
Lifting clouds from the ends of the earth to bring the rains,
He placed the rain in the air with lightning as signs.
The Lord Himself, steward of all, led the wind from His storehouse; <note>original: "thēkēn" — literally a chest or repository where the winds are kept.</note>
He subdued every first-born of the race of the Nile, <note>A poetic reference to the final plague of Egypt.</note>
Slaying the herds of four-footed beasts along with mortal men.
Performing ten thousand divine wonders in the midst of the Egyptian land;
Pharaoh knew this by his suffering, and his servants did not escape the ruin.
He Himself struck down ten thousand tribes of enemies,
Taking away mighty kings without sparing them:
Strong Sihon, king of the Amorites,
And mighty Og, the lord of heavy Bashan,
And the other kingdoms of Canaan, the child-slayer. <note>original: "paidophonēos" — likely an epithet referring to the historical violence or child-sacrifice practices associated with the Canaanite tribes in biblical narrative.</note>
Having taken their land, He distributed it as an inheritance,
An inheritance for His servant Israel, as was his due.
Let the name of the All-King be forever ageless;
From generation to generation let the good memorial of God endure;
For the Lord shall indeed judge for His own people.

<summary>This page contains a poetic Greek paraphrase of Psalm 135, praising God's power over nature and His historical deliverance of Israel from Egypt. The text uses epic Homeric vocabulary to describe the plagues of Egypt and the conquest of the kings Sihon and Og.</summary>
<keywords>Psalm 135, Homeric hexameters, Pharaoh, Exodus, Sihon, Og, inheritance of Israel, All-King, Shepherd</keywords>

--- Page 235 ---
<meta>This page continues the poetic paraphrase of the Psalms by Apollinaris of Laodicea. The text concludes the paraphrase of Psalm 134 (Septuagint numbering), focusing on the vanity of idols compared to the living God, and then begins Psalm 135 (the "Great Hallel"), famous for its repetitive refrain celebrating God’s enduring mercy.</meta>

# APOLLINARIS

...having a consoling remedy for his dear servants. <note>This line concludes a thought from the previous page regarding God's compassion for His people.</note>
The idols of the nations are gold and silver,
the feeble handiwork of the skilled hands of men.
No voice belongs to them, even if a mouth was fashioned for them;
they carry no light within their polished eyes;
ears they have only, but they do not hear a sound;
no holy scent of air reaches their nostrils;
they touch nothing at all, even if they are equipped with hands;
they will press no footprint upon the nourishing earth with their feet;
nor can they send forth a voice from their throat;
for indeed, no breath pours out through their mouth.
May the craftsmen become like unto their idols,
and all those who trust in them with their minds.
Sing to me of the King, O house of Israel;
O house of Aaron, shout to the All-King; <note>original: "pambasilēa" — a grand title meaning "King of All" or "Universal Sovereign."</note>
O glorious house of Levi, sing to the Shepherd of the world;
You whose hearts care for the fear of God, shout to the Sovereign.
Praiseworthy from Zion shall be the most high Sovereign,
dwelling in the God-pleasing houses around Jerusalem.

## Psalm 136 <note>Numbered as Psalm 135 in the Greek Septuagint. It is a liturgical hymn where every verse traditionally ends with the refrain "For His mercy endures forever."</note>

->To the thirteenth decade of glorious tidings,<-
->a sweet and clear fifth song is added.<- <note>The poet uses a riddle to identify the Psalm number: 13 decades (130) plus 5 equals Psalm 135.</note>

O peoples, sing of the gentleness of the All-King;
> because He ceaselessly increases His kindness. <note>This is the poet's hexameter version of the famous refrain "For His mercy endures forever."</note>
Sing of the immortal All-King of the blessed ones; <note>original: "makarōn" — a term for the divine. Here it refers to the "God of gods."</note>
> because He ceaselessly increases His kindness.
Sing to me of the King of mighty kings;
> because He ceaselessly increases His kindness.
Who alone knows how to perform unspeakable wonders;

# PSALM 136 <note>Numbered as Psalm 135 in the Greek Septuagint. This Psalm is known as the "Great Hallel," characterized by the rhythmic repetition of God's enduring mercy.</note>

...because he unceasingly increases his own mercy. <note>This line completes the first verse of the Psalm started on the previous page: "O peoples, sing [of the Lord]..."</note>
&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;He fashioned the starry heaven with wise counsel;
because he unceasingly increases his own mercy.
&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;He established the earth upon the waters, where it was not before;
because he unceasingly increases his own mercy.
&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;Who fashioned the mighty lights <note>referring to the sun and moon</note> by his own word;
because he unceasingly increases his own mercy.
&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;The shining sun, the ruler of the beautiful dawn;
because he unceasingly increases his own mercy.
&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;The stars and the moon, the queen of the nocturnal paths;
because he unceasingly increases his own mercy.
&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;He likewise subdued the offspring of the Egyptian firstborn;
because he unceasingly increases his own mercy.
&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;Taking Israel from their midst, he led them forth;
because he unceasingly increases his own mercy.
&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;With a truly bold hand, relying entirely upon his arm;
because he unceasingly increases his own mercy.
&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;Who many times split the Red Sea asunder;
because he unceasingly increases his own mercy.
&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;He led Israel through the middle of it as they marched;
because he unceasingly increases his own mercy.
&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;Who seized and slew the power of Pharaoh in the Red Sea;
because he unceasingly increases his own mercy.
&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;And he led Israel through the pathless land; <note>referring to the forty years of wandering in the wilderness.</note>
because he unceasingly increases his own mercy.
&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;Who with overmastering strength struck down great kings;
because he unceasingly increases his own mercy.
&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;And easily he plundered the most glorious lords;
because he unceasingly increases his own mercy.
&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;Mighty Sihon, the king of the Amorites; <note>Sihon was an ancient king who refused to let the Israelites pass through his territory; his defeat was a milestone in the journey to the Promised Land.</note>

M 3 <note>This "M 3" is a printer's signature, used by early modern bookbinders to keep the pages in the correct order.</note>

### APOLLINARIS <note>Apollinaris of Laodicea was a 4th-century bishop who rewrote the Psalms in the style of Homeric epic poetry to ensure Christian students could study classical meter without pagan content.</note>

because he unceasingly increases his own mercy.

-> PSALM 137 <- <note>Numbered as Psalm 136 in the Septuagint. This is the "By the Rivers of Babylon" psalm.</note>

From the thirteenth decade <note>original: <term>τρισκαιδεκάτης δεκάδος</term>, referring to the 130th series of psalms</note> of wise David,
a song of lamentation concerning Jeremiah <note>In the Septuagint tradition, this psalm is often associated with the prophet Jeremiah's perspective on the exile.</note>, which was made the sixth in its group,
concerning Jerusalem, when they came to the Babylonian waters.
&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;We wept as we sat beside the rivers of Babylon,
remembering Zion and its ever-lofty heights;
for in the midst of the rivers, upon the fruit-withering willow <note>original: <term>ὀλεσίκαρπον ἰτέαν</term>, a Homeric-style epithet meaning "fruit-spoiling" or "fruit-dropping."</note>
our clear-sounding lyres <note>original: <term>φόρμιγγες</term>, the stringed instrument of Greek epic bards.</note> were hung up.
There, our captors, lifting us up, spoke to us,
and those who carried us away commanded us to sing a hymn for them, saying:
"Speak a song from the melodies of glorious Zion."
&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;How is it possible for me to sing a song in a foreign land?
If I should forget you, much-beloved Jerusalem,
let my dear right hand be forgotten <note>This is a poetic rendering of the biblical "let my right hand wither" or "lose its skill."</note>.

# PSALM 137 <note>Numbered 136 in the Septuagint. This page begins mid-poem with the singer's oath of loyalty to Jerusalem.</note>

...may my tongue be parched and fixed to my throat,
if I were not always mindful of you in my heart,
so that I might set Jerusalem forward as my chief joy.
And you, Blessed One <note>original: <term>μάκαρ</term>, a common Homeric epithet for a god, here applied to the Lord.</note>, never forget the sons of Edom,
who said, "Make Jerusalem a ruin," as we held it dear;
those who once shouted, "Empty it, let it be emptied!
Do not spare it, until you reach its very foundations."
O daughter of Babylon, you shall be forever doomed;
blessed is the man who shall pay you back for your former violence,
who, coming upon you, shall seize and dash your infants against the rocks. <note>This jarring conclusion reflects the "lex talionis" (law of retaliation) and the deep trauma of the Judean captives whose own children were killed during the siege.</note>

With an unceasing heart, O King, I will sing your praise;

In the presence of the heavenly dwellings <note>original: <term>θεοδμήτων σταθάων</term>, literally "god-built stalls" or "stations," likely referring to the assembly of angels.</note>, O Blessed One, I will sing to you;

### PSALM 138 <note>Numbered 139 in the Hebrew Bible and modern English translations. This is one of the most famous hymns regarding God’s omnipresence and omniscience.</note>
->David sang a clear song about being turned back,<- <note>original: <term>διαστρεφὴς</term>. This refers to a "turning" or "conversion," likely alluding to the soul's return to God.</note>
->it was composed as the eighth song in the 130s.<-

You yourself, O King, have known me, and you have searched me out;
never do I escape you, whether I am going out or coming in. <note>original: <term>ἐξόδμιός τε καὶ ἐρχόδμιός</term>. These are rare, rhythmic terms for exiting and entering.</note>
From afar, you understood the thoughts within my breast,
tracing my footprint and my straight path;
not one of my ways was forgotten by your eyes.
I have not yet poured out an unrighteous word from my tongue,
and yet you already spoke, knowing both the last things and those of old.
Having fashioned me, you placed your hand upon my head.
Wonder seizes me when I consider your counsel <note>original: <term>μῆτιν</term>, the word used in the Odyssey for "cunning" or "wisdom."</note> in my heart;
nor am I able to fully grasp its great power with my mind.
Where then could I escape the great power of your spirit?
Where could I hide myself away from your sight?
If I should go to heaven, that is where your throne is built;
if I should go down to Hades <note>original: <term>ἀΐδην</term>, the Greek underworld, here used for the Hebrew "Sheol."</note>, you rule even among the dead.
If I should spread my wings on the swift-traveling winds,
or dwell in the furthest parts of the loud-roaring sea,
even there I would need the guidance of your hand,
and your right hand, so that my life might be made firm.
And I said: "Perhaps the man-destroying darkness <note>original: <term>φθισίμβροτος ὄρφνη</term>, a vivid Homeric epithet for the gloom of night or death.</note> will hide me";
yet the night became my delight, and the gloom drove away the light.
For your light has known how to cover even the darkness,

<header>APOLLINARIS</header>

And light-bringing day <term>φαεσίμβροτον</term> <note>A classic Homeric epithet meaning "bringing light to mortals," usually applied to the Sun (Helios) or the Dawn (Eos).</note> brings the dark night to an end; no one could fail to know whether there were thick darkness or light. For you, O King, took possession of my vitals; <note>original: <term>νεφροῖς</term> (kidneys). In ancient biology, the kidneys were seen as the seat of the conscience and hidden emotions.</note> my heart, O Blessed One, was within the womb of the mother who bore me. 

I will cry out to my King, I who am a mortal made in a terrifying way; <note>This mirrors the famous line: "I am fearfully and wonderfully made."</note> my soul, O Blessed One, has recognized your works with great wonder. You know the bones of mine which you fashioned in secret, and the strength of my life is found in your foundations on high. 

You saw me, O Lord, while I was still unformed in the womb of my mother; all things were written together upon your pure book of wondrous works. <note>original: <term>δαιδάλων</term>. This word implies intricate, artistic craftsmanship, like the work of the mythical smith Daedalus.</note> Nor was anyone present openly while they were being crafted. But truly, your friends are held in high honor by me; the power of those who rule appears especially superior. 

I would count them more than the sand. Having awakened, I still remain continuously with you. If only you would slay the servants of wickedness! I command my spirit to flee quickly from bloodthirsty men, those who speak vain things before your gates. 

Did I not hate those who hate you, O Lord? My soul wasted away because of your enemies. I hated all those enemies with a most perfect hatred, and they were hostile to my heart. But search out my mind with great care; examine my tracks with inescapable testing. See if I have been kept unacquainted with the path of evils, so that you might lead me in the everlasting ways.

## PSALM 139 <note>Numbered 140 in the Hebrew Bible. This is a prayer for deliverance from "evil men" and "violent men."</note>

->This too was a clear melody of David’s song,<-
->the ninth song from the thirteenth decade.<- <note>Apollinaris numbers the psalms by "decades" (groups of ten). This is the 139th psalm.</note>

--- Page 241 ---
<meta>This page transitions from the end of the paraphrase of Psalm 139 (Septuagint 138) into the beginning and completion of Psalm 140 (Septuagint 139). # APOLLINARIS

If only, O Blessed One, you would save me from human wickedness, and grant that I might escape a lawless man. For they have pondered lawless things within their hearts, and all day long they equipped themselves for destructive battles. They have sharpened their tongues like those of evil snakes, having the man-killing poison of an asp beneath their lips. 

If only I might be guarded from the hateful hand of the sinners; you will easily save me from unjust men, O Blessed One. They were intending in their hearts to trip up my steps. Arrogant men fixed snares for me in secret; they stretched out nets of twine around my path; they placed stumbling-blocks <term>δολῇσιν</term> <note>Literally "deceits" or "treacherous traps," here used to describe the moral snares of the psalmist's enemies.</note> upon the ways.

I said to God the King: "You are my hope, O God; hear my supplication, O Shepherd most high." You are unquenchable, O Blessed One, and have become the light of my life; you protect my head as a shadow in the heat of battle. Do not scatter my desire, and do not grant glory to the lawless. Do not leave me, O Blessed One, to be the victim of their intent, lest they triumph and go mad with pride. 

Let the evil-devising splendor of their own head surround them, and let the toil of their destructive lips cover them. May coals of unwearying fire <term>ἀκαμάτοιο πυρὸς</term> <note>A classic Homeric formula used to describe a fire that never grows tired or burns out.</note> fall upon them; may you cast them down to the earth with fire rushing from above. Never shall they be subdued in heart by painful evils. 

May the evil-devising hour overtake the man of the streets <term>λαυραγορίην</term> <note>This term refers to a "street-talker" or a slanderer who gossips in the alleyways (laurae).</note>; and may lawless calamities hunt him in his own soul. The King shall well perform unshaken justice for the poor, and the right of the needy shall never escape his sight. Nevertheless, the holy race of mortals shall sing to you; and the upright shall dwell together in the sight of the King.

# PSALM 141

### Psalm 141.

->I sing a glorious hymn with my beloved lyre<-
->Of David, the hundred and forty-first.<- <note>original: "δεκάτης καὶ τετράκις δεκάδος" — The Greek verse literally counts to fifty, but the header and context identify this as the 141st Psalm (Septuagint 140).</note>

O King, hear the prayers of our shouting; stretch out your ever-hearing ear to my supplication, nor let me call upon your helping hand in vain, O Blessed One. Let this prayer of yours be directed like incense; and I lift up my hands like an evening sacrifice. Set a guard around our teeth and a fence <term>ἕρκος ὀδοῦσι</term> <note>A famous Homeric expression ("the fence of the teeth") used here to describe the physical boundary of the mouth that should restrain speech.</note> for them, and fix secure bolts upon the gates of my lips.

Turn my soul away from lawless words, and let me not care for the pretext of sin with its arrows, joined with men of evil-working lawlessness; let me not be seen among those indiscriminate people, even in the marketplaces. May I be subdued by the mercy and the rebukes of the holy; but let me never be anointed on the head with the deadly oil of a wicked man. 

Still my prayer is against their devices; let their judges be swallowed up near the rock. Then let them hear my bold and powerful words, which are like a hill split and torn from its very root; even so their bones have been scattered here near the gates of Hades. 

I have yearned for you, O Blessed One, and I have stretched out my eyes to you; do not drive away the heart of one who hopes in you. Quickly save me from the deceptive snares of which they were the companions, and from the evil-stitching wickedness of those who stir up trouble. May the net of the Immortal catch those laborers of wickedness; but let me pass by in peace, alone and keeping watch.

### Psalm 141.

A poem of the wise David <note>original: "Δαβίδδος πινυτῆς"</note> when he, haunting the cave, sent up a prayer to God the King, crying aloud; nevertheless, this is the hundred and forty-first <note>In the Septuagint (Greek Old Testament) tradition followed here, this is Psalm 141. In most modern English Bibles based on the Hebrew text, this is Psalm 142.</note> together in this first great concern.

I sent up a cry to our King, crying aloud, <margin>(141.</margin>
and speaking, I entreated the merciful Shepherd of the world;
may I pour out my supplication before the sight of the King of All,
and may I report to him my much-suffering toil.
When my spirit was struck with trembling and confusion,
you observed my paths with your divine eyes.
They were hiding deceitful nets <term>λίνα</term> <note>Literally "linen threads," but used here in the Homeric sense of hunting nets or snares.</note> around my paths;
I gazed toward my right hand, stretching my eyes to see,
nor did any of my friends think to notice me at all.
My means of escape has perished before me as I long for flight;
nor is there anyone who seeks out my soul as I flee.
But I, shouting, said: "You have become my hope,

look upon my supplication, for I have been greatly brought low.
Save me from the destructive malice of my enemies,
because they are much stronger than I in their might.
Would that you would lead my soul out of this unbreakable prison,
so that I may wisely sing of your name;
generations of the righteous await my reward from you."

# PSALM 143 <note>original: "Ψ Α Λ Μ. ρμγ". Following the Septuagint numbering, this is Psalm 142; in modern English Bibles, it is Psalm 143.</note>

Hearken to our prayer, most excellent Shepherd,
yield to my entreaties as I call out in truth;
may you hear me as I cry out, O Lord, in your righteousness;
and do not enter into a contest of judgment with me.
For no mortal <term>μερόπων</term> <note>A Homeric term meaning "articulate-speaking men," often used to signify the fragility of the human race.</note> could appear holy before you.
The enemy has persecuted my groaning soul, driving me on;
he has brought my life low into the fallow lands of the earth,
making me sit in darkness like the dead of ages past;
and my spirit is fettered by dense helplessness.
Distressed by sorrows, my heart within me is troubled.
But I, remembering the time of former days,
meditated in my mind on the God-given works of the King of All;
and carefully I recounted the divine works of your hands,
stretching out my hands to the much-invoked King.
My soul longs for you, like the furrows of a thirsty land;
hear me quickly, before I give up my spirit of thoughts.
Do not turn your glory-bringing face <note>original: "κυδιάνειραν... ὀπωπήν"</note> away from me,
lest I seem like those going down into the pit.
O Blessed One, let me hear of your mercy in the morning;
for you have become for us a hope that never grows old.
Grant me a law, a favorable path where I must travel;
because I hold my soul always fluttering toward you.
Most Blessed One, save me quickly from the hand of opponents,
since, in my fear, I have always found you an unbreakable tower.
You yourself are my Lord; teach me your mind;
and let the good Spirit of God lead me to the level land.
Grant me a law of righteousness, preserving my life for your sake.
O Lord, save my soul from groaning toil;
and destroy my opponents because of your mercy.
Subduing all the destroyers of my soul, O King;

# Psalm 143 <note>original: "Ψαλμὸς ρμγ̅". This follows the Septuagint numbering; it is Psalm 144 in modern English Bibles.</note>

Blessed be God always with auspicious songs,
who has armored my palms for battle;
and fashioned the iron-strong fingers of our hands.
O Lord, you have always been my mercy and my tower,
delivering me from evil, and warding off the overbearing,

subduing my people under our hands.
What is a mortal, O Lord, that you should appear openly to him,
or the offspring of man <term>μεροπῆς</term> <note>original: "μεροπῆς" (meropes). An ancient Homeric epithet for humans, meaning "articulate-speaking," used here to contrast human frailty with divine majesty.</note>, that you should honor him so in your heart?

his days pass by like his own misty shadow.

But I will sing a new song to you, O Most Supreme;
upon the harmony of the ten-stringed lyre I will sing your praise;
You who grant the light of life to our kings,
and delivered your servant David from the cruel sword.

# Psalm 143 <note>original: "Ψ Α Λ Μ. ρμγ.". This corresponds to Psalm 144 in modern English Bibles.</note>

Rescue me mightily from the hands of foreign sons,
whose mouth has spoken empty-boasted words in vanity;
and their right hand, lawless, always acts in deceit.
Their sons are like young saplings in the bloom of youth;
and for their daughters, there is such great beauty of ornament,
one would say their eyes behold a gold-crowned temple <term>χρυσάμπυκα νηὸν</term> <note>original: "chrysampyka nehon." A "chrysampyx" was a gold headband worn by noblewomen or used to adorn the horses of gods in Homeric poetry. Apollinaris uses this vivid imagery to describe the architectural elegance and beauty of the daughters of the prosperous.</note>.
Their storehouses are heavy, overflowing with all kinds of goods;
from one to another there flows an unutterable abundance.
For them, well-fed sheep and many offspring are brought forth,
and fat cattle, and an abundance of the finest grain.
May all the wounds of ruin stay far from them,
nor may there be any cry in their broad streets.
All men count as happy those people for whom such things are;
yet truly blessed is the people whose God provides for them in His heart.

Earth’s tribes shall cry out to your tribes, Most Blessed,
and they shall tell of your air-traversing strength <term>σθένος ἀεροπόλον</term> <note>original: "sthenos aeropolon." <meta>This page continues the poetic paraphrase of the Psalms by Apollinaris of Laodicea, written in the style of Homeric hexameters. The text here continues David’s hymn of praise, focusing on God’s majesty, mercy, and providence over all creation.</meta>

# APOLLINARIS <note>original: "Α Π Ο Λ Ι Ν Α Ρ." The author, Apollinaris of Laodicea, was a 4th-century bishop who rewrote much of the Bible in classical Greek poetic forms to ensure Christian students could study high literature without relying on pagan myths.</note>

And all your wondrous works, O Most Blessed, are declared;
The report of your fearful deeds <term>δείματι</term> <note>original: "deimati." Literally "in fear" or "dread." The poet suggests that the sheer awe of God's power leaves men trembling as they speak of it.</note> shall remain on their lips.
May your great and boasted works never pass by unspoken;
May men ever-eloquently proclaim your unshakable strength.
They shall be clothed in the righteousness of the King of All;
The King is most high, compassionate, and merciful,
Possessing a patient heart, and increasing his own mercy.
God has learned to maintain gentleness toward all,
Pitying the works as many as he fashioned with his own hands.
With a tireless heart, most blessed one, the divine world shall cry out to you,
And the race of the holy ones shall sing your glorious praise.
They shall speak of the most high glory of your kingship,
And they shall hymn your unshakable strength, O Lord;
So that your immortal strength may be known to mortal men,
And the magnificent glory of your lovely kingdom.
Your dominion ever rules over every generation.
God weaves faithfulness around his pure words, <note>This line and the following correspond to the verse often missing from the standard Hebrew Bible but found in the Septuagint and Dead Sea Scrolls: "The Lord is faithful in all his words and holy in all his works."</note>
And his justice is blended with his works.
The Immortal One has learned to always raise those who fall,
To straighten the man who has fallen under a destructive blow.
All things that you have fashioned look unto you, Blessed One;
<margin>see: folio 136</margin> And you give to them all their immortal food in due season.
Filling every kind of creature, whenever you open your hand.
Justice follows the paths of the King from every side;
And out of his good leadership, the Holy One shines through his works.
He stands near to everyone who prays, and guards them—
To whoever would soothe him with wholehearted supplication.
He ever fulfills the desire of his servants;
And hearing their supplication, he shall deliver them from ruin.
The Lord ever saves his own laborers from wickedness;

# PSALM 145 <note>original: "Ψ Α Λ Μ. ρμε̅." This is Psalm 145 in the Septuagint (LXX) numbering, which corresponds to Psalm 146 in modern English Bibles.</note>
-> N <-

But he shall subdue the servants of wickedness with destruction.

### Psalm 145

This was a common hymn of Zechariah and Haggai, <note>In the Septuagint tradition, several of the final "Hallelujah" psalms are attributed to the prophets Haggai and Zechariah, who encouraged the rebuilding of the Temple after the Babylonian exile.</note>
Having the fifth combined sound of "Alleluia." <note>This refers to its position as the fifth of the final group of Hallelujah psalms in the Greek tradition.</note>
Nevertheless, it is truly the one hundred and fortieth. <note>original: "ἑκατοστὸς ἔφυ καὶ τεσσαρακοστός." Curiously, the poem identifies this as the 140th psalm, despite the header marking it as 145. This may reflect a different numbering system used by the poet or a variant in the manuscript tradition.</note>

Sing, my dear soul, to God who rules over your life;
I will hymn the much-entreated King as long as I walk;
I will continuously serve the Immortal One with the lyre in song.
Never be bold in your heart toward leaders,
Nor toward the children of mortals, from whom salvation <term>φάος</term> <note>original: "phaos." Literally "light." In Homeric and biblical Greek, light is frequently a metaphor for safety, deliverance, or salvation.</note> never comes.
When their breath returns, they go back to their own earth;
And with them, all the plans of those who die perish utterly.
Blessed is he whose helper is the All-Highest God,
And whose hope rests in the commands of the King,
Who fashioned the green earth and the wide heaven,
The loud-roaring sea, and all that the ocean swell nourishes.
Always eager in his mind, he keeps the truth;
He who reveals justice to those who are oppressed;
And gives life-sustaining food <term>ζωαρκέα δαῖτα</term> <note>original: "zoarkea daita." A poetic compound meaning a feast or meal that suffices to support life.</note> to all the hungry.
He himself knows how to save those bound in heavy chains;
The Lord himself raises up men from the terrible fall;
The Lord himself makes a wise mind for the blind;
The King of All himself loves the holy race;
The Lord himself has learned to protect the stranger,
And to lift up the widow and the orphan in his arms.
But he shall wipe out the mournful way of sinners.

<summary>This page contains the conclusion of Psalm 144 and the beginning of Psalm 145 (LXX), emphasizing God's role as the creator and the protector of the vulnerable.</summary>
<keywords>Psalm 145, Psalm 146, Apollinaris of Laodicea, hexameter, Haggai, Zechariah, Alleluia, protection of orphans, providence</keywords>

### APOLLINAR.

All your wondrous works shall be declared, O most blessed one;
And let your work, which cannot be held back, remain on the tongue with awe.
Nor let your boast-worthy deeds pass by unspoken;
Let one speak of your unshakable <note>original: "ἀσυφέλικτον" (asyfelikton) — a Homeric term meaning unmoved or firm.</note> strength with eternal eloquence;
And let them be clothed in the righteousness of the King of All.
The King most high is compassionate and merciful,
Having a patient heart, and ever-increasing his own mercy.
God has learned to keep kindness <note>original: "μειλιχίην" (meilichiēn) — gentleness or soothing kindness.</note> among all,
Pitying all the works that he fashioned with his own hand.
With a ceaseless heart, let all life cry out to you, most blessed one,
And let the race of the holy sing your glorious praise.
Let them speak of the most high glory of your kingship,
And they shall hymn your unshakable strength, O Lord;
So that you may show your imperishable strength to mortal men, <note>original: "μερόπεσσιν" (meropessin) — a classic Homeric epithet for humans, literally "articulate-speaking."</note>
And the magnificent glory of your lovely kingdom.
Your kingdom is forever, ruling over every generation.
With pure words, let God weave faith about [his works],
And for him, justice is mingled with the lives of men. <note>This verse reflects a Greek addition found in the Septuagint: "The Lord is faithful in all His words, and holy in all His works."</note>
The Immortal One has always known how to raise those who fall,
To set upright the man who has fallen under a destructive ruin.
All things look toward you, Blessed One, crouching in your presence;
<margin>see folio 136</margin>
And you give to all their imperishable food in due season.
You fill every living kind when you spread out your hand.
Justice follows the paths of the King from every side;
And he shines with holiness through his good leadership in our lives.
He stands around everyone who prays, and guards them—
Whosoever would soothe him with whole-hearted supplication.
He always fulfills the desire of his servants;
And hearing their supplication, he shall deliver them from calamity.
The Lord always saves his companions from wickedness;

### PSALM 146

But he shall subdue the servants of wickedness with destruction. 
Let my faithful mouth sing the praise of the King of All, 
And let the vast tribes of mortals chant the glorious name of God, 
From generation to generation among unceasing lineages.

->Psalm 146<-

The hymn of Zechariah and Haggai is held in common, 
Having the "Alleluia" <note>original: "ἀλληλούϊα" — a Hebrew liturgical call meaning "Praise Yahweh," preserved here in Greek transliteration.</note> as its fifth collective cry. 
Nevertheless, it remains the hundred and forty-sixth [psalm].

**S**ing, my dear soul, to God who rules over your life; 
I will hymn the much-entreated King as long as I walk; 
I will continuously serve the Immortal One with hymns upon the lyre. 
Never be overconfident in your mind toward leaders, 
Nor in the children of mortals, from whom salvation <note>original: "φάος" (phaos) — literally "light," but in the Homeric tradition used to mean deliverance or safety in battle.</note> never arises. 
When their breath departs, they return again to their own earth; 
For them, likewise, all their burning plans perish. 
Blessed is he whose helper is the God most high, 
And whose hope rests upon the commands of the King, 
Who fashioned the earth and the wide heaven, <gloss>(of the sea</gloss>
The loud-roaring sea, and all that the swell of the sea nourishes. 
He who, being eager, guards the truth within his heart forever; 
Who reveals justice for those who are oppressed, 
And gives a life-sustaining feast to all who are hungry. 
He himself knows how to save the one bound in chains; 
He himself raises up men from a terrible fall; 
The Lord himself makes the mind of the blind wise; 
The King of All himself dearly loves the holy race. 
The Lord himself has learned to guard the newly-arrived man <note>original: "νεήλυδα φῶτα" (neēlyda phōta) — a poetic way of describing the "stranger" or "sojourner" mentioned in the Bible.</note>, 
And to lift up the widow and the orphan in his arms. 
But may he bring to ruin the mournful way of sinners.

And all your divine wonders are declared, O most blessed one;
It is not fitting to keep silent about such a work in the mouth through fear.
Let your great-boasting works not pass by unuttered;
I shall declare your unshakable strength with constant wisdom;
And let them be steeped in the righteousness of the King of All. <note>original: "παμβασιλῆος" (pambasileus) — a title meaning King of All or Universal Monarch, emphasizing God's total sovereignty.</note>
The King most high is compassionate and merciful,
Possessing a patient spirit and increasing his own mercy.
God has learned to maintain gentleness toward all,
Pitying the works as many as he fashioned with his hands.
With an unceasing heart, O most blessed, the path <note>The Greek "βῆμα" (bema) usually refers to a step or a judgment seat; here it likely personifies the created order or the "walk" of the faithful crying out to God.</note> shall cry out to you,

God weaves faith around his pure words,

The Immortal One has always known how to raise those who fall,

<note>Ligature for "and" (καὶ) used at the start of the line</note> And you give to all alike their life-giving food in due season.

Hearing their supplication, he will deliver them from calamity.

### Psalm 146

->This was a common hymn of Zechariah and Haggai,<- <note>Refers to the post-exilic prophets Zechariah and Haggai, to whom the Septuagint often attributes the "Hallelujah" psalms.</note>
->having its fifth-sounding cry without celebration.<- <note>The Greek "πεμπταῖον... θρόον" likely refers to its liturgical placement or a specific musical mode used in the ancient church.</note>
->Yet truly, it was the one hundred and forty-sixth.<- <note>In the Septuagint numbering, this is Psalm 145; in the Hebrew/KJV, it is Psalm 146. The author notes its place in the sequence.</note>

Sing of God, O dear soul, who rules over your life;
I shall hymn the much-entreated King as long as I walk the earth;
I shall continuously hymn the Immortal One with the lyre.
Never let your heart be confident in earthly leaders,
Nor in the children of mortals, from whom no light of salvation ever sprang.
When their spirit departs, they return again to their own earth;
And together with them as they perish, all their worldly plans vanish.
Blessed is he for whom the most high God is a helper,
And whose hope rests upon the commands of the King,
Who fashioned the green earth and the wide heaven,
The loud-roaring sea, and all that the swell of the sea nourishes.
He always keeps watch with an unswervable mind;
He reveals justice for those who are oppressed;
And he gives a life-sustaining feast to all who hunger.
He himself knows how to save the one bound in cruel chains;
The Lord himself raises up men shivering from their fall;
The Lord himself grants a wise mind to the blind;
The King of All himself dearly loves the race of the holy;
The Lord himself has learned to guard the stranger,
And he lifts up the widow and the orphan in his arms;
But he brings to ruin the mournful path of the sinful.
