<header>APOLLINARIS</header>

The pure one shall reign unceasingly and with great glory,
The God of Zion ruling from generation to generation.

### Psalm 146

->This was a common hymn of Zechariah and Haggai,<- <note>As with the previous psalm, the Septuagint attributes this song to the post-exilic prophets Zechariah and Haggai.</note>
->possessing a sixth-day cry of truth;<- <note>original: "ἐκταῖον... θρόον" — This likely refers to its liturgical use on the sixth day of the week (Friday) or a specific musical pitch.</note>
->nevertheless, it was the one hundred and forty-sixth.<-

Sing of the King of All, since a noble song has been fashioned.
Praise shall be pleasing to our King.
The glorious Lord is building Jerusalem, making it a delight to God.
He shall gather the scattered tribe of Israel.
He is the physician to those whose heart is broken,
And he shall bind up the wounds of their suffering. <note>The Greek "περιστίλξοι μογεδῶνας" uses a rare, poetic term for binding or soothing pains.</note>
He alone knows the number of the unmeasurable stars,
Calling all the ends of the heavens by name.
Our God is great, and great is his strength;
No man has perceived the glorious measures of the Blessed One's wisdom.
The gentle King knows how to spare the gentle-hearted,
But he subdues the impious in the lowest parts of the earth.
Beginning your prayer, celebrate the King with divine song;
Sing to the King of All, to God, with a clear-toned lyre, <note>original: "φόρμιγγι λιγείῃ" — The 'phorminx' was the ancient stringed instrument used by Homeric bards.</note>
Who covers the brazen heaven with his clouds,
Preparing his life-giving rain for his earth.
Who makes so much grass grow even on the high-foliaged mountains,
Raising up honey-sweet herb for the beasts of burden of mortals.
Who provides unfailing food to the herds of four-footed beasts,
And satisfies the young of the ravens when they cry in prayer.
The Lord does not praise the immense strength of a horse;
Nor does he at all admire the swiftness of a man's legs. <note>A poetic rendering of Psalm 147:10: "His delight is not in the strength of the horse, nor his pleasure in the legs of a man."</note>
Rather, he will show his goodness toward his servants.

<header>PSALM 147</header>

To those for whom mercy is a heartfelt and unceasing desire. <note>This line concludes the previous psalm's thought on God's favor toward those who hope in His mercy.</note>

### Psalm 147

->This was a shared hymn of Zechariah and Haggai,<-
->possessing a disputed seventh-day cry in its message;<- <note>original: "ἕβδομον ἀμφήριστον" — Like the previous psalm, this refers to a liturgical tradition or a specific musical pitch associated with the seventh day (Sabbath).</note>
->nevertheless, it was the one hundred and forty-seventh.<-

Praise, O Jerusalem, the all-powerful King;
Zion, celebrate in song your King of life;
For he has forged the sturdy bars of your gates;
Within you, he has blessed your wise and glorious children.
He is the one who established your borders in peace,
And filled you with the rich strength of the marrow-bearing grain. <note>original: "μυελοφόρεος ἀκτῆς" — A classic Homeric epithet for grain, literally "marrow-carrying," denoting its life-giving richness.</note>
He is the one who sent his resounding word to the much-nourishing earth,
And it shall go here and there, running swiftly;
Spreading the power of cold snow from on high like wool;
Strewing the parching mist like smoky ash;
Hurling down the ice-crystals shaped like pebbles; <note>original: "ψαμμοῖσιν" — literally "sand" or "pebbles," comparing the size of hail or sleet to small stones.</note>
Who can withstand the strength of his cold?
All things shall melt at his living word when it is sent forth;
The waters shall murmur under his breezes;
Proclaiming his dear word to wise Jacob,
And telling his glorious judgments to famous Israel;
Never has he done such for foreign nations,
Nor has he revealed his righteous decrees to their peoples.

<meta>This page continues the "Metaphrasis Psalmorum" (Poetic Paraphrase of the Psalms) attributed to Apollinaris of Laodicea. Having concluded the previous section, it now presents a complete poetic rendition of Psalm 148 in dactylic hexameter, the meter of Homer’s Iliad and Odyssey.</meta>

<header>APOLLINARIS</header>

Sing for me the great glory of the King from heaven;
sing of him with shouts upon the highest thrones;
sing of him, you hosts of mighty armies;
sing of him, all you renowned company of powers;
sing, both the sun and the gleaming moon;
sing to God, all you stars and the unspeakable light;
sing above the wide heaven on the floors of the firmament,
and the glorious water above the heavenly spheres.
Let all together sing the famous name of the Sovereign King;
for he himself spoke, and all things sprang into being,
and by his unbending commands they were rooted firm.
They stood forever by the command that he himself ordained.
Never shall the divine decree of the Blessed One pass away.
Sing to the Sovereign King upon the wide-wayed earth, <note>original: "εὐρυοδείης" — A standard Homeric epithet for the earth, emphasizing its vast paths.</note>
both the secret dragons <note>original: "δράκοντες" — referring to the great sea monsters or "tanninim" mentioned in the Hebrew text.</note> and the murky abysses;
fire with ice-crystals, and snow following with hail,
and the air-wandering breath of the dark storm,
ever serving the unerring word of God;
the mountains, and the shaggy-necked peaks of the tree-crowned hills,
all kinds of cedars, and flourishing trees,
herds of four-footed cattle, and the mountain beasts of the earth,
creeping things, and all winged birds that soar on high,
all kinds of peoples, and mighty kings,
law-giving judges alongside all the leaders,
noble youths and maidens in their trailing robes, <note>original: "ἑλκεσίπεπλοι" — A classic Homeric description of women, literally "those who drag their robes."</note>
strong young men and elders coming together as one,
let them sing with one voice the famous name of the Sovereign King;
for his royal name alone has been exalted.
His praise covers both the earth and the heaven,
and he lifts up the triumphant horn of his own people. <note>In biblical imagery, the "horn" represents strength, power, and victory.</note>

And let a song of fair fame be the care of the righteous in their hymns;

### Psalm 149.

-> This sweet song is the one hundred and forty-ninth, <-
-> bearing a heavenly throne of equal age. <note>original: "ὁμηλίκια" — This term suggests the psalms are of a "matching age" or "contemporaneous" with the celestial order.</note> <-

Sing for me a lovely song to the new King; <note>original: "βασιλῆϊ νέῳ" — A reference to the Messiah or Christ, interpreted as the 'New King' in this poetic tradition.</note>
always a remembered hymn in the assembly of the holy ones.
Let Israel rejoice, trusting in his dear Lord.
Let the sons of Zion in turn delight in their King.
Let them sing the royal name in choral dances;
let them sing to him, raising the drum and the lyre;
because his own people have become dear to his heart.
And with light he has exalted the cherished honor of the gentle-hearted.
There, let the lovely generations of the holy ones exult;
and in their sacred beds let them be glad with a dear heart.
Bearing the high praises of the King in their throats,
having a two-edged sword in the straight-striking palms of their hands,
to destroy the evil-doing men of all kinds of nations;
and to rebuke the peoples who cast off the immortal yoke.
And he has bound kings in painful shackles, <note>original: "ἀλυκτοπέδῃσι" — A Homeric term for inescapable or "galling" chains.</note>
fastening their hands in iron fetters.
He will fulfill the written, unspoken law among them.
Thus noble glory was brought to pass among all his holy ones.

### Psalm 150.

-> The song of the fifteenth decade [the 150th], of equal age. <-

Sing for me the King, and let the dances of the righteous follow.
Sing of him, most powerful in the firm strength of his might.

<header>A P O L L I N A R I S</header>

<note>The following lines conclude the poetic paraphrase of Psalm 150, which calls for all creation and all instruments to praise God.</note>
Sing to him with your own swift-running flowers of song; <note>original: "ἀϋδρόμοισιν ἀνθέεσσι" — A poetic metaphor where "flowers" represent verses or melodies.</note>
Sing to him, who is greatly glorified in his many works;
Sing to him with the crashes of the strong-sounding trumpet;
Sing to him with the lyre and lovely harmony.
Sing to him with drums and well-spoken strings;
Sing to him with the kithara <note>A professional version of the lyre used in ancient Greece.</note> and with clear-sounding strings;
Sing to him, striking clear-ringing cymbals with your hands;
Sing to him with those who hold cymbals of shouting in their palms.
Let every breath of every kind sing out to the All-King.

## Psalm 151.

-> This hymn stands outside the fifteen decades <note>The "fifteen decades" refers to the 150 psalms that make up the standard biblical Psalter.</note> <-
-> which David sang when he fought Goliath in single combat. <-

<margin>M</margin>
Among my brothers, a great number were born,
but I was the youngest boy in the house, listening after all,
shepherding flocks of woolly sheep in the fields.
My hands fashioned a clear-toned lyre,
and my fingers crafted a harmony pleasing to God.
Who might bring word to my strong King?
The Lord himself has learned to hear my words.
He himself sent a swift messenger from the heavenly host,
and took me from my father’s flock of sheep,
and he himself anointed my body with rich oil.
Truly, my brothers were tall and noble;
but the King did not wish to grant them noble glory.
I went against a man of another race who challenged me,
but he cursed me, trusting in his idols;
but drawing his sharp sword from his thigh,
I stood over him and cut the head from his towering neck;
and I drove away the disgrace from wise-minded Israel.

<vocab>Psalm 151, Apollinaris, David, Goliath, Israel, Metaphrasis</vocab>

I went out to meet the Philistine stranger,

beheaded him, and took away the reproach

-> I offer praise to you, God most high, King of kings, <note>original: "Αἶνον ἄγω σοὶ Θεὸν ὑψίστατε ὦν βασιλεῦσιν"</note> <-
-> Because you have safely helped me to complete this whole book, <-
-> To finish winding <term>winding: "ἐκτολυπεῦσαι" — A textile metaphor meaning to finish a long and complex task, like unwinding a ball of wool.</term> the beautiful work of the wise David, <-
-> [You who] granted strength to my wretched <note>original: "παναπότμον" — literally "all-unfortunate" or "ill-fated," a common expression of humility by ancient authors.</note> race among men nonetheless. <-

<column-break/>

### Translator's Lexicon

| Greek Term | English Translation | Definition |
| :--- | :--- | :--- |
| **Αἶνον** | **Praise** | A song of celebration or a hymn of glory. |
| **Θεὸς** | **God** | The Divine, often used here in the Homeric "God-King" style. |
| **Δαβίδου** | **Of David** | Referring to the biblical King David, the traditional author of the Psalms. |
| **ἐκτολυπεῦσαι** | **To complete** | Literally to "wind off" a task, as one finishes spinning thread. |
| **γενέθλην** | **Race / Lineage** | One's birth, family, or the human condition itself. |
| **παναπότμον** | **Wretched** | Extremely unfortunate; used here as a gesture of the author's humility. |
